### [1] [Pixel-Precise Explainable Stress Indexing: A Semantic Segmentation Framework for Disease Severity Quantification in Field Crops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06585)
*Raunak Kumar,Soumyashree Kar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Unified deep learning pipeline for plant stress quantification using segmentation, regression-based severity estimation, and classification. U-Net with MobileNetV2 achieves top performance on the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset; severity correlates strongly with expert scores; suitable for real-time use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual stress assessment is labor-intensive and subjective, contributing to errors and yield losses. There is a need for accurate, scalable, real-time quantification of plant stress to enable precision agriculture.

Method: A pipeline combining semantic segmentation (evaluated models: U-Net with MobileNetV2, SegFormer, FCN, PSPNet), regression-based severity estimation, and disease classification. Severity is categorized into four levels (Low to Very High) based on the proportion of infected leaf area. Evaluated on the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset (1,641 samples, six classes). Metrics include pixel accuracy, meanIoU (mIoU), and detection accuracy; processing speed reported as 14.7 ms per image.

Result: U-Net with MobileNetV2 yields best performance: 98.20% pixel accuracy, 0.70 mIoU, and 99.41% detection accuracy, at 14.7 ms per image. SegFormer achieves competitive mIoU (0.66); FCN and PSPNet show lower spatial accuracy (~0.49 mIoU). The computed severity index correlates strongly with expert annotations (r = 0.968, R^2 = 0.937).

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline provides reliable automated crop monitoring and decision support with real-time capabilities, leveraging segmentation to quantify stress and a severity index aligned with expert assessments.

Abstract: Plant diseases, resulting from both biotic and abiotic stresses, cause an estimated 20-40% loss in global agricultural yield annually, resulting in economic damages exceeding USD 220 billion. Accurate and scalable stress quantification is essential for precision agriculture, yet traditional manual assessments are labour-intensive and subjective. This paper proposes a unified deep learning pipeline integrating semantic segmentation, regression-based severity estimation, and disease classification. Stress severity is categorised into four levels (Low to Very High) based on the proportion of infected leaf area. Experiments on the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset (1,641 samples, six classes) evaluate four models: U-Net (MobileNetV2), SegFormer, FCN, and PSPNet. U-Net with MobileNetV2 achieves the best performance with 98.20% pixel accuracy, 0.70 mIoU, and 99.41% detection accuracy at 14.7 ms per image, making it suitable for real-time use. SegFormer performs competitively (mIoU 0.66), while FCN and PSPNet show lower spatial accuracy (approximately 0.49 mIoU). The computed severity index strongly correlates with expert annotations (r = 0.968, R^2 = 0.937), demonstrating the system's reliability for automated crop monitoring and decision support.

</details>


### [2] [CoFINN: Conservation Flux Informed Neural Networks for Physics Problems Governed by Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06587)
*Adnan Harun Doğan,Mert Deniz,Hande Alemdar,Özgür Uğraş Baran*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoFINN is a physics-informed CNN surrogate that enforces finite-volume conservation, yielding more physically consistent compressible-flow predictions with improved drag calculations and stronger data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional CNN surrogates optimize pixel-level similarity and standard PINNs enforce differential residuals, which can misalign with CFD practice and degrade conservation; a conservation-consistent, finite-volume–based surrogate is needed to improve physical fidelity and data efficiency in transonic flows.

Method: Interpret CNN outputs as structured finite-volume grid cells; incorporate conservation through finite-volume flux calculations in the loss, rather than computing PDE residuals; architecture-agnostic, data-efficient; maintains CNN efficiency while enforcing conservation laws.

Result: Significant improvements in aerodynamic force predictions: drag error reduced up to 34% at extreme angles of attack and about 15% on average across tests; especially effective in limited-data regimes due to the conservation-based regularizer; framework preserves computational efficiency and is extendable to other conservation-law systems.

Conclusion: A finite-volume conservation loss provides a powerful physical regularizer for CNN surrogates in compressible flow, yielding more accurate, physically consistent predictions than standard data-driven or residual-based physics-informed methods; the approach is architecture-agnostic and broadly extensible to other conservation-law governed systems.

Abstract: We present CoFINN (Conservation Flux Informed Neural Networks), a physics-informed deep learning framework for predicting compressible flow fields governed by conservation laws. Unlike conventional data-driven convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which optimize only pixel-wise similarity metrics, CoFINN embeds finite-volume conservation physics directly into the training process. Unlike classical physics-informed methods which enforce differential-equation residuals at collocation points through automatic differentiation, CoFINN adopts a finite-volume perspective consistent with modern CFD methodology. CoFINN interprets CNN output fields as structured computational grids, where each pixel represents a finite-volume cell, and enforces conservation consistency through sophisticated numerical flux calculations. The framework is evaluated on transonic flow prediction around airfoils at (M=0.7, Re=6 * 10^6), including challenging conditions involving shock waves and high angles of attack. Results show that CoFINN improves aerodynamic force prediction accuracy, reducing drag prediction error by up to 34% at extreme angles of attack and by approximately 15% on average across the test set. Improvements are particularly significant in limited-data regimes, demonstrating that the conservation-based loss acts as an effective physical regularizer. The proposed approach maintains the computational efficiency advantages of CNN surrogates while significantly improving physical consistency and conservation behavior. The framework is architecture-agnostic and extensible to broader classes of conservation-law-governed physical systems.

</details>


### [3] [AI for Cultural Heritage Textiles: Fine-Tuned Latent Diffusion for Novel Ulos Motif Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06590)
*Humasak Tommy Argo Simanjuntak,Jesika Purba,Sitogab Girsang,Widya Manurung,Samuel Situmeang,Arlinta Barus,Daniel Oranova Siahaan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A generative AI framework fine-tunes Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4 on curated Ulos motif motifs to generate culturally consistent yet novel designs; Protogen outperforms, with substantial improvements in FID and IS; strength and guidance scale tunes fidelity-diversity balance; supports cultural heritage renewal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preserve and revitalize Ulos textiles by expanding motif diversity while maintaining tradition and symbolisms; reduce design time and allow contemporary design demands.

Method: Fine-tune two pretrained latent diffusion models (Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4) on a curated, annotated dataset of high-resolution Ulos motifs. Evaluate using quantitative metrics (FID, IS) and qualitative assessments by traditional weavers and the public. Analyze the impact of strength and guidance scale on generation quality.

Result: Protogen v3.4 outperforms Stable Diffusion v1.4 with substantially lower FID (~10.5x) and higher IS (~2x). Lower strength yields higher fidelity; higher strength increases diversity, indicating a fidelity-diversity tradeoff. Guidance scale of 5-9 provides the best balance, stabilizing FID, KID, and IS across configurations.

Conclusion: Carefully fine-tuned generative AI can support the creative renewal of intangible cultural heritage like Ulos while preserving its stylistic and symbolic integrity.

Abstract: Preserving and revitalising traditional textiles such as Ulos, a cultural heritage of the Batak ethnic group in North Sumatra, Indonesia, requires balancing fidelity to tradition with innovative approaches that meet contemporary design demands. Traditional Ulos weaving faces two key limitations: a narrow range of motifs and a time-intensive design process. This study presents a generative AI framework that fine-tunes two pretrained latent diffusion models: Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4, on a curated, annotated dataset of high-resolution Ulos motifs to generate culturally consistent yet novel designs. Model performance is evaluated quantitatively using Frechet Inception Distance (FID), Inception Score (IS), and qualitatively through assessments by traditional weavers and members of the public. Protogen v3.4 consistently outperforms Stable Diffusion v1.4, achieving substantially lower FID (~10.5x) and higher IS (2.0x), indicating superior visual fidelity, diversity, and closer alignment with the real Ulos motif distribution. We further examine the effects of strength and guidance scale on generation quality across both models. Lower strength values consistently yield higher fidelity (lower FID), while higher strength values increase generative diversity at the cost of realism, revealing a clear fidelity-diversity tradeoff for both models. Across all tested configurations, a guidance scale of 5-9 provides the most effective balance between fidelity and diversity, stabilising FID, KID, and IS, and is recommended as the operating range for high-quality, diverse Ulos motif generation. These findings demonstrate that carefully fine-tuned generative AI can support the creative renewal of intangible cultural heritage while preserving its stylistic and symbolic integrity.

</details>


### [4] [LipSSD: Lipschitz-Constrained Single-Shot Detection for Adversarially Robust Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06592)
*Vincent Lébé,Yannick Prudent,Corentin Friedrich,Thomas Massena,Ronan Sicre,Franck Mamalet*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LipSSD: a Lipschitz-constrained Object Detector offering robustness-by-design, complementary to adversarial training, with attack-agnostic robustness across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object detectors face adversarial perturbations; robustness research is lagging behind classification. Lipschitz constraints offer principled control of input–output sensitivity to yield attack-agnostic robustness without task-specific adversarial training.

Method: Introduce Lipschitz-constrained variants of SSD (LipSSD). Evaluate robustness under multiple white-box attacks and datasets (Pascal VOC, LARD, KITTI). Analyze accuracy–robustness trade-off via a single hyperparameter. Show that Lipschitz constraints complement adversarial training (on Pascal VOC, adversarially trained LipSSD improves mAP@50 on unseen attacks by up to 15 points).

Result: Lipschitz constraints provide a tunable trade-off between clean accuracy and robustness. They yield attack-agnostic robustness and improve performance when combined with adversarial training across diverse datasets, with notable gains on unseen attacks while largely preserving clean performance.

Conclusion: Architectural Lipschitz control is a practical, attack-agnostic direction for enhancing object detector robustness, complementary to data-driven adversarial training.

Abstract: Object detectors have many applications in safety-critical systems, but they are known to be sensitive to worst-case perturbations such as adversarial attacks, which limits their applicability in real-world scenarios. Compared with classification, adversarial robustness for object detection has received less attention, and existing methods are often tied to adversarial training, whose performance may not transfer across attacks, perturbation budgets, or architectures. In this work, we introduce Lipschitz-constrained variants of object detection architectures as robust-by-design alternatives to standard detectors. We validate this approach with LipSSD, a Lipschitz-constrained Single Shot MultiBox Detector (SSD), and provide a comprehensive study of its adversarial robustness using multiple white-box adversarial attacks and datasets. We first analyze the accuracyrobustness trade-off induced by Lipschitz constraints and show that it can be controlled through a single training hyperparameter. We then demonstrate that Lipschitzconstrained detectors are complementary to adversarial training: under the same training setup on the Pascal VOC dataset, adversarially trained LipSSD improves mAP@50 on unseen attacks by up to 15 points over classical adversarially trained SSD. Finally, we use more specific safety-critical datasets such as LARD and KITTI, and show that Lipschitz-constrained detectors can improve robustness while largely preserving clean performance. These results suggest that architectural Lipschitz control is a practical and attack-agnostic direction for improving the robustness of object detectors.

</details>


### [5] [MiLSD: A Micro Line-Segment Detector for Resource-Constrained Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06600)
*Parsa Hassani Shariat Panahi,Amir Hossein Jalilvand,M. Hassan Najafi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MiLSD: MCU-friendly line-segment detector using the F-Clip representation and an optimized fully-convolutional backbone; achieves strong accuracy under sub-megabyte budgets, with 8-bit quantization preserving full-precision performance and a clear accuracy-memory trade-off across representations, bit-widths, and post-processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable accurate line-segment detection on resource-constrained MCUs, addressing the gap between tiny models and GPU-scale parsers by mapping accuracy against memory usage.

Method: Develop a compact fully-convolutional detector MiLSD; systematically compare three output representations; propose F-Clip center-with-length-and-angle; evaluate 8- and 4-bit quantization (with quantization-aware training); add sub-pixel decoding, test-time augmentation, and a lightweight verifier; run experiments under around 1 MB activation budget on ShanghaiTech Wireframe.

Result: 8-bit quantization preserves full-precision performance; 4-bit quantization degrades, especially angle regression; QA training recovers part of the loss. At 1 MB budget, sAP10 on ShanghaiTech Wireframe improves from 10.6 (25k params, 0.25 MB) to 24.1. The work also provides an accuracy-memory map across representations, bit-widths, capacities, and post-processing strategies.

Conclusion: F-Clip is effective at small model sizes; quantization and post-processing choices critically impact MCU-scale line-segment detection; MiLSD demonstrates substantial MCU-grade accuracy gains and offers a framework for memory-aware embedded vision design.

Abstract: Line segment detection is a key building block in visual SLAM, 3D reconstruction, and industrial inspection. Recent deep learning methods have greatly improved accuracy, yet even the smallest models require several megabytes of memory, exceeding low-cost MCU capacity. This work investigates the maximum achievable accuracy under a sub-megabyte budget. We propose MiLSD, a detector tailored for MCU-level constraints, and systematically compare three output representations within a compact fully-convolutional backbone. Our study shows that the proposed F-Clip center-with-length-and-angle formulation learns most effectively at small model sizes. We find that 8-bit quantization preserves full-precision performance, while 4-bit quantization causes significant degradation, particularly in angle regression, with quantization-aware training recovering only part of the loss. With a one-megabyte activation budget and inference enhancements including sub-pixel decoding, test-time augmentation, and a lightweight verifier, MiLSD improves sAP10 on ShanghaiTech Wireframe from 10.6 (25k parameters, 0.25 MB) to 24.1 within 1 MB. Rather than competing with GPU-scale parsers, we map the accuracy memory trade-off across representations, bit-widths, capacities, and post-processing strategies for embedded vision systems.

</details>


### [6] [Do Counterfactually Fair Image Classifiers Satisfy Group Fairness? -- A Theoretical and Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06603)
*Sangwon Jung,Sumin Yu,Sanghyuk Chun,Taesup Moon*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Counterfactual fairness (CF) and group fairness (GF) are not inherently aligned in image classification; CF does not guarantee GF, unlike some tabular data findings. This work builds dedicated CF evaluation datasets for images, analyzes the CF–GF relationship, attributes the discrepancy to a latent attribute G correlated with the sensitive attribute, and introduces Counterfactual Knowledge Distillation (CKD) to reduce G reliance, achieving GF for CF models on their datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify the relationship between CF and GF in image classification, where counterfactual samples are hard to obtain, by constructing datasets that enable simultaneous CF and GF evaluation and investigating underlying causes of the CF–GF gap.

Method: Create high-quality edited image datasets (oursCeleb and oursLFW) labeled by humans to enable CF evaluation alongside GF baselines. Analyze whether CF implies GF empirically and theoretically; identify a latent attribute G correlated with, but not caused by, the sensitive attribute (e.g., hair length and sex). Propose Counterfactual Knowledge Distillation (CKD) to reduce reliance on G during training. Conduct extensive experiments to assess CF and GF compatibility.

Result: Empirically, CF does not imply GF in image classification; opposite of some tabular-dataset findings. The discrepancy can be explained by a latent attribute G correlated with the sensitive attribute, creating spurious CF–GF relations. CKD successfully reduces dependence on G, enabling CF models to satisfy GF on the new datasets when G influence is mitigated.

Conclusion: The CF–GF relationship in image tasks is mediated by latent confounders; disentangling or reducing reliance on such attributes is key to achieving GF for CF models. CKD offers a simple, effective baseline to align CF with GF on image datasets and provides a framework to study the interplay between CF, GF, and latent attributes.

Abstract: The notion of algorithmic fairness has been actively explored from various aspects of fairness, such as counterfactual fairness (CF) and group fairness (GF). However, the exact relationship between CF and GF remains to be unclear, especially in image classification tasks; the reason is because we often cannot collect counterfactual samples regarding a sensitive attribute, essential for evaluating CF, from the existing images (\eg, a photo of the same person but with different secondary sex characteristics). In this paper, we construct new image datasets for evaluating CF by using a high-quality image editing method and carefully labeling with human annotators. Our datasets, \oursceleb and \ourslfw, build upon the popular image GF benchmarks; hence, we can evaluate CF and GF simultaneously. We empirically observe that CF does not imply GF in image classification, whereas previous studies on tabular datasets observed the opposite. We theoretically show that it could be due to the existence of a latent attribute $G$ that is correlated with, but not caused by, the sensitive attribute (\eg, secondary sex characteristics are highly correlated with hair length). From this observation, we propose a simple baseline, Counterfactual Knowledge Distillation (CKD), to mitigate such correlation with the sensitive attributes. Extensive experimental results on \oursceleb and \ourslfw demonstrate that CF-achieving models satisfy GF if we successfully reduce the reliance on $G$ (\eg, using CKD).

</details>


### [7] [Overview of the NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 1: Difficulty-Aware Multilingual and Multimodal Medical Instructional Video Understanding Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06618)
*Shenxi Liu,Kan Li,Mingyang Zhao,Yuhang Tian,Bin Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DA-MIVQA is a new NLPCC 2026 benchmark for medical instructional video QA with three difficulty-aware tracks (temporal grounding in a single video, video corpus retrieval, and temporal grounding in a video corpus), built from public medical instructional channels and annotated for evidence complexity to test multi-modal reasoning across textual, visual, and procedural dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a scalable, difficulty-aware benchmark that honors prior multimodal medical QA challenges while explicitly modeling the varying evidence and reasoning complexity required for real-world medical instruction scenarios.

Method: Construct a dataset from public medical instructional channels, manually annotate each question with difficulty and required evidence type; define three tracks (DA-TAGSV, DA-VCR, DA-TAGVC); establish evaluation protocols and provide participation overview, competition results, and baseline/representative systems.

Result: The paper reports on dataset construction, evaluation protocol, participation and competition results, and presents representative systems, illustrating how DA-MIVQA challenges models across simple to complex cross-modal reasoning in medical instructional content.

Conclusion: DA-MIVQA offers a practical, multi-track benchmark for evaluating medical instructional video QA systems under varying textual, visual, temporal, and procedural reasoning demands, guiding future research in the field.

Abstract: Following the CMIVQA, MMI-VQA, and M4IVQA challenges in NLPCC 2023--2025, we introduce the Difficulty-Aware Medical Instructional Video Question Answering (DA-MIVQA) shared task for NLPCC 2026. DA-MIVQA extends previous multilingual and multimodal medical video benchmarks by explicitly distinguishing questions according to the type and complexity of evidence required for answering. Specifically, simple questions can often be answered from subtitle-based textual cues, whereas complex questions require visual grounding, procedural understanding, and cross-modal evidence integration. The challenge contains three tracks: Difficulty-Aware Temporal Answer Grounding in Single Video (DA-TAGSV), Difficulty-Aware Video Corpus Retrieval (DA-VCR), and Difficulty-Aware Temporal Answer Grounding in Video Corpus (DA-TAGVC). The dataset is collected from public medical instructional channels, covers diverse scenarios such as first aid, emergency response, rehabilitation, nursing, and general medical education, and is manually verified with difficulty annotations. This paper presents the task motivation, dataset construction, evaluation protocol, participation overview, competition results, and representative systems of DA-MIVQA. DA-MIVQA provides a practical benchmark for evaluating medical instructional video question answering systems under varying textual, visual, temporal, and procedural reasoning requirements.

</details>


### [8] [SpaR3D-MoE: Adaptive 3D Spatial Reasoning from Sparse Views Meets Geometry-Inductive Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06620)
*Haida Feng,Hao Wei,Haolin Wang,Shiwei Li,Chade Li,Yihong Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpaR3D-MoE presents an end-to-end framework for 3D-aware multimodal LLMs using geometry-aware adaptive spatiotemporal sampling and a heterogeneous Mixture-of-Experts with an instruction-pose aware router, achieving state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, ScanQA, and SQA3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract targets the chemistry between 2D semantic understanding in MLLMs and 3D spatial geometry, highlighting bottlenecks: reliance on costly 3D-specific data or RGB-only approaches with sampling heuristics and shallow fusion that disrupt spatiotemporal connectivity and cause modality contention. The goal is to enable geometry-aware reasoning from sparse RGB inputs.

Method: 1) Adaptive spatiotemporal manifold sampling builds a geometry-aware spatiotemporal graph to extract informative keyframes while reducing sequence redundancy and preserving topology. 2) A heterogeneous geometry-inductive Mixture-of-Experts, guided by an instruction-pose aware router, routes multimodal tokens to specialized experts to mitigate cross-modal contention inherent in monolithic fusion.

Result: Empirical evaluation on VSI-Bench, ScanQA, and SQA3D shows state-of-the-art performance. SpaR3D-MoE attains an average VSI-Bench score of 63.5, 7.8 points higher than the strongest baseline, with relative improvements of 35.4% (Route Plan) and 51.4% (Relative Direction).

Conclusion: SpaR3D-MoE demonstrates effective geometry-aware multimodal reasoning from sparse RGB inputs, achieving superior results and validating the proposed adaptive sampling and Mixture-of-Experts routing as a robust mechanism for bridging 2D semantics and 3D geometry.

Abstract: Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle to bridge the representational gap between 2D semantic understanding and 3D spatial geometry. Existing 3D-aware models either rely on costly 3D-specific data or utilize RGB-only inputs with heuristic sampling and monolithic, shallow fusion, which respectively disrupt essential spatiotemporal connectivity and induce modality contention across diverse spatial tasks. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce SpaR3D-MoE, an end-to-end framework that enables adaptive spatial reasoning by equipping MLLMs with geometry-aware capabilities from only sparse RGB inputs. First, we propose an adaptive spatiotemporal manifold sampling mechanism that constructs a geometry-aware spatiotemporal graph to extract informative keyframes, effectively mitigating sequence redundancy while preserving the scene's topological connectivity. Second, we introduce the heterogeneous geometry-inductive Mixture-of-Experts driven by an instruction-pose aware router, which adaptively routes multimodal tokens to specialized experts, resolving the cross-modal contention inherent in monolithic fusion. Extensive experiments on VSI-Bench, ScanQA, and SQA3D demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance. Notably, SpaR3D-MoE achieves the highest average score of 63.5 on VSI-Bench, outperforming the strongest baseline by 7.8 absolute points, alongside relative improvements of 35.4% and 51.4% in Route Plan and Relative Direction tasks, respectively.

</details>


### [9] [Dynamic-in-Few-Step: Unifying Dynamic Computation and Few-Step Distillation for Efficient Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06631)
*Yu Cheng,Siyue Yao,Zhongang Qi,Shanyan Guan,Wei Li,Fajie Yuan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces a post-training acceleration framework for video diffusion models that jointly learns denoising steps and dynamic structured sparsity to form a step-specific Mixture-of-Models (MoM); achieves substantial speedups with preserved quality, beyond conventional distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VDMs have high computational costs; static architectures during distillation ignore the varying computational demands across noise levels. Need a flexible, cost-aware approach that reduces per-step FLOPs without sacrificing generation quality.

Method: Jointly optimize denoising steps and structured sparsity to convert a pre-trained VDM into a step-specific MoM. Introduce Progressive Training Strategy and Output Rollout Mechanism to stabilize learning across timesteps. Develop a specialized inference engine for efficient MoM deployment. Framework is orthogonal to existing accelerations.

Result: On Wan-14B, achieves 24% per-step FLOP reduction on top of 4-step distillation, yields 1.2x wall-clock speedup, and reaches ~30x speedup over a 50-step teacher while maintaining competitive generation quality.

Conclusion: Dynamic, step-aware sparsification within the distillation pipeline is effective for accelerating VDMs, enabling substantial speedups with minimal quality loss and can complement existing acceleration methods.

Abstract: Video Diffusion Models (VDMs) have demonstrated superior generation quality but suffer from prohibitive computational costs. While recent few-step distillation techniques significantly accelerate inference, they typically enforce a static model architecture across all denoising stages, ignoring the varying computational demands inherent to different noise levels. In this work, we propose a novel post-training acceleration framework that exploits this redundancy by integrating dynamic structural sparsification directly into the distillation process. Unlike conventional post-hoc compression applied to a fixed diffusion pipeline, our approach jointly optimizes the denoising steps and structured model sparsity, transforming a pre-trained VDM into a compact, step-specific Mixture-of-Models (MoM). To address the training instability arising from this joint optimization, we introduce a Progressive Training Strategy coupled with an Output Rollout Mechanism, which ensures the coherent learning of structural decisions across timesteps. Furthermore, we develop a specialized inference engine to deploy the resulting MoM efficiently. Our method is orthogonal to existing acceleration techniques and highly effective: On Wan-14B, it removes 24% of the per-step FLOPs on top of 4-step distillation, adding a 1.2x wall-clock gain and reaching a 30x speedup over the 50-step teacher while preserving competitive generation quality.

</details>


### [10] [ProMoE-FL: Prototype-conditioned Mixture of Experts for Multimodal Federated Learning with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06633)
*Aavash Chhetri,Bibek Niroula,Eduard Vazquez,Yash Raj Shrestha,Prashnna Gyawali,Loris Bazzani,Binod Bhattarai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ProMoE-FL introduces a prototype-conditioned Mixture-of-Experts for multimodal federated learning with missing modalities. It builds a global prototype bank to capture modality priors across institutions and uses prototype- and modality-conditioned routing to synthesize missing features, achieving robust performance in both homogeneous and heterogeneous settings on four chest X-ray datasets, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Missing modalities in multimodal federated learning degrade model performance and privacy-preserving data sharing is needed. Existing solutions rely on public data or naive feature synthesis that ignores cross-institution priors. There is a need for a method that leverages clinically meaningful modality priors and adaptive, direction-aware feature synthesis under client heterogeneity.

Method: A global client-aware prototype bank is learned to capture modality priors across institutions. A Mixture of Experts (MoE) is conditioned on these prototypes and modality indices to enable direction-aware routing, dynamically synthesizing missing features during federated learning. Evaluations are conducted on four public chest X-ray datasets (MIMIC-CXR, NIH Open-I, PadChest, CheXpert) under both homogeneous and heterogeneous settings.

Result: ProMoE-FL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both homogeneous and heterogeneous federated learning settings across all four chest X-ray datasets, demonstrating robust missing-modality feature synthesis and improved clinical relevance in feature priors.

Conclusion: Prototype-conditioned MoE with a global prototype bank provides robust, direction-aware synthesis of missing modalities in multimodal FL. This approach leverages cross-institution modality priors to improve performance and resilience to data heterogeneity, with strong empirical gains on diverse chest X-ray datasets.

Abstract: In this paper, we address the problem of multimodal federated learning with missing modality. Existing methods utilize an additional public dataset or perform naive feature synthesis that is based solely on the available modality. To address these limitations, we propose ProMoE-FL, a Prototype-conditioned Mixture-of-Experts framework for robust missing-modality feature synthesis in multimodal federated learning. ProMoE-FL builds a global client-aware prototype bank that captures clinically meaningful modality priors across institutions. Our Mixture of Experts is conditioned on these prototypes and modality indices to enable direction-aware expert routing for dynamically synthesizing missing features. We perform extensive quantitative and qualitative evaluations on four public chest X-ray datasets (MIMIC-CXR, NIH Open-I, PadChest, and CheXpert) and demonstrate that ProMoE-FL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both homogeneous as well as the more challenging heterogeneous settings.

</details>


### [11] [CoMind: Understanding Collaborative Human Activity from Multiple Minds and Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06691)
*Alexey Gavryushin,Dingxi Zhang,Zhao Huang,Alexandros Delitzas,Jiaqi Chen,Ben Ellis,Cedric Zöllner,Manthan Patel,Manuel Kaufmann,Marc Pollefeys,Xi Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces CoMind, an egocentric and exocentric video dataset for real-world collaboration in cooking, with multi-modal data (video, audio, gaze, 3D scans) and rich annotations, plus benchmarks for Joint Attention Estimation, Socially Conditioned Object Interaction Anticipation, and Collaborative Handover Prediction; enables multimodal perception and proactive AI in collaborative tasks; data available at the project site.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study cognitive processes underlying collaboration (e.g., Theory of Mind) in natural settings, addressing the gap of scarce richly annotated multimodal datasets that capture joint attention and social interactions during real-world tasks.

Method: Collects multi-perspective video, high-quality audio, gaze tracking, and 3D scene/object scans of real-world cooking collaborations; annotates shared attention to objects, social cues, interactions, and agent-object interactions; defines and benchmarks tasks for Joint Attention Estimation, Socially Conditioned Object Interaction Anticipation, and Collaborative Handover Prediction; ensures temporal alignment across modalities.

Result: Provides a richly annotated, temporally aligned multimodal dataset and associated benchmarks (CoMind) to advance research in multimodal perception, proactive assistance, and collaborative planning in human-human collaboration scenarios.

Conclusion: CoMind enables modeling of complex social interactions and reasoning about human behaviors in collaborative environments, supporting future AI systems capable of predicting and assisting in joint tasks; dataset released publicly at the project site.

Abstract: Human-human collaboration is a fundamental aspect of everyday life, essential to success in a wide range of goal-directed activities from household tasks to professional teamwork. While much research has focused on modeling coordination and task execution, the cognitive processes that support such collaboration, particularly Theory of Mind (the ability to infer the mental states of others), remain difficult to study in natural settings. To address this gap, we introduce a novel egocentric and exocentric video dataset capturing real-world collaboration in cooking scenarios. The dataset integrates multi-perspective video, high-quality audio, gaze tracking, and 3D scene and object scans, with annotations for shared attention to objects, social cues and interactions between agents, as well as agent-object interactions. We establish benchmarks for Joint Attention Estimation, Socially Conditioned Object Interaction Anticipation, and Collaborative Handover Prediction, enabling research on multimodal perception, proactive assistance, and collaborative planning. By providing temporally aligned, richly annotated multimodal data, CoMind facilitates the development and evaluation of AI systems capable of modeling complex social interactions and reasoning about human behaviors in collaborative environments. Our dataset and benchmarks are made available at https://comind.ethz.ch/.

</details>


### [12] [SPEAR: A Simulator for Photorealistic Embodied AI Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06701)
*Mike Roberts,Renhan Wang,Rushikesh Zawar,Rachith Dey-Prakash,Quentin Leboutet,Stephan R. Richter,Matthias Müller,German Ros,Rui Tang,Stefan Leutenegger,Yannick Hold-Geoffroy,Kalyan Sunkavalli,Vladlen Koltun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPEAR is a Python-based bridge to Unreal Engine that renders photorealistic scenes at 73 FPS, exposes ~14k UE functions, and provides ground-truth modalities and a graph-driven workflow to support fast, programmable, multi-agent embodied AI research across diverse UE projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photorealistic embodied AI simulators lag in generality, programmability, and speed. There is a need for a flexible, fast, and feature-rich platform that can drive complex experiments, multi-agent control, and data generation.

Method: Develop SPEAR as a modular Python library with a plugin architecture that connects to Unreal Engine apps. Expose ~14k UE functions to Python, render 1080p images directly into NumPy arrays at 73 FPS, and provide additional ground-truth modalities (non-diffuse intrinsic images, material IDs, PBR parameters). Introduce a high-level graph-based programming model to express dependencies among UE tasks and execute them deterministically within a single UE frame. Demonstrate capabilities across multi-agent control, city-scale environments, procedural content generation, multi-view face rendering, co-simulation with MuJoCo, and language-assisted scene editing.

Result: A single SPEAR instance achieves 73 FPS at 1920x1080, outperforming existing UE plugins by about an order of magnitude. It exposes over 14k Unreal Engine functions, offers ground-truth modalities not available in prior UE-based simulators, and enables deterministic execution of complex UE workflows within one frame. Demonstrations cover diverse applications and integrations.

Conclusion: SPEAR provides a highly programmable, fast, and photorealistic simulation platform for embodied AI research, enabling complex, data-rich workflows and broad applicability across agents, environments, and tasks while offering new ground-truth data modalities and deterministic execution within UE frames.

Abstract: Interactive simulators have become powerful tools for training embodied agents and generating synthetic visual data, but existing photorealistic simulators suffer from limited generality, programmability, and rendering speed. We address these limitations by introducing SPEAR: A Simulator for Photorealistic Embodied AI Research. At its core, SPEAR is a Python library that can connect to, and programmatically control, any Unreal Engine (UE) application via a modular plugin architecture. SPEAR exposes over 14K unique UE functions to Python, representing an order-of-magnitude increase in programmable functionality over existing UE-based simulators. Additionally, a single SPEAR instance can render 1920x1080 photorealistic beauty images directly into a user's NumPy array at 73 frames per second - an order of magnitude faster than existing UE plugins - while also providing ground truth image modalities that are not available in any existing UE-based simulator (e.g., a non-diffuse intrinsic image decomposition, material IDs, and physically based shading parameters). Finally, SPEAR introduces an expressive high-level programming model that enables users to specify complex graphs of UE work with arbitrary data dependencies among work items, and to execute these graphs deterministically within a single UE frame. We demonstrate the utility of SPEAR through a diverse collection of example applications: controlling multiple embodied agents with distinct action spaces (e.g., humans, cars, and robots) across several in-the-wild UE projects; rendering photorealistic city-scale environments; manipulating UE's procedural content generation systems; rendering synchronized multi-view images of detailed human faces; coordinating an interactive co-simulation with the MuJoCo physics simulator; and editing scenes with natural language via an AI coding assistant.

</details>


### [13] [A Good Initialization is All You Need for Faithful Visual Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06726)
*Zihan Gu,Jiayu Wang,Hua Zhang,Yue Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two forward-only, search-based methods (CoPAIR and TRACE) produce compact top-k evidence masks for faithful attribution, outperforming prior full-order ordering under forward-call accounting, with strong results on ImageNet backbones and MLLM repair/attribution tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many applications (e.g., MLLM attribution/repair) require compact top-k evidence masks rather than full region orderings, but exact k-mask is combinatorially hard and one-step scoring misses high-value combinations. The work targets efficient, faithful top-k attribution.

Method: CoPAIR: PhaseWin–Greedy gap diagnosis to generate coarse singleton/pair candidates for warm-starting full-order search. TRACE: fixed-cardinality fine-region masks via cross-entropy sampling, elite retention, and distribution updates, plus finite-budget recovery analysis.

Result: Establishes new state-of-the-art for faithful full-ordering attribution under inclusive forward-call accounting on ImageNet with multiple backbones (CLIP ViT-L/14, CLIP RN101, ResNet-101). TRACE+Greedy yields strongest MLLM attribution on POPE/RePOPE with large VL models; direct TRACE masks achieve single-point repair rates of 94.44% and 96.00%.

Conclusion: Compact evidence masks are actionable attribution outputs beyond prefixes of full rankings; the proposed forward-only methods enable robust, efficient top-k attribution suitable for MLLM attribution and repair tasks.

Abstract: Faithful visual attribution identifies which image regions support a model prediction. Search-based perturbation methods lead the insertion--deletion faithfulness frontier by masking regions and measuring score changes, but they usually output a complete ordering of all regions. Many applications, especially MLLM attribution and repair, only need a compact top-\(k\) evidence mask. We study this mask-first attribution problem. An exactly \(k\)-region mask is combinatorial: useful evidence can depend on interactions among fine regions. Coarse grouping can stabilize early search but aggregates redundant content, whereas one-step scoring can miss high-value combinations. We introduce two forward-only methods. \textsc{CoPAIR} uses a PhaseWin--Greedy gap diagnosis to construct coarse singleton/pair candidates that warm-start full-ordering search. \textsc{TRACE} directly searches fixed-cardinality fine-region masks with cross-entropy sampling, elite retention, and distribution updates, with a finite-budget recovery analysis. The resulting evidence set can be returned as a compact attribution mask or used to initialize Greedy or PhaseWin when a complete ranking is required. Across ImageNet classification with CLIP ViT-L/14, CLIP RN101, and ResNet-101, our initialized search methods establish a new state-of-the-art frontier for faithful full-ordering attribution under inclusive forward-call accounting. On POPE and RePOPE with Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct and LLaVA-v1.5-7B, \textsc{TRACE}+Greedy gives the strongest search-based MLLM attribution results. Direct \textsc{TRACE} masks further achieve single-point RePOPE repair rates of \(94.44\%\) and \(96.00\%\), showing that compact evidence masks can be actionable attribution outputs, not merely prefixes of full rankings.

</details>


### [14] [Hardware-aware Graph Neural Networks prunning for embedded event-based vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06739)
*Piotr Wzorek,Kamil Jeziorek,Tomasz Kryjak*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hardware-aware pruning and quantization for Graph CNNs on embedded FPGA achieves on-chip memory savings with limited accuracy loss, enabling efficient event-based data processing; design-space exploration guides architecture adaptation across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable real-time, low-latency, low-power processing of event-based camera data on resource-constrained embedded FPGA platforms by adapting Graph CNN architectures through hardware-aware pruning and quantization.

Method: Combine hardware-aware pruning and quantization with Fine Grid Search and Greedy layer-wise Iterative Deepening Search to explore the design space and tailor GCN architectures to on-chip memory constraints, targeting BRAM reductions while controlling accuracy loss on CIFAR-10, MNIST-DVS, and N-Caltech101.

Result: BRAM reductions: 28.8% (CIFAR-10) with 1.65% accuracy drop; 31.4% (MNIST-DVS) with 3.55% accuracy drop; 26.5% (N-Caltech101) with 5.18% accuracy drop.

Conclusion: A hardware-aware optimization workflow with pruning, quantization, and guided design-space search enables efficient GCN deployment on embedded FPGA for event-based vision, balancing memory savings against modest accuracy degradation across multiple datasets.

Abstract: Event-based cameras are gaining popularity as the sensor of choice for mobile robotics, due to their high performance in dynamic environments. However, these applications require efficient real-time data processing with low latency and power consumption. One strategy to meet these stringent requirements is hardware acceleration of efficient algorithms that preserve the temporal sparsity of event data. In this work, we propose an optimization strategy for Graph Convolutional Neural Networks models aimed at adapting their architecture to the limited resources of embedded heterogeneous FPGA platforms. Our method incorporates hardware-aware pruning and quantization, taking into account the trade-off between on-chip memory savings and inference accuracy. Strategic exploration of the design space with Fine Grid Search and Greedy layer-wise Iterative Deepening Search methods enables flexible adaptation of the model architecture to the target platform. Our approach was evaluated across various network configurations and multiple datasets, resulting in BRAM memory reductions of 28.8% for CIFAR-10 (with a 1.65% decrease in accuracy), 31.4% for MNIST-DVS (accuracy drop of 3.55%), and 26.5% for N-Caltech101 (with a 5.18% accuracy reduction).

</details>


### [15] [URS-Stereo: Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search for Real-Time Stereo Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06779)
*Pouya Sohrabipour,Chaitanya kumar reddy Pallerla,Dongyi Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: URs-Stereo introduces an uncertainty-guided residual search module to adapt local cost-volume centers based on propagated disparity confidence, improving robustness of coarse-to-fine stereo while preserving real-time speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coarse-to-fine stereo methods rely on propagated disparities; when those estimates are inaccurate, the true correspondences may lie outside the local search range, causing unrecoverable failures during refinement. An uncertainty-aware mechanism to adapt search regions is needed to boost robustness without sacrificing efficiency.

Method: An Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM) predicts the reliability of propagated disparities and residual search offsets, then adaptively relocates the centers of local cost volumes before disparity refinement. The search region is dynamically adjusted according to the confidence of the propagated disparity to maintain accurate correspondence estimation with low computation.

Result: Experiments on SceneFlow, KITTI 2012, KITTI 2015, Middlebury, and ETH3D show that URS-Stereo consistently improves disparity estimation while maintaining real-time inference speed.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-guided search effectively improves robustness of local correspondence estimation in coarse-to-fine stereo without sacrificing speed, making URS-Stereo a practical solution for real-time stereo matching.

Abstract: Real-time stereo matching is crucial for robotics, autonomous systems, and embedded vision applications, where both computational efficiency and disparity accuracy are required. Recent coarse-to-fine stereo matching methods improve efficiency by progressively refining disparity estimates using local cost volumes at higher resolutions. However, these methods rely heavily on the accuracy of propagated disparity estimates from previous stages. When the propagated disparity is inaccurate, the ground-truth correspondence may fall outside the predefined local search range, leading to unrecoverable matching failures during subsequent refinement. In this paper, we propose URS-Stereo, a real-time coarse-to-fine stereo matching framework that addresses this limitation through uncertainty-guided search adaptation. Specifically, we introduce an Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM), which predicts the reliability of propagated disparities together with residual search offsets to adaptively relocate the centers of local cost volumes before disparity refinement. By dynamically adjusting the search region according to the confidence of the propagated disparity, the proposed method significantly improves the robustness of local correspondence estimation while preserving the computational efficiency of coarse-to-fine stereo matching. Extensive experiments on SceneFlow, KITTI 2012, KITTI 2015, Middlebury, and ETH3D demonstrate that URS-Stereo consistently improves disparity estimation while maintaining real-time inference speed, validating the effectiveness of the proposed uncertainty-guided search strategy

</details>


### [16] [Rail Track Extraction from Rasterized Classified Point Clouds Using a Full-Resolution, Fully Convolutional Recurrent Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06829)
*Alexander Gribov,Jie Chang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A synthetic-data trained FC-RNN-based pipeline for rail-track extraction from classified 3D point clouds, converting to vectorized centerlines via rasterization, denoising, morphological processing, DTW-based consolidation, and 3D-2D projection with smoothing, yielding high-quality rail centerlines with minimal manual intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable robust, automated extraction of rail tracks for asset management and maintenance in automated inspection/mapping workflows, leveraging synthetic data to boost per-pixel quality while avoiding manual labeling.

Method: Rasterize points corresponding to railroad tracks from classified 3D point clouds. Apply a fully convolutional recurrent neural network that preserves full spatial resolution to denoise and refine the track representation. Use morphological operations to clean and prepare for vectorization, followed by centerline extraction. Smooth centerlines to remove irregularities in 3D, project 3D information onto 2D polylines with vertical smoothing, and compute a single centerline for rail pairs using Dynamic Time Warping (DTW). Output rail top centerlines and track centerlines with minimal manual intervention.

Result: Demonstrates high-quality rail track extraction with cleaner track representations suitable for vectorization and accurate centerline extraction for rail pairs. Experimental validation confirms the method's effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline provides accurate, robust rail track centerlines from noisy, classified 3D point clouds using synthetic training and DTW-based consolidation, reducing manual intervention and enhancing automated rail asset mapping.

Abstract: Rail track extraction is essential for effective railway asset management and maintenance, especially in automated inspection and mapping workflows. This paper introduces a novel method for extracting rail tracks from classified 3D point clouds using a fully convolutional recurrent neural network that preserves full spatial resolution and is trained exclusively on synthetically generated data. This approach enhances per-pixel quality and is particularly suited for rail track extraction. The proposed method begins by rasterizing points corresponding to railroad tracks, then applies the neural network to reduce noise and yield a cleaner track representation suitable for vectorization [1]. Subsequent morphological operations further refine the resultant data, enabling accurate track centerline extraction. Next, the extracted centerlines undergo smoothing to eliminate residual irregularities [2, 3]. Finally, the algorithm transfers 3D information from lidar points onto 2D polylines and applies additional vertical smoothing. A single centerline for both tracks is found using the Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) algorithm [4]. The final outcome consists of rail top centerlines and track centerlines derived for rail pairs, with minimal manual intervention. Experimental validation confirms the effectiveness of this method in yielding high-quality rail track extraction.

</details>


### [17] [WildCity: A Real-World City-Scale Testbed for Rendering, Simulation, and Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06838)
*Xiangyu Han,Mengyu Yang,Jiaqi Li,Bowen Chang,Ziyu Chen,Hexu Zhao,Rahul Kumar Agrawal,Anthony Rodriguez,Fiona Hua,Marco Pavone,Chen Feng,Yiming Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WildCity introduces a real-world city-scale multimodal dataset (18 long trajectories ~83.7 km avg), plus an urban reconstruction baseline and a path toward simulation-ready digital twins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A lack of city-scale data limits AI development for large-scale spatial perception, memory, and reasoning. The work aims to bridge perception and digital-twin-ready city representations by providing data, baselines, and a simulator.

Method: Collects real-world urban trajectories via autonomous fleets; multimodal sensing; proposes an urban-tailored reconstruction baseline; converts reconstructions into a closed-loop urban simulator; analyzes scalability, extrapolation, and uncertainty.

Result: Dataset released; reconstruction baseline established; closed-loop urban simulator created; systematic analysis of key challenges (scalability, extrapolation, uncertainty) to guide city-scale rendering and AI spatial cognition.

Conclusion: WildCity establishes a foundation for city-scale perception, memory, and reasoning, accelerating progress toward AI capable of understanding and navigating urban spaces at scales commensurate with human cognition and enabling digital-twin research.

Abstract: Humans can navigate an unfamiliar city and gradually form a coherent spatial mental map spanning tens of square kilometers. Can AI build spatial representations at a comparable scale? Although recent foundation models have advanced scene reconstruction and embodied intelligence, scaling to entire cities remains an open challenge, primarily due to the lack of city-scale data. To bridge the gap, we introduce WildCity, a real-world multimodal dataset collected by autonomous fleets traversing complex urban environments. Our dataset includes 18 trajectories, each averaging 83.7 kilometers in length, and preserves the core challenges of in-the-wild perception, e.g., dynamic objects, lighting variations, and imperfect camera poses. We further establish an urban-tailored reconstruction baseline and convert the reconstructed environments into a closed-loop simulator. Beyond the dataset and baseline, we systematically analyze the key challenges on the path to simulation-ready urban digital twins: scalability, extrapolation, and uncertainty. Ultimately, WildCity aims to catalyze progress not only in city-scale rendering, but more broadly in the pursuit of AI that can perceive, remember, and reason across space at a scale comparable to human cognition. Project page: https://han-xiangyu.github.io/Wild-City/

</details>


### [18] [Retrieving and Refining Winning Noise Tickets for Diffusion-Based Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06843)
*Sakuya Ota,Qing Yu,Kent Fujiwara,Satoshi Ikehata,Ikuro Sato*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Training-free, model-agnostic WINRO framework selects and refines initial Gaussian noise “tickets” to improve text-to-motion fidelity and temporal consistency in diffusion-based models, via KL-regularized refinement and optional LoRA. It enhances alignment across base models (MDM, MotionLCM) on HumanML3D, boosts temporal robustness on MTT, and generalizes to stylization and spatial constraints without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based text-to-motion often drifts semantically and loses temporal coherence, especially for long or compositional sequences. The initial Gaussian noise space likely contains latent structure (“noise tickets”) that biases the denoising toward certain motion semantics; exploiting these tickets can stabilize the trajectory and maintain alignment with input text.

Method: Map random noises to motion features under null prompts; retrieve the noise that best aligns with the target text; refine the selected noise using a KL-regularized objective that reduces residual semantic gap while preserving the Gaussian prior. An optional LoRA adapter can amortize the refinement into a single forward pass. The approach is training-free and model-agnostic, designed to be applied before diffusion sampling across base models.

Result: WINRO consistently improves text-motion fidelity across base models (MDM and MotionLCM) on HumanML3D without retraining, enhances temporal robustness on the MTT benchmark, and generalizes to motion stylization and spatial constraint satisfaction.

Conclusion: A training-free, model-agnostic strategy that leverages structured noise in the diffusion process to tighten text-to-motion alignment and temporal consistency, with minimal overhead through KL-regularized refinement and optional LoRA amortization.

Abstract: Diffusion-based text-to-motion models synthesize realistic human motions but often exhibit semantic drift from the input text. Motion is inherently temporal, especially in compositional and long-duration sequences that require semantic consistency across multiple action segments and smooth kinematic transitions throughout the trajectory. We posit that the initial noise is central to this consistency: within the Gaussian noise space, certain instances, i.e. winning noise tickets, carry latent structure that biases denoising toward particular motion semantics, even under null prompts. We propose WInning Noise Retrieval and Optimization (WINRO), a training-free, model-agnostic framework that improves text-motion alignment by selecting and refining such tickets before diffusion sampling. WINRO maps random noises to motion features generated under null prompts, retrieves the best-aligned noise for a given text, and refines it via a KL-regularized objective that reduces the residual semantic gap while preserving the Gaussian prior. An optional LoRA-based adapter amortizes this refinement into a single forward pass. WINRO consistently improves text-motion fidelity across different base models, MDM and MotionLCM, on HumanML3D without retraining, improves temporal robustness on the MTT benchmark, and generalizes to applications such as motion stylization and spatial constraint satisfaction.

</details>


### [19] [Gen4U: Unifying Video Generation and Understanding via Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06856)
*Michael King,Aravindh Mahendran,Matthew Koichi Grimes,Fedor Kitashov,Adham Elarabawy,Pedro Velez,Maks Ovsjanikov,Viorica Pătrăucean*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: State-of-the-art video diffusion models encode high-level semantics in a structured latent space and can be repurposed as encoders; Gen4U enables single-pass understanding by freezing models and preserving generation capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion representations historically emphasize low-level geometry while underrepresenting high-level semantics; uncover latent structure across depth and noise and leverage these representations for efficient understanding without fine-tuning.

Method: Systematically probe intermediate activations with mutual-kNN alignment metrics to map latent evolution across depth and noise; identify conditions where global semantics are linearly separable and where fine-grained details require attention for decoding; develop Gen4U to map generative representations to downstream tasks in a single forward pass using frozen, large-scale video diffusion models as encoders.

Result: Frozen, large-scale video diffusion models achieve competitive performance across semantic and non-semantic tasks (video classification, depth estimation, camera pose estimation, image/video captioning); Gen4U unifies generation and understanding in a single forward pass without fine-tuning, while preserving generation quality.

Conclusion: Diffusion models serve as robust, unified backbones for both generation and understanding; Gen4U demonstrates that generation-capable, frozen diffusion models can act as strong encoders across diverse vision tasks without sacrificing generative capabilities.

Abstract: Prior work suggests that diffusion representations capture low-level geometry but struggle with high-level semantics. We demonstrate that state-of-the-art video diffusion models overcome this limitation. By systematically probing their intermediate activations using recent mutual-kNN alignment metrics, we reveal a highly structured latent space where visual representations evolve across both network depth and noise levels. We show that while moderate noise levels yield linearly separable global semantics, fine-grained details persist at lower noise levels but become spatially scattered, requiring attention mechanisms to decode. Building on these insights, we introduce Gen4U (Generation for Understanding), a framework that repurposes these generative representations with a single forward pass. Our experiments establish that frozen, large-scale video diffusion models function as highly competitive video encoders across a wide spectrum of tasks, spanning semantic and non-semantic objectives (video classification, depth estimation, camera pose estimation, image and video captioning). Bypassing fine-tuning, Gen4U unifies the generation and understanding paradigms, achieving strong perception performance while fully preserving the model's ability to generate high-quality video.

</details>


### [20] [Geometric Collapse: When Vision Models Fail to Verify Physical Causality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06871)
*Wentao Zhang,Jinhu Qi,Weiqiang Jin,Yifei Zhang,Chan-Tong Lam,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Edge-like perturbations degrade dense depth predictions far more than energy-matched noise, revealing a global geometric collapse and limited repair capability, highlighting the need for explicit plausibility checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether large-scale self-supervised dense depth predictors exhibit inference-time physical plausibility checks and quantify susceptibility to unsupported edge cues.

Method: Introduce Scrambled Edges: a controlled counterfactual that injects salient edge-like cues while violating surface continuity, illumination coherence, and occlusion ordering; compare against energy-matched and structure-matched controls; evaluate across CNN/ViT/SSL depth predictors on NYU Depth v2 and KITTI; extend to diffusion and flow-matching depth estimators; analyze deviation from clean predictions, global propagation, and repair with oracle masking.

Result: Scrambled Edges induce up to 3.2x larger deviation from clean predictions than energy-matched noise; geometric collapse propagates globally; even with oracle knowledge of corrupted regions, output-level repair recovers only 47%, with substantial error outside the mask.

Conclusion: Current dense predictors lack reliable mechanisms to quarantine physically unsupported edge cues; results motivate explicit plausibility scoring and selective cue integration.

Abstract: Recent progress in large-scale self-supervised learning has improved dense geometric prediction, but it remains unclear whether such scaling yields inference-time physical plausibility checks. We propose Scrambled Edges, a controlled counterfactual that injects salient edge-like cues while violating surface continuity, illumination coherence, and occlusion ordering. With energy-matched and structure-matched controls, we isolate the effect of unsupported edge evidence from high-frequency energy and edge sparsity. Across CNN/ViT/SSL depth predictors on NYU Depth v2 and KITTI, Scrambled Edges induce up to 3.2x larger deviation from clean predictions than energy-matched noise; additional diffusion and flow-matching depth estimators show attenuated but still significant collapse. The resulting Geometric Collapse propagates globally: even with oracle knowledge of the corrupted region, output-level repair recovers only 47%, with substantial error outside the mask. These findings provide controlled behavioral evidence that current dense predictors lack reliable mechanisms to quarantine physically unsupported edge cues, motivating explicit plausibility scoring and selective cue integration.

</details>


### [21] [Ensemble Deep Learning Approaches for AI-Altered Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06872)
*Laiba Khan,Hung-Mao Wu,Wei Lin,Frank Bi,Yousef Abdelhadi,Joshua Jung*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multimodal deepfake detector combines audio and visual cues via an ensemble of models to improve robustness across varied deepfakes, but generalization to unseen manipulations remains challenging, with about 70% accuracy on diverse tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deepfake detectors often rely on single models and exhibit poor cross-dataset generalization. Leveraging multimodal signals (audio+visual) and ensemble fusion aims to improve robustness to unseen manipulations.

Method: Input video → extract audio and MTCNN-based face frames → apply AASIST (audio) and visual models (EfficientNet, XceptionNet, MesoNet) to compute fake-likelihood scores → ensemble these scores using mean fusion and a stacking meta-model to produce final decision.

Result: Individual models perform well on their training datasets but degrade on diverse, unseen datasets. The ensemble approach improves robustness and yields more consistent performance across different deepfake types, with average accuracy around 70% on unseen manipulations.

Conclusion: Multimodal analysis and ensemble fusion enhance deepfake detection robustness, but generalization to unseen manipulations remains the central challenge; combining audio and visual cues is more reliable than single-modality methods.

Abstract: The increasing accessibility of artificial intelligence has led to a rapid rise in AI-generated videos, making it more difficult to distinguish between real and manipulated content. Many existing detection methods rely on a single model and often struggle to generalize across different types of deepfakes. In this work, we developed a multimodal deepfake detection system that combines both audio and visual analysis using an ensemble of models. The system includes AASIST for audio-based detection, and EfficientNet, XceptionNet, and MesoNet for analyzing visual features in video frames. The pipeline takes a video as input, separates the audio, and extracts face frames using MTCNN. Each model produces a score indicating the likelihood of the input being fake. These scores are then combined using ensemble strategies, including mean averaging and stacking. Mean fusion provides a simple and stable baseline, while stacking uses a trained meta-model to learn how to combine predictions more effectively. Results show that while individual models perform well on the datasets they were trained on, their performance drops when tested on more diverse datasets. The ensemble approach helps improve overall robustness by combining predictions from multiple models, leading to more consistent performance across different types of deepfakes. This suggests that using both audio and visual information together is a more reliable approach for deepfake detection. Our results highlight generalization to unseen manipulations as the central open challenge, with average accuracy around 70%.

</details>


### [22] [Video2Reaction: Mapping Video to Audience Reaction Distribution in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06875)
*Trang Nguyen,Sidong Zhang,Shiv Shankar,Gauri Jagatap,Deepak Chandran,Andrea Fanelli,Madalina Fiterau*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes Video2Reaction: a multimodal dataset mapping video segments to distributions of induced viewer emotions, enabling reaction prediction and benchmarking; achieves strong annotation via a two-stage multi-agent LLM pipeline and shows finetuned models outperform zero-shot baselines but still struggle with dominant reaction accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable prediction and analysis of audience reactions for content creation, recommendation, and media analytics, addressing the lack of large-scale, in-the-wild emotion-distribution data and the noise/subjectivity in annotations.

Method: Construct Video2Reaction (>10k videos) linking short movie segments to emotion distributions derived from social-media signals. Develop a two-stage, multi-agent annotation pipeline using open-source LLMs for cost-effective continuous annotations. Benchmark pretrained foundation video models (zero-shot) versus finetuned models for predicting full reaction distributions and dominant reactions.

Result: Annotation pipeline achieves 86% correctness under blind human verification. Zero-shot video models underperform; finetuning yields state-of-the-art performance predicting both full distributions and dominant reactions. Top-3 F1 for dominant reaction reaches 77% (LLaVA-Next). Dataset and code released on project page.

Conclusion: First benchmark for video-to-reaction-distribution prediction in-the-wild; demonstrates feasibility and promise of reaction-prediction with finetuning, while highlighting substantial room for improvement in modeling collective audience reactions.

Abstract: Understanding and forecasting audience reactions to video content are crucial for improving content creation, recommendation systems, and media analysis. To enable audience reaction prediction and other content engagement applications, we introduce $\textbf{Video2Reaction}$, a multimodal dataset that maps short movie segments to a distribution of $\textit{induced emotions}$ of viewers in the wild, as expressed through social media. $\textbf{Video2Reaction}$ spans more than 10,000 videos and serves as a reliable benchmark as well as a training resource for audience reaction prediction. To enable cost-effective continuous annotations as reactions may change over time, we develop a two-stage multi-agent pipeline using only open-source LLMs, achieving 86% correctness under blind human verification despite the inherently noisy and subjective nature of the task. We establish the first benchmark for video-to-reaction-distribution prediction in the wild and show that pretrained foundation video models fail in zero-shot settings, while finetuning transforms them into state-of-the-art predictors capable of modeling both full reaction distributions and dominant responses from video alone. However, the task remains challenging: even the strongest methods achieve only 77% Top-3 F1 in dominant reaction prediction (LLaVA-Next), highlighting a substantial gap in modeling collective audience reaction. \modification{Dataset and code are available at our project page: https://information-fusion-lab-umass.github.io/video2reaction-bench.github.io

</details>


### [23] [ReMoDEx: A Local-to-Global Relevance-Based Model Decision Explainability Framework for large-Scale Image Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06889)
*Abhay Kumar Pathak,Mrityunjay Chaubey,Manjari Gupta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ReMoDEx is a scalable framework for evaluating image classifier decision strategies by aggregating multiple local explainer maps into global decision clusters, exposing shortcut cues that standard metrics miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models are opaque and may rely on irrelevant cues or artifacts; heatmaps are not scalable for large datasets, necessitating dataset-scale assessment of decision behavior.

Method: A stepwise pipeline: model inference -> target class selection -> relevance map generation (GradCAM++, Integrated Gradients, Occlusion, Layerwise Relevance Propagation) -> heatmap standardisation -> similarity-based grouping of relevance patterns -> cluster-level interpretation -> spatial relevance assessment. A global module summarises relevance maps across the dataset into a few decision-strategy clusters. Evaluation applied to a VGG16-based classifier for COVID-19, Normal, Lung Opacity, and Viral Pneumonia.

Result: Despite solid performance (86.27% test accuracy, 0.9624 test AUC), explainer-derived maps consistently revealed two strategies: central thoracic region reliance and border/corner sensitivity, suggesting shortcut learning not captured by conventional metrics. Masked image validation showed classifier behavior changed when central or peripheral regions were occluded.

Conclusion: ReMoDEx provides a scalable, relevance-based framework to assess model decision behavior at dataset scale and complements accuracy-based evaluation by exposing shortcut-driven decision strategies.

Abstract: Deep learning image classifiers achieve strong predictive performance yet remain opaque in how decisions are formed. A model may predict correctly while relying on irrelevant cues, shortcut associations, peripheral structures, or device level artifacts instead of task relevant regions. On large scale datasets this opacity is especially problematic, since inspecting heatmaps one sample at a time cannot scale to thousands of predictions. We propose Relevance Based Model Decision Explainability (ReMoDEx), a framework for systematic, dataset scale assessment of model decision behaviour in image classification. ReMoDEx defines a stepwise pipeline: model inference, target class selection, relevance map generation, heatmap standardisation, similarity based grouping of patterns, cluster level interpretation, and spatial relevance assessment. Local methods GradCAM++, Integrated Gradients, Occlusion Sensitivity, and Layerwise Relevance Propagation are each combined independently with a single global module that summarises an entire set of relevance maps into a few decision strategy clusters, replacing sample by sample inspection with an automatic, scalable summary. To demonstrate ReMoDEx, we applied it to a VGG16 based classifier distinguishing COVID-19, Normal, Lung Opacity, and Viral Pneumonia. The classifier showed stable performance (86.27% test accuracy, 0.9624 test AUC). However, each explainer combined with the global module consistently produced two recurring strategies: central thoracic region decisions and border/corner sensitive decisions, indicating possible shortcut learning that conventional metrics could not reveal. Masked image validation confirmed that model confidence and predicted class changed when central or peripheral regions were occluded. ReMoDEx thus provides a scalable relevance based decision assessment framework and an essential complement to accuracy based evaluation.

</details>


### [24] [Seeing What Matters: Lesion-Aware High-Resolution Patch Discovery and Fusion for Chest X-ray Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06909)
*Yingshu Li,Yunyi Liu,Zhenghao Chen,Tong Chen,Zailong Chen,Lingqiao Liu,Lei Wang,Luping Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LePaX enables high-resolution chest X-ray perception under a fixed visual token budget without increasing tokens, addressing limitations of downsampled inputs in radiology report generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the loss of subtle, clinically important cues in downsampled CXRs due to fixed vision-token budgets, and to avoid token inflation from naive high-res tiling or loss from global compression.

Method: Introduce LePaX with Learnable Spatial Resolution Allocation (LSRA) to allocate high-resolution capacity to diagnostically relevant regions and Global-Regional Fusion (GRF) to fuse high-res regional evidence back to the global feature grid without increasing token count. Treat high-resolution perception as a constrained spatial resolution allocation problem under a fixed token budget.

Result: Demonstrates consistent improvements in clinical and linguistic metrics across multiple CXR benchmarks; achieves native-resolution perception (up to 1920x1920) with more than 10× fewer visual tokens than naive tiling, validating efficiency and effectiveness.

Conclusion: LePaX is the first RRG framework to enable efficient high-resolution CXR perception under a fixed token budget, balancing diagnostic fidelity with token efficiency, and offering a scalable solution for high-resolution radiology report generation.

Abstract: Despite rapid advances in chest X-ray (CXR) foundation models, most radiology report generation (RRG) systems still rely on heavily downsampled inputs (e.g., 256x256) due to the fixed visual token budgets of pretrained vision encoders, suppressing subtle yet clinically important cues present in native-resolution images. However, enabling high-resolution (high-res) perception remains challenging: naive tiling causes prohibitive token inflation, while global compression suppresses subtle lesions and degrades diagnostic fidelity. Inspired by radiologists' workflow, localizing suspicious regions before detailed high-res assessment. We propose Lesion-Aware High-Resolution Patch Discovery and Fusion for Chest X-ray Reporting (LePaX), the first RRG framework that enables efficient high-res CXR perception (up to 1920x1920) without increasing the vision-token count. LePaX formulates high-res perception as a constrained spatial resolution allocation problem under a fixed token budget and introduces two key components: Learnable Spatial Resolution Allocation (LSRA), which learns a spatial utility map that adaptively allocates limited high-res capacity to diagnostically relevant regions, enabling targeted extraction of high-res patches from native CXRs; and Global-Regional Fusion (GRF), which performs token-preserving region-to-global refinement by projecting high-resolution regional evidence back onto the global feature grid through spatially aligned resolution write-back, avoiding token inflation. Experiments on multiple CXR benchmarks demonstrate that LePaX consistently improves both clinical and linguistic metrics while enabling native-resolution CXR perception with over 10x fewer visual tokens than naive high-res tiling.

</details>


### [25] [Smart Scissor: Coupling Spatial Redundancy Reduction and CNN Compression for Embedded Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06915)
