These results establish uncertainty-aware fusion as a principled and effective paradigm for online 3D scene understanding. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/yyyyangyi/PUF.

</details>


### [50] [Stage-Aware Adaptation and Distribution Calibration for Subject-Driven Personalized Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07173)
*Wenyan Xu,Alizer Wong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes SPaRa-DCAL: a two-part framework for personalized text-to-image generation that uses stage-aware low-rank adaptation (SPaRa) during training and distribution-calibrated candidate selection (DCAL) at inference, improving identity-consistency and alignment metrics while trading off representation diversity; highlights the need to evaluate personalization on identity, text alignment, and diversity rather than identity alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable subject-driven personalization in diffusion-based T2I with few references while preserving subject identity and ensuring sample diversity. Uniform low-rank constraints and identity-centric sample selection can under- or over-constrain different denoising stages and reduce diversity.

Method: Decompose personalization into SPaRa (training-time stage-aware low-rank adapters) and DCAL (inference-time distribution-calibrated candidate selection), with SPaRa-DCAL as the combined framework. Theoretical analysis shows timestep-dependent scaling governs perturbation magnitude of a low-rank adapter, and identity-biased candidate selection bounds the feature radius around a reference center under certain conditions. Empirical evaluation on SDXL and DreamBooth (30-subject protocol) uses a fixed LoRA pool to show DCAL improves multiple perceptual and alignment metrics (1-LPIPS, CLIP-I, DINO-I, CLIP-T) but trades off pairwise diversity metrics.

Result: DCAL yields improved identity-consistent and text-aligned representations (better 1-LPIPS, CLIP-I, DINO-I, CLIP-T) for a fixed LoRA pool, at some cost to diversity across samples and pairwise similarity; SPaRa-DCAL demonstrates the importance of stage-aware and distribution-aware components for personalized generation.

Conclusion: Personalized generation should be evaluated on identity consistency, text alignment, and representation diversity rather than identity metrics alone; stage-aware adaptation and distribution-aware candidate selection are effective components for balancing identity preservation with visual diversity.

Abstract: Subject-driven personalized text-to-image generation requires a pretrained diffusion model to acquire a specific subject from a few reference images while preserving subject identity, following novel text prompts, and maintaining sample diversity. Existing optimization-based methods instantiate subject adaptation through full fine-tuning, textual embedding optimization, or low-rank parameter updates; PaRa further constrains personalization from the perspective of parameter rank reduction. However, a uniform low-rank constraint or a uniform adapter strength cannot explicitly distinguish the capacity requirements of different denoising stages. Moreover, inference-time candidate selection driven mainly by identity similarity may compress the selected samples in the visual representation space. We decompose the problem into two complementary components: SPaRa denotes training-side stage-aware low-rank adaptation, DCAL denotes inference-side distribution-calibrated candidate selection, and SPaRa-DCAL denotes the combined framework. Theoretical analysis shows that timestep-dependent scaling controls the effective perturbation magnitude of a low-rank adapter, while identity-biased candidate selection restricts the radius of selected features around the reference center under explicit conditions. Auditable experiments under the SDXL and DreamBooth 30-subject protocol show that DCAL improves 1-LPIPS, CLIP-I, DINO-I, and CLIP-T on a fixed LoRA candidate pool, while revealing a clear trade-off with CLIP/DINO pairwise diversity and pairwise LPIPS. These results indicate that personalized generation should be evaluated through identity consistency, text alignment, and representation diversity rather than identity metrics alone.

</details>


### [51] [Comparative Study of Domain-adapted VLMs for General Document Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07179)
*Miguel Lopez-Duran,Elena Marrero,Julian Fierrez,Marta Robledo-Moreno,Ruben Vera-Rodriguez,Daniel DeAlcala,Aythami Morales,Ruben Tolosana,Oscar Delgado,Alvaro Ortigosa,Javier Ortega-Garcia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Evaluates 8 open-source pretrained Vision-Language Models on DocVQA across industrial docs, infographics, and slides, under zero-shot, finetuning, and few-shot transfer; finds visual understanding is the bottleneck; large models excel zero-shot on structured layouts but degrade on complex visuals; finetuning boosts smaller models; 50-shot adaptation enables rapid domain transfer, sometimes surpassing fully supervised.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand robustness and transferability of VLMs to DocVQA across diverse domains and determine whether limitations are due to knowledge or visual understanding; assess cross-domain and few-shot transfer capabilities.

Method: Systematic evaluation of 8 open-source pretrained VLMs on DocVQA in three document domains; experiments include zero-shot, fully supervised fine-tuning with inter- and intra-domain splits, and few-shot cross-domain transfer; analysis of scaling effects and bottlenecks; use target domain data (as few as 50 samples) to study rapid adaptation.

Result: Large pretrained VLMs give strong zero-shot baselines for structured layouts; performance drops with visually complex infographics and slides; parameter scaling is a key factor; supervised finetuning yields larger relative gains for smaller architectures; cross-domain and few-shot experiments indicate visual understanding is the main bottleneck rather than knowledge; 50-shot finetuning enables rapid adaptation, sometimes outperforming fully supervised models.

Conclusion: To advance DocVQA, prioritize improving visual reasoning and layout understanding; exploit cross-domain few-shot finetuning to adapt models with limited data; significant gains can be achieved with small domain-specific datasets, while larger models benefit mainly in zero-shot settings but still require robust visual comprehension.

Abstract: Document Visual Question Answering (DocVQA) presents a complex multimodal challenge, requiring models to exploit visual, textual, and layout information from documents. Although Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have shown remarkable performance in text-vision tasks, their robustness and transferability to different document domains remains underexplored. In this study, we present a comprehensive evaluation of 8 open-source pretrained VLMs on DocVQA in three different document domains: industrial documents of varying type, infographics, and presentation slides. We systematically assess model performance under zero-shot evaluations, fully supervised finetuning with inter- and intra-dataset evaluations, and few-shot learning evaluations of knowledge transfer between domains. Our findings demonstrate that while large pretrained VLMs possess strong zero-shot baselines for structured layouts, their performance strongly decreases on visually complex layouts of infographics and slides. Although parameter scaling is a dominant factor on performance, supervised finetuning yields higher relative gains in smaller architectures. Furthermore, our cross-domain and few-shot experiments show that visual understanding is the main bottleneck for DocVQA, not a lack of knowledge from the VLMs. Using 50 target domain samples, the models finetuned in DocVQA with datasets of different domains rapidly adapt to the target domain documents, even surpassing their fully supervised counterparts in some cases.

</details>


### [52] [EditVerse3D: High-Quality 3D Object Editing with Region-Aware Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07187)
*Youtan Yin,Yanning Zhou,Jiacheng Wei,Xiaofeng Yang,Jun Zhang,Jiayang Bai,Jingwen Ye,Weidong Zhang,Guosheng Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EditVerse3D enables high-quality 3D edits guided by coarse region hints and a single 2D reference image, using a region-aware adaptive loss, targeted data augmentation, and a large-scale dataset to achieve superior results over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D editing methods require precise boundaries or full 2D edits, failing to support coarse, user-friendly guidance for local edits. There is a need for a framework that can produce coherent, high-fidelity edits from coarse 3D boxes and a 2D reference image.

Method: EditVerse3D takes a 3D object, a coarse 3D bounding box for the target region, and a 2D reference image describing the desired modification. It introduces a region-aware adaptive loss that focuses on hard-to-learn regions and balances edits with preservation. The training protocol includes data augmentations (e.g., scaled 3D masks) and filtering of unrealistic editing pairs. A large-scale 3D editing dataset derived from parts information supports training. The approach aims to produce coherent, high-fidelity edited 3D objects under coarse guidance.

Result: Extensive experiments show that EditVerse3D achieves superior visual quality and quantitative performance relative to existing 3D editing approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively enables high-quality, region-guided 3D editing from coarse spatial cues and a 2D reference, supported by a specialized loss, robust augmentation, and a large 3D editing dataset; it surpasses prior methods on both qualitative and quantitative metrics.

Abstract: Local editing of 3D objects remains a long-standing challenge. When interacting with 3D content, humans naturally tend to specify a coarse region of interest for modification rather than defining precise editing boundaries. However, previous methods rely on fully edited 2D images, precise 3D masks, or redundant pipelines, which present a gap. To bridge this gap, we propose EditVerse3D, a novel 3D editing framework that enables high-quality object editing under such coarse guidance. Our approach takes as input a 3D object to be edited, a coarse 3D bounding box indicating the target region, and a reference 2D image describing the desired modification. It produces a coherent, high-fidelity edited 3D object. To facilitate this editing, we introduce a novel region-aware adaptive loss that emphasizes hard-to-learn regions and balances the objective between target and preserved areas. Complementing our loss function, we enhance model robustness and generalization through targeted data augmentations, such as training with scaled 3D masks and filtering out unrealistic editing pairs. We construct a large-scale 3D editing dataset derived from parts information. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EditVerse3D achieves superior visual quality and quantitative performance compared to existing 3D editing approaches. Please visit our project page at https://editverse3d.github.io.

</details>


### [53] [Prototype-Anchored Generalized Manifold Regression for Unknown-Domain Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07192)
*Zihao Zhang,Aming Wu,Yang Li,Yahong Han*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MR-DCoT reframes unseen-domain generalization as manifold regression, combining VLM-guided semantic evolution with diffusion-based structural perturbations to generate off-manifold hard examples and a Class-Specific Prototype Anchoring rectification operator to align deviant features with the source semantic manifold; closed-loop learning yields robust single-domain generalization to multiple unseen domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-Domain Generalized Object Detection (Single-DGOD) suffers from overfitting to synthetic augmentations and limited robustness to real-world variations. The authors argue that semantic features lie on a compact low-dimensional manifold, and robust generalization requires rectifying samples back to this semantic manifold rather than exhaustively simulating perturbations.

Method: Introduce Visual-Text Dual Chain-of-Thought (DCoT) to fuse VLM-guided semantic evolution with diffusion-based structural perturbation, creating structured off-manifold hard examples. Then learn a rectification operator via Class-Specific Prototype Anchoring (C-SPA) to project deviant features toward the source semantic manifold. The approach integrates outlier generation and semantic correction in a closed-loop framework to narrow the distribution gap.

Result: Extensive experiments on three benchmarks—adverse-weather detection, real-to-art generalization, and zero-shot semantic segmentation—demonstrate the method’s effectiveness and versatility, with substantial improvements over baselines and strong robustness to unseen shifts.

Conclusion: MR-DCoT provides a principled framework for unseen-domain object detection by anchoring representations to a semantic manifold, enabling robust single-domain generalization without relying solely on extensive data augmentation or synthetic perturbations.

Abstract: In this paper, we study Single-Domain Generalized Object Detection (Single-DGOD), which aims to transfer a detector trained on a single source domain to multiple unseen domains. Existing methods mainly rely on simulation-driven strategies, such as data augmentation or textual prompts, to enlarge the training distribution. However, finite simulations can hardly cover the dynamic variations of real-world scenarios, often causing overfitting to synthetic styles and limited robustness to complex structural degradations. Inspired by the manifold hypothesis, we argue that semantic features, despite diverse visual changes, should lie on a compact and stable low-dimensional manifold. Therefore, robust generalization requires rectifying deviant samples back to this semantic manifold, rather than exhaustively simulating external perturbations. To this end, we propose Manifold Regression with Visual-Text Dual Chain-of-Thought (MR-DCoT), which formulates unknown-domain generalization as a manifold regression problem. MR-DCoT first uses a Visual-Text Dual Chain-of-Thought module to combine VLM-guided semantic evolution with diffusion-based structural perturbation, generating structured off-manifold hard examples. It then introduces Class-Specific Prototype Anchoring to learn a rectification operator that projects deviant features toward the source semantic manifold. By integrating outlier generation and semantic correction into a closed loop, MR-DCoT effectively narrows the distribution gap and improves robustness under unseen shifts. Extensive experiments on three complementary benchmarks, including adverse-weather detection, real-to-art generalization, and zero-shot semantic segmentation, demonstrate the effectiveness and versatility of our method.

</details>


### [54] [DiffCVE: Diffusion-based Compressed Video Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07195)
*Wenqiang Xiao,Wenzhuo Ma,Junxi Zhang,Zhenzhong Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A diffusion-based compressed video enhancement method (DiffCVE) that integrates coding priors (residuals, motion vectors) and compression-aware prompts with LoRA into a dual-conditioning diffusion framework, plus a coding-prior guided weighted fusion in the VAE decoder to improve perceptual quality under severe compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Severe video compression creates complex artifacts and information loss. Diffusion-based restorations can hallucinate structure and may not respect compression degradation; leveraging coding priors and compression-aware guidance can yield more faithful restorations.

Method: CPDC: Coding Prior-enhanced Dual Conditioning with branches that jointly model compressed video and coding priors (residuals and motion vectors). CDSP: Compression Degradation Semantic Prompting using QP-conditioned textual prompts and LoRA fine-tuning to inject compression severity into diffusion. CPWF: Coding Prior-guided Weighted Fusion in the VAE decoder to fuse VAE and coding-prior encoder features with QP-predicted weights.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate improved perceptual quality, especially under severe compression settings; a project page with demonstrations is provided.

Conclusion: The proposed diffusion-based CVE framework effectively integrates coding priors and compression-aware guidance to reduce structure-inconsistent artifacts and enhance perceptual quality under challenging compression levels.

Abstract: Perceptual quality enhancement of severely compressed videos remains challenging due to complex artifact patterns and substantial information loss. Recent diffusion models have demonstrated strong generative capability for visual restoration, but directly applying them to compressed video often ignores compression degradation characteristics and may introduce structure-inconsistent hallucinations. To address this issue, this paper presents a diffusion-based compressed video enhancement method, named DiffCVE. Coding Prior-enhanced Dual Conditioning (CPDC) branches are designed to jointly model compressed video and coding prior conditions, where coding priors including residuals and motion vectors provide complementary structural and motion guidance during the diffusion denoising process. To make the diffusion process aware of compression severity, a Compression Degradation Semantic Prompting (CDSP) mechanism is introduced to leverage QP-conditioned textual prompts together with LoRA fine-tuning. In addition, a Coding Prior-guided Weighted Fusion (CPWF) module is incorporated into the VAE decoder to fuse VAE encoder and coding prior encoder features with QP-predicted weights. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in improving perceptual quality, especially under severe compression settings. The project page with enhanced video demonstrations is available at https://wqmaker.github.io/projects/DiffCVE/.

</details>


### [55] [Why Fake ? Unveiling the Semantic Vocabulary of Deepfake Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07216)
*Vazgken Vanian,Alexandros Doumanoglou,Dimitris Zarpalas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Post-hoc explainable AI using Encoding-Decoding Direction Pairs (EDDP) is applied to state-of-the-art deepfake detectors to uncover their internal concept space and reading/writing of concept information. This enables deeper, grounded explanations beyond binary real/fake labels, including global understanding, spatial localization, and counterfactual analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deepfake detectors typically output a binary real/fake decision with little justification or grounding, limiting their use in real-world/legal contexts. Existing XAI approaches often rely on manual artifact annotations or produce textual explanations without solid grounding. There is a need to reveal the detector's learned concepts and decision mechanics.

Method: Apply Encoding-Decoding Direction Pairs (EDDP) as a post-hoc XAI technique to analyze black-box DF detectors. Use EDDP to uncover the detectors' concept space (semantic vocabulary) and to study how concept information is encoded/decoded within internal representations. Leverage the approach to perform global model analysis, spatially aware concept localization, and counterfactual what-if scenarios.

Result: Identification of previously hidden real and fake features learned during detector training, enabling nuanced explanations that go beyond conventional methods. Provides a framework for global understanding, spatial localization of concepts, and counterfactual reasoning about detector decisions.

Conclusion: EDDP offers deeper, grounded explanations of deepfake detection strategies by elucidating the concept space and internal mechanics of detectors. This facilitates more transparent and justifyable DF detection, including spatial localization and counterfactual analysis, advancing explainable DF detection.

Abstract: Deepfake (DF) technology poses a significant threat to information integrity, driving the need for robust detection methods. Most DF detectors only consider predicting a binary label for whether the input is real or fake, lacking the justification required for real-world applications like legal proceedings. Explainable DF Detection has emerged to address this limitation, but existing techniques frequently fall short by either relying on human annotations for precise artifact localization or generating superficially plausible textual explanations without grounding. This work investigates the use of post-hoc explainable AI (XAI) to analyze the decision-making process of state-of-the-art black-box DF detectors. Specifically, we employ Encoding-Decoding Direction Pairs (EDDP), a technique suitable for uncovering the concept space of DF detectors (their semantic vocabulary) as well as the mechanism for writing and reading concept information to and from internal representations. Our analysis reveals previously hidden real and fake features learned implicitly during detector training, offering nuanced explanations unattainable through conventional methods. This enables global model understanding, spatially aware concept localization, and counterfactual what-if analysis, all contributing to a deeper comprehension of DF detection strategies.

</details>


### [56] [Vision Foundation Models in Radiology: A Scoping Review of Data, Methodology, Evaluation and Clinical Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07219)
*Alejandro Vergara-Richart,Xavier Rafael-Palou,Almudena Fuster-Matanzo,Ignacio Iborra Roncales,Ángel Alberich-Bayarri,Ana Jiménez-Pastor*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A scoping review (2017–2026) of radiology-only vision foundation models (VFMs) finds transformer-based, self-supervised methods are prevalent; datasets span brain MRI, thoracoabdominal CT, and chest X-ray with large-to-massive scales; transferability appears promising but translation to practice is hindered by representativeness, inconsistent benchmarks, and limited deployment-focused evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiology VFMs are defined, developed, and evaluated inconsistently across studies. A structured mapping is needed to assess data scale, architectural choices, pretraining strategies, and generalization, and to align evaluation with deployment needs.

Method: PRISMAScR scoping review of peer-reviewed studies (Jan 2017–Mar 2026) describing VFMs trained exclusively on radiological imaging. 67 studies were included and mapped across three pillars: data scale/heterogeneity, architectural/pretraining scalability, and downstream transferability/generalization. Common data modalities were brain MRI, thoracoabdominal CT, and chest X-ray. Predominant methods were transformer-based architectures with self-supervised pretraining (masked image modeling, contrastive learning, multi-stage). Evaluation focused on segmentation and classification; cross-center/scanner/modality validation and deployment-oriented evaluation were inconsistently reported; FUTURE-AI alignment was uneven.

Result: Findings indicate radiology-specific VFMs exhibit promising transferability, yet clinical translation is limited by: (1) insufficient data representativeness and heterogeneity; (2) benchmarks lacking standardization; (3) incomplete or inconsistent reporting; (4) scarce deployment-focused validation and real-world evaluation.

Conclusion: Radiology VFMs hold potential for generalization across modalities and centers, but realizing clinical impact requires improved data representativeness, standardized and comprehensive benchmarking, transparent reporting, and deployment-oriented evaluation aligned with FUTURE-AI principles.

Abstract: Vision foundation models (VFMs) are increasingly being developed for radiological imaging, yet their definition, development and evaluation remain heterogeneous. We conducted a PRISMAScR scoping review of peer-reviewed studies published between January 2017 and March 2026 describing foundation models trained exclusively on radiological imaging data. Sixty-seven studies were included and mapped across three pillars: data scale and heterogeneity, architectural and pretraining scalability, and downstream transferability and generalization. Datasets primarily covered brain MRI, thoracoabdominal CT, and chest X-ray, ranging from fewer than 100,000 samples to multi-million-image cohorts. Transformer-based architectures and self-supervised pretraining predominated, particularly masked image modeling, contrastive learning and multi-stage approaches. Evaluation focused mainly on segmentation and classification, whereas cross-center, cross-scanner, anatomical and modality-shift validation was inconsistently reported. Alignment with FUTURE-AI principles was uneven. Overall, radiology-specific VFMs show promising transferability, but clinical translation remains constrained by limited data representativeness, heterogeneous benchmarks, incomplete reporting and insufficient deployment-oriented evaluation.

</details>


### [57] [`Attention-Guided Cross-Temporal Clustering for Self-Supervised Video Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07230)
*Waqas Arshid,Mohammad Awrangjeb,Alan Wee-Chung Liew,Yongsheng Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A self-supervised framework for video object segmentation that learns mid-level, part-aware representations via cross-temporal consistency and lightweight clustering, using attention-guided token selection on a frozen transformer to achieve scalable, temporally coherent segmentation without dense labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To eliminate dependence on densely annotated data for VOS while maintaining spatial accuracy and temporal coherence, addressing scalability and generalization gaps of flow-based or pretraining-based self-supervised methods in unconstrained multi-object scenarios.

Method: Cross-Temporal Consistency and Clustering framework that builds mid-level part representations through attention-guided token selection and lightweight temporal clustering. It aligns soft part assignments across time using a saliency-weighted symmetric consistency objective, operating on a frozen transformer backbone with adaptive token selection and multi-offset temporal alignment for efficiency across resolutions and motion patterns.

Result: Demonstrates scalable self-supervised VOS by achieving robust cross-frame part-consistency and efficient handling of varying resolutions and motion patterns, enabled by saliency-weighted symmetric consistency and lightweight temporal clustering on a frozen transformer backbone.

Conclusion: This approach shows that self-supervised learning of mid-level, part-aware representations via cross-temporal consistency can provide scalable VOS without dense labels, with strong potential for generalization to unconstrained multi-object scenarios.

Abstract: Video object segmentation (VOS) is a fundamental task in video understanding, requiring accurate delineation and consistent tracking of objects across frames. While supervised methods achieve strong performance, they rely on densely annotated datasets that are costly to obtain and have limited domain coverage. Self-supervised learning offers a promising alternative by removing the need for manual labels; however, existing approaches often struggle to jointly maintain spatial accuracy and temporal coherence, particularly in unconstrained multi-object scenarios. Many rely on optical flow, synthetic motion cues, or task-specific pretraining, limiting scalability and generalisation. We propose a self-supervised framework, Cross-Temporal Consistency and Clustering, that learns mid-level, part-aware representations by combining attention-guided token selection with lightweight temporal clustering. Instead of operating at the pixel or whole-object level, the method aligns soft part assignments across time using a saliency-weighted symmetric consistency objective. The framework leverages a frozen transformer backbone with lightweight modules for adaptive token selection and multi-offset temporal alignment, enabling efficient scaling across resolutions and motion patterns.

</details>


### [58] [Unraveling Machine Behavior by Multi-Level Bias Analysis and Detection: Methodology and Application to Computer Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07236)
*Ignacio Serna,Aythami Morales,Julian Fierrez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces SpaceBias, ActivationBias, and WeightBias for multi-level bias detection inside NNs; shows biases manifest in latent space, activations, and weights; validated on DiveFace gender classification and colored-MNIST; large-scale study with 127k+ models; internal disparities decrease as training data becomes balanced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To diagnose bias inside neural networks across multiple internal levels (latent representations, activations, parameters) rather than only evaluating outputs, enabling deeper interpretability and actionable insights for bias mitigation.

Method: SpaceBias analyzes latent space priors before the final layer using neighbor-probability distributions and a two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test on per-group distributions. ActivationBias extends InsideBias by evaluating final Conv-layer activations with a Mann-Whitney U test, based on the observation that underrepresented groups have lower activations. WeightBias trains a secondary neural network to identify biased patterns directly in the model parameters. Empirical evaluation across two tasks (DiveFace gender classification with ~72k faces and colored-MNIST) with >127k models varying bias severity; performance of detection correlates with degree of imbalance.

Result: The trio of detectors provides interpretable diagnostics of where bias resides inside the network (latent space, activations, and weights) and shows that internal disparities and detection performance decline smoothly as training distributions approach balance. The approach yields deeper insight than outcome-only metrics and is validated across multiple biases and datasets.

Conclusion: Multi-level bias analysis is valuable for understanding how biases operate inside neural networks. The proposed SpaceBias, ActivationBias, and WeightBias extend existing methods to reveal bias mechanisms across latent representations, activations, and parameters, offering practical diagnostics and encouraging more nuanced bias mitigation and auditing.

Abstract: This study investigates the presence and propagation of bias within Neural Networks through a comprehensive multi-level analysis spanning the learned latent space, layer activations, and the network's parameters. Based on this taxonomy, we propose three bias detection approaches: 1) SpaceBias (new method), which characterizes the latent space prior to the final classification layer using neighbor-probability distributions and quantifies bias with the two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test on the per-group distributions. 2) ActivationBias (extension of the existing method InsideBias), which analyzes the activations of neural network filters and quantifies bias via a Mann-Whitney U test, based on the observed fact that underrepresented groups exhibit lower activation levels in the final convolutional layers. 3) WeightBias (extension of the existing method IFBiD), which uses a secondary neural network trained to identify biased patterns directly in the parameters of task-specific models. Unlike conventional methods, which assess neural network outcomes and treat the model as a black box, our proposed techniques provide insight into how biases manifest within the network architecture itself at different levels, offering a more nuanced and detailed understanding. Experiments are conducted on two complementary applications: gender classification in the DiveFace dataset (72,000 face images) and digit classification on a colored-MNIST benchmark with controlled bias severity. In total, more than 127,000 models with varying degrees and types of bias were trained and evaluated. The severity sweep shows that the internal disparity, and with it the detection performance, decreases smoothly as the training distribution approaches balance. The results highlight the importance of methods that provide deeper insight into the behavior of AI models.

</details>


### [59] [An Edge-aware Prompt-enhanced SAM for Ultrasound Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07240)
*Wenhao Li,Fangyi Liu,Bo Du*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EP-SAM enhances ultrasound image segmentation by making SAM edge-aware and prompt-driven, using multi-block encoder features to stabilize coarse-to-fine representations and produce robust prompts that improve boundary delineation under speckle noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultrasound images suffer from poor boundary visibility and speckle noise, causing standard SAM to underperform in medical segmentation; adapting SAM with edge cues and targeted prompts aims to improve contour accuracy and robustness.

Method: Integrates multi-block features from the image encoder to enrich semantic representations across scales and enforces edge-aware supervision on the encoder. The model uses these cues to generate high-quality prompts that guide SAM to target regions, producing refined segmentation boundaries.

Result: On multiple ultrasound benchmarks, EP-SAM consistently surpasses existing SAM-based methods, demonstrating improved boundary delineation and robustness to contour ambiguity and speckle.

Conclusion: Edge-aware supervision combined with prompt-enhanced adaptation effectively tailors SAM for ultrasound segmentation, yielding superior performance and suggesting that edge cues and multi-scale feature enrichment are key for medical image prompting.

Abstract: Ultrasound image segmentation is essential for delineating anatomical structures and lesions, providing the foundation for accurate diagnosis. While the Segment Anything Model (SAM) has demonstrated remarkable success on natural images, its performance on ultrasound data is often hindered by poor boundary delineation. To address this limitation, we propose EP-SAM, an edge-aware and prompt-enhanced adaptation of SAM. Specifically, we leverage multi-block feature extraction from the image encoder to enrich coarse-to-fine semantic representations, while edge-aware supervision of the image encoder improves robustness to contour ambiguity and speckle noise. By integrating these complementary cues, EP-SAM generates high-quality prompts that effectively guide the model toward target regions of interest. Experimental results on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that EP-SAM consistently outperforms existing SAM-based methods.

</details>


### [60] [Naming the Concepts Classifiers Rely On: Language-Anchored Decomposition for Faithful Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07264)
*Ahsan Habib Akash,Dipkamal Bhusal,Stacey Jones,Donald A. Adjeroh,Binod Bhattarai,Prashnna Kumar Gyawali*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LAD is a post-hoc, non-modifying explanation method that yields named, faithful concepts by anchoring language-grounded maps as fixed coefficients in an inverted NMF, enabling stable, human-readable explanations across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the trade-off between faithful but unnamed post-hoc explanations and named but model-altering methods, by producing named yet faithful explanations without retraining the model.

Method: For each class, a language model proposes a concept vocabulary. CLIP-based similarity maps localize these concepts across image regions. LAD performs an inverted non-negative matrix factorization, fixing the language-grounded maps as the coefficient matrix and learning a concept basis to reconstruct the encoder activations, making naming a structural constraint.

Result: LAD yields spatially precise, faithful explanations that remain accurate under concept insertion and deletion across natural, scene, and medical images, and provides stable, human-interpretable concept names.

Conclusion: LAD succeeds in delivering named, faithful, model-unchanged explanations across diverse domains, addressing the interpretability-explanation trade-off and enabling robust, interpretable insights.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are widely deployed in high-stakes visual applications where interpretability is critical, yet existing explanations face a trade-off: post-hoc concept methods recover factors that are faithful to a model's behavior but unnamed, while naming and by-design methods attach human-readable concepts only by retraining or altering the classifier. We propose Language-Anchored Decomposition (LAD), a post-hoc framework that delivers concepts which are simultaneously named, faithful, and obtained without modifying the model. For each class, a large language model proposes a concept vocabulary that CLIP-based similarity maps localize across image regions. Inverting standard non-negative matrix factorization, LAD fixes these language-grounded maps as the coefficient matrix and learns only a concept basis that reconstructs the frozen encoder's activations, so naming becomes a structural constraint and the model's own feature geometry determines which concepts are retained. Removing this anchor preserves accuracy but collapses attribution faithfulness. Across natural-image, scene, and medical-imaging benchmarks, LAD produces spatially precise explanations that are decision-relevant under both concept insertion and deletion, while uniquely providing stable, human-interpretable concept names.

</details>


### [61] [InfraQR: Edge-Placed QR-Inspired Structured Patch Attacks on Infrared Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07288)
*Xin Li,Jiaju Han,Ma Yaqi,Chengyin Hu,Yingying Zhao,Jiahuan Long,Fengyu Zhang,Yahui Chai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: InfraQR is a QR-inspired structured patch attack targeting infrared vision-language models, placing a compact edge-based patch optimized via surrogate CLIP-style encoders, leading to severe cross-task degradation in classification, captioning, and VQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is limited exploration of infrared vision-language robustness against structured, boundary-adjacent perturbations; most work focuses on object-centric attacks on detectors, leaving cross-task safety risks unexamined.

Method: Propose InfraQR: a patch placed along image boundaries; learns grid cells with surrogate CLIP-style encoders; patch appears near-binary and structured but need not be a valid QR; evaluated on classification, caption transfer, and VQA.

Result: On a 300-image infrared benchmark, reduces OpenAI CLIP accuracy from 98.67% to 0.70%; adversarial images transfer to black-box captioning and VQA systems, degrade captions semantically and yield more error-prone answers (GPT-5.4-based eval).

Conclusion: InfraQR demonstrates vulnerability of infrared vision-language models to structured edge perturbations and motivates broader cross-task robustness research beyond occlusion.

Abstract: Infrared vision-language models are increasingly used for perception under low-light and adverse visual conditions, yet their robustness to localized structured perturbations remains underexplored. Existing infrared adversarial studies mainly focus on object detectors, leaving the security of infrared vision-language models less systematically examined. We present InfraQR, a QR-inspired structured patch attack for infrared vision-language models. Unlike localized attacks that attach perturbations to the target object, InfraQR places a compact structured patch along image boundaries and optimizes learnable grid cells through surrogate CLIP-style encoders. The resulting patch has a near-binary structured appearance, but is not required to be a valid or machine-readable QR code. We evaluate InfraQR on infrared classification, caption transfer, and question-answer-aware visual question answering (VQA) tasks. On a 300-image infrared benchmark, InfraQR sharply reduces the accuracy of multiple CLIP-style classifiers, including reducing OpenAI CLIP accuracy from 98.67% to 0.70%. The generated adversarial images also transfer to black-box captioning and VQA models, causing semantic degradation in captions and more error-prone answers under GPT-5.4-based evaluation. These results show that infrared vision-language models remain vulnerable to structured edge-placed perturbations, motivating further study of cross-task robustness beyond direct object occlusion.

</details>


### [62] [CarbonCLIP: Enhance Carbon Prediction from Satellite Imagery via Integrated Street-View Semantics and Temporal Context Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07292)
*Zeru Yang,Fang-Ying Gong,Steve H. L. Yim,Chau Yuen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A dual-branch multimodal distillation framework (CarbonCLIP) improves satellite-based urban carbon emission prediction by injecting semantic priors from LMM-generated street-view text and monthly temporal priors, training with multimodal data but inferring from satellite imagery only; validated on Beijing and Singapore with improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous data sources and lack of fine-grained semantic-temporal context in satellite data hinder cross-city applicability and scalable urban carbon modeling; a data-efficient, scalable approach is needed.

Method: A dual-branch architecture: a spatial branch incorporates fine-grained textual priors generated from street-view images by Large Multimodal Models to encode building functions, infrastructure, and urban activities; a temporal branch uses a month encoder to capture monthly emission variation. The model is trained with multimodal data via contrastive learning to distill knowledge into a unified satellite representation. At inference, only satellite imagery is used.

Result: Experiments on Beijing and Singapore show CarbonCLIP outperforms baselines in both cities, indicating successful transfer of multimodal knowledge into satellite representations and improved accuracy for satellite-based urban carbon modeling.

Conclusion: CarbonCLIP provides a scalable solution for cross-city urban carbon prediction by leveraging multimodal priors during training while requiring only satellite data at inference; demonstrates robustness and potential for broader deployment.

Abstract: Accurately estimating urban carbon emissions is critical for sustainable urban planning, yet many existing approaches remain difficult to apply consistently across cities due to data-source heterogeneity and the lack of fine-grained semantic-temporal context in remote sensing data. We propose CarbonCLIP, a task-oriented multimodal distillation framework that improves satellite-based carbon emission prediction by transferring contextual knowledge into a unified satellite representation through dual-branch contrastive learning. Unlike conventional methods that rely on static visual features, CarbonCLIP explicitly bridges the gap between top-down satellite views and ground-level human activities. Specifically, the spatial branch uses fine-grained textual descriptions automatically generated from street-view images by Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) to provide semantic priors reflecting building functions, infrastructure, and urban activities, while the temporal branch employs a month encoder to encode temporal priors associated with monthly emission variation. CarbonCLIP requires multimodal data only during the pretraining phase; during inference, it relies solely on satellite imagery, thereby supporting scalable deployment when ground-level data are unavailable at inference. Experiments on Beijing and Singapore demonstrate that CarbonCLIP outperforms baselines in both study cities. The results validate that our method effectively transfers multimodal knowledge into satellite representations, offering a robust solution for satellite-based urban carbon modeling.

</details>


### [63] [SoccerNet 2026 Challenges Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07320)
*Anthony Cioppa,Silvio Giancola,Håkan Ardö,Mohamad Dalal,Jan Held,Jérémie Ochin,Jiayuan Rao,Karen Sanchez,Renaud Vandeghen,Artur Xarles,Olivier Barnich,Albert Clapés,Mathieu Delvaux,Sergio Escalera,Bernard Ghanem,Cédric Hons,Antoine Houet,Sotiris Manitsaris,Tom Michel,Pierre Miralles,Thomas B. Moeslund,Mikael Nilsson,Bogdan Stanciulescu,Marc Van Droogenbroeck,Yanfeng Wang,Weidi Xie,Faisal Altawijri,Mohamed Atef,Semen Budennyy,Vasiliy Chelpanov,Puhua Chen,Yixin Chen,Lechao Cheng,Jianling Chu,Ju-Seong Do,Oleg Durygin,Omar Fetouh,Mirco Fuchs,Youssef Ghallab,Falguni Ghosh,Wonjun Heo,Yufeng Hu,Weixuan Huang,Phuong-Linh Huynh-Ha,Matvey Isupov,Yangguang Ji,Siyuan Jiang,Zhenxiang Jiang,Wonyong Jo,Ho-Young Jung,SeongHeon Kang,MinJae Kim,Youngseon Kim,Jakub Komosa,Artem Konshin,Trung-Hoang Le,Jongmin Lee,Lingling Li,Litao Li,Vadim Linkov,Fang Liu,Haoxuan Ma,Shun Makino,Ismail Mathkour,Konstantin Mitin,Mikhail Moiseev,Takumi Nagaya,Yuki Nakamura,Thanh-Khoi Nguyen,Hoang-Phuc Nguyen,Trong-Thuan Nguyen,Christian Orduz,Kwanyong Park,Fabian Perez,Parthsarthi Rawat,SuHyun Rim,Hoover Rueda-Chacón,Atom Scott,Minori Sugimura,Yuyang Sun,Shengeng Tang,Minh-Triet Tran,Ikuma Uchida,Juan Vanegas,Thanh-Nhan Vo,Jiangtao Wang,Yaxiong Wang,Xiaogang Wang,Ruifeng Wang,Rio Watanabe,Jiali Wen,Yongliang Wu,Di Yang,Xu Yang,Zhuo Yang,Xinyu Ye,Yibo Yu,Zihan Zhai,Yu Zhang,Zhenyu Zhao,Zhun Zhong,Yixi Zhou,Xingyu Zhu,Wenbo Zhu,Julian Ziegler*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SoccerNet 2026 Challenges (sixth edition) introduce five vision-based tasks for football video understanding, with annotated data, unified evaluation, and public baselines. The edition attracted broad participation (427 teams, 1,129 entries; 28 reviewed reports). The paper details each task, evaluation protocols, leaderboards, and the top submissions to document the state of the field on held-out data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advance computer vision research in sports video understanding by providing standardized benchmarks across multiple tasks, data, and evaluation protocols to track progress and enable fair comparisons.

Method: Organize five tasks (Ball Action Anticipation; Player-Centric Ball Action Spotting; Novel View Synthesis; Spiideo SoccerNet Synloc localization from a single image; Visual Question Answering) with annotated data, unified evaluation protocols, and public baselines; evaluate and rank submissions on held-out challenge data; report leaderboards and summarize leading submissions.

Result: Broad participation with 427 teams and 1,129 entries across tasks; 28 teams produced reviewed technical reports; leaderboards and top submissions are summarized to reflect the current state across tasks on held-out data.

Conclusion: The paper documents the challenge design, evaluation protocol, and current state of performance across all tasks, providing a benchmark for future work and a record of progress in SoccerNet challenges.

Abstract: The SoccerNet 2026 Challenges constitute the sixth annual edition of the SoccerNet open benchmarking effort, dedicated to advancing computer vision research in sports video understanding. This year's challenges span five vision-based tasks: (1) Ball Action Anticipation, predicting the timing and class of ball-related actions within a short future window from a preceding observation window; (2) Player-Centric Ball Action Spotting, temporally localizing and classifying ball-related actions while assigning each action to the acting player through team affiliation and jersey number; (3) Novel View Synthesis, rendering images from unobserved camera poses in multi-view football scenes; (4) Spiideo SoccerNet Synloc, localizing athletes in real-world pitch coordinates from a single calibrated static-camera image; and (5) Visual Question Answering, answering multiple-choice questions about football broadcasts across text, image, and video inputs. For each task, participants were provided with annotated data, a unified evaluation protocol, and a public baseline. This edition saw broad participation, with 427 teams submitting 1,129 entries across the five tasks and 28 teams contributing reviewed technical reports. This paper describes each task and its evaluation protocol, presents the challenge leaderboards, and summarizes the leading submissions, with the aim of documenting the current state of each task as measured on held-out challenge data.

</details>


### [64] [HAJJv2-CrowdCount: Zero-Shot Benchmark for Dense Crowd Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07322)
*Reem AlYabis,Fares AlTuwaim,AlJawharh AlOtaibi,Mohamed Eltahir*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HAJJv2-CrowdCount provides per-second annotations for HAJJv2 test videos and benchmarks three zero-shot counting methods (SAM3Count, YOLO-World, APGCC). SAM3Count has the best overall MAE, but all methods deteriorate in the densest, highly occluded frames, where a point-based counter remains more robust. The dataset annotations enable reproducibility and extension for deployment-focused crowd management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hajj crowd counting presents extreme challenges: near-vertical camera angles, severe occlusion, and very high crowd counts per frame. Existing benchmarks are private or lack per-second granularity, hindering evaluation of zero-shot counting methods and deployment-ready assessment.

Method: Annotate per-second crowd counts for testing videos in the HAJJv2 dataset (HAJJv2-CrowdCount) and benchmark three zero-shot counting approaches: an open-vocabulary detector (YOLO-World), a point-based counter (APGCC), and a promptable segmentation-based counter (SAM3Count). Evaluate using MAE and per-density regime.

Result: SAM3Count achieves the lowest MAE (70.4; 95% CI 56.0–86.1) ahead of YOLO-World (92.0) and APGCC (152.9). In the densest frames, detection- and segmentation-based counters degrade sharply (MAE > 300), while APGCC remains more robust (MAE 114.9). An inversion between overall and dense regimes has deployment relevance for precise counts in densest scenes. Annotations are released to support reproducibility and extension.

Conclusion: Per-second annotations for HAJJv2 enable robust benchmarking of zero-shot counting methods. In dense, occluded crowds typical of Hajj footage, point-based counting offers superior stability, while segmentation and detection-based approaches falter; releasing the annotations facilitates future work and practical deployment considerations.

Abstract: Automated crowd counting in Hajj video is difficult not because current models lack capacity, but because the footage violates the assumptions those models were built on: cameras observe the crowd from steep, near-vertical angles, individuals occlude one another extensively, and a single frame can contain well over a thousand people. Benchmarks that test crowd counting in such an environment are either private or not detailed per second. We revisit the HAJJv2 dataset and contribute HAJJv2-CrowdCount: per-second human-annotated crowd counts for its testing videos. Using these annotations, we benchmark three recent zero-shot counting paradigms: an open-vocabulary detector (YOLO-World), a point-based counter (APGCC), and a promptable segmentation-based counter (SAM3Count). SAM3Count attains the lowest overall mean absolute error (MAE 70.4, 95% CI 56.0-86.1), ahead of YOLO-World (92.0) and APGCC (152.9). This ordering reverses, however, in the regime most relevant to deployment: on the densest frames, the detection- and segmentation-based counters both degrade sharply (MAE exceeding 300), while the point-based counter degrades far more gracefully (MAE 114.9). This inversion is decision-relevant for Hajj crowd management, where reliable counts are needed most precisely in the densest and most occluded scenes. The annotations are released to support reproduction and extension of these results.

</details>


### [65] [BUS: Brain-Inspired Unsupervised Self-Reflection for Advanced Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07361)
*Jiacheng Yang,Tongying Xiao,Yunkai Dang,Cong Wang,Yuekun Yang,Qi Fan,Wenbin Li,Feng Miao,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A label-free training framework (BUS) for Vision-Language Models that uses backward prediction to enhance reflective reasoning with unlabeled data, compatible with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL), validated on 8 benchmarks with notable improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLMs struggle with complex, fine-grained, consistent reasoning and rely on large annotated datasets. Inspired by neuroscience, backward prediction is explored as an efficient mechanism for reasoning and test-time reflectivity.

Method: 1) Verify that mainstream VLMs can perform backward prediction. 2) Propose Brain-inspired Unsupervised Self-reflection (BUS), enabling backward prediction without labels and providing explicit learning signals. 3) Demonstrate compatibility with SFT and RL. 4) Validate on 8 benchmarks with unlabeled data.

Result: BUS achieves notable improvements over base models using only unlabeled data across diverse tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of backward prediction as a core mechanism for VLM reasoning.

Conclusion: Backward prediction is a critical capability for VLM reasoning. BUS offers a practical, label-free training framework that enhances reflective reasoning and integrates with common fine-tuning pipelines.

Abstract: Current Vision-Language Models (VLMs) often struggle to handle complex visual tasks that require consistent and fine-grained reasoning. Recent methods aim to train models to facilitate self-reflective reasoning, i.e., reviewing and improving the generated reasoning. However, they require large volumes of annotated data and lack explicit reflective behavior during test time. This work aims to bridge this gap through inspiration from neuroscience. The human brain exhibits efficient backward prediction, i.e., predicting which current states are likely to precede a given future state. In this work, we first verify that mainstream VLMs can perform backward prediction, similar to the human brain. Then, we propose Brain-inspired Unsupervised Self-reflection (BUS), a label-free training framework to enhance reflective reasoning capability in challenging image analysis. BUS enables VLMs to perform backward prediction and provide explicit learning signals on data without ground-truth labels. In this way, BUS eliminates reliance on annotated data while improving reasoning performance. Notably, BUS is compatible with popular fine-tuning methods, such as Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL). Finally, extensive experiments on 8 benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of BUS across a wide range of complex visual tasks. It achieves notable improvements over the base model while using only unlabeled training data. Our experimental findings validate that backward prediction capability is critical for VLM reasoning.

</details>


### [66] [MMAgent-R$^2$: Learning to Rerank and Reject for Agentic mRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07383)
*Tao Zhang,Ziqi Zhang,Zongyang Ma,Yuxin Yang,Bing Li,Chunfeng Yuan,Kang Rong,Fengyun Rao,Jing Lyu,Weiming Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MMAgent-R^2 improves knowledge-based VQA by adding internal verification (visual reranking and active rejection) to an agentic mRAG framework, reducing distractor errors from visually similar entities and enabling dynamic candidate retrieval; achieves state-of-the-art on multiple KB-VQA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KB-VQA requires precise retrieval of relevant visual entities from large encyclopedic knowledge bases. Existing multimodal Retrieval Augmented Generation (mRAG) approaches rely on global visual features and struggle when many visually similar entities exist, causing factually mismatched distractors and error propagation.

Method: Proposes MMAgent-R^2 with visual reranking that directly compares query and candidate images to identify the target among similar candidates, and active rejection to discard unreliable results and fetch additional candidates when no confident match is found. Introduces a composite reward function with step-level verification rewards and jointly optimizes external retrieval, internal verification, and answer generation via GRPO training.

Result: Empirical results on InfoSeek, E-VQA, and MMhops demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, with notable gains in challenging retrieval scenarios and complex multi-image multi-hop reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Internal verification and dynamic candidate management in mRAG effectively mitigate error propagation from retrieval failures and enhance KB-VQA performance.

Abstract: Knowledge-based Visual Question Answering (KB-VQA) requires models to retrieve visual entities matching the query image from large-scale encyclopedic knowledge bases and answer related questions. Existing multimodal Retrieval Augmented Generation (mRAG) methods rely on global visual features to match candidate entities, yet when the knowledge base contains numerous visually similar entities, the retriever struggles to distinguish them, populating the candidate set with visually similar but factually mismatched distractors. Since subsequent processing steps such as noise filtering are also confined to this fixed candidate set, errors from failed retrieval inevitably propagate to the final answer. To address these challenges, we propose MMAgent-R$^2$, an agentic mRAG framework that integrates visual reranking and active rejection as its internal verification mechanism. Visual reranking directly compares query and candidate images, capturing discriminative details beyond textual descriptions to precisely identify the target entity among similar candidates; active rejection discards unreliable results and retrieves additional candidates when no confident match is found, moving beyond the fixed candidate pool. We design a composite reward function with step-level verification rewards and achieve joint optimization of external retrieval, internal verification, and answer generation via GRPO training. Experiments on InfoSeek, E-VQA, and MMhops demonstrate that \ours{} achieves state-of-the-art performance, with particularly notable advantages in challenging retrieval scenarios and complex multi-image multi-hop reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [67] [When Prompts Ignore Structure: Graph-Based Attribute Reasoning for Calibrated VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07395)
*Tanay Sodha,Aditya Sharma,Ramya Hebbalaguppe,Vinti Agarwal,Pranav Murthy Yeluripaty*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ARGTCA uses a Symbolic Attribute Graph with a Graph Attention Network to model inter-attribute dependencies for better calibration in test-time adaptation of vision-language models; introduces ARGTCA-DIV and ARGTCA-DISC strategies; yields substantial ECE reductions across nine benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calibration remains a key bottleneck in test-time adaptation for vision-language models. Entropy-driven overconfidence degrades calibration when prompt tuning improves zero-shot accuracy. Existing methods ignore relational structure among class attributes; there is a need to model inter-attribute dependencies to improve reliability.

Method: Construct a Symbolic Attribute Graph where nodes are (class, attribute) pairs. Train a Graph Attention Network (GAT) with contrastive objectives to learn embeddings that capture inter-attribute dependencies. Propose two attribute selection strategies: ARGTCA-DIV (intra-class diversity) and ARGTCA-DISC (inter-class discrimination).

Result: Across nine benchmarks, ARGTCA-DIV reduces average calibration error (ECE) by ~37% over baselines; ARGTCA-DISC is consistently the second-best variant, reducing average ECE by ~17% over baselines.

Conclusion: Modeling symbolic attribute interactions via a graph-based framework provides a principled and effective approach for reliable test-time adaptation in vision-language models, improving calibration without sacrificing zero-shot performance.

Abstract: Reliable confidence estimation remains a key limitation of test-time adaptation in vision-language models (VLMs), where prompt tuning improves zero-shot accuracy but often degrades calibration due to entropy-driven overconfidence. Prior approaches mitigate this using LLM-derived class attributes and contrastive regularization, yet treat attributes independently, ignoring their relational structure. We propose ARGTCA, which represents (class, attribute) pairs as nodes in a Symbolic Attribute Graph and trains a Graph Attention Network (GAT) using contrastive objectives to produce structurally informed embeddings that capture inter-attribute dependencies. We introduce two attribute selection strategies: ARGTCA-DIV for intra-class diversity and ARGTCA-DISC for inter-class discrimination. Experiments across nine benchmarks show that ARGTCA-DIV reduces average Expected Calibration Error (ECE) by approximately ~37% over baselines, while ARGTCA-DISC consistently performs as the second-best variant, reducing average ECE by approximately ~17% over baselines. These results suggest that modeling symbolic attribute interactions provides a principled approach for reliable test-time adaptation in VLMs.

</details>


### [68] [Heterogeneity-Adaptive Diffusion Schrodinger Bridge for PET-Guided Whole-Body MRI Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07401)
*Chengbo Wang,Jiacheng Yu,Linjie Bian,Ming Qi,Xiaosheng Liu,Tongtong Che,Jichang Zhang,Shuyu Li,Shaoli Song,Xiuying Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Diffusion-based whole-body MRI translation with heterogeneity adaptation (HA-DSB) that uses region-aware context from a vision-language model and PET-guided lesion priors to handle cross-region variability and improve lesion fidelity; achieves superior translation across body regions and lesion areas; code available on Github.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce PET-MR acquisition time by translating MRI from faster acquisitions, addressing challenges of whole-body scans with heterogeneous anatomy and pathology distributions.

Method: Modeling translation as a stochastic transport via a Heterogeneity-Adaptive Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge (HA-DSB). Incorporates region context embeddings from a vision-language model to tailor translation per anatomical region. Integrates lesion-aware metabolic priors from PET through dual-stage guidance: (1) PET-guided noise modulation in the forward diffusion, (2) PET-feature-guided attention in the reverse diffusion to amplify lesion-relevant structures.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that HA-DSB achieves superior translation quality across different body regions in whole-body MRI translation and improves lesion-area translation when guided by PET information.

Conclusion: The proposed HA-DSB framework effectively handles whole-body heterogeneity for MRI translation by combining region-aware contextual guidance and lesion-focused PET priors, enabling more accurate and clinically relevant translations; code is publicly available.

Abstract: While whole-body multimodal medical imaging scanners have been increasingly recognized for more effective medical applications, the excessive long acquisition time in PET-MR scanning is a major obstacle in more efficient clinical practice. Deep learning-based MRI translation provides a potential solution to reduce scan duration. However, current models often focus on specific anatomical regions and face challenges for whole-body scans that consists of highly heterogeneous feature distributions mainly due to (1) different anatomical regions across whole-body, and (2) lesions or pathological tissues. This paper tackles the challenges through a novel Heterogeneity-Adaptive Diffusion Schrodinger Bridge (HA-DSB) framework. By explicitly modeling translation as stochastic transport between source and target distributions, HA-DSB incorporates region context embeddings derived from a vision-language model (VLM) to enable region-specific modeling. To enhance fidelity of the pathological tissue, lesion-aware metabolic prior from PET is integrated directly into the bridge dynamics through a dual-stage guidance mechanism. Specifically, a PET-guided noise modulation module adaptively scales spatial diffusion perturbations during the forward process, while PET features are leveraged during the reverse process to selectively amplify lesion-relevant structures via an attention mechanism. Experiments demonstrate the superiority of our method across different body regions in whole-body MRI translation and show improved translation quality in lesion areas under PET guidance. Our code is available at Github.

</details>


### [69] [VCDP: Variation-Conditioned Distributional Proxy Learning for Semi-Supervised Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07416)
*Zimu Zhang,Yiheng Zhong,Zhuoru Zhang,Yingzhen Hu,Yanan He,Fanliang Meng,Xiaofeng Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-only regularizer for semi-supervised 3D medical image segmentation that learns class-specific Gaussian distributions with multiple variation prototypes and a variation-conditioned compatibility score to align voxel embeddings with both global organ identity and local intra-class variation, improving segmentation of small/ambiguous organs without extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semi-supervised segmentation methods (consistency regularization, pseudo-labeling, co-training) improve prediction robustness but often fail to organize features in space for anatomically complex structures, particularly small organs and ambiguous boundary regions with high intra-class variation.

Method: Introduce VCDP: for each class, learn a Gaussian distribution representing shared semantics plus multiple variation prototypes for intra-class variability. A variation-conditioned compatibility score fuses distributional similarity with soft variation aggregation to guide voxel embeddings toward both global identity and local variations. Implemented as a training-only regularization attached to decoder features; removed at inference, yielding no extra inference cost.

Result: Experiments on multi-organ segmentation benchmarks show that VCDP improves most evaluated baselines, with pronounced gains for small, ambiguous, and highly variable organs.

Conclusion: VCDP provides an effective, plug-and-play training regularizer that enhances feature-space organization in semi-supervised 3D segmentation without increasing inference cost, enabling better segmentation of challenging organs; code is publicly released.

Abstract: Semi-supervised 3D medical image segmentation reduces the need for dense voxel-level annotations by exploiting unlabeled volumes. Although existing methods such as consistency regularization, pseudo-labeling, and co-training improve prediction-level robustness, they often provide insufficient feature-space organization for anatomically complex structures, especially small organs and ambiguous boundary regions with large intra-class variations. To address this issue, we propose Variation-Conditioned Distributional Proxy Learning (VCDP), a plug-and-play training-only regularization module for semi-supervised 3D medical image segmentation. VCDP represents each class with a learnable Gaussian distribution for shared class semantics and multiple variation prototypes for fine-grained intra-class patterns. A unified variation-conditioned compatibility score is further formulated to fuse distributional similarity and soft variation aggregation, guiding voxel embeddings to align with both global organ identity and local anatomical variations. VCDP is attached to decoder features during training and removed during inference, introducing no additional inference cost. Experiments on multi-organ segmentation benchmarks show that VCDP improves most evaluated baselines, particularly for small, ambiguous, and highly variable organs. Our anonymous code is released at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/VCDP_code-41ED.

</details>


### [70] [Two-Stage Multi-Modal Fusion with Adaptive Alignment for Action Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07438)
*Kanglei Zhou,Ruizhi Cai,Xinning Wang,Yijian Zheng,Liyuan Wang,Jianguo Li,Xiaohui Liang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DualAlign is a two-stage, adaptive multi-modal fusion framework for Action Quality Assessment that stabilizes visual representations across RGB, optical flow, and skeleton, then integrates textual semantics; it is evaluated on MM--JDM and standard benchmarks, showing state-of-the-art gains and robustness to missing modalities and label scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unimodal AQA misses subtle movement cues; while multi-modal data helps, heterogeneity causes cross-modal misalignment and unstable fusion, and collecting diverse, well-annotated data is costly, limiting dataset diversity.

Method: Stage 1: build a coherent visual representation by maximizing shared structure across RGB, optical flow, and skeleton. Stage 2: fuse textual semantics after stabilizing the visual manifold, so high-level descriptions augment rather than distort visuals. Also introduce MM--JDM dataset with RGB, flow, skeleton, and structured text, designed to exhibit modality noise, class imbalance, and label scarcity.

Result: DualAlign improves average correlation on MM--JDM by 21.16% over SOTA; gains of 3.53% (RG) and 5.95% (Fis-V); robust to missing modalities and scarce labels.

Conclusion: Adaptive, two-stage fusion with controlled textual integration yields stronger, more robust AQA performance and better generalization across diverse multi-modal conditions, addressing cross-modal misalignment and data scarcity challenges.

Abstract: Action Quality Assessment (AQA) aims to evaluate how well a person performs a movement, which is essential in applications such as sports scoring, skill assessment, and healthcare. However, unimodal approaches often struggle to capture subtle cues of movement quality in real-world settings. Although multi-modal inputs provide complementary information, existing methods still face two major challenges: heterogeneous modalities often lead to cross-modal misalignment and unstable fusion, and reliable multi-modal annotation is costly, resulting in limited dataset diversity. To address these challenges, we propose DualAlign, a two-stage multi-modal fusion framework with adaptive alignment. The framework first constructs a coherent visual representation by maximizing shared structural information across RGB video, optical flow, and skeleton modalities. Textual semantics are then incorporated after visual stabilization, allowing high-level descriptions to complement rather than distort the underlying visual manifold. To evaluate the framework under realistic multi-modal conditions, we introduce MM--JDM, a movement-quality assessment dataset integrating RGB videos, optical flow, skeleton sequences, and structured text. MM--JDM naturally exhibits modality noise, class imbalance, and label scarcity, making it a challenging benchmark for studying multi-modal fusion and alignment. Extensive experiments show that DualAlign improves average correlation on MM--JDM by 21.16% over the state-of-the-art methods and achieves gains of 3.53% and 5.95% on the RG and Fis-V benchmarks, respectively. DualAlign also remains robust under missing-modality and label-scarce conditions.

</details>


### [71] [A Theory of Contrastive Learning with Natural Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07470)
*Antonio Torralba,Yair Weiss*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Optimal representation under contrastive loss for stationary data can be attained by a CNN with sinusoidal first-layer filters, followed by a nonlinearity, global average pooling, and partial whitening; the sinusoid frequencies and weights are determined by a waterfilling solution on the data power spectrum; SGD-trained networks empirically learn these sinusoids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why simple augmentations in contrastive learning yield useful representations and derive the form of the optimal representation under the contrastive objective, connecting to inductive bias of first-layer filters and whitening.

Method: Analytical derivation of the optimal representation for a range of basic augmentations and any image dataset with stationary statistics. Proves that the optimum can be realized by a CNN with sinusoidal first-layer filters, followed by a nonlinearity, global average pooling, and a partial whitening layer; extends to more complex augmentations where the optimal weights remain sinusoids. Introduces a waterfilling algorithm to compute sinusoid frequencies and weights from the dataset power spectrum. Empirical validation on multiple datasets and augmentations with SGD.

Result: The optimal first-layer filters are sinusoids; for certain augmentations the optimum is achieved by sinusoids, and for more complex augmentations the weights remain sinusoidal. The sinusoid frequencies and weights are obtained via a simple waterfilling procedure using the data’s power spectrum. Empirical experiments show SGD-trained networks learn sinusoidal first-layer filters and perform partial whitening.

Conclusion: Provides a principled explanation for why simple augmentations and contrastive learning work, showing sinusoids and partial whitening emerge as optimal inductive biases under the considered setting. Offers practical guidance such as initialization or architectural bias toward sinusoidal filters and whitening when training with contrastive objectives.

Abstract: Why does contrastive learning with simple images and augmentations yield useful representations for downstream tasks? We address this question by analytically computing the optimal representation in terms of a contrastive loss for a range of basic augmentations and any image dataset with stationary statistics. We show that for certain augmentations the optimum can be attained by a CNN whose first layer filters are sinusoids, followed by a pointwise nonlinearity, global average pooling, and a final linear layer that performs partial whitening. We also show that the optimal weights in such CNNs for more complicated augmentations are still sinusoids. The frequencies of the sinusoids and their weights can be computed using a simple waterfilling algorithm given the dataset's expected power spectrum. Experiments with different image datasets and augmentations show that such CNNs trained with SGD empirically learn sinusoids in their first layer and to perform partial whitening

</details>


### [72] [Discovering Geometric Biases in 3D Face Reconstruction: A Curvature-Aware Spectral Framework for Fairness Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07486)
*Veronika Shilova,Emmanuel Malherbe,Giovanni Palma,Panagiotis-Alexandros Bokaris,Laurent Risser,Jean-Michel Loubes*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces curvature-based evaluation for 3DMM reconstructions using Laplace-Beltrami Operator (LBO) to produce high-resolution curvature error maps; reveals age-related biases and preliminary gender/ethnicity biases; advocates curvature-aware metrics for fairness and accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Morphable Models inherit biases from limited training data, which can impair generalization across diverse populations. Standard Euclidean error metrics miss subtle geometric discrepancies that matter for perceived quality. A curvature-aware evaluation can better reflect surface-level differences and potential demographic biases.

Method: Compute high-resolution curvature error maps via the Laplace-Beltrami Operator between ground-truth and reconstructed meshes. Derive a curvature-based error metric and validate it through a user study. Evaluate across multiple 3DMM bases and fitting algorithms to expose biases.

Result: Curvature error maps provide localized, geometrically meaningful discrepancies. The curvature-based metric correlates more strongly with human perception than traditional Euclidean distances. Experiments reveal systematic age-related biases and preliminary evidence of gender and ethnicity biases in 3DMM reconstructions.

Conclusion: Adopt curvature-aware evaluation protocols to ensure demographic fairness and geometric precision in 3D face reconstruction research; curvature-based metrics can reveal biases not captured by conventional measures.

Abstract: 3D Morphable Models (3DMMs) remain the standard parametric shape priors for many state-of-the-art 3D face reconstruction algorithms. However, as these models are derived from a finite number of 3D face samples, they inherit the morphological biases of their training data, potentially limiting their generalizability across diverse global populations. In this paper, we propose a novel framework to analyze 3DMM reconstructions through the lens of surface curvature, with the objective to discover, quantify and visualize biases. While standard evaluation metrics often rely on Euclidean distances, our reconstruction error captures subtle surface nuances such as local topology or undulations. To do so, we leverage the Laplace-Beltrami Operator (LBO) to generate high-resolution curvature error maps, providing a localized and geometrically meaningful visualization of discrepancies between ground truth faces and reconstructed meshes. We derive from it an error metric that we validated through a user study, observing a significantly higher correlation to human perception compared to traditional methods. Furthermore, we conduct extensive experiments across several 3DMM bases and fitting algorithms, uncovering systematic age-related biases and providing preliminary evidence of biases associated with gender and ethnicity. Our findings highlight the necessity of adopting curvature-aware evaluation protocols to ensure demographic fairness and geometric precision in future 3D face reconstruction research.

</details>


### [73] [HIVE: Understanding Post-Hallucination Reasoning in Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07507)
*Feng He,Zhenting Wang,Qifan Wang,Qiang Guan,Dongfang Liu,Ruixiang Tang,Qiankun Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PHR is a previously unexplored stage where hallucinated semantics affect downstream inference in VLMs; the authors propose HIVE to systematically study this across tasks/models and find modality-dependent effects where hallucinations can improve vision-language task performance but have less predictable effects on text-only tasks; their results suggest hallucinated cues expand semantic coverage and alter reasoning dynamics while keeping inference stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap in understanding how hallucinated information in VLMs influences subsequent reasoning, not just generation, by studying the post-hallucination reasoning stage.
