Method: Integrates motion primitives to construct candidate serves, applies Model Predictive Control for flight/contact dynamics and constraint satisfaction, and uses Bayesian Optimization to tune parameters and primitives for desired spin/speed and stability.

Result: Achieves spins up to 550 rad/s and speeds up to 6.7 m/s, generating a wide and controllable variation, matching or surpassing elite players; serves comply with official rules.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a viable pipeline for robot serve generation with rich spin-speed controllability; enables broader exploration of service strategies; future work includes real-world robot trials, robustness to perturbations, and extension to multi-objective optimization under uncertainty.

Abstract: Table tennis, a dynamic, compact, and popular sport, has received significant attention as a robotics benchmark over the last decades. Most of the research has focused on the rally aspect - returning an incoming ball - requiring high-speed vision, agile motion planning, and tight closed-loop control. However, the other component of table tennis gameplay - the serve - is comparatively a quite unexplored research problem, that in fact requires pushing physics modeling and control to the extremes. Achieving competitive serves with a robot presents domain-specific challenges, such as high-spin generation from a spinless ball, precise aiming, or multi-objective optimization. In this work, we present a novel approach for generating official rule-compliant serves by combining motion primitives, Model Predictive Control, and Bayesian Optimization. Serves generated in this way offer a wide and controllable variation of spins of up to 550 rad/s, and speeds of up to 6.7 m/s, matching and even surpassing those of elite table tennis players.

</details>


### [99] [A Closed-Loop Multi-Agent Framework for Robust Multi-Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06990)
*Yi-Xiang He,Lan Wei,Haoming Cen,Jian-Jian Jiang,Zhuohao Li,Guanxing Lu,Yihan Yang,Dandan Zhang,Wei-Shi Zheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical, closed-loop LLM-based framework for robust multi-robot manipulation that uses three specialized agents—Planning, Manipulation, and Verification—to ground high-level reasoning into real-world actions and feedback. Real-world experiments show improved success, robustness, and generalizability from single to cross-workspace tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based approaches either rely on single-robot systems with rich manipulation but poor coordination, or multi-robot frameworks that treat manipulation as an idealized primitive and ignore real-world execution uncertainties. There is a need to ground high-level reasoning in physical multi-robot execution with continuous feedback.

Method: Introduce a three-agent architecture: Planning Agent decomposes instructions into sub-tasks for allocation; Manipulation Agent (per robot) executes actions using adaptive tool use; Verification Agent monitors physical outcomes and provides semantic corrections back to the Planning Agent, forming a closed loop. The framework operates across varying workspace configurations and is validated through extensive real-world experiments.

Result: The approach yields superior success rates and robust adaptability across tasks, from single to cross-workspace manipulation, demonstrating generalizability to diverse manipulation tasks in real-world settings.

Conclusion: A closed-loop, hierarchical agentic LLM framework effectively grounds high-level planning into robust multi-robot manipulation. The architecture accommodates uncertainties and flexible workspaces, suggesting broad applicability and avenues for scaling to more robots and tasks.

Abstract: Multi-robot systems provide the parallelism and redundancy necessary for long-horizon tasks, while Large Language Models (LLMs) offer the reasoning capabilities to decompose these objectives into actionable plans. However, effectively grounding this high-level reasoning in physical multi-robot execution remains an open challenge. Existing LLM-based approaches fall mainly into two categories: Single-robot methods achieve robust contact-rich manipulation but lack the coordination mechanisms required for tasks spanning multiple workspaces. Current multi-robot frameworks focus on high-level planning, often treating manipulation as an idealized primitive that fails to account for real-world execution uncertainties. To address this, we propose a hierarchical closed-loop agentic LLM-based framework to ensure robust multi-robot manipulation. Our system consists of three specialized agents: the Planning Agent decomposes instructions into allocated sub-tasks, the Manipulation Agent for each robot executes actions via adaptive tool use, and the Verification Agent closes the loop by monitoring physical outcomes and feeding back semantic corrections. Extensive real-world experiments demonstrate that our framework achieves superior success rates, ensures robust adaptability ranging from single to cross workspace manipulation, and offers a generalizable approach for diverse manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [100] [PriGo: Test-Time Primitive Guidance to Diffusion and Flow Policies for Adaptive Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07076)
*Zezeng Li,Enda Xiang,Thuy Tran,Di Huang,Momath Thiam,Liming Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PriGo is a test-time, non-retraining framework for robust robotic manipulation that uses a primitive-guided guidance mechanism. It adds PANet to predict primitive distributions from observations and refines diffusion/flow policies during inference to improve robustness and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning policies for robotics often generalize poorly because they imitate superficial action correlations rather than underlying intent. A hierarchical primitive structure and test-time adaptation could bridge this gap.

Method: Introduce PANet to predict primitive distributions from observations; implement a differentiable primitive guidance mechanism to refine actions during inference; PriGo operates entirely at test time and integrates with pretrained diffusion/flow policies without retraining.

Result: Extensive experiments on LIBERO, CALVIN, SIMPLER, and real-world tasks show PriGo consistently improves robustness, long-horizon execution, and generalization for both diffusion- and flow-based policies.

Conclusion: PriGo provides a practical, non-retraining approach to robust robotic manipulation by leveraging primitive-guided guidance at test time to steer policies toward semantically consistent behaviors.

Abstract: Imitation learning has enabled remarkable progress in robotic manipulation, especially with diffusion and flow-based policies that generate complex visuomotor behaviors directly from demonstrations. Yet, despite their strong performance, these policies often fail to generalize across tasks and environments. A key reason is that existing policies tend to imitate superficial action correlations rather than the underlying intent. Inspired by the compositional structure of human behaviors, we propose PriGo, a primitive-guided test-time adaptive framework for robust robotic manipulation. PriGo introduces PANet, a lightweight primitive prediction module that infers primitive distributions directly from observations. We further propose a differentiable primitive guidance mechanism that refines generated actions during inference, steering trajectories toward semantically consistent behaviors. Unlike prior primitive-conditioned approaches, PriGo operates entirely at test time and can be seamlessly integrated into pretrained diffusion and flow policies without retraining. Extensive experiments on LIBERO, CALVIN, SIMPLER, and real-world robotic tasks demonstrate that PriGo consistently improves robustness, long-horizon execution, and generalization ability across both diffusion and flow-based policies.

</details>


### [101] [GeoProp: Grounding Robot State in Vision for Generalist Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07101)
*Guoyang Zhao,Quanhao Qian,Gongjie Zhang,Wenhao Li,Jiuniu Wang,Xiaowei Lu,Deli Zhao,Ran Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GeoProp is a lightweight adapter that geometrically grounds proprioception to vision, enabling explicit 3D-2D grounding and look-ahead visual context to improve embodied policy performance with minimal parameter overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Proprioception is often fused as an isolated vector without explicit alignment to visual feature maps, causing grounding gaps and degraded manipulation performance. Explicit 3D–2D correspondence is needed to better ground the robot state in the scene.

Method: Project the robot state onto the image plane and sample localized visual features to form a grounded state token. Use FiLM modulation to inject state-derived spatial priors into corresponding visual features. Additionally, sample features at a short-horizon predicted coordinate from recent kinematics to provide look-ahead visual context. Integrate this grounding into diffusion and other embodied policies with minimal overhead.

Result: Across 67 tasks, GeoProp improves Diffusion Policy by 8.7% on 63 simulation tasks and pi_0 by 4.0% on the RoboTwin subset, plus a 10.6% average gain across both policy families in the real world, with only 2–3% additional parameters.

Conclusion: GeoProp provides a simple yet high-impact inductive bias that improves grounding between proprioception and vision, leading to substantial performance gains across simulation and real-world tasks with small parameter overhead.

Abstract: Proprioception is fundamental to robotic manipulation, yet standard fusion methods often treat it as an isolated vector lacking explicit alignment with visual tokens. Without a direct correspondence between 3D kinematics and 2D feature maps, manipulation policies struggle to ground the robot's state within the scene, frequently underperforming even vision-only baselines. To address this, we introduce GeoProp, a lightweight, plug-and-play adapter that aligns proprioception with vision through explicit geometric grounding and spatial feature sampling. GeoProp projects the robot state onto the image plane to sample localized visual features, constructing a grounded state token. It then injects state-derived spatial priors into the corresponding visual features via FiLM modulation. To capture motion intent, GeoProp further samples features at a short-horizon predicted coordinate derived from recent kinematics, providing look-ahead visual context. Across 67 tasks, GeoProp improves Diffusion Policy by 8.7% on 63 simulation tasks and pi_0 by 4.0% on the RoboTwin subset, and yields a 10.6% average gain across both policy families in the real world, while adding only 2-3% to the parameter count. These results demonstrate that GeoProp is a simple yet high-impact inductive bias for generalist embodied policies. Project page: https://alibaba-damo-academy.github.io/GeoProp/.

</details>


### [102] [Compositional Motion Generation from Demonstration with Object-Centric Neural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07129)
*Ahmet Ercan Tekden,Yasemin Bekiroglu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A compositional, object-centered generative framework links perception and motion via a spatial-temporal MoE and 3D scene representations, enabling data-efficient learning and robust generalization for long-horizon robotics tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot learning from perception-to-action faces data inefficiency and limited generalization when mapping visual input to motor trajectories; a principled, scalable, and interpretable compositional framework is needed that grounds motion in object-level scene structure.

Method: Object-centric neural scene representations that fuse canonical neural fields with latent-conditioned deformations to render scenes; a temporal mixture-of-experts (MoE) with a gating mechanism combines object-conditioned movement primitives over time to produce complete trajectories; also demonstrates language-based segmentation for category-level generalization and direct operation on 3D scenes.

Result: In simulation, long-horizon manipulation tasks are completed with substantially less training data than image-based baselines; real-world experiments show robustness to noise, enable category-level generalization via language-based segmentation models, and operate directly on 3D scene representations.

Conclusion: The approach validates spatial-temporal compositionality grounded in visual structure as an effective, data-efficient pathway for scalable robot learning, offering strong generalization across diverse scenes and enabling 3D-aware execution.

Abstract: Compositionality, by organizing complex behavior as combinations of simpler elements, enables robot learning that is scalable and data efficient. Leveraging this principle, we propose a generative learning-from-demonstration framework that enables compositional modeling of robotic behavior by connecting perception and motion through shared object-level representations. We render scenes from object-centric neural representations that integrate canonical neural fields with latent-conditioned deformations, capturing positional and geometric variations in a smooth, consistent, and interpretable way. For motion generation, a temporal mixture-of-experts (MoE) employs a gating mechanism to combine object-conditioned movement primitives over time, producing complete trajectories. This spatial-temporal compositionality maintains the data efficiency of movement primitives while grounding motion in visual structure, enabling systematic generalization across diverse scene configurations. In simulation, long-horizon manipulation tasks are successfully completed using the proposed model, which requires significantly less training data than other image-based baselines. Real-world experiments further demonstrate the method's robustness to noise, its ability to generalize at the category level through language-based segmentation models, and its capacity to operate directly on 3D scene representations.

</details>


### [103] [Learning Spatiotemporal Tubes for Full Class of Signal Temporal Logic Tasks for Control of Unknown Systems under Input Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07136)
*Ahan Basu,Ratnangshu Das,Soumyodipta Nath,Siyuan Liu,Pushpak Jagtap*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: STT-based control framework for unknown nonlinear EL systems under input constraints to satisfy STL tasks. Uses PINN Parameterized time-varying balls as tubs, with robustness losses guiding training; multi-agent global robustness for collision avoidance; closed-form control to keep trajectory in tube within constraints; validated by case studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between formal temporal logic specifications (STL) and control of general unknown nonlinear EL systems with input limits, enabling safety- and task-driven execution.

Method: Define Spatiotemporal Tubes (STTs) as time-varying balls whose center/radius are modeled by a physics-informed neural network (PINN). Embed STL robustness metrics as training loss; for multi-agent setups, add a global robustness term to prevent inter-tube collisions. Develop a closed-form control law that keeps the trajectory inside the STT and bounds tube evolution consistent with input constraints.

Result: Validated on several case studies showing that STT-PINN tubes can enforce STL specifications and prevent collisions while respecting input constraints; demonstrates feasibility for both single- and multi-agent EL systems.

Conclusion: The STT-PINN framework provides a data-driven yet formally guided method to achieve STL-compliant control under unknown dynamics and input constraints, with potential for extending to more complex multi-agent scenarios and richer STL requirements.

Abstract: This paper presents a Spatiotemporal Tube (STT)-based control framework for general unknown nonlinear Euler-Lagrange (EL) systems subject to input constraints, with the objective of satisfying Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specifications, where confinement of the system trajectory within the STT guarantees the satisfaction of the corresponding STL task. For both single and multi-agent scenarios, the STT corresponding to each agent is modeled as a time-varying ball, whose center and radius are jointly parameterized using a physics-informed neural network (PINN). The robustness metric associated with the STL specification corresponding to the agents is incorporated into the training process as a loss function, enabling the learned tube to encode task-level temporal requirements. For a multi-agent scenario, we introduce an additional robustness metric corresponding to the global task, which, when satisfied, ensures the tubes do not collide with each other. To ensure that the system trajectory remains within the learned STT and thereby satisfies the local and global STL specifications, we propose a control strategy that explicitly accounts for input constraints. In particular, a closed-form control law is developed to keep the trajectory inside the tube while regulating the motion of the tube by enforcing bounds on its evolution depending on the input constraints of the system. The proposed approach has been validated over several case studies.

</details>


### [104] [Disturbance-aware Motion Planning for Over-actuated Underwater Vehicles Exploiting Actuation Redundancy for High-fidelity 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07139)
*Yuer Gao,Tongqing Xu,Qingyang Liu,Yi Cai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An eight-thruster ROV uses null-space thrust allocation to minimize disturbance in the sensing region while achieving the same motion, via a control-oriented thruster-wake proxy and real-time redundancy resolution; validated with PIV and 440 trials, yielding substantial improvements in sensing quality and reconstruction accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional vehicle-centric control ignores how actuation perturbs the sensing environment; in delicate sensing tasks underwater, thruster turbulence degrades image and perception quality. Exploit redundancy in over-actuated platforms to decouple actuation and perception.

Method: Formulate thrust allocation in the null space of the task to minimize predicted disturbance in a target region using a thruster-wake proxy based on actuator-disk theory with directional attenuation; implement a real-time redundancy-resolving allocator at 10 Hz (45 ms per solve) for an eight-thruster ROV; validate wake model with particle image velocimetry (PIV).

Result: Wake model shows R^2 ≈ 0.99 along wake axis and >0.82 in primary wake; across 440 trials, target-region particle velocity reduced by 67% (p<0.001); 3D reconstruction RMSE improved by 55% (1.9±0.4 mm vs 4.3±1.8 mm); reconstruction success rate 98.5%; method supports autonomous scanning and operator-assisted inspection.

Conclusion: Redundancy-aware control that accounts for actuation-to-perception coupling improves sensing quality in delicate underwater tasks and is feasible in real time; applicable to autonomous sensing missions and operator-assisted inspection, with quantitative gains in image-based reconstruction.

Abstract: Underwater robots often operate near delicate targets where high-power thrusters resuspend sediments and induce turbulence, degrading image quality at the sensor input. Conventional controllers optimize vehicle-centric objectives, such as tracking and stability, without accounting for the impact of actuation on sensing. We address this actuation-to-perception coupling by exploiting redundancy in over-actuated platforms. For an eight-thruster ROV, multiple thrust allocations can yield the same motion; we search this null space to minimize predicted disturbance in a task-relevant target region while enforcing motion constraints. Our method uses a control-oriented thruster-wake proxy derived from actuator-disk theory with directional attenuation and validated by PIV ($R^2 = 0.99$ near the wake axis; $R^2 > 0.82$ in the primary wake region), together with a real-time redundancy-resolving allocator running at 10 Hz (45 ms/solve). Across 440 trials, the approach reduces target-region particle velocity by 67% ($p < 0.001$), improves 3D reconstruction RMSE by 55% versus a disturbance-unaware baseline ($1.9 \pm 0.4$ mm vs. $4.3 \pm 1.8$ mm), and achieves a 98.5% reconstruction success rate. The framework supports autonomous scanning, which is quantitatively evaluated, and operator-assisted inspection, which is demonstrated in the supplementary materials.

</details>


### [105] [Validate the Dream Before You Trust Its Verdict: Admissibility for World-Model Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07196)
*Christian Oefinger,Finn Rasmus Schäfer,Korbinian Moller,Mattia Piccinini,Johannes Betz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An accreditation framework (admissibility ladder L0-L4) for world models (WMs) used as test oracles in robotics, arguing that fidelity metrics (e.g., Fréchet Video Distance) do not guarantee action-following reliability; applied to autonomous driving with two WMs, shows higher visual fidelity does not imply better closed-loop action robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To ensure WM-based verdicts about policy safety are trustworthy, WMs must be accredited; current fidelity-focused metrics ignore policy interaction; inspired by VV&A and SOTIF practices.

Method: Propose an admissibility ladder L0-L4 for WMs, embodiment-agnostic; instantiate in autonomous driving; evaluate two driving WMs, compare ranking across ladders vs visual fidelity.

Result: Found reversal: higher visual fidelity (L0) correlates with worse action-following (L1-L2); thus, visual realism does not predict action robustness of closed-loop verdicts.

Conclusion: WM verdicts used as assurance evidence require accreditation; visual fidelity alone is insufficient; adopt a safety-inspired evaluation framework to validate WMs; ladder reveals misalignment between perceptual quality and behavioral fidelity.

Abstract: Across robotics, World Models (WMs) are increasingly used to evaluate action policies by simulating the consequences of actions in an imagined world, and returning a success or safety verdict. Yet a verdict is only as trustworthy as the WM that produced it, and the WM itself needs to be certified. In video-generation WMs, fidelity metrics such as Fréchet Video Distance (FVD) reward visual realism, but ignore whether the world responds correctly to the policy's actions, including those unseen in training. Classical simulation-based validation assumes a trusted simulator evaluating an untrusted policy, whereas generative WMs are themselves unverified learned artifacts. Hence, we argue that any WM used as a test oracle must first be accredited before its verdicts can serve as evidence. Building on credibility practices from safety-critical simulation, including Verification, Validation & Accreditation (VV&A), Safety of the Intended Functionality (SOTIF), and scenario-based testing standards, we define an admissibility ladder (L0-L4) that a WM must climb before its closed-loop verdicts are accepted as assurance evidence. Our framework is embodiment-agnostic, and is instantiated in autonomous driving (AD), where assurance methods for traditional simulation are most mature. Applied to two driving WMs, the lower rungs reveal a reversal: the model that ranks higher on visual generation quality (L0) ranks lower on action-following (L1-L2), so visual fidelity does not predict the action-robustness a closed-loop verdict depends on.

</details>


### [106] [Manual, Joystick, or Haptic Control? An In Vitro Comparison of Navigation Strategies for Robotic Interventional Neuroradiology Procedures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07253)
*Benjamin Jackson,Nikola Fischer,Harry Robershaw,Xingyu Chen,S. H. Hadi Sadati,Yang Li,Jeremy Lynch,Nasr Abdelsalam,Jonathon Buwanabala,Matthew Benger,Sara Sciacca,Naga Kandasamy,Marco Mancuso-Marcello,Parthiban Balasundaram,Sahan Guruge,Neelan Das,Alejandro Granados,Kawal Rhode,Thomas C Booth*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: In vitro evaluation of robotic interfaces for interventional neuroradiology shows device-mimicking controllers outperform joystick; manual control is fastest; haptic feedback shows potential but not statistically significant; safety within 0.70 N threshold; expertise improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess safety and usability of robotic interfaces for neuroradiology with force-sensing feedback to quantify vessel-wall interactions and ensure safe operation.

Method: Custom endovascular robot and device-mimicking controllers were developed alongside a sensorized neurovascular phantom. Ten interventional neuroradiologists (4 novices, 6 experts) navigated using four modalities: device-mimicking controller with haptics, device-mimicking controller without haptics, joystick input, and manual navigation. Outcomes: navigation time, peak vessel-wall forces, incorrect catheterisations, prolapse events; plus qualitative user analyses.

Result: Manual navigation was fastest (mean 47.7 s) vs haptic-on 248.7 s, haptic-off 314.7 s, joystick 392.6 s (p<0.001). Vessel-wall forces remained below 0.70 N for all modalities; thus all deemed safe. Joystick yielded more prolapse events than manual (1.56 vs 0.13; p=0.018). Experts made fewer incorrect catheterisations (0.25 vs 0.62; p=0.035) and applied less vessel-wall force (p<0.0005); effects persisted across controllers and were amplified with haptics. Haptic conditions were rated as intuitive as each other, and more intuitive than joystick (p=0.033).

Conclusion: Device-mimicking robotic controllers outperform joystick on most metrics; haptic feedback shows promising but non-significant performance benefits in this in vitro study.

Abstract: Objective: To evaluate robotic controller interfaces for interventional neuroradiology procedures in-vitro incorporating a force-sensing platform to assess safety. Methods: A custom endovascular robot, device-mimicking controller, and sensorized neurovascular phantom were developed. Ten interventional neuroradiologists (4 novices, 6 experts) performed simulated navigations using four control modalities: device-mimicking controllers with and without haptic feedback, joystick-based input, and manual navigation. Navigation time, peak vessel-wall forces, incorrect catheterisations, and prolapse events were assessed, alongside user analyses. Results: Manual navigation was fastest (mean 47.7 s) compared to haptic-on (248.7 s), haptic-off (314.7 s), and joystick (392.6 s) modalities (p<0.001). Regardless of controller type, vessel-wall forces were below the 0.70 N puncture threshold; therefore all modalities were considered safe. Joystick produced significantly more prolapse events than manual control (1.56 vs 0.13; p=0.018). Operator experience was relevant to performance: experts made fewer incorrect catheterisations than novices (0.25 vs 0.62; p=0.035) and applied less vessel-wall force (p<0.0005); these effects were sustained across controllers but accentuated when haptics were on. Users perceived haptic on and haptic off as similarly intuitive, and more intuitive than joystick (p=0.033). Conclusion: Device-mimicking robotic controllers outperform joystick interfaces on most metrics; haptic feedback shows promising but non-significant performance benefits.

</details>


### [107] [Programmable Synchronization Graphs for Adaptive and Fault-Tolerant Modular Miniature Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07281)
*Okan Kulekcioglu,Arqam Bin Ahmad,Ines Garcia,Filipe Serra Alves,Onur Ozcan,M. Selim Hanay*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Programmable synchronization-graph framework coordinates multiple actuator-sensor modules in modular robots via graph coupling, enabling synchronization, tunable phase relationships, fault tolerance, and online learning of inter-subgraph links without a centralized leader or dense communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address coordination of many imperfect, resource-limited modules in modular robots—without leaders, fixed gait templates, or dense coupling—by introducing a scalable, robust, and programmable control layer.

Method: Model each actuator-sensor pair as a graph node. Use fixed intra-subgraph links to synchronize heterogeneous groups; impose a small number of signed inter-subgraph links to set phase relationships between groups. Explore sparse d-regular graph topologies to preserve synchronization while reducing coupling; employ an upper-confidence-bound (UCB) edge-selection algorithm to learn effective inter-subgraph links toward target phase states. Validate experimentally with up to nine modules; compare against centralized leader-follower control; assess fault tolerance by varying graph degree and module deactivations.

Result: Graph coupling yields synchronization; signed links modulate phase differences from in-phase to out-of-phase patterns. Floor experiments show gallop-like and trot-like gait patterns in five-module assemblies. Sparse topologies preserve synchronization and reduce coupling burden. Increasing graph degree improves fault tolerance to module loss. UCB-based edge selection learns inter-subgraph links steering toward target phases. In deactivation benchmarks, graph-based control avoids leader-specific failure modes and reduces worst-case phase error by ~3× compared with baseline.

Conclusion: A programmable network-topology layer serves as a compact, adaptable controller for gait phase programming, online adaptation, and robustness to unit loss in modular miniature robots, enabling scalable coordination without centralized leadership and with tolerance to module failure.

Abstract: Modular miniature robots could provide scalable function in constrained environments, but coordinating many imperfect modules remains difficult when computation, communication and reliability are limited. A central robotics challenge is to coordinate many actuator-sensor modules without assigning a privileged leader, prescribing a fixed gait template, or relying on dense communication. Here we introduce a programmable synchronization-graph framework for modular miniature robots in which each actuator-sensor pair is represented as a network node and locomotor coordination is encoded through graph coupling. Fixed intra-subgraph links synchronize heterogeneous actuator groups, whereas a small number of signed inter-subgraph links program phase relationships between groups. In physical robot collectives with up to nine modules, graph coupling drives the emergence of synchronization, signed links tune the phase difference from in-phase to out-of-phase motion, and floor experiments produce gallop-like and trot-like contact patterns in a five-module robot assembly. Replacing dense all-to-all coupling with sparse d-regular topologies preserves synchronization while reducing the coupling burden. The same graph representation also captures fault tolerance: increasing graph degree increases the number of module deactivations tolerated before desynchronization. Finally, an upper-confidence-bound edge-selection algorithm learns inter-subgraph links that drive the system toward target phase states. In a separate deactivation benchmark, the graph-based controller avoids the leader-specific failure mode observed in centralized leader-follower control and reduces worst-case phase error by about threefold. These results establish programmable network topology as a compact control layer for gait phase programming, online adaptation and robustness to unit loss in modular miniature robots.

</details>


### [108] [TouchWorld: A Predictive and Reactive Tactile Foundation Model for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07287)
*Jianyi Zhou,Feiyang Hong,Yunhao Li,Yicheng Zhao,Yongjue Cen,Zirui Liu,Jiakang Huang,Zirui Chen,Ruiyang Zhang,Weizhuo Zhu,Xuhua Song,Shuo Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TouchWorld introduces a hierarchical tactile foundation model (TouchWorld) that integrates vision-language planning with a predictive tactile world-model and fast tactile feedback to improve dexterous manipulation; six tasks show 65.0% success in clean settings and 53.7% under perturbations, outperforming the strongest baseline by 15.7 and 18.5 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision and language provide semantic and geometric guidance but struggle to reveal hidden contact states (force, slip, contact stability). Tactile sensing exposes these cues, yet prior policies treat touch as a slow, monolithic signal. There is a need to separate long-horizon planning from fast tactile feedback to better handle contact dynamics.

Method: Introduce a hierarchical policy: (i) High-Level Planning Layer that produces executable subtasks and predicts tactile subgoals; (ii) Visuo-Tactile Goal-Conditioned Policy that generates nominal action chunks; (iii) Tactile-Conditioned Refinement Policy that performs online residual corrections using recent tactile and proprioceptive feedback. TouchWorld includes a tactile world-model and uses touch as both predictive reference and fast feedback, enabling improved local contact adaptation while preserving vision-language generalization.”

Result: In six long-horizon and contact-rich dexterous manipulation tasks, TouchWorld achieves 65.0% success in the clean setting and 53.7% under human perturbations, outperforming the strongest baseline by 15.7 and 18.5 percentage points respectively.

Conclusion: TouchWorld preserves semantic generalization from vision-language-action policies while enhancing local contact adaptation via predictive tactile modeling and high-frequency tactile feedback, providing a robust framework for dexterous manipulation under varying conditions.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation in everyday environments requires both anticipation and reaction: a robot must predict how contact should evolve while rapidly correcting local errors caused by slip, misalignment, unstable grasping, or force mismatch. Vision and language provide semantic and geometric guidance, but they cannot reliably reveal hidden contact states such as force, slip, and contact stability. Although tactile sensing exposes these physical cues, most existing policies treat touch as a low-frequency observation stream within a monolithic action model, coupling slow task reasoning, action generation, and fast contact feedback in a single loop. We introduce TouchWorld, a predictive-and-reactive tactile foundation model for dexterous manipulation. TouchWorld uses a hierarchical policy that separates vision-language subtask planning, tactile world-model prediction, visuo-tactile goal-conditioned action generation, and high-frequency tactile residual refinement. A High-Level Planning Layer produces executable subtasks and predicts tactile subgoals; a Visuo-Tactile Goal-Conditioned Policy generates nominal action chunks; and a Tactile-Conditioned Refinement Policy performs online residual correction using recent tactile and proprioceptive feedback. By using touch as both a predictive contact reference and a fast feedback signal, TouchWorld preserves the semantic generalization of vision-language-action policies while improving local contact adaptation. Across six long-horizon and contact-rich dexterous manipulation tasks, TouchWorld achieves 65.0% success in the clean setting and 53.7% success under human perturbations, outperforming the strongest baseline by 15.7 and 18.5 percentage points, respectively.

</details>


### [109] [Multimodal Voice Activity Projection for Turn-Taking in Social Robots with Voice-Activity-Related Pretrained Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07294)
*Antonio Cano,Guillermo Pérez,Luis Merino,Randy Gomez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A multimodal extension of Voice Activity Projection (MM-VAP) for turn-taking in mediator human-robot interaction, combining audio-visual backbones with Low-Rank Adaptation, independent speaker encoders, inter-speaker attention, and a semantic consistency loss to predict future voice activity, achieving improvements on NoXi/NoXi+J and Haru EDR datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for accurate turn-taking prediction in mediation-oriented social robots. Audio-only VAP lacks multimodal cues; leveraging audio-visual information with a self-supervised future projection objective can better anticipate conversational dynamics.

Method: Extend audio-visual pretrained backbones via Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); independently encode speakers; apply inter-speaker attention to model relational dynamics for future voice activity projection; preserve self-supervised future-projection objective; introduce a semantic consistency loss to regularize a 256-state output space aligned with higher-level dialogue activity patterns.

Result: Empirical evaluation on NoXi and NoXi+J shows improvements over baselines for certain turn-taking events; additional evaluation on the Haru EDR corpus corroborates the approach's suitability for mediation-oriented HRI.

Conclusion: MM-VAP successfully extends VAP to multimodal inputs using efficient adapter-based fine-tuning, achieving better anticipation of turn-taking in mediator scenarios and indicating promise for social robot collaboration; further work could explore broader datasets and modalities.

Abstract: Turn-taking prediction is a key requirement for social robots involved in human-human interaction, particularly in mediator settings, where the robot must anticipate conversational dynamics rather than merely react to pauses. This work presents a Multimodal Voice Activity Projection (MM-VAP) framework that extends the original audio-only VAP formulation to synchronized audio-visual inputs while preserving its self-supervised future-projection objective. The proposed approach builds on pretrained audio-visual backbones originally optimized for speech-related tasks and adapts them through Low-Rank Adaptation to the multimodal turn-taking problem. After independent speaker encoding, an inter-speaker attention stage models the relational dynamics required to project future voice activity. In addition, a semantic consistency loss is introduced to regularize the 256-state output space according to higher-level dialogue activity patterns. Experiments on NoXi and NoXi+J showed improvements over the current baselines, particularly for some turn-taking events. Additional evaluation on the Haru EDR corpus further supported the suitability of this direction for mediation-oriented human-robot interaction.

</details>


### [110] [Towards Reliable Aerial Ground Vehicle Collaboration: An Integrated Planning and Autonomy Framework for Field Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07350)
*Md Safwan Mondal,Luca Russo,James D. Humann,James M. Dotterweich,Pranav Bhounsule*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DRL-based energy-constrained cooperative routing for UAV-UGV systems enabling mobile recharging, including a two-layer YAML mission API, an online Rendezvous Aware Replanner (RARP), and an autonomy stack; validated outdoors with improvements in mission time and energy-margin handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome UAV endurance limits for visiting multiple AOIs; achieve reliable field deployment via energy-aware, uncertainty-tolerant coordination between UAV and UGV.

Method: Formulate as energy-constrained cooperative routing; use a Deep Reinforcement Learning planner to jointly optimize UAV visitation order and UGV rendezvous points; provide a two-layer YAML mission API to connect planning with execution; integrate with PX4/MAVSDK on UAV and ROS 2/Nav2 on UGV; introduce RARP for online replanning under environmental uncertainty.

Result: DRL-based planner outperforms baseline heuristics in minimizing total mission time; RARP reduces energy-margin violations from 83.33% to 20%; validated in outdoor field experiments, including a search-and-rescue scenario with vision-language-model-based hazard detection.

Conclusion: An integrated planning-and-autonomy framework enables reliable, energy-aware cooperative UAV-UGV missions with dynamic adaptability, demonstrated in field deployments.

Abstract: Limited flight endurance significantly restricts the operational range of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) in long duration missions such as surveillance and inspection, where multiple spatially distributed Areas of Interest (AOIs) must be visited. These tasks require efficient routing determining the sequence of visits which directly impacts mission time, energy consumption, and overall feasibility. Pairing UAVs with unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) for mobile recharging offers a promising solution, but introduces a tightly coupled cooperative routing problem involving UAV route planning, UGV road constrained movement, energy management, and rendezvous scheduling under uncertainty. In this work, we present an integrated planning and autonomy framework for reliable field deployment. We formulate the problem as an energy constrained cooperative routing task and solve it using a Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) based planner that jointly optimizes the UAV visitation sequence and rendezvous locations with the UGV, outperforming baseline heuristics in minimizing total mission time. To bridge the gap between planning and execution, we introduce a standardized two layer YAML based mission API that captures environment states and structures lightweight, synchronized action sequences. This framework is supported by a complete autonomy stack using PX4/MAVSDK for UAV control and ROS 2/Nav2 for UGV navigation. Furthermore, we propose a lightweight Rendezvous Aware Replanner (RARP) that operates online to handle environmental uncertainties, reducing energy margin violations from 83.33% to 20.00%. The full system is validated through outdoor field experiments, demonstrating robust cooperative navigation and adaptability in dynamic tasks, including a search and rescue scenario with vision language model (VLM) based hazard detection

</details>


### [111] [HumAIN: Human-Aware Implicit Social Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07357)
*Daeun Song,Nhat Le,Jeffrey Chen,Mohammad Nazeri,Amirreza Payandeh,Rohan Chandra,Reuth Mirsky,Ross Mead,Ling Xiao,Xuesu Xiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumAIN: a transformer teacher-student distillation framework that fuses multi-modal social cues (historic images, skeletal keypoints, robot state, goal) into planning for human-aware navigation; a lightweight student learns from the teacher for real-time, socially compliant trajectory prediction, achieving ~29.8% improvement over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for human-aware robot navigation that leverages subtle human cues (e.g., gait, pose) and whole-body information in a real-time setting; bridge the gap between human behavior prediction and robot trajectory planning using efficient models for resource-constrained platforms.

Method: Train a transformer-based teacher model that ingests multi-modal inputs (history images, skeletal keypoints, robot state, target goal) to learn robust social representations for future trajectory planning. Distill this knowledge into a lightweight student model that optimizes simultaneous trajectory reconstruction and latent feature alignment with the teacher, enabling inference with minimal inputs.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows an average improvement of 29.8% in trajectory prediction metrics over state-of-the-art baselines across all metrics, demonstrating effective use of implicit, whole-body cues for human-like navigation on limited hardware.

Conclusion: A distilled, efficient architecture can bridge prediction and planning for socially aware navigation. The approach leverages implicit whole-body cues to achieve adaptive, robust, and socially compliant navigation on resource-constrained platforms.

Abstract: Effective social robot navigation requires sensitivity to human behavior, often revealed through subtle skeletal cues like gait and orientation. We present Human-Aware Implicit Social Robot Navigation (HumAIN), a novel framework that fuses implicit social cues directly into the planning loop via knowledge distillation. We first employ a transformer-based teacher model that fuses rich multi-modal inputs, including historic images, skeletal keypoints, robot state, and a robot's target goal, to learn robust, human-aware representations for the robot's future trajectory planning. To enable real-time deployment, we then distill this knowledge into a lightweight student model. By optimizing for both trajectory reconstruction and latent feature alignment with the teacher, the student learns to infer complex social dynamics from minimal inputs. Bridging the prediction-planning gap with an efficient distilled architecture, our method enables robots to reason about human behavior in a manner that is adaptive, robust, and socially compliant. We validate HumAIN through extensive experiments, where it improves trajectory prediction metrics by an average of 29.8% across all metrics compared to state-of-the-art baselines. These results highlight the benefit of using implicit, whole-body cues to achieve human-like navigation awareness on resource-constrained platforms.

</details>


### [112] [Behavior Foundations for Quadruped Robots: ABot-C0 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07370)
*Xufeng Zhao,Fuzhi Yang,Jianhui Chen,Li Gao,Zhang Meng,Jie Gao,Yao Zheng,Wenyu Liu,Menglin Yang,Minqi Gu,Yaru Zhao,Honglin Han,Shihui Su,Zixiao Tang,Liu Liu,Mu Xu,Yang Cai,Wenbin Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ABot-C0 is a generalist quadruped motion-control system that scales data and learning through a data pyramid, uses a Flow-Matching generalist policy, and employs a privileged-to-perceptive deployment framework with LiDAR memory to enable robust, real-world locomotion and interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the scarcity of animal motion data and fragile cross-embodiment retargeting for quadrupeds; develop scalable data pipelines, robust multi-policy learning, and reliable real-world deployment.

Method: Construct a data pyramid from conditional video-generation synthesis, annotated motion capture, teleoperation, and human design to produce 16,074 physically feasible motion clips; train a Flow-Matching generalist policy that exhibits a quadruped motion-tracking scaling law and zero-shot motions; implement a three-stage privileged-to-perceptive framework with temporal LiDAR memory and terrain-predictive supervision for all-terrain locomotion; deploy via a unified stack coordinating multi-policy execution, transitions, energy efficiency, and safety.

Result: Demonstrates a scalable motion-tracking capability that improves with training data size (scaling law) and zero-shot ability to track unseen motions; achieves robust all-terrain locomotion; validates in urban-terrain autonomous navigation and multimodal interaction, indicating readiness for product-level behavioral intelligence.

Conclusion: ABot-C0 constitutes a comprehensive generalist motion-control platform for quadrupeds, integrating scalable data generation, multi-policy learning, and dependable deployment to advance real-world, product-level quadruped behavior.

Abstract: In embodied intelligence systems, the motion controller serves as the critical bridge between semantic reasoning and physical execution. Humanoid control has progressed rapidly through large-scale human motion-capture data and motion-tracking paradigm. However, producing quadruped robots motion corpora with scalability and physical feasibility faces more fundamental obstacles: animal motion data is scarce, and cross-embodiment retargeting remains fragile. We present ABot-C0, a generalist motion-control system for quadruped robots that establishes three complementary behavior foundations: a scalable multi-source motion-data pipeline, robust policy learning across motion tracking, locomotion, and scene interaction, and a unified deployment stack for reliable real-world operation. Fundamentally, we construct a data pyramid through conditional video-generation synthesis, annotated motion capture, teleoperation and human design, producing 16,074 physically feasible motion clips as the data foundation for various motion learning demands. We then train a Flow-Matching generalist policy that demonstrates for the first time quadruped motion tracking scaling law that its performance improves consistently as training scales up, with zero-shot capability to track unseen motions. Then, we push a step further for robust all-terrain traversal locomotion by adopting a three-stage privileged-to-perceptive framework with temporal LiDAR memory and terrain-predictive supervision. Collectively, these components form a motion generalist that coordinates multi-policy execution, smooth behavior transitions, energy-efficient control, and safety mechanisms for real-world deployment. Extensive experiments on urban-terrain autonomous navigation and companion-style multimodal interaction demonstrate that quadruped robots move beyond single-function demos toward product-level behavioral intelligence.

</details>


### [113] [PLED-VINS: A Point-Line Event-Based Visual Inertial SLAM for Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07374)
*Seunghun Lee,Jihun Nam,Dong-Uk Seo,Hyun Myung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Dynamic environments challenge visual SLAM; PLED-VINS uses event camera data with entropy-recency score maps to assess temporal reliability of point/line features and a unified robust bundle adjustment for geometric reliability, plus adaptive weighting including motion-conditioned line reliability; demonstrates improved state estimation in dynamic sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust monocular event-based VINS in dynamic scenes by quantifying and leveraging both temporal (event-based) and geometric reliability of features.

Method: Introduce entropy-recency score maps for point and line features based on event temporal statistics; estimate geometric reliability via a unified point-line robust bundle adjustment; develop an adaptive weighting scheme that fuses temporal and geometric reliability, with motion-conditioned modeling for lines.

Result: Experimental results show that PLED-VINS achieves improved state estimation on dynamic sequences with moving objects compared to baselines.

Conclusion: By jointly modeling temporal reliability and geometric robustness, PLED-VINS provides robust monocular event-based VINS for dynamic environments through adaptive reliability weighting.

Abstract: Dynamic environments remain a fundamental challenge for visual SLAM, where unreliable observations from moving objects and rapid motion degrade state estimation accuracy. Although event cameras preserve fine-grained spatio-temporal information, most existing event-based SLAM frameworks still assume static scenes and lack approaches to estimate the reliability of features. To this end, we propose PLED-VINS, a monocular event camera-based visual-inertial SLAM framework that enables robust state estimation in dynamic environments. We propose an entropy-recency score map to characterize the temporal reliability of both point and line features based on event temporal statistics. Concurrently, geometric reliability is estimated via a unified point-line robust bundle adjustment. Building upon these, we design an adaptive weighting strategy that fuses temporal and geometric reliability, including motion-conditioned reliability modeling for line features, to suppress unreliable observations. Experimental results demonstrate that PLED-VINS improves state estimation on the evaluated dynamic sequences with moving objects.

</details>


### [114] [Communicative Efficiency of Single vs. Multi-Axis Robot Neck Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07390)
*Chapa Sirithunge,Haewon Jeong,Qinghua Guan,Fumiya Iida,Josie Hughes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An information-theoretic framework treats robot neck motion as a communication channel; two-DoF neck movements maximize communicative information (5.26 bits) before information declines at 3 DoF despite higher energy, revealing a morphological information bottleneck and guiding design of robotic expressiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify how neck morphology affects robotic expressiveness and establish quantitative design criteria, challenging the assumption that greater anatomical completeness improves communication.

Method: Experiment with a three-DoF robotic neck using 84 video stimuli spanning amplitude, acceleration, and frequency. Compute Shannon entropy of pixel-change signals and energy consumption. Conduct perceptual validation of motion interpretations. Introduce Motor Information Space to map entropy against energy and identify parameter effects (additive vs nonlinear).

Result: Communicative information peaks at two DoF; three DoF yields lower information despite higher energy. Optimal configuration achieves 5.26 bits at a competitive energy level. Perception shows multi-axis movements reduce clarity. The Motor Information Space reveals the efficiency landscape; findings support a morphological bottleneck where more axes do not guarantee greater expressiveness.

Conclusion: Morphological design for humanoid robots should optimize information-energy efficiency rather than maximize DoF; the study provides a quantitative framework to guide robotic morphology for effective nonverbal communication.

Abstract: Nonverbal communication through head and neck movement is fundamental to human social signalling, yet how robotic neck morphology translates motion into communicative information remains poorly understood. We present an information-theoretic framework characterising robot neck movement as a communication channel, quantifying information transmitted and energy expended across varied configurations. Using a robotic neck platform, we recorded 84 video stimuli spanning three rotational degrees of freedom (DoF), varying amplitude, acceleration, and frequency, measuring Shannon entropy of pixel-change signals alongside energy consumption. A perceptual study validated communicative interpretations of each motion. While humans typically engage one axis per gesture, robots are unconstrained by biological architecture, motivating tests up to 3 DoF. Yet communicative information peaks at two DoF and decreases at three despite rising energy cost, a phenomenon we term the morphological information bottleneck. Motion parameter effects were parameter-dependent, some additive, others non-linear. We introduce the Motor Information Space, a framework mapping entropy against energy to expose communicative efficiency across morphologies, in which the optimal configuration achieves 5.26 bits at competitive energy cost. Perception data further confirm multi-axis movements reduce clarity. These findings challenge the assumption that anatomical completeness improves robotic expressiveness, establishing a quantitative basis for morphological design in robots, especially humanoids.

</details>


### [115] [Initiation Safety: A Missing Dimension in Generalist-Robot Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07420)
*Zhijin Meng,Francisco Cruz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Frames initiation authorization as a distinct safety layer for generalist robots, proposing PAS (probe-authorize-speak), and evaluating it on a doorway humanoid against direct-init on traces; outlines a three-condition user study and raises governance and metric questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety discussions for generalist robots emphasize motion and dialogue, but lack a dedicated mechanism to obtain consent before initiating social actions (e.g., greetings, uninvited contact, entering someone’s space). The paper argues consent-based initiation is essential for safe interaction.

Method: Conceptual framing of initiation authorization; development of PAS (probe-authorize-speak) as a safety loop; implementation on a doorway humanoid; comparison with direct-init using logged traces; design of a three-condition user study; discussion of metrics, governance, and where initiation ends and foundation-model generation begins.

Result: Proposes a concrete safety framework (initiation authorization) and a comparative experimental setup (PAS vs direct-init); provides initial implementation on a doorway humanoid and outlines a three-condition study; no definitive empirical results are reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: Initiation authorization is a distinct safety layer necessary for ethical and safe social interaction by generalist robots; governance, metrics, and the boundary between initiation and model-generated content require explicit definition; the work highlights open questions about when initiation should occur and how it interacts with foundation-model generation.

Abstract: Safety for generalist robots is usually discussed in terms of motion or dialogue. We argue a third question is missing: should the robot take its first hard-to-undo social action at all, such as a greeting, an uninvited grasp, or stepping into someone's space? We call this initiation authorization. Current frameworks rarely treat it as a separate safety layer. Today's stacks often skip this step: a high engagement score or a confident VLA rollout is treated as permission to act. But seeing a person is not the same as having their consent to be addressed. We frame initiation authorization within generalist-robot safety and contrast it with post-plan VLA guardrails, implementing PAS (probe-authorize-speak) on a doorway humanoid, comparing it with direct-init on logged traces, and proposing a three-condition user study, with open questions on metrics, governance, and where initiation ends and foundation-model generation begins.

</details>


### [116] [Immersive Social Interaction with VR and LLM-Assisted Humanoids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07430)
*Niraj Pudasaini,Geeta Chandra Raju Bethala,Pranav Doma,Anthony Tzes,Yi Fang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An immersive, language-assisted teleoperation framework for whole-body humanoid control using Apple Vision Pro, integrating voice locomotion, VR manipulation, and social interaction; evaluated on Unitree H1 with 80% object manipulation and 70% social task success, with multimodal data collection for imitation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce physical effort and cognitive load of teleoperation in remote or hazardous environments by leveraging natural language, VR, and multimodal feedback; enable accessible, data-rich humanoid control.

Method: Combine voice-controlled locomotion (LLM-guided), VR-based manipulation, and bidirectional social interaction; map human hand motions to robot via inverse kinematics and PD control; record multimodal data (egocentric video, audio/text, joints, hands, eye gaze); evaluate on Unitree H1 with dexterous hands.

Result: Novice users reach operational proficiency after brief familiarization; 80% manipulation success; 70% success on social cube-passing; demonstrates feasibility and data-collection potential for future imitation learning and autonomy.

Conclusion: Shows that immersive, language-assisted teleoperation is a viable and accessible interface for humanoid interaction, remote assistance, and multimodal data collection, with potential for advancing autonomous capabilities through the collected dataset.

Abstract: Humanoid robots can extend human presence to remote, constrained, or hazardous environments, but existing teleoperation interfaces often require physically demanding motion tracking or cognitively demanding low-level control. This paper presents an immersive teleoperation framework that integrates voice-controlled locomotion, VR-based manipulation, and bidirectional social interaction for whole-body humanoid control. Using Apple Vision Pro, the operator receives egocentric visual feedback, issues natural-language locomotion commands, and teleoperates the robot's arms and dexterous hands through wrist and finger tracking. An LLM-assisted voice-control module converts spoken instructions into high-level locomotion commands, while the manipulation module retargets human hand motions to the robot through inverse kinematics and PD control. The system also records multimodal data, including egocentric RGB observations, voice/text commands, joint states, hand motions, and eye-gaze signals, supporting future imitation learning and autonomy. We evaluate the framework on a Unitree H1 humanoid equipped with dexterous hands in manipulation and social interaction tasks. Results show that novice users can successfully operate the system after brief familiarization, achieving 80\% success in object manipulation and 70\% success in a social cube-passing task. These results demonstrate the potential of immersive, language-assisted teleoperation as an accessible interface for humanoid interaction, remote assistance, and multimodal data collection.

</details>


### [117] [GeoGS-SLAM: Geometry-Only Gaussian Splatting for Dense Monocular SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07452)
*Lipu Zhou,Yaoyun Kang,Junxiang Pang,Shengkai Sun,Tingting Bao,Kehan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces Geometry-only Gaussian Splatting (GeoGS) and GeoGS-SLAM to perform dense 3D reconstruction and SLAM using geometry-focused representations, reducing per-primitive parameters, speeding convergence, and improving robustness to illumination, with a loop-closure map update to prevent tearing; claims state-of-the-art performance in mapping efficiency and geometry quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In dense 3D geometry-based SLAM, scene geometry is more critical than appearance; existing methods model appearance, incur complexity, and are sensitive to lighting; a geometry-centric approach could improve efficiency and robustness for robotic tasks.

Method: Propose GeoGS: a geometry-only Gaussian Splatting representation that keeps only spatial parameters, reducing per-primitive parameters by >80%. Train with single-view and multi-view geometric and photometric supervision; employ a local-plane-driven initialization to align primitives with local structures; develop a map update strategy for loop closures that globally transforms the Gaussian map to match corrected poses.

Result: Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show GeoGS-SLAM outperforms state-of-the-art methods in online mapping efficiency and geometric reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: Focusing on geometry-only representations can accelerate dense 3D reconstruction and improve robustness to illumination changes, enabling efficient and reliable dense SLAM with reduced complexity.

Abstract: Dense visual SLAM is a fundamental problem in robotics. Recent advances in 3DGS have demonstrated its potential for dense SLAM. Existing 3DGS frameworks focus on both appearance and geometry modeling. However, scene geometry is typically more critical for SLAM than novel view synthesis because downstream robotic tasks, such as navigation and obstacle avoidance, rely primarily on accurate spatial geometry rather than photorealistic rendering. This observation raises a natural question: Is it feasible for 3DGS to perform 3D reconstruction without scene appearance modeling? Motivated by this, we propose Geometry-only Gaussian Splatting (GeoGS), which directly reconstructs scene geometry, and further present GeoGS-SLAM, a dense visual SLAM system built upon this representation. Specifically, GeoGS retains only spatial parameters to reduce the number of per-primitive parameters by over 80%. In contrast to existing 3DGS methods, GeoGS focuses solely on geometric reconstruction, which significantly reduces the number of Gaussian primitives, accelerates geometric convergence, and enhances robustness to illumination variations. In addition, we present an effective training framework that optimizes the Gaussian primitives via single-view and multi-view geometric and photometric supervision, and speeds up geometry convergence with a local-plane driven initialization that better aligns primitives with local structures. Furthermore, we introduce a map update strategy for loop closure that globally transforms the Gaussian map to align it with the corrected pose estimates, thereby preventing map tearing caused by inconsistent per-viewpoint pose corrections in existing methods. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that our method outperforms SOTA methods in terms of online mapping efficiency and geometric reconstruction quality.

</details>


### [118] [EmbodiedGen V2: An Agentic, Simulation-Ready 3D World Engine for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07459)
*Xinjie Wang,Liu Liu,Taojun Ding,Andrew Choi,Chaodong Huang,Mengao Zhao,Ziang Li,Jackson Jiang,Chunlei Yu,Shengxiang Liu,Wei Xu,Zhizhong Su*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EmbodiedGen V2 provides a unified, editable sim-ready pipeline for generating policy-ready 3D environments by linking cross-simulator assets, interaction affordances, and large multi-room scenes using stateful Vibe Coding, enabling scalable embodied AI training, evaluation, and real-world transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current asset-to-environment workflows remain largely manual, hindering scalable closed-loop learning. A unified, reusable representation that connects assets, affordances, task-driven worlds, and cross-simulator deployment is needed to accelerate embodied policy development.

Method: Develop a unified sim-ready representation that interoperates across simulators, supports cross-simulator assets, interaction affordances, large-scale multi-room scenes, and stateful Vibe Coding; build a generative, editable, and reusable simulation pipeline enabling manipulation, navigation, and mobile manipulation with cross-simulator deployment; integrate with embodied policy training workflows.

Result: Asset pipeline achieves 96.5% human acceptance and 98.6% collision success; 83.3% of task-driven worlds are directly usable without modification; online RL with generated environments raises simulated success from 9.7% to 79.8%, and real-robot task success from 21.7% to 75.0%.

Conclusion: EmbodiedGen V2 establishes a scalable simulation infrastructure for training, evaluating, and deploying embodied policies across simulators, accelerating policy development and improving sim-to-real transfer.

Abstract: We present EmbodiedGen V2, a generative 3D world engine for building executable sim-ready environments for embodied intelligence. Sim-ready 3D asset generation has advanced rapidly, yet assembling such assets into policy-ready task environments remains largely manual, limiting scalable closed-loop learning. EmbodiedGen V2 addresses this gap through a unified sim-ready representation that connects cross-simulator assets, interaction affordances, task-driven worlds, large-scale multi-room scenes, and stateful Vibe Coding into a generative, editable, and reusable simulation pipeline. The generated environments support manipulation, navigation, mobile manipulation, cross-simulator deployment, and embodied policy training. In evaluation, the asset pipeline achieves 96.5% human acceptance and 98.6% collision success, and 83.3% of task-driven worlds are directly usable for downstream simulation without manual modification. Online reinforcement learning with generated environments further improves simulation success from 9.7% to 79.8%, and transfers to real robots with task success increasing from 21.7% to 75.0%. These results establish EmbodiedGen V2 as scalable simulation infrastructure for training, evaluating, and deploying embodied policies.

</details>


### [119] [Agent-Exploitation Affordances: From Basic to Complex Representation Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07475)
*Bastien Dussard,Aurélie Clodic,Guillaume Sarthou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a tractable ontological representation of cooperative affordances in social robotics to extend agents' action possibilities through inter-agent interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent robotics, social context enables agent interactions that extend capabilities, but there is a lack of formal representations for cooperative affordances.

Method: Develops an ontological representation of cooperative affordances; builds on elementary interaction patterns and demonstrates by composing them into diverse scenarios.

Result: Shows that the ontological patterns can be combined to depict a variety of cooperative action possibilities; provides a usable framework for agents to reason about joint actions.

Conclusion: Ontology enables practical reasoning about cooperative affordances, enabling agentexploitation and richer social robotics capabilities.

Abstract: In robotics, the capability of an artificial agent to represent the range of its action possibilities, i.e. affordances, is crucial to understand how it can act on its environment. While functional affordances, which refer to the use of tools and objects, have been broadly studied in knowledge representation, the implications of a social context and the presence of other agents have remained unexplored in this field. Consequently, in the field of social robotics, a multi-agent context enables the agents to engage in new actions that are potentially complementary to their individual capabilities, leading to the perspective of agentexploitation. This work focuses on the concept of cooperative affordance within the realm of social affordances. Cooperative affordances refer to situations where agents interact with each other to extend their action possibilities range. From this definition, this paper proposes a tractable ontological representation of this concept with the aim of making it usable by an artificial agent. Expanding on those elementary patterns, we illustrate the effectiveness of these representations by combining them to depict a diverse range of scenarios.

</details>


### [120] [Smooth Operator: A Real-Time Sampling-Based Algorithm for Kinematic Hand Retargeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07491)
*Robert Jomar Malate,Erik Bauer,Norica Bacuieti,Stefanos Charalambous,Elvis Nava,Robert K. Katzschmann,Benedek Forrai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes Sampling-Based Retargeter (SBR), a gradient-free, sampling-based retargeting method for low-jitter, real-time teleoperation; outperforms gradient-based baselines in simulation and a real-world 18-participant study, achieving 54.1% task success and 36.4 NASA-TLX; provides benchmarking methodology for future retargeting research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gradient-based retargeting often converges to local minima and induces jitter, degrading data quality for learning-based robotics.

Method: Design of SBR using sampling-based control techniques to achieve low-jitter real-time retargeting; evaluated in both simulation and real-world with a user study of 18 participants across 3 tasks.

Result: Highest overall task success rate (54.1%) and lowest NASA-TLX (36.4/100) compared to gradient-based baselines; demonstrates SBR's effectiveness and intuitive nature; establishes benchmarking methodology.

Conclusion: SBR is an effective, intuitive retargeter for dexterous manipulation and provides a benchmarking framework for the community.

Abstract: Advances in learning-based robotic manipulation, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and Video Action Models (VAMs), heavily rely on high-quality teleoperation data. Their capabilities are strictly upper-bounded by the quality of the underlying human demonstrations. Current gradient-based retargeting algorithms often converge to different local minima, resulting in jitter that affects data quality and teleoperation experience. To address this, we introduce the Sampling-Based Retargeter (SBR), a novel gradient-free retargeting method drawn from the rich literature of sampling-based control and explicitly designed for low-jitter, real-time kinematic retargeting. We evaluate SBR both in simulation and through a rigorous real-world user study involving 18 participants performing 3 complex manipulation tasks. Compared to gradient-based baselines, SBR achieved the highest overall task success rate (54.1%) while significantly reducing operator cognitive fatigue, recording the lowest NASA-TLX workload score (36.4 out of 100). Ultimately, we establish SBR as a highly effective, intuitive retargeter for dexterous manipulation, providing the community with a rigorous benchmarking methodology to guide future retargeting research.

</details>


### [121] [Generating Personalized Lower-Limb Kinematics Across Walking Speeds Using Subject-Conditioned Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07533)
*Diya Dinesh,Adrian Krieger,Changseob Song,Dongho Park,Aaron J. Young,Inseung Kang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Subject-conditioned residual diffusion model converts gait trajectories from a seen speed to an unseen speed, enabling personalized, speed-adaptive lower-limb kinematics from minimal data with strong cross-population generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized exoskeleton assistance requires gait data across speeds and tasks, but collecting such data is costly, time-consuming, and especially burdensome for clinical populations; gait varies substantially with speed, making cross-speed transfer challenging.

Method: A transformer-based denoiser within a diffusion framework outputs a residual to transform a seen-speed sagittal-plane hip/knee/ankle trajectory into an unseen-speed trajectory. The denoiser is conditioned on the subject's gait and the two speeds via feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM). Trained solely on able-bodied data, it predicts the residual to apply to the seen trajectory.

Result: MAE of 3.4° on held-out able-bodied subjects; 6.0° MAE on out-of-training-distribution stroke subjects without stroke-specific fine-tuning; over 70% MAE reduction compared to supervised feed-forward baselines; a single seen speed achieves accuracy comparable to four speeds within 0.4°; retains subject identity for clinical gait.

Conclusion: Subject-conditioned residual diffusion can synthesize personalized gait across speeds from minimal data, reducing the collection burden for downstream exoskeleton personalization, with demonstrated generalization to stroke without explicit fine-tuning.

Abstract: Personalizing exoskeleton assistance requires user-specific gait data across many locomotor tasks, yet collecting this data demands repeated motion capture sessions that are costly, time-intensive, and especially burdensome for clinical populations. This challenge is most acute across walking speeds, where gait changes substantially and deviates further in clinical gait. This work introduces a subject-conditioned residual diffusion framework that generates personalized lower-limb kinematics at unseen walking speeds from a subject's gait sequence at a single seen speed. Given sagittal-plane hip, knee, and ankle trajectories at a seen speed and a desired unseen speed, the model generates a residual that transforms the seen trajectory into the unseen one, using a transformer denoiser conditioned on the subject's gait and the two speeds through feature-wise linear modulation. Trained only on able-bodied data, the model achieved a mean absolute error (MAE) of 3.4° on held-out able-bodied subjects. Without any stroke-specific fine-tuning, it achieved a 6.0° MAE on out-of-training-distribution stroke subjects, retaining subject identity for clinical gait. The framework reduced the MAE by over 70% relative to supervised feed-forward baselines, and a single seen speed matched the accuracy of four speeds within 0.4°. These results demonstrate that subject-conditioned residual diffusion can synthesize personalized gait across speeds from minimal data, reducing the collection burden for downstream exoskeleton personalization.

</details>


### [122] [SonoRank: Towards Calibration-Free Real-Time Finger Flexion Detection from Forearm Ultrasound Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07542)
*Dean Zadok,Alon Wolf,Alex M. Bronstein,Oren Salzman*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SonoRank uses pairwise ranking of forearm ultrasound sequences to pretrain a calibration-free finger flexion detector; fine-tuned on rest-referenced data; yields 28% F1 gain over non-ranking baselines in leave-one-subject-out cross-validation on 12 subjects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: sEMG-limited prosthetic control; ultrasound offers real-time observation and more degrees of freedom but currently requires per-user fine-tuning; a calibration-free method is needed for practical commercialization.

Method: Train a model to rank pairs of ultrasound sequences by relative motion magnitudes for each finger; use the ranking as a pretraining signal; then fine-tune to classify active finger flexion using a rest reference captured at operation start; evaluation uses 12 subjects with leave-one-subject-out cross-validation (12-fold).

Result: 28% improvement in F1 score over direct classification baselines that skip the ranking stage; demonstrates pairwise ranking as an effective pretraining signal for subject-independent ultrasound-based prosthetic control.

Conclusion: Pairwise ranking is a promising calibration-free pretraining signal for ultrasound-based prosthetic control, bringing closer to practical deployment; further work could explore larger cohorts, real-time deployment, and integration with prosthetic hardware.

Abstract: Powered prosthetic hands are frequently abandoned, largely due to the limited functionality of current devices that rely on surface electromyography (sEMG). Sonomyography (ultrasound) has emerged as a promising alternative, owing to its ability to observe muscle activity in real time and control a greater number of degrees of freedom. Yet, existing ultrasound-based methods require per-user fine-tuning, limiting their commercialization. We propose SonoRank, an important step towards calibration-free finger flexion detection from forearm ultrasound video. SonoRank first learns to rank pairs of ultrasound sequences by their relative motion magnitude for each of the five fingers. The learned representations are then fine-tuned to classify whether each finger is actively flexing, using a rest reference that is captured at the beginning of the operation. Under 12-fold leave-one-subject-out cross-validation on a dataset of twelve subjects with synchronized kinematics, SonoRank achieves a 28% improvement in F1 score over direct classification baselines that skip the ranking stage. These results establish pairwise ranking as an effective pretraining signal for subject-independent control, bringing ultrasound-based prosthetics closer to practical, calibration-free deployment.

</details>


### [123] [Context-Aware Force Estimation for Deformable Tool Manipulation in Robotic Environmental Swabbing via Few-Shot Continual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07574)
*Siavash Mahmoudi,Chaitainya Kuppar Reddy,Yang Tian,Dongyi Wang*

Main category: cs.RO
