TL;DR: A data-driven, proprioception-based framework using an LSTM backbone and FiLM-based few-shot adaptation enables tip-level contact-force estimation for Deformable Tool Manipulation without embedded sensors, improving generalization across surfaces and maintaining low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In robotic surface sampling, deformable tool dynamics create nonlinear hysteresis that decouples wrist force measurements from true contact forces, while tool-tip sensors are often impractical due to sterility, disposability, and maintenance constraints. A model-free, sensor-lean approach is needed to reliably estimate contact forces across changing surfaces and tool compliances.

Method: Evaluate temporal models to identify a compact LSTM as the best-performing architecture in terms of estimation error and sub-millisecond latency. Address domain shift with a parameter-isolated few-shot adaptation: freeze the recurrent backbone and add low-dimensional FiLM-conditioned context embeddings to capture surface/compliance variations, enabling rapid adaptation without catastrophic forgetting. Validation on a UR5e across nine tool–surface regimes.

Result: The LSTM-based estimator achieves the lowest error with sub-millisecond inference. The FiLM-based adaptation significantly boosts robustness under domain shift, reducing zero-shot estimation error by up to 63% while preserving baseline performance and avoiding catastrophic forgetting.

Conclusion: Separating shared deformation-history dynamics from domain-specific conditioning enables reliable, probe-free force estimation for Deformable Tool Manipulation in non-stationary environments.

Abstract: Robotic surface swabbing requires sustained interaction between a compliant tool and heterogeneous environments, where accurate estimation of tip-level contact force is critical for consistent sampling performance. However, deformable tool dynamics introduce nonlinear viscoelastic hysteresis that decouples wrist-mounted force measurements from true contact forces, while tool-integrated sensors are impractical for deployment due to sterility and disposability constraints. This paper presents a data-driven framework for contact force estimation in Deformable Tool Manipulation (DTM) that leverages proprioceptive sensing without requiring explicit physical models or permanent embedded sensing hardware at the tool tip. A recurrent architecture is first identified through a comparative evaluation of temporal models, where a compact LSTM achieves the lowest estimation error and sub-millisecond inference latency. To address generalization across unseen surfaces and tool compliance conditions, we introduce a parameter-isolated few-shot adaptation strategy that augments a frozen recurrent backbone with low-dimensional context embeddings using feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM). Experiments on a UR5e platform across nine tool-surface interaction regimes demonstrate that the proposed approach significantly improves robustness under domain shift, reducing zero-shot estimation error by up to 63\% while preserving baseline performance without catastrophic forgetting. These results show that separating shared deformation-history dynamics from domain-specific conditioning enables reliable force estimation for DTM in non-stationary environments.

</details>


### [124] [CARLA-GS: Decoupling Representation, Reasoning, and Physics Simulation for Autonomous Driving Corner-Case Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07601)
*Kaicong Huang,Meng Ma,Ruimin Ke*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A modular corner-case synthesis pipeline (CARLA-GS) that decouples visualization, semantic reasoning, and physics execution to generate photorealistic, semantically controlled, physically feasible autonomous-driving corner cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety evaluation for autonomous driving is dominated by rare, safety-critical interactions. Existing approaches either focus on isolated components or lack spatiotemporal coherence; a unified framework is needed to combine realistic visuals, semantic reasoning, and physically realistic motion.

Method: From real driving data, reconstruct an editable Gaussian scene with geometry-consistent constraints. A multi-agent LLM performs scene-level reasoning to identify risky interactions and generate intent-level waypoint trajectories. Low-level motion control is handled by CARLA with a PID controller to ensure kinematic/dynamic feasibility. The simulated states are re-projected into the Gaussian scene for ego-centric rendering.

Result: Experiments on the Waymo Open Dataset show controllable corner-case generation with photorealistic, spatiotemporally consistent videos aligned with semantic intent and physically feasible motion.

Conclusion: The framework enables high-level semantic reasoning, physically executable low-level motion, and photorealistic corner-case generation within a unified, modular pipeline.

Abstract: Safety evaluation for autonomous driving is dominated by rare, safety-critical interactions, motivating simulators that can deliberately synthesize corner cases with photorealistic observations. Corner-case generation is inherently a multi-source problem spanning visual representation, scene reasoning, and vehicle trajectory generation and control. Prior knowledge- and model-based approaches typically focus on scene or trajectory components in isolation, while diffusion-based methods attempt end-to-end generation but still struggle to ensure spatiotemporal consistency and physical realism. To unify these aspects within a single framework, we propose CARLA-GS, a modular corner-case synthesis pipeline that decouples visual representation, semantic reasoning, and physics-based execution while maintaining tight cross-module coupling. Starting from real driving data, we reconstruct an editable gaussian scene with additional geometry-consistent constraints. A multi-agent LLM then performs scene-level reasoning to identify risky interactions and generate intent-level waypoint trajectories, while the low-level motion control is delegated to CARLA, where a PID controller ensures kinematic and dynamic feasibility. The simulated vehicle states are finally re-projected into the gaussian scene for ego-centric rendering. This design enables high-level semantic reasoning, low-level physically executable motion, and photorealistic corner-case generation within a unified pipeline. Experiments on the Waymo Open Dataset show, both quantitatively and qualitatively, that our framework enables controllable corner-case generation and produces photorealistic, spatiotemporally consistent videos aligned with semantic intent and physically feasible motion.

</details>


### [125] [Dual Latent Memory in Vision-Language-Action Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07608)
*Hongyu Qu,Jianzhe Gao,Xiaobin Hu,Shaohuan Yang,Xinlei Yu,Rui Yan,Wenguan Wang,Xiangbo Shu,Shuicheng Yan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LaMem-VLA introduces latent-memory-native VLA framework that interleaves historical experience with current perception by embedding memory as latent tokens, enabling long-horizon reasoning within a single latent space. It uses four components: curator, seeker, condenser, and weaver to manage and utilize short-term and long-term memory vaults.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA models predominantly rely on a Markovian assumption and treat memory as external or auxiliary to latent reasoning, which hampers deep temporal dependencies. There is a need to integrate memory seamlessly into the latent reasoning space.

Method: Four coordinated modules: (i) curator organizes history into two memory vaults (short-term and long-term); (ii) seeker retrieves context-relevant evidence from both vaults using multimodal cognition; (iii) condenser reconstructs retrieved evidence into compact latent memory tokens; (iv) weaver injects these tokens with the current observation and instruction into one continuous embedding sequence. All memory representations live in the same latent space as VLA reasoning.

Result: Experiments on SimplerEnv and LIBERO show that LaMem-VLA outperforms baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of latent-memory-native design for long-horizon VLA tasks.

Conclusion: By embedding and consuming historical experience directly in the latent space, memory becomes a first-class component of VLA reasoning, improving action generation under bounded context.

Abstract: Mainstream Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models predict actions primarily from the current observation under a Markovian assumption, thus struggling with long-horizon, temporally dependent tasks. Existing memory-augmented VLAs either expand the observation window or retrieve history from the memory bank as auxiliary policy-side context. However, they leave memory outside the native latent embedding space of VLA reasoning, preventing historical experience from being fluidly interleaved with multimodal reasoning and action formation. To this end, we introduce LaMem-VLA, a latent-memory-native framework that reconstructs historical experience into latent memory tokens and directly interweaves them with VLA reasoning. At its core, LaMem-VLA introduces four coordinated components: (i) a curator that organizes historical experience into two complementary short-term and long-term memory vaults; (ii) a seeker that queries both vaults using the multimodal cognition to retrieve context-relevant evidence; (iii) a condenser that reconstructs the retrieved evidence into compact short-term and long-term latent memory tokens; and (iv) a weaver that injects these memory tokens with the current observation and instruction into one continuous embedding sequence. By representing, retrieving, and consuming historical experience entirely in the same continuous latent space, LaMem-VLA enables memory to directly participate in VLA reasoning and guide action generation under a bounded context. Extensive experiments on SimplerEnv and LIBERO demonstrate the superiority of our LaMem-VLA.

</details>


### [126] [Continuous and large-scale: ELEANOR, the soft architected arm inspired by the elephant trunk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07622)
*Giovanna A. Naselli,Anderson B. Nardin,Seonggun Joe,Ryan Drinkwater,Enrico Donato,Diego Bianchi,Egidio Falotico,Michel C. Milinkovitch,Lucia Beccai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Biomimetic, continuous continuum arm inspired by elephant trunk using a 3D-printed, volumetrically tessellated structure with tendon-driven actuation achieves elephant-like movements and grasping without prescribing behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Push beyond modular, small-scale continuum robots by capturing the trunk's structural continuity and dynamic properties to enable adaptable, natural manipulation.

Method: Design guided by macroscopic trunk properties; 3D-printed 85 cm long compliant tapered continuum arm; volumetrically tessellated; tendon-driven actuation mimicking longitudinal and oblique muscles; demonstration of whole-body grasping.

Result: Demonstrated elephant-like movements and grasping of objects with varying shapes and sizes; comparison to the biological trunk discussed to highlight similarities/differences between biology and robotics.

Conclusion: A biomimetic design based on macroscopic trunk properties can realize natural, adaptable trunk-like manipulation, underscoring the value of structural continuity and tendon-based actuation in continuum robotics and enabling richer biology–robotics integration.

Abstract: The elephant trunk is a dexterous and versatile manipulator whose performance is still unmatched in robotics. In previous works, modularity was prioritized and relatively small-scale continuum robots were built. We take the natural proboscis of the *Loxodonta africana* species as a model and propose a different design approach which favors structural continuity and dynamic properties that plausibly emulate those of the natural trunk, while conferring high adaptability to the environment and humans. Instead of targeting prescribed behaviors, we show that a biomimetic design based on the macroscopic properties of the natural system enables elephant-like movements and grasping. We build by 3D printing an 85 cm long, compliant, tapered, volumetrically tessellated continuum arm, which is combined with tendon-driven actuation mimicking the longitudinal and oblique muscles of the natural model. We demonstrate whole-body grasping of objects having different shapes and dimensions and discuss a comparison to the biological trunk highlighting aspects of both biology and robotics.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [127] [AgentLens: Production-Assessed Trajectory Reviews for Coding Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06624)
*Andrey Podivilov,Vadim Lomshakov,Sergey Savin,Matvei Startsev,Roman Pozharskiy,Maksim Parshin,Sergey Nikolenko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentLens is a production-assessed benchmark for interactive code agents that evaluates the full agent trajectory, not just pass/fail, by pairing formal verification with LLM-written trajectory reviews and side-by-side comparisons to produce readable explanations of scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current code-agent benchmarks collapse runs to a single binary outcome, but real users experience the entire interaction trajectory (instruction following, tool use, self-verification, recovery, and user communication). A benchmark that assesses the whole trajectory with explanations is needed.

Method: Combine formal verification (objective checks) with large-language-model–written trajectory reviews and side-by-side comparisons for each run, yielding a readable explanation of the score; deployable in a nightly evaluation pipeline.

Result: Produces readable, diagnostic explanations of performance, enabling model behavior diagnosis, comparison across agent versions, and detection of regressions; released as open-source.

Conclusion: AgentLens extends benchmarking beyond simple ranking to practical diagnostics and product-quality assurance for interactive code agents; available at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: We present AgentLens, a production-assessed benchmark for interactive code agents. Most code-agent benchmarks reduce a run to a single bit -- did the task pass? -- but the people who actually use these agents experience the entire trajectory: how the agent follows instructions, uses its tools, verifies its own work, recovers from mistakes, and talks to them along the way. AgentLens evaluates that whole trajectory. It pairs formal verification, where an objective check exists, with LLM-written trajectory reviews and side-by-side comparisons, so that each run yields a readable explanation of why the score is what it is. This makes AgentLens useful for more than ranking models: we use it to diagnose model behavior, compare successive versions of our own agent, and catch product regressions in a nightly evaluation pipeline. We release the benchmark as open source at https://github.com/agent-lens/agent-lens-bench.

</details>


### [128] [When Does In-Context Search Help? A Sampling-Complexity Theory of Reflection-Driven Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06720)
*Yotam Wolf,Noam Wies,Amnon Shashua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In-context search (ICS) for LLMs can be framed as approximate inference over reasoning traces, with the base model providing a prior and self-reflection providing feedback as posterior updates. Under conditions where reflections localize early mistakes, ICS yields exponential improvements in success probability with only a polynomial number of sequential attempts; if reflections fail to localize, conditioning on past attempts offers no asymptotic benefit compared to parallel sampling. The gains are robust and learnable, with approximate posterior updates and cross-entropy training on search rollouts reproducing the desired behavior with polynomial sample complexity. In a stagewise RL view with verifiable rewards, the optimal policy implements the same posterior reweighting rule. Empirical validation on real large reasoning models confirms key qualitative predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the conditions under which in-context search improves reasoning in LLMs, quantify the sampling complexity of corrective iterations, and connect these dynamics to learnability and RL-like policy updates.

Method: Model the base LLM as defining a prior over reasoning traces and model self-reflection as posterior updates during in-context search; analyze the resulting sequential sampling process to derive when localizing reflections yield exponential gains; show that approximate posteriors and cross-entropy-trained search rollouts recover the beneficial behavior with polynomial samples; relate to stagewise RL with verifiable rewards to show the optimal policy aligns with the posterior reweighting rule.

Result: When reflections reliably localize early mistakes, ICS achieves exponential improvements over the base model, solving problems with exponentially small zero-shot pass rates using only a polynomial number of sequential attempts. If localization fails, conditioning on past attempts provides no asymptotic advantage over parallel sampling. The gains are robust and learnable: approximate posterior updates suffice, and cross-entropy training on search rollouts reproduces the desired behavior with polynomial sample complexity. Under a stagewise RL abstraction with verifiable rewards, the optimal policy implements the same posterior reweighting rule. Empirical validation on real large reasoning models supports the key qualitative predictions.

Conclusion: ICS can dramatically improve LLM reasoning efficiency under certain localization conditions, with a solid theoretical basis, learnability guarantees, and practical validation; the framework explains when and why iterative reasoning plus self-critique helps and when it does not.

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) with extended reasoning has enabled in-context search, in which models iteratively generate, critique, and revise solution attempts. We provide a theoretical analysis of in-context search by modeling it as approximate inference over reasoning traces, where the base model defines a prior and self-reflection provides feedback for posterior updates, and study the resulting inference-time sampling complexity - the number of sequential attempts needed to achieve high success probability. We show that when reflections reliably localize early mistakes, in-context search can yield exponential improvements over the base model, solving problems with exponentially small zero-shot pass rates using only a polynomial number of sequential attempts, whereas when this property fails, conditioning on past attempts offers no asymptotic benefit over parallel sampling. We further show that these gains are robust and learnable: approximate posterior updates suffice, and cross-entropy training on search rollouts recovers the required behavior with polynomial sample complexity. Finally, we show that under a stagewise abstraction of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, the optimal policy extension implements the same posterior reweighting rule. We validate key qualitative predictions of the theory on real large reasoning models.

</details>


### [129] [LLM-powered reasoning in agent-based modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06757)
*Sifat Afroj Moon,Dakotah Maguire,Adam Spannaus,Joe Tuccillo,Maksudul Alam,Sudip K. Seal,John Gounley,Heidi Hanson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid Agent-based and Language-driven Epidemic (HALE) framework combines ABM with LLMs to adapt agent decisions in real-time, demonstrated on COVID-19 in Salt Lake County.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ABMs traditionally use static priors and struggle to capture dynamic human decision-making; LLMs offer data-driven predictions for decision processes, enabling adaptive simulations.

Method: Integrate a scalable HALE framework where LLMs generate decision rules or decisions for agents within an ABM, enabling language-driven, real-time adaptation; apply to a COVID-19 ABM in Salt Lake County, UT.

Result: Proof-of-concept demonstrates feasibility by simulating COVID-19 and its effects in the specified region; no detailed metrics reported in abstract.

Conclusion: HALE provides a scalable pathway to inject adaptive, language-informed decision-making into ABMs, improving policy-relevance; regional case study supports viability.

Abstract: Agent-based modeling (ABM) has the capability to model millions of individuals and their interactions, which is useful for policy making. However, ABMs have traditionally relied on static prior, which prevents the models from adapting to real-time changes. Our research provides a novel approach to addressing this information gap. Large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities to predict human decision-making. Here, we introduce a scalable Hybrid Agent-based and Language-driven Epidemic (HALE) modeling framework that leverages LLMs to predict human decision-making in an ABM simulation. As a proof-of-concept, we use HALE to simulate COVID-19 and its effects in Salt Lake County, UT.

</details>


### [130] [QANTIS: Hardware-Calibrated Sequential POMDP Belief Updates on IBM Heron](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06760)
*Bayram Yuksel Eker,Suayb S. Arslan,Ozgur Nazli,Mustafa Serhat Demirgil,Furkan Deligoz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hardware-calibrated quantum belief-update service (QANTIS) preserves Bayes posterior in Tiger POMDP across horizons, using all-step fixed-point amplitude amplification, within an operating envelope; no end-to-end speedup claim.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess reusability of a quantum posterior-update primitive in sequential POMDP planning without perturbing planner decisions.

Method: Controlled hardware case study on IBM Heron comparing amplification strategies (no amp, guarded Grover, all-step FPAA) across 8/12 primary runs and 20/32 controls; evaluate posterior consistency with exact Bayes and downstream actions; use boundary-aware BIQAE and rare-event sweep to characterize amplitude estimation and evidence envelope.

Result: All-step FPAA maintained Tiger posterior across 8/12-step runs; 20/32-step controls remained within operating band; hardware posterior matched exact Bayes posterior for immediate actions in all decision checks; BIQAE stabilized amplitudes near 0/1; rare-event sweep mapped one-in-a-million evidence envelope.

Conclusion: Provides an operating envelope for a hardware-calibrated belief-update primitive; does not claim hardware-based autonomy advantage or speedup.

Abstract: Autonomous systems under partial observability act on beliefs, not raw sensor events. QANTIS treats the quantum processor as a calibrated belief-update service in that loop: it receives a prior and an observation model, estimates the rare-event evidence term, and returns an ordinary posterior to a classical planner. This paper asks whether that service can be reused across a sequential Tiger POMDP horizon on present IBM Heron hardware without corrupting the planner-facing posterior. We answer with a controlled hardware case study rather than an end-to-end autonomy or wall-clock speedup claim. The study compares no amplification, guarded Grover amplification, and all-step fixed-point amplification on the same trajectory, then checks whether the returned posterior would change the downstream action. All-step FPAA preserves the Tiger posterior across the reported 8-step and 12-step primary runs, and the 20-step and 32-step controls remain inside the same operating band. In every reported decision check, the hardware posterior and the exact Bayes posterior select the same immediate action. Boundary-aware BIQAE stabilizes amplitude estimation near zero and near one, while a rare-event sweep maps the logical sample-complexity envelope for one-in-a-million evidence. The result is an operating envelope for a hardware-calibrated belief-update primitive, not a standalone hardware-advantage claim.

</details>


### [131] [Cost-Effective Agent Harnesses for Abstract Reasoning and Generalization on ARC-AGI-1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06764)
*Kabir Moghe,Peter Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Architecture-only ARC-AGI-1 system using Explorer-Definer Pipeline and Reflective Orchestrator on open-weight DeepSeek V3.2; achieves 57.50% pass@2 at $0.25 and 67.25% pass@2 at $0.62 per task on 400-task ARC-AGI-1 without fine-tuning; shows generation-bound limitations and the significance of the 'think tool'; ablations quantify contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate architectural sufficiency for ARC-AGI-1 with open weights under budget constraints, separating pattern discovery from program synthesis to gauge what can be recovered without task-specific training.

Method: Two-stage Explorer-Definer Pipeline (pattern discovery + executable transformation) plus Reflective Orchestrator enabling autonomous re-exploration when hypotheses fail; evaluated on ARC-AGI-1 400-task set with analysis of pass@k, cost per task, and ablations; includes unbiased diagnostic analyses and re-exploration experiments.

Result: Achieves 57.50% pass@2 at $0.25 and 67.25% pass@2 at $0.62; lifts baseline from 15.50% one-shot to ~67.25% (≈+52 points); unbiased pass@k suggests generation-bound limitations; adaptive re-exploration yields +9.81 pp in pass@1; removing the think tool reduces pass@2 by 5.75 pp.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that architecture-led, non-fine-tuned approaches can yield substantial gains on ARC-AGI-1; improvements likely require broader generation rather than improved ranking; governance of exploration and tool use (think tool) are critical components.

Abstract: Recent progress on ARC-AGI-1 from disclosed architectures has come broadly from two regimes: heavy test-time compute over frontier models (evolutionary search, exhaustive sampling, extended chain-of-thought), or benchmark-specific training in which small models are fine-tuned on ARC data, often with task-specialized architectures. We study a third regime: an open-weight model in non-thinking mode (DeepSeek V3.2) under a strict budget, with no ARC-specific fine-tuning. We study what is recoverable through architecture alone, building agentic harnesses that decompose pattern-discovery and program-synthesis stages explicitly. First, we introduce an Explorer-Definer Pipeline that separates pattern discovery from executable transformation synthesis, implemented as a two-stage agent pipeline. Next, we present the Reflective Orchestrator, which augments the pipeline with autonomous exploration of new transformations when previous hypotheses fail on training pairs. On the ARC-AGI-1 public 400-task evaluation set, the pipeline reaches 57.50% pass@2 at \$0.25 per task, and the orchestrator reaches 67.25% pass@2 at \$0.62 per task. Together these architectures lift a 15.50% one-shot baseline by ~52 points without benchmark-specific training or heavy test-time compute. Furthermore, the orchestrator-driven lift tests a falsifiable diagnostic the pipeline produces; unbiased pass@k analysis suggests the pipeline is generation-bound, not selection-bound (selection via training-pair accuracy captures ~95% of the candidate ceiling) and predicts that significant improvement requires broader generation, not better ranking. The orchestrator implements this prediction via adaptive re-exploration and confirms it (unbiased pass@1 lift +9.81 pp, matching selection-mediated pass@2 lift). An additional pipeline ablation identifies its think tool as a significant component, with removal reducing pass@2 by 5.75 pp.

</details>


### [132] [Evaluating SageMath-Augmented LLM Agents for Computational and Experimental Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06820)
*Pavel Snopov,German Magai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAS-augmented agentic LLM workflow with SageMath feedback and Context7 docs, plus refinements to RealMath; shows consistent performance gains across frontier models, narrowing gaps between open/closed models; supports automated conjecture discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the underexplored role of computer algebra systems (CAS) in agentic LLM workflows and the need for verifiable feedback and up-to-date documentation in computational mathematics research.

Method: A ReAct-style agent combines LLM reasoning with SageMath for verifiable feedback and Context7 for live documentation; evaluates on RealMath-based frontier-model experiments; introduces multi-step post-processing and a multi-stage validation pipeline to refine the problem set; compares models including Qwen-3.7-Max and GPT-5.5.

Result: SageMath access yields +9.7 percentage points average gains across models (range 1.5–27.8 pp), narrowing the gap between open-weight and closed models. Qwen-3.7-Max benefits most; GPT-5.5 achieves the highest solve rate (75.2%) and lowest token usage among tool-enabled configurations.

Conclusion: CAS-augmented agents show promise for assisting computational mathematics and automated conjecture discovery; the work provides a step toward integrating formal tooling and up-to-date documentation in AI-assisted mathematical research; repository is available online.

Abstract: Recent advances in AI for Mathematics have focused largely on autoformalization and theorem proving, leaving the role of Computer Algebra Systems (CAS) in agentic LLM workflows underexplored. We propose a ReAct-style agentic setup that combines LLM reasoning with verifiable feedback from SageMath, together with Context7 for the up-to-date documentation. We evaluate this agentic setup across frontier models for solving research-level mathematical problems from the RealMath benchmark in a setting that emulates a computational-mathematics research loop. We also propose a refinement to the RealMath benchmark by introducing a multi-step post-processing procedure and a multi-stage validation pipeline, both of which improve the quality and reliability of the extracted problem set. Our experiments reveal substantial performance gains from SageMath access across all evaluated models on +9.7~pp on average, the gains range from 1.5~pp to 27.8~pp and narrow the gap between open-weight and closed models. Qwen~3.7-Max benefits from SageMath the most, while GPT-5.5 achieves the highest solve rate of $75.2\%$ and the lowest token usage among tool-enabled configurations. Our findings suggest that CAS-augmented agents represent a promising direction for assisting mathematicians in computational exploration, and we believe that this work is a step towards automated conjecture discovery. The project repository is available online.

</details>


### [133] [The Harness Effect: How Orchestration Design Sets the Token Economics of Enterprise Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06906)
*Muayad Sayed Ali,Aliaksandra Novik,Anji Boddupally,Artem Yavorskyi,Chris Nickerson,Daniel Rica,Emily DuGranrut,Felix Leung,Garrett Prince,Grace Barnett,Heath Robinson,Hosain Al Ahmad,Jesse Resnick,Juan Carlos Farah,Jyothi Swaroop Meruga,Leonid Kuznetsov,Luke Gorham,Marie Schmoll,Michael Paciullo,Saumya Das,Sharath Sheripally,Tommy Griscom,Mykyta Osadchyi,Neha Mantri,Nick Westrum,Olivia Benowitz,Parikshith Kulkarni,Radik Chernyshov,Rakshith Vasudev,Rohith Nadimpally,Vikas Gangadevi,Waseem AlShikh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An orchestration-layer harness dramatically improves AI task efficiency across multiple models by controlling context, tools, and governance, outperforming token-maxing strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Token-based scaling causes costs to grow faster than task value. An orchestration layer could unlock proportional efficiency gains by managing context, tooling, and governance.

Method: Controlled experiment swapping the orchestration layer between a frozen conventional production loop and the Writer Agent Harness across 22 evaluation tasks and six foundation models, measuring cost, latency, tokens per task, and task quality. Analyze mechanism families, compare with other agent systems, and derive formal token economics at the orchestration layer.

Result: Harness reduces blended cost per task by 41% (0.21 -> 0.12), median wall-clock time by 44% (48s -> 27s), and tokens per task by 38% (14.2k -> 8.8k). Task-completion quality is at parity to slightly improved (0.78 -> 0.81). Efficiency gains are model-invariant (33-61% cheaper). Gains correlate strongly with model baseline strength (r=0.99, n=6). Quality per dollar rises 82%; task-completions per million tokens rise from 54.9 to 92.0. The orchestration layer’s impact exceeds the full model price spread on this workload. Six mechanism families support the effect (cache-shape discipline to failure-spend governance); the harness is proposed as the multiplier across any model portfolio.

Conclusion: The orchestration layer (harness) is the dominant lever for efficiency in agent-based AI systems, multiplying model efficiency across portfolios. The paper formalizes token economics for orchestration, outlines six mechanism families, and argues harness leverage is model-invariant and scalable for present and future deployments.

Abstract: Agentic AI development today runs on token maxing: buying capability with tokens -- longer reasoning traces, more turns, wider tool payloads, bigger replayed contexts -- so tokens per task grow faster than task value. Falling per-token prices mask the pattern; total spend rises anyway. We argue the decisive lever against token maxing is the harness: the orchestration layer that assembles context, exposes tools, sequences turns, delegates work, and carries enterprise observability and governance. We isolate it with a controlled swap: 22 locked evaluation tasks, six foundation models (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3.1, Gemini Flash 3.5, Qwen 3.6, GLM 5.1, Palmyra X6), changing only the orchestration layer -- a frozen conventional production loop versus the Writer Agent Harness. Holding models constant, the harness cuts blended cost per task 41% ($0.21->$0.12), median wall-clock 44% (48s->27s), and tokens per task 38% (14.2k->8.8k), with task-completion quality at parity (0.78->0.81, directional at this sample size). Efficiency is model-invariant -- every model gets cheaper (33-61%) -- while quality gains are capability-dependent: a model's gain correlates almost perfectly with its baseline strength (r=0.99, n=6), a phenomenon we term harness leverage. Quality per dollar rises 82%; task-completions per million tokens rise from 54.9 to 92.0. On this workload the orchestration layer moved cost per task more than the full spread of the model menu did. We formalize token economics at the orchestration layer (including effective input price under prompt caching), detail the six mechanism families behind the effect -- cache-shape discipline to failure-spend governance -- compare six widely used agent systems on the same axes, and argue the harness is the one component whose efficiency multiplies across every model an organization runs -- present and future.

</details>


### [134] [Grounding Spatial Relations in a Compact World Model: Instruction Leakage and a Goal-Free Dynamics Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06925)
*Yufeng Wang,Lu Wei,Haibin Ling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that high relation-readout accuracy in goal-conditioned world models often comes from transcribing the instruction, not true perception, revealing a leakage problem that undermines grounding of relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine when language-guided relations grounded by compact world models are genuinely grounded, and to diagnose and remedy instruction leakage where the scored quantity is directly named in the instruction.

Method: Systematic evaluation across three settings with a goal-conditioned predictor, with and without the goal, and a counterfactual instruction. Ablations include a tabletop/BabyAI benchmark comparison and a Language-Table forward-dynamics model. The analysis isolates whether the high accuracy is due to instruction transcription and examines remedies by separating the read path and excluding the goal from dynamics.

Result: Identified a confound: instruction leakage yields ~0.90 accuracy when the instruction names the answer, collapsing to 0.27 without the goal (three seeds). A counterfactual instruction makes anchors follow the false instruction 94.5% of the time (true scene 2.3%, N=256). Leakage persists across three settings but can be mitigated by supervising the read path and keeping the goal out of dynamics, achieving 0.88 accuracy with and without the goal. Language-Table leaks do not occur when instruction names referents unless the instruction is augmented to name direction; degrading the action does not increase leakage.

Conclusion: The detection protocol and remedy apply to any goal-conditioned world model whose instruction names the scored quantity. Effective grounding requires separating the planner's cost (goal) from dynamics and supervising the read path to ensure instruction-independent grounding.

Abstract: Compact world models that condition on a language goal promise to ground relations such as ``put the red block left of the blue block'' using a sparse set of explicit \emph{reference anchors}. We ask when such references actually ground a relation, and identify a trap: a goal-conditioned predictor reaches a striking $0.90$ relation-readout accuracy, yet this is \emph{instruction transcription}, not perception. Withholding the goal collapses it to chance ($0.90\!\to\!0.27$, three seeds) and a counterfactual instruction makes the predicted anchors follow the \emph{false} instruction $94.5\%$ of the time (true scene $2.3\%$; $N{=}256$). Tested across three settings and a within-task ablation, our central claim characterizes the confound: \textbf{instruction leakage occurs when the scored quantity is transcribable from the instruction (when the instruction names the answer) and is essentially independent of how predictive the non-instruction inputs are.} Our tabletop and the external BabyAI benchmark leak, whereas a Language-Table forward-dynamics world model whose instruction names \emph{referents} does not, until the instruction is augmented to name the direction; and degrading the action never increases leakage, the opposite of what predictor-competition predicts. The diagnosis prescribes the fix: keep the goal out of the dynamics (it belongs to the planner's cost) and supervise the \emph{read} path, recovering genuine, instruction-independent grounding ($0.88$, identical with and without the goal). The detection protocol and remedy apply to any goal-conditioned world model whose instruction names the scored quantity.

</details>


### [135] [Large Behavior Model: A Promptable Digital Twin of the Retail Customer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06993)
*Wachiravit Modecrua,Krittin Pachtrachai,Touchapon Kraisingkorn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Large Behavioral Model (LBM) learns customer decision-making from retail transactions using a unified Person-Environment framework, combining behavioral profiles with retrieval-augmented generation, through continued pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning to predict and simulate behavior with cross-domain transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between predictive accuracy and explainability in customer behavior modeling by grounding language models in real behavioral data, enabling scalable digital twins and behavior simulation.

Method: Represent customer state via a behavioral profile from historical purchases; encode product context with retrieval-augmented generation; train via continued pre-training on verbalized behavioral data, followed by supervised fine-tuning for decision generation; apply reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards to calibrate evidence-based decisions; evaluate on purchase prediction, hard-negative discrimination, basket completion, promotion response, and cross-domain voucher redemption; perform ablations to assess the contribution of pre-training, retrieval, and RL.

Result: LBM consistently outperforms frontier general-purpose language models on in-domain retail tasks and exhibits strong zero-shot and fine-tuned transfer across retailers and decision domains. Ablations show continued pre-training as the main driver of generalization, retrieval benefits when used during both training and inference, and RL enhancing reliance on explicit behavioral evidence over priors.

Conclusion: Behavioral knowledge encoded in transaction histories can be effectively learned by language models, providing a scalable foundation for customer digital twins and behavior simulation.

Abstract: Customer behavior modeling underpins recommendation, marketing, and decision support, yet existing approaches either optimize predictive accuracy without explaining decisions or simulate users without grounding them in real behavioral data. We present the Large Behavioral Model (LBM) that learns customer decision making directly from large-scale retail transactions through a unified Person-Environment formulation. Customer state is represented by a behavioral profile derived from historical purchases, while product context is incorporated through retrieval-augmented generation. The model is trained using continued pre-training on verbalized behavioral data, supervised fine-tuning for decision generation, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for evidence-based calibration. We evaluate the proposed framework on purchase prediction, hard-negative discrimination, basket completion, promotion response, and cross-domain voucher redemption. The model consistently outperforms frontier general-purpose language models on in-domain retail tasks while demonstrating strong zero-shot and fine-tuned transfer across retailers and decision domains. Ablation studies show that continued pre-training is the primary driver of behavioral generalization, retrieval is most effective when applied during both training and inference, and reinforcement learning improves reliance on explicit behavioral evidence over generic language-model priors. These results demonstrate that behavioral knowledge encoded in transaction histories can be effectively learned by language models, providing a scalable foundation for customer digital twins and behavior simulation.

</details>


### [136] [Learning social norms enhances compatibility in dynamic human-AI coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07021)
*Yi Yang,Siyuan Liu,Xin Gao,Huamu Sun,Chao Liu,Qing Zhou,Bingbing Nie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formalizing tacit social norms into explicit, quantifiable principles improves human-AI coordination in dynamic pedestrian-vehicle interactions by extracting outcome predictability, value alignment, and advantage awareness from 3,456 human interactions and incorporating them into AI agents, leading to substantial performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap where AI agents, including LLMs, struggle to coordinate with humans in dynamic interactions due to reliance on human demonstrations without explicit norms. The work seeks to quantify underlying social norms to improve coordination.

Method: Develop a simplified experimental platform for pedestrian-vehicle interactions; collect 3,456 dynamic human interactions; identify three normative principles (outcome predictability, value alignment, advantage awareness); encode these norms into AI agents (LLMs) and test in closed-loop human-AI interaction tasks.

Result: Normative-informed LLM achieved nearly fourfold higher total score than the baseline strategy and outperformed human-human interactions by 43% in closed-loop tasks; overall this approach improves mutual coordination.

Conclusion: Formalizing tacit social norms into explicit, quantifiable principles enables AI agents to achieve more natural, mutually beneficial coordination in dynamic interactions, supporting smoother integration of AI into human social environments.

Abstract: Humans continuously coordinate with others in dynamic interactions, often through implicit, hard-to-quantify social norms that act as shared tacit expectations among interacting agents. As AI agents, including large language models (LLMs), become embedded in daily life, they increasingly participate in such interactions and reshape social interaction structures. Yet they often fail to coordinate with humans in an effective, considerate, and natural manner. We hypothesize that this gap arises because existing approaches align model behavior with human demonstrations without explicitly quantifying the underlying norms that generate such behavior. We selected pedestrian-vehicle interaction as a representative dynamic interaction and developed a simplified experimental platform that captures its key interactive features. From 3,456 dynamic human interactions collected via this platform, we identified three principles underlying human social norms: outcome predictability, value alignment, and advantage awareness. Incorporating these principles into AI agents significantly improves human-AI coordination. In the closed-loop interaction task with humans, the social-norm-informed LLM achieved a nearly fourfold higher total score than the baseline strategy and outperformed human-human interactions by 43%. These findings indicate that formalizing tacit social norms into explicit, quantifiable principles can enable AI agents to achieve mutually beneficial coordination in dynamic interactions, supporting their more natural integration into human society.

</details>


### [137] [Measuring Intelligence Beyond Human Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07040)
*Jerry Han,Rafael Moschopoulos,Ella Colby,Vishrut Goyal,Andrew Tu,Kia Ghods,Mark Braverman,Elad Hazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A paradigm shift to relative, model-generated challenges for evaluating AI intelligence beyond human benchmarks, yielding an adversarial psychometric rating that scales with progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Absolute-scale benchmarks saturate and become unverifiable above human capability; need scalable, robust evaluation that adapts with AI advances.

Method: Models generate public evaluation challenges, evaluate rivals on these tasks, aggregate outcomes into a psychometric rating; design protocols to minimize private information use, enable judge-free adjudication, and scale with capabilities; apply to both verifiable and open-ended domains.

Result: Framework instantiated across verifiable and non-verifiable domains; demonstrates model-generated evaluation can continue to measure systems beyond human frontier.

Conclusion: Relative, adversarial evaluation offers scalable, verifiable-to-open-domain measurement beyond human frontier, addressing incentive and adjudication challenges; promising path for future AI assessment.

Abstract: How can we measure intelligence beyond human capability?
  Human-authored benchmarks saturate, and above human capability, examiners may not know which tasks are both hard and verifiable. We argue that this difficulty is inherent to absolute-scale evaluation and propose a new paradigm based on relative measurement in which models generate public challenges that separate other systems. Aggregating these outcomes yields an adversarial psychometric rating system that can scale with the systems being measured. We describe practical protocols that reduce incentives for private-information attacks, support judge-free adjudication, and naturally scale with agent capabilities. We instantiate the framework across verifiable and open-ended, non-verifiable domains, illustrating how model-generated evaluation can continue to measure systems beyond the human frontier.

</details>


### [138] [Operational Reframing and Approval-Framed Delegation in Multi-Agent LLM Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07097)
*Lifei Liu,Haoran Yu,Xiaochong Jiang,Su Wang,Pin Qian,Yihang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper decomposes pipeline safety into reframing, planner refusal/transformation, and delegation framing, showing that aggregate “pipeline” effects are unstable across models and scenarios; a five-condition design reveals reframing as the strongest, most portable risk signal, with planner behavior and delegation framing also shaping compliance; reporting these factors separately is necessary for fair architecture-level conclusions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle the drivers of safety in multi-agent LLM systems and avoid attributing failures to architecture alone, by separating reframing, planner actions, and delegation cues across diverse models and scenarios.

Method: A five-condition controlled contrast design evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios and a four-benchmark external validation set, using LLMs to judge compliance. Analysis parses effects from reframing, planner refusal/transformations, and delegation framing across model pairings.

Result: Operational reframing is the most portable risk signal, boosting compliance for GPT, Gemini, and DeepSeek, while Claude is more resistant. Planner behavior can offset risk mainly via refusal; executable planner steps can unexpectedly increase executor compliance. Approval-framed delegation is highly sensitive to prompts, model pairing, and scenario origin, with skeptical executor prompts reducing compliance. Raw-direct rankings can mislead about planner-executor behavior, e.g., Gemini safest directly but highly amplified with Claude planner; GPTs show near-zero pipeline effect that hides reframing gains.

Conclusion: Multi-agent safety evaluations should report reframing, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model-pairing effects separately rather than attributing failures to architecture alone.

Abstract: Safety evaluations of multi-agent LLM systems often compare a direct prompt with a planner-executor pipeline and report the difference as a single "pipeline effect." We argue that this aggregate is difficult to interpret because it conflates three mechanisms: harmful intent may be reframed as plausible operational work, the planner may refuse or transform the request, and the executor may act under delegation prompts implying prior approval. To separate these factors, we introduce a five-condition controlled contrast design, evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios and an exploratory external validation set from four agent-safety benchmarks using LLM-judged compliance.
  Our results show that aggregate pipeline safety is not a stable architectural property. Operational reframing is the most portable risk signal, increasing compliance for GPT, Gemini, and DeepSeek across both scenario sets, while Claude is comparatively resistant. Planner behavior can offset this risk mainly through refusal; however, when the planner produces executable steps, the executor may become more compliant than under the direct operational baseline. Approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design, model pairing, and scenario source, and a skeptical executor prompt sharply reduces compliance.
  Raw-direct model rankings can also mispredict deployed planner-executor behavior. Gemini is safest under raw direct prompts in the primary set yet shows the largest amplification with a Claude planner, rising from 8.9 percent to 38.9 percent compliance. GPTs near-zero aggregate pipeline effect instead hides a reframing increase canceled by planner refusal. These findings suggest that multi-agent safety evaluations should report reframing, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model pairing separately before attributing failures to architecture itself.

</details>


### [139] [Does AI Understand Imaging? A Systematic Benchmark of Agentic AI for Computational Imaging Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07189)
*Ethan Chung,Chuanjun Zheng,Jasper Tan,Jingxi Li,Haopeng Zhang,Huaijin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic vision-language models underperform specialized imaging methods on physics-based computational imaging tasks; planner offers limited gains; semantic plausibility does not ensure physical fidelity; ImagingBench provides unified benchmarking to track progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether vision-language models and agentic AI can handle physics and inverse problems in computational imaging, where prior work excels at semantic tasks but is weaker on physics-based tasks.

Method: Introduce ImagingBench with 20 tasks across five categories (ray/wave optics, image signal processing, inverse reconstruction, computational sensing, calibration); evaluate three settings (Expert, Planner, Forward); benchmark multiple image-centric multimodal systems (Gemini, GPT, Qwen) and compare to task-specific baselines; perform cross-task analysis.

Result: Across tasks, agentic models are consistently weaker than specialized methods, with pronounced gaps on computational sensing tasks (lensless imaging, event-based reconstruction, time-of-flight imaging, holography). Planner guidance yields only modest and inconsistent gains over the fixed Expert baseline. Outputs are often visually plausible but exhibit poor reference-based fidelity, highlighting a gap between semantic visual competence and physically grounded imaging performance.

Conclusion: ImagingBench provides a unified testbed to quantify and monitor the gap between semantic abilities of agentic AI and physically grounded imaging performance, guiding future work to improve fidelity and physics-aware reasoning in visual AI.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) and agentic AI have shown strong performance on semantic visual tasks, but it remains unclear whether they can handle the physics and inverse problems that underlie computational imaging. We present ImagingBench, a benchmark of 20 computational imaging tasks spanning five categories: ray and wave optics, image signal processing, inverse reconstruction, computational sensing, and calibration. ImagingBench evaluates three complementary settings: Expert, fixed expert-guided inverse reconstruction; Planner, planner-guided inverse reconstruction; and Forward, forward-system simulation for consistency checking. We benchmark leading proprietary and open-source image-centric multimodal systems, including Gemini, GPT, and Qwen, and compare them with representative task-specific non-agentic baselines. Across tasks, agentic models remain consistently weaker than specialized methods, especially on computational sensing problems such as lensless imaging, event-based reconstruction, time-of-flight imaging, and holography. Planner guidance provides only modest and inconsistent gains over the fixed-prompt Expert baseline. Although the models often generate visually plausible outputs, their reference-based fidelity remains poor, revealing a substantial gap between semantic visual competence and physically grounded imaging performance. ImagingBench provides a unified testbed for measuring this gap and tracking progress in agentic AI for computational imaging.

</details>


### [140] [Reasoning Consistency Scanning: A Framework for Auditing Chain-of-Thought Validity in AI Safety Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07229)
*Silvia Santano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes reasoning consistency scanning to detect whether stated chain-of-thought is logically consistent with the final answer in safety evaluation transcripts; formalizes a six-subtype inconsistency taxonomy, builds a 60-transcript benchmark, implements InspectScout, and empirically demonstrates detectability and model/task variation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Faithfulness of chain-of-thought is hard to assess with interventions; consistency offers an intervention-free, transcript-only proxy for evaluating reasoning quality in AI safety contexts.

Method: Formalize reasoning consistency; define a six-subtype taxonomy; assemble a 60-transcript benchmark adapted from InstrumentalEval; implement InspectScout scanner; evaluate four generator models across three safety-evaluation tasks.

Result: Reasoning inconsistency is present and detectable; its prevalence varies by model and task; the scanner reliably identifies inconsistencies across transcripts.

Conclusion: Reasoning consistency is a viable, scalable evaluation signal for safety transcripts; the work provides a reusable method and an implementable tool (InspectScout) for assessing transcript-level reasoning quality.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning is often unfaithful: a model's stated reasoning does not reliably reflect the process that produced its output. Detecting unfaithfulness, though, requires controlled experimental interventions, which cannot be applied to evaluation transcripts after the fact. We turn instead to a more tractable question that has received less attention: whether the stated reasoning is logically consistent with the answer it accompanies. Unlike faithfulness, consistency can be assessed from a transcript alone, with no intervention. We introduce reasoning consistency scanning, a reusable method for detecting this property in AI safety evaluation transcripts. Our contributions are fourfold. First, we formalize reasoning consistency as distinct from faithfulness and define a six-subtype taxonomy of inconsistency. Second, we build a validated benchmark of 60 transcripts, manually adapted from InstrumentalEval outputs. Third, we implement a working scanner for InspectScout, the first to target this property in safety evaluation transcripts. Fourth, we report results across four generator models and three evaluations from inspect_evals, showing that reasoning inconsistency is present, detectable, and varies systematically across both models and task types.

</details>


### [141] [From Atomic Actions to Standard Operating Procedures: Iterative Tool Optimization for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07321)
*Haipeng Ding,Yuexiang Xie,Zhewei Wei,Yaliang Li,Bolin Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSOP reframes tool use in LLM agents by synthesizing atomic actions into reusable SOPs, enabling self-evolution through a lifecycle of construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning, which improves success and reduces interaction rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, granular toolsets force agents to reinvent low-level logic for recurring workflows, causing high reasoning overhead and failure. SOPs offer reusable, higher-order tools to encapsulate multi-step logic and enable self-improvement.

Method: Extract SOPs from execution trajectories and apply a lifecycle (construction, merging, evaluation, pruning) to build and refine a toolset of SOP-based tools; treat SOPs as callable higher-order tools that replace or augment atomic actions.

Result: Experiments show EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates and substantially reduces the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines, while promoting more reliable and efficient tool-use patterns.

Conclusion: EvoSOP provides a scalable pathway for self-evolving agents by enabling iterative tool optimization via SOP-based tooling, reducing engineering effort and improving robustness; future work could explore broader domains, safety, and generalization.

Abstract: Tool utilization enables Large Language Model (LLM) agents to interact with the real world and resolve complex tasks. However, existing agent frameworks predominantly rely on static toolsets composed of granular atomic actions (e.g., basic file I/O or single-turn search), which forces agents to reinvent low-level logic for every recurring workflow, leading to increased reasoning overhead and failure rates. In this study, we propose that agents can achieve self-evolution by synthesizing these atomic actions into reusable Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs), which function as callable higher-order tools that encapsulate multi-step logic. We further introduce EvoSOP, a framework that empowers agents to extract SOPs from execution trajectories and iteratively optimize the toolset through a systematic lifecycle of construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates while substantially reducing the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines. Our analysis also reveals that iterative tool optimization fosters reliable and efficient tool-use patterns, providing a scalable pathway for the development of self-evolving agents.

</details>


### [142] [Physics-Audited Agentic Discovery in Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07379)
*Diab W. Abueidda,Bilal Ahmed,Panos Pantidis,Mostafa E. Mobasher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PA-SciML is a verification-first workflow that audits physics constraints for agentic SciML surrogates, beyond error minimization, using fixed scoring, machine-checkable physics requirements, per-candidate physics checks, and targeted input-range tests; experiments show physics-based verification can outperform pure error-based selection in static elasticity and transient elastodynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure surrogate models respect essential physics (boundary conditions, causality, superposition, stiffness scaling) rather than only minimizing numerical error.

Method: Fix scoring evaluator before search; derive machine-checkable physics requirements; evaluate each trained candidate against physics checks; independently search prescribed input ranges or load histories for high-violation cases without reference solutions; report a surrogate as verified only if the checks pass; optionally enable advisory numerical probes and test one modeling change at a time to link score gains to edits.

Result: Static elasticity: a surrogate with lower validation error than the error-only baseline is selected, and both pass common linear-elastic checks. Transient elastodynamics: an error-only baseline with similar mean error fails a stricter causality check by responding to future loading history, while the selected surrogate passes the checks. Overall, per-candidate physics evidence guides selection beyond aggregate error.

Conclusion: Physics-audited verification improves reliability and trustworthiness of SciML surrogates by ensuring predicted fields satisfy core physics; the per-candidate physics justification distinguishes it from traditional error-based scoring and can be generalized to other physics-informed surrogate discovery scenarios.

Abstract: In agentic scientific machine learning (SciML), large language model (LLM) agents can discover surrogate models and select one by an automated score, typically an error metric. A low error, however, does not establish that the predicted fields satisfy the physics that matter for mechanics, such as boundary conditions, superposition, stiffness scaling, or causality. We introduce Physics-Audited Agentic SciML (PA-SciML), a verification-first workflow for agentic SciML discovery. The workflow fixes a scoring evaluator before search, derives reviewable machine-checkable physics requirements, checks each trained candidate on its outputs, and separately searches prescribed input ranges or measured load-history spans for high-violation cases without reference solution fields. A surrogate is reported as verified only under the stated checks. When enabled, the workflow also adds advisory numerical probes before training and tests one modeling change at a time to record which isolated edits are associated with score gains before reuse. In the reported computational-solid-mechanics numerical examples, the static elasticity run selects a surrogate with lower validation error than the error-only baseline while both selected models pass the common linear-elastic checks. In the transient elastodynamics run, an error-only baseline with similar mean error fails a stricter causality check by responding to future parts of the loading history, while the selected surrogate passes the stated checks. The main distinction is per-candidate physics evidence on predicted fields, not a richer aggregate score.

</details>


### [143] [MIRA-Math: A Benchmark for Minimal Information Requesting and Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07391)
*Charbel Al Bateh,Samer Saab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIRA-Math is a diagnostic benchmark for mathematical reasoning where the solver's view lacks exactly one atomic fact; the solver must request the missing fact in natural language under a fixed budget, and a fixed LLM responder can only return the exact quoted fact or decline. It includes 2,310 instances across 22 mathematical families, and shows that request success and final-answer accuracy are separable. The creators release generators, verifiers, prompts, and metadata for reproducible evaluation of minimal-information requesting in math reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isolate and study the minimal information-gathering capability required for correct mathematical reasoning, bridging static problem-solving benchmarks and interactive, tool-using benchmarks. By constraining the solver-facing view to miss a single canonical fact, the benchmark probes whether models can request the precise information and correctly integrate it into a final solution.

Method: Deterministic instance generation with typed hint specifications and validation. A fixed LLM responder sees only the dataset-provided atomic fact and must either quote the exact fact when the user request matches it, or decline. Evaluation tracks (a) whether the model can request the canonical fact within a budget and (b) whether the final, exact answer is correct after incorporating the fact.

Result: Empirical results show that the ability to request the appropriate fact and the ability to compute the final answer are separable: models may request the right information but still fail downstream, or fail to obtain the canonical hint altogether. The dataset comprises 2,310 instances across 22 mathematical families (algebra, probability, linear systems, discrete structures, etc.). The authors provide generators, verifiers, prompts, run metadata, and documentation to support reproducible evaluation of minimal-information requests in mathematical reasoning.

Conclusion: MIRA-Math offers a controlled, reproducible framework to study constrained information-seeking in mathematical reasoning. It clarifies failure modes (information retrieval vs computation) and provides resources to benchmark minimal-information requesting. However, the fixed-responder constraint may exaggerate certain errors and limit naturalistic tool-use scenarios.

Abstract: Mathematical reasoning benchmarks typically provide all facts needed to solve each problem, while interactive benchmarks often mix reasoning with tools, retrieval, and long-horizon dialogue. We introduce MIRA-Math, a benchmark for a narrower diagnostic capability: solving mathematical problems whose full latent state has a unique answer, but whose solver-facing view is missing exactly one necessary atomic fact. The solver must request the missing information in natural language under a strict budget and then integrate the returned fact into an exact final answer. A fixed constrained LLM responder sees only the dataset-provided atomic fact and must either offer the quoted fact when the request matches it, or decline otherwise. Thus, instance generation, typed hint specifications, validation, and final-answer verification are deterministic, while request metrics are measured under a fixed LLM-mediated responder channel. MIRA-Math contains 2{,}310 generated instances from 22 typed mathematical families spanning algebra, probability, linear systems, discrete structures, signal processing, Markov chains, circuits, interpolation, and numerical boundary-value problems. Experiments across frontier and small models show that request success and final-answer accuracy are separable: models may ask for the right fact yet fail the downstream computation, or fail before obtaining the canonical hint. We release generators, verifiers, prompts, run metadata, and dataset documentation to support reproducible evaluation of minimal information requesting in mathematical reasoning.

</details>


### [144] [Agentic Data Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07397)
*Elaine Ang,Chenxi Huang,Georgios Liargkovas,Jerry Liu,Jinhui Liu,Nikos Pagonas,Charlie Summers,Haonan Wang,Jiakai Xu,Tianle Zhou,Yusen Zhang,Zhou Yu,Zhuo Zhang,Tianyi Peng,Kostis Kaffes,Eugene Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents the concept of Agentic Data Environments as an execution substrate for agents that enhances capability while enforcing safety guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automation promises speed and scale but failures can be costly; there is a need to extend safety from traditional databases to broader data ecosystems, reframing data systems as active substrates for safe execution.

Method: Early, conceptual exploration outlining the architecture and principles of Agentic Data Environments, emphasizing how they amplify agent capabilities while providing safety guarantees.

Result: Proposes a framing and initial design direction for Agentic Data Environments; highlights the potential of treating data systems as active substrates rather than passive storage to enable safe autonomous agents.

Conclusion: Data systems can be redesigned as active, safety-oriented execution substrates that enable scalable, reliable agentic automation.

Abstract: Autonomous agents promise substantial gains in speed, scale, and labor efficiency, but their failures can impose abrupt and often irreversible costs. The central challenge for agentic automation is therefore to increase the benefits of automation while bounding the consequences of failure.
  While databases remain central to modern computing, agents operate over a broader data environment spanning files, APIs, applications, and system state. In this talk, I will outline early work on Agentic Data Environments -- the execution substrate in which agents operate -- that both amplify agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees. This perspective reframes data systems from passive stores of state into active substrates for safe, reliable execution.

</details>


### [145] [Reason Less, Verify More: Deterministic Gates Recover a Silent Policy-Violation Failure Mode in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07405)
*Vikas Reddy,Sumanth Reddy Challaram,Abhishek Basu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates reduce silent policy-violating writes by tool-using LLMs in policy-permissive environments, improving task success but not guaranteeing it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Silent wrong-state failures (e.g., bookings canceled, counts changed) occur when LLMs issue writes through tools despite policy violations. A safety boundary mechanism is needed to mitigate this failure mode.

Method: Empirical evaluation in the tau^2-bench airline domain. Measured silent wrong-state failures of budget agents. Implemented a four-gate, deterministic, read-only pre-execution filter that inspects each proposed tool call against the current state before permitting writes. Evaluated across GPT-4o-mini and GPT-5.2, with multiple seeds and negative controls (policy-permissive vs self-enforcing domains). Reported task-level performance, seeding, and significance tests.

Result: Silent wrong-state failures accounted for 78% of observed failures on budget agents, with reproducible aggregate failure rates across seeds. Four-gate policy filter increased full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% on GPT-4o-mini (+12.4 percentage points; P=0.0012) and similar gains on a disjoint 15-seed set (+12.3pp; P=0.0008). Improvement concentrated on the 26 of 50 tasks where gates fired; non-firing tasks showed no exclusion of zero. Negative controls showed gates are beneficial when tools are policy-permissive and add little where tools self-enforce. At frontier (GPT-5.2), success rose from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020; n=5, no replication). The gates provide a bounded reliability improvement: they can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary, but do not guarantee task success.

Conclusion: Deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates constitute a bounded safety mechanism that prevents a specific class of silent policy-violating writes in tool-using LLMs. They improve reliability in policy-permissive environments but are not a complete solution to alignment or task success.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents can violate the very policies they are deployed to enforce while appearing to complete the task successfully. In policy-permissive environments, a tool may execute any well-formed call even when the corresponding state transition is forbidden by domain policy. The result is a silent wrong state (a booking cancelled, a passenger count changed, a claim acted on without verification) that neither the tool nor the agent's self-report exposes.
  We study this failure mode in the $τ^2$-bench airline domain. On a budget agent, 78% of observed failures are silent wrong-state failures with no tool error, and the aggregate failure rate is reproducible across disjoint seeds, not sampling noise. We then evaluate a lightweight intervention: deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates that inspect the proposed call and current state before allowing a write. A four-gate suite raises full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% on gpt-4o-mini (+12.4pp; paired task-level bootstrap P=0.0012), and the lift reproduces on a disjoint 15-seed set (+12.3pp; P=0.0008).
  The effect is concentrated where the gates fire: on the 26/50 firing tasks, success rises by +19.2pp, while movement on the 24 non-firing tasks does not exclude zero. Two negative controls (a self-enforcing retail domain and BFCL) bound the mechanism: gates help when tools are policy-permissive and add little where tools already self-enforce. As suggestive evidence, not a central claim, the same failure mode persists at the frontier: gpt-5.2 at default reasoning still attempts policy-violating writes, and the same suite improves success from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020; n=5, no replication). The contribution is a bounded evaluation and reliability result: deterministic gates do not guarantee task success, but they can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary.

</details>


### [146] [InductWave: Inductive Multi-Hop Logical Query Answering on Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07422)
*Mayank Kharbanda,Michael Cochez,Rajiv Ratn Shah,Raghava Mutharaju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InductWave: a wavelet-based inductive method for logical multi-hop query answering on large knowledge graphs, achieving competitive performance with fewer message-passing layers and scalable to massive graphs; validated on FB15k-237 and Wiki-KG with varying train/test splits; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address inductive reasoning to unseen entities and resource scarcity in large KBs. Traditional EFO-query models are largely transductive and rely on training on the full graph; a scalable inductive approach is needed for large, evolving KGs.

Method: Proposes InductWave, a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical query answering on large KGs. Training graphs contain fewer nodes than test graphs. The model reduces required message-passing layers and demonstrates competitive performance with fewer resources. Focuses on existential First Order Logic queries (conjunction, disjunction, negation) and evaluates across varying train-test graph proportions, including large graphs such as Wiki-KG.

Result: InductWave matches or slightly exceeds baseline models using half as many message-passing layers and performs best in many settings when using about 75% of the layers. It scales to massive graphs and is validated on FB15k-237 with varying train-test proportions and on Wiki-KG. Code and datasets are publicly available.

Conclusion: InductWave enables scalable inductive logical query answering on large knowledge graphs with reduced resource requirements, maintaining strong accuracy and enabling evaluation on massive graphs; demonstrates the practicality of wavelet-based inductive representations for LQE in real-world settings.

Abstract: Logical Multi-Hop Query Answering over Knowledge Graphs (KGs) can be formulated as querying, with an implicit completeness assumption. Current works mainly focus on Existential First Order Logic (EFO) queries. These EFO queries contain conjunction, disjunction, and negation operators. Most existing works employ transductive reasoning, meaning they are not capable of reasoning over entities unseen during training. In the real world, there is a resource scarcity, and we cannot train a model with all the nodes of a large KG. Hence, we propose InductWave, a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical query answering on large KGs. Here, the training graph consists of fewer nodes than the test graph. Our model performs on par with the baseline models while having half the number of message-passing layers. It outperforms all of them in most cases, with 75% of the layers. These fewer resource requirements enable us to evaluate InductWave on massive graphs, such as Wiki-KG. We test our model using extensive experiments across varying train-test graph proportions of the FB15k-(237) dataset, comparing it with the state-of-the-art models. The code and datasets for the model are available at https://github.com/kracr/inductwave/.

</details>


### [147] [The Blind Curator: How a Biased Judge Silently Disables Skill Retirement in Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07436)
*Xing Zhang,Yanwei Cui,Guanghui Wang,Ziyuan Li,Wei Qiu,Bing Zhu,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Biased judges can silently disable skill retirement via false-pass bias; symmetric noise preserves retirement; a sharp threshold governs retirement, with outcomes depending on whether corruption also hinders skill synthesis; a defect-injection audit can reveal the judge's position before deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how biased reward signals from referees/judges affect the safety mechanism of skill retirement in self-evolving agents, especially for reference-free tasks where judge feedback may be skewed.

Method: Corrupted-reward analysis: inject corruption on top of a deterministic reward to isolate causal channels; behavioral study on a reference-free report-writing testbed with a code-generation cross-check; compare symmetric noise vs false-pass bias; separate genuine retirement from cap-eviction churn across domains and failure rates.
