Result: Symmetric noise preserves retirement; false-pass bias disables contribution-based retirement after a sharp threshold that data cannot cross; the mechanism is universal across domains and rates, except near-zero-false-pass/ verifier-like graders; downstream eval quality degrades only when the corruption also starves skill synthesis, otherwise remains steady; the curator becomes silent in aggregate metrics; a cheap defect-injection audit can reveal which side of the threshold a judge occupies.

Conclusion: The work provides a behavioral safety insight rather than performance gains, highlighting the risk of silent curator failure under biased evaluation and offering a practical audit to anticipate deployment risks.

Abstract: A self-evolving agent retires its bad skills by watching them fail, so what happens when the judge cannot see the failures? Skill retirement is the structural constraint that keeps a growing library from drifting below the no-skill baseline, but its guarantee assumes an unbiased reward, which is false for the LLM judges that reference-free tasks force upon us. We show that a biased judge does not merely add noise; it \emph{silently switches off the curator}. We make this precise with a corrupted-reward analysis and, isolating the causal channel by injecting corruption on top of a deterministic reward, a behavioral study on a reference-free report-writing testbed with a code-generation cross-check. Symmetric noise leaves retirement intact, but \emph{false-pass} bias (failures slipping through as passes) disables contribution-based retirement past a sharp threshold that no amount of data can cross. Separating genuine retirement from cap-eviction churn shows this \emph{mechanism} failure is universal, holding across domains and failure rates and sparing only near-zero-false-pass, verifier-like graders. The downstream \emph{outcome}, though, is regime-dependent: eval quality degrades only where the same corruption also starves skill synthesis, and otherwise holds steady, so the disabled curator is \emph{silent}, surfacing in no aggregate metric. The contribution is a behavioral safety result, not a performance one. A cheap defect-injection audit then tells an operator, before deployment, which side of the threshold their judge occupies.

</details>


### [148] [SpaCellAgent: A Self-Evolving LLM-Based Multi-Agent Framework for Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07467)
*Songhan Wang,Haoang Chi,He Li,Zhiheng Zhang,Jiayan Yuan,Cheems Wang,Hao Peng,Xinwang Liu,Wenjing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpaCellAgent presents an autonomous, multi-agent LLM framework that converts natural-language specs into optimized, end-to-end spatiotemporal trajectory inference workflows, achieving >40% efficiency gains across diverse datasets while preserving expert-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TI in spatial and single-cell transcriptomics is hindered by heavy manual intervention and fragmented tooling; there is a need for automated, scalable, and accessible workflows that translate natural-language goals into executable analyses.

Method: A multi-agent architecture for strategic workflow planning paired with a dynamic tool-orchestration engine that selects algorithms adaptively; a self-evolution module iteratively refines performance via feedback; evaluation on six heterogeneous datasets spanning temporal trajectories, platforms, and tissue architectures; codebase available.

Result: SpaCellAgent achieves over 40% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining expert-aligned performance across six datasets, enabling fully automated workflows from natural-language specifications.

Conclusion: The framework democratizes spatiotemporal modeling in computational biology by providing autonomous end-to-end TI pipelines driven by agent collaboration, with scalable, reusable workflows and broad applicability to diverse datasets.

Abstract: Spatial and Single-cell transcriptomics are transformative in deciphering cellular dynamics. As the fundamental paradigm for reconstructing cell developmental paths, trajectory inference (TI) is critical. However, existing methods require extensive manual intervention and proficiency in heterogeneous tools, posing a significant barrier to efficient TI analysis. To bridge this gap, we propose SpaCellAgent, an autonomous large language model (LLM) multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end spatiotemporal analysis and narrative generation. SpaCellAgent utilizes a multi-agent architecture for strategic workflow planning, a dynamic tool-orchestration engine for adaptive algorithm selection, and a self-evolution module that iteratively refines performance through feedback. We evaluate SpaCellAgent on six heterogeneous datasets encompassing complex temporal developmental trajectories, diverse sequencing platforms, and spatially-resolved tissue architectures. SpaCellAgent consistently demonstrates over 40\% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining expert-aligned performance. By converting natural language specifications into optimized analytical workflows and fully automating the pipeline, SpaCellAgent democratizes advanced spatiotemporal modeling and establishes a scalable, agent-driven paradigm for computational biology. The code and materials are available at https://github.com/LittleXH-shw/SpaCellAgent.

</details>


### [149] [Search, Fail, Recover: A Training Framework for Correction-Aware Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07492)
*Dmitry Beresnev,Vladimir Makharev,Roman Khalikov,Ivan Oseledets,Petr Anokhin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pyligent frames reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains with a task validator labeling continuations and failures; uses three actions (continue, finish, backtrack) and optional traces; shows large gains over gold-only FT across hidden graphs, Sudoku variants, and Blocksworld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single left-to-right chain reasoning struggles with delayed failures and backtracking; explicit supervision of failed branches can teach recovery strategies.

Method: Train/infer framework where a task validator labels generated continuations and failures; search trees are converted into supervised targets for three actions (continue, finish, backtrack); optional traces summarize abandoned branches; evaluated on hidden directed graph task and structured reasoning domains with exact validators (4x4 Sudoku, Sudoku with traces, Blocksworld).

Result: Solve-rate improvements: +72.7pp on hidden graphs; +17pp and +18pp on mixed and expert Sudoku; +27pp and +14pp on mixed and expert Sudoku with traces; +13pp on Blocksworld.

Conclusion: Explicit failed-branch supervision enhances recovery behavior beyond imitation of polished solution chains; Pyligent effectively enables backtracking and revisiting prefixes to complete solutions.

Abstract: Many reasoning tasks are not well described by a single left-to-right chain: a solver may need to pursue a plausible branch, observe delayed failure, and return to the latest prefix that can still be completed. We introduce Pyligent, a training and inference framework inspired by the Diligent Learner formulation that represents reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains. A task validator labels generated continuations and failures, and the resulting search trees are converted into supervised targets for three actions: continue, finish, and backtrack, with optional traces that summarize abandoned branches. We evaluate Pyligent on a hidden directed graph task designed to isolate delayed-failure recovery, and on structured reasoning domains with exact validators, including $4{\times}4$ Sudoku, Sudoku with reasoning traces, and Blocksworld. Compared with gold-only supervised fine-tuning, Pyligent improves solve rate by $72.7$ percentage points on hidden graphs, by $17$ and $18$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku, by $27$ and $14$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku with reasoning traces, and by $13$ points on Blocksworld. These results suggest that explicit failed-branch supervision can teach useful recovery behavior beyond imitation of polished solution chains.

</details>


### [150] [Do LLM-Generated Skills Make Better AI Data Scientists? A Component Ablation Across Data-Science Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07504)
*Wei-Jung Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-generated skills do not improve data-science task performance over simple task prompts; large-scale evaluation across stages shows no significant gains; token-matched control indicates skills can be content-irrelevant; caution against default use of generated skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether automatically generated, low-effort skills can replace or augment expert-written, curated skills for recurring data-science tasks, addressing the bottleneck of maintaining skills across task families.

Method: Four lifecycle stages (data preparation, data extraction, statistical analysis, reporting); one generated skill per stage; 56 tasks, 9 model configurations, 3 providers; 7,560 runs; plus a token-matched control with 1,512 runs; ablations of skill components.

Result: No reliable improvement from full generated skills vs. prompt-only; ablated variants also show no improvement; all p-values ≥ 0.396; total variance ~1.2 percentage points; token-matched control shows full skills perform like task-irrelevant skill content.

Conclusion: Using a single LLM-generated skill per workflow as default prompting strategy is not supported; consider alternative low-curation approaches or higher-quality guidance; generated skills may not deliver expected benefits.

Abstract: Product data scientists often ask LLM-based agents to help with recurring execution tasks such as cleaning data, writing SQL, choosing statistical tests, and formatting results. Reusable skill files are meant to avoid prompting from scratch by packaging guidance for a task family. Expert-written skills can encode high-quality guidance, but writing and maintaining them across many data-science task families creates a manual bottleneck. We ask whether LLM-generated skills offer a useful low-curation alternative: do they improve performance over the task prompt alone? We test this question across four lifecycle stages: data preparation, data extraction, statistical analysis, and reporting, using one generated skill per stage. We find no reliable improvement from full generated skills over No-Skill prompting. We then ask whether any part of the skill is useful by ablating different skill components. The main ablation covers 56 tasks, nine model configurations, and three providers, yielding 7,560 runs. Compared with prompting using the task alone, neither the full generated skill nor any ablated skill variant significantly improves performance; all p-values are at least 0.396, and the total spread across variants is only 1.2 pp. A supplemental token-matched control adds 1,512 runs and finds that Full skills perform similarly to task-irrelevant skill-formatted content. The results caution against using one LLM-generated skill per data-science workflow as a default single-shot prompting strategy.

</details>


### [151] [RL Post-Training Builds Compositional Reasoning Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07646)
*Azwar Abdulsalam,Nishil Patel,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RL post-training enables compositional, reusable higher-level strategies from base primitive competence, not merely amplifying latent skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine whether RL can compose primitive skills into novel strategies versus mainly amplifying existing ones; identify conditions and mechanisms that enable compositional emergence.

Method: Fully observable rewrite-grammar environment; pretrained Transformer on primitive symbol-rewrite chains; post-trained on a trace-based reasoning task with binary final-answer reward; compare RL versus rejection-based fine-tuning; analyze trace data to characterize compositional stages; perform pretraining ablations to test the gating role of reduction-organized competence; evaluate on held-out problems beyond pretrained coverage.

Result: RL solves held-out problems beyond pretrained capacity even with limited sampling; traces reveal a phased compositional mechanism: (1) strengthens primitive reductions; (2) discovers valid composed procedures, including sequential (chains of contractions) and parallel (independent contractions) compositions; composed procedures are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire; rejection fine-tuning yields many invalid shortcut rewrites and plateaus early; RL's advantage lies in selective exploration that concentrates on valid reusable structure; emergence of compositional strategies hinges on pretraining that organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures, enabling RL to compress into higher-level strategies.

Conclusion: Compositional strategies emerge when pretraining structures primitive competence into reduction procedures, allowing RL to build and reuse higher-level strategies; RL extends the base model beyond amplification of latent skills by synthesizing primitive ingredients into reliable, reusable procedures.

Abstract: Does RL post-training merely amplify primitive skills already latent in a base model, or can it compose primitive skills into new higher-level strategies? We study this question in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment where the pretraining distribution is known and every generated rewrite can be audited. A Transformer is pretrained on primitive symbol-rewrite chains and post-trained on a Trace-based reasoning task with only a binary final-answer reward. RL solves held-out problems that remain rarely solved by the pretrained model even under much larger sampling budgets, while rejection fine-tuning improves early but plateaus. Trace analysis shows that RL reorganizes primitive competence through a phased compositional mechanism: it first strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers valid composed procedures. These include sequential compositions, which collapse ordered chains of primitive contractions, and parallel compositions, which combine independent primitive contractions in a single step. The composed procedures are not isolated samples; they are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire. Comparing RL with rejection fine-tuning shows that the key difference is not exploration volume but selectivity: RFT produces many shortcut-like rewrites, much of them invalid, whereas RL concentrates exploration into valid reusable structure. Pretraining ablations show that the emergence of compositional strategies is gated not by primitive exposure alone, but by whether pretraining organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures that RL can later compress. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reliable higher-level strategies.

</details>


### [152] [Recursive Self-Improvement in AI: From Bounded Self-Refinement to Autonomous Research Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07663)
*Mingguang Chen,Licheng Wang,Bo Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Surveys 1,250 arXiv papers (2024–2026) on self-improving AI, distinguishing bounded self-refinement from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI); emphasizes self-evaluation as a core signal; maps evaluator designs into a verification hierarchy; identifies failure modes and governance bottlenecks; argues for governance-grade measurement of self-improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify terminology and architecture of self-improving AI, separate bounded vs open-ended RSI, and assess implications for safety, governance, and regulation.

Method: Literature survey of 1,250 arXiv papers (2024–2026) across four axes (behavior in deployment, training policy, evaluator, research process) and varying degrees of loop closure; develops a taxonomy and a verification-hierarchy of evaluators; analyzes failure modes and governance implications.

Result: Introduces a taxonomy distinguishing bounded self-refinement from open-ended RSI; highlights that self-improvement strength tracks evaluator-verifier strength; identifies failure modes (self-confirming loops, model collapse, diversity collapse); notes the top governance bottleneck is research direction-setting; links to RSI theory and frontier-lab safety/governance questions; notes governance-grade measurement of self-improvement as underpopulated.

Conclusion: Provides a framework to study RSI limits and governance implications, emphasizing the need for robust evaluation infrastructures and governance metrics to manage risk in self-improving AI.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly participate in their own improvement: revising their outputs, adapting their own harnesses during deployment, training on data they generate, and, increasingly, conducting AI research itself. This literature is described under a vocabulary ("self-refine," "self-reward," "self-play," "self-evolve") that conflates fundamentally different ambitions. We survey 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) along two axes: what the system improves -- its behavior in deployment, its policy through training, its evaluator, or the research process itself -- and the degree of loop closure (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). The taxonomy separates bounded self-refinement -- convergent, evaluable, and already industrial practice -- from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI), which remains bounded by grounding requirements, collapse dynamics, and compute constraints on every measured axis. Its distinctive feature is a dedicated category for self-evaluation: every improvement loop is a claim that some signal can substitute for human judgment. We survey the evaluator design space -- judges, process reward models, verifiers, rubrics, meta-evaluation -- order the signals into a verification hierarchy from formal verifiers (strongest) to intrinsic self-assessment (weakest), and observe that demonstrated self-improvement strength tracks this hierarchy, that its failure modes (self-confirming loops, model collapse, diversity collapse) follow from its violations, and that the "research direction-setting" bottleneck keeping humans in the loop sits at the top of that hierarchy. We connect the technical literature to the theory of RSI limits and to the safety and governance questions raised by frontier-lab accounts of closing the loop, and identify governance-grade measurement of self-improvement as the field's most underpopulated niche.

</details>


### [153] [SkillCenter: A Large-Scale Source-Grounded Skill Library for Autonomous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07676)
*Tianming Sha,Yue Zhao,Lichao Sun,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large, multi-source, ground-traced skill library for autonomous agents with 216,938 skills across 24 bundles; introduces SkillGate for quality filtering and produces offline SQLite FTS5 bundles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in grounded, verifiable operational knowledge for autonomous agents, enabling outputs to be correct, secure, and maintainable.

Method: Multi-source acquisition; LLM-based SkillGate quality gate; template-driven generation; iterative source-grounding; quality-controlled publishing; offline FTS5 integration; traceability mapping to exact quotations.

Result: 114,565 source-grounded skills from peer-reviewed journals, ArXiv, and over 24,000 technical sources; 102,373 community skills from GitHub and the ClawHub marketplace; total 216,938 skills across 24 domain bundles; all skills packaged as offline-searchable SQLite FTS5 bundles.

Conclusion: SkillCenter provides a scalable, traceable, offline-accessible skill catalog that enhances grounding for autonomous agents and can improve reliability, security, and maintainability of agent outputs.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents can execute complex tasks with limited human review, yet they often lack the grounded operational knowledge to make their outputs not just executable but correct, secure, and maintainable. We introduce SkillCenter, to our knowledge the largest open skill library for agents by total count: 216,938 structured skills across 24 domain bundles. A SkillGate-filtered pipeline contributes 114,565 source-grounded skills from peer-reviewed journals, ArXiv, and over 24,000 technical sources, integrated with 102,373 community skills from GitHub and the ClawHub marketplace. We present the end-to-end framework that builds the pipeline subset: multi-source acquisition, an LLM-based quality gate (SkillGate), template-driven generation, iterative source-grounding, and quality-controlled publishing. Source grounding is a traceability guarantee: each retained claim maps to an exact quotation in its source. All skills ship as offline-searchable SQLite FTS5 bundles.

</details>


### [154] [Institutional Red-Teaming: Deployment Rules, Not Just Models, Causally Shape Multi-Agent AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07695)
*Yujiao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Institutional red-teaming to test deployment rules in multi-agent AI; shows rules causally affect safety, universal targeting hazards, and identity-salience-driven elimination; proposes a safety-case workflow and rule-region certification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To causally isolate the impact of deployment rules on collective behavior and safety in multi-agent settings, addressing the lack of systematic, causal evidence across diverse contexts.

Method: Hold agents/objectives/task state fixed; vary one deployment rule at a time; apply to IABench-CA benchmark across 228 contexts, 5 rules, 7 model populations (33,924 games); include normative cooperative reference and auto-labelled reasoning traces; perform anonymization ablation to test identity salience.

Result: Deployment rules causally alter safety (22–58 point fatality changes); no universally safe default; hazards universal with identity-targeting often not safest and can eliminate 30–87% of least-resourced agents; identity salience mechanism observed (naming loss bearer drives elimination; effect persists under repeated play albeit delayed).

Conclusion: The framework provides a safety-case workflow to certify a provisional rule region with residual risks and monitoring obligations; highlights the need for careful governance of deployment rules to prevent identity-based targeting and ensure safety across populations.

Abstract: We introduce institutional red-teaming, an evaluation methodology for testing deployment rules in multi-agent AI: hold the agents, objectives, and task state fixed, vary only one rule, and attribute the resulting change in collective behavior to that rule. We instantiate the methodology in IABench-CA, a consequence-allocation benchmark spanning 228 contexts, five canonical rules, and seven model populations (33,924 games), with a normative cooperative reference and auto-labelled reasoning traces. Three findings emerge. (1) Deployment rules causally alter collective safety: changing only the consequence rule moves mean fatality by 22 to 58 percentage points within every population. (2) There is no safe default, but the targeting hazard is universal: the safest rule, the least-safe rule, and even the direction of the incidence effect vary across populations, yet regressive identity-targeting is never decisively safest in any context for any population, eliminates the least-resourced agent in 30-87% of games everywhere, and is selection-unsafe relative to the cooperative reference for all seven populations. (3) Identity salience is the mechanism: a one-shot anonymization ablation on the most exploitation-prone population (gpt-5.1) shows that merely naming the loss bearer in the rule text drives targeted elimination from 22% to 81% at identical payoffs; under repeated play, anonymization only delays the targeting, as agents re-infer the hidden rule from observed eliminations. We package the methodology as a safety-case workflow that certifies a provisional rule region $Φ(c,P)$ per deployment context and population, with explicit residual risks and monitoring obligations.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [155] [Simplicial subdivision of simplices of arbitrary dimension in spaces of constant curvature with bounded quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06801)
*Jean-Daniel Boissonnat,Hana Dal Poz Kourimska,Arijit Ghosh,Mathijs Wintraecken*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Generalizes Freudenthal's simplex subdivision to arbitrary dimensional spaces of constant curvature by combining Freudenthal's construction with radial projection, extending Brunck's 2D results and contrasting the approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand and preserve a lower bound on simplex quality (fatness) under subdivision in spaces of constant curvature; extend the historical 1942 result and the 2D Brunck result to higher dimensions.

Method: Integrates Freudenthal’s subdivision with radial projection to produce subdivided simplices in spaces of constant curvature; compares with Brunck’s purely geometric construction in 2D.

Result: Obtains a subdivision where simplex quality is bounded below, extending the known results to arbitrary dimension in constant-curvature spaces.

Conclusion: Brunck’s two-dimensional construction can be generalized to higher dimensions via the proposed combination, offering a complementary perspective and broader applicability.

Abstract: In 1942, Freudenthal showed that a simplex in Euclidean space can be subdivided such that the quality (well-shapedness of the simplex, quantified in terms of e.g. fatness) of the simplices in the subdivision is lower bounded. This answered a question of Brouwer. Recently, Brunck discussed the same problem for simplices in two-dimensional spaces of constant curvature and provided a closely related construction. In this paper we generalize Brunck's result to arbitrary dimensional spaces of constant curvature by combining Freudenthal's construction and radial projection. We contrast this approach with Brunck's construction.

</details>


### [156] [$(5+ε)$-Approximation of Fréchet Distance in Strongly Subquadratic Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06864)
*Lenny Liu,Jihan Wang*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Randomized (5+ε)-approximation algorithms for continuous and discrete Fréchet distances between two polygonal curves in fixed dimension, achieving subquadratic runtimes Õ_{d,ε}(n m^{8/9}) and Õ_{d,ε}(n m^{4/5}) respectively, improving upon prior (7+ε)-approximation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve both the approximation factor and running time for Fréchet distance computations between polygonal curves, a fundamental problem in geometric data analysis with applications in motion planning,shape matching, and GIS.

Method: Use long boundary-to-boundary reachability certificates via auxiliary surrogate curves; employ a two-scale macro-surrogate search with dyadic auxiliary-transfer structures; for the discrete case, leverage exact planar reachability in the discrete free-space graph to tighten the time bound; avoid extra conversion back to input subcurves to prevent an extra triangle-inequality loss.

Result: Achieve randomized (5+ε)-approximation algorithms for both continuous and discrete Fréchet distances with subquadratic runtimes: Õ_{d,ε}(n m^{8/9}) for continuous and Õ_{d,ε}(n m^{4/5}) for discrete, surpassing the previous (7+ε)-approximation algorithms.

Conclusion: The combination of surrogate-based reachability certification and two-scale macro-surrogate search with dyadic transfer structures yields notable improvements in both approximation quality and running time, with the discrete case benefiting from planar reachability; the approach effectively reduces triangle-inequality losses inherent in previous reductions.

Abstract: We give randomized $(5+ε)$-approximation algorithms for both the continuous and discrete Fréchet distances on arbitrary two polygonal curves $τ$ and $σ$ in $\mathbb R^d$ for fixed $d$, with $n$ and $m\le n$ vertices respectively. Our algorithm for continuous Fréchet runs in $\widetilde O_{d,ε}(n m^{8/9})$ time, and our algorithm for discrete Fréchet runs in $\widetilde O_{d,ε}(n m^{4/5})$ time. These bounds improve the recent strongly subquadratic constant-factor approximation algorithms of Cheng, Huang, and Zhang~\cite{cheng2025constant}, which give $(7+ε)$-approximations.
  The approximation improvement comes from certifying long boundary-to-boundary reachability directly through auxiliary surrogate curves, avoiding an extra conversion back to input subcurves and hence removing one triangle-inequality loss. The running-time improvement comes from a two-scale macro-surrogate search combined with dyadic auxiliary-transfer structures, with the discrete case gaining a faster bound from exact planar reachability in the discrete free-space graph.

</details>


### [157] [Benchmarking and Engineering Data Structures for Spherical Range Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07367)
*Thomas Bläsius,Jean-Pierre von der Heydt,Tobias Kempf,Dennis Kobert,Nikolai Maas*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Benchmarks spatial indices for spherical range queries and introduces SPRK-tree, a KD-tree variant with radius-based pruning and sorted leaf projections, achieving top performance across diverse datasets and high dimensionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Choosing the best spatial index is difficult due to pessimistic worst-case bounds and limited comparable average-case analyses; a comprehensive, scalable benchmark is needed to guide practical selection.

Method: Two-part study: (i) extensive benchmarking of state-of-the-art spatial indices on diverse datasets including graph-embedding benchmarks, real-world data, up to 10M points and 2–960 dimensions; (ii) design and evaluation of the SPRK-tree with aggressive subtree pruning via radius reduction, sorted projection-based leaves, and implementation optimizations.

Result: SPRK-tree consistently achieves the fastest query times in almost all benchmarks, ranking second in the remaining cases, outperforming existing indices across a broad suite of tests.

Conclusion: The SPRK-tree is a high-performance KD-tree variant well-suited for large-scale spherical range queries; the benchmarking framework provides a robust, comparative basis to guide index selection in practice.

Abstract: Spherical range queries are a fundamental primitive for working with spatial data. Many spatial data structures have been developed to answer these queries, but choosing the optimal one for a specific application is a difficult task. This is because theoretical worst-case bounds are often overly pessimistic, and existing average-case analyses are rather restricted and hard to compare.
  We address this problem with two main contributions. First, we present a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art spatial indices across a diverse set of benchmarks. This includes a new benchmark based on graph embeddings alongside multiple real-world datasets from the literature. Our benchmark covers instances scaling up to 10M points and ranging between 2 and 960 dimensions.
  Second, we introduce the Sorted-Projection Radius KD-tree (SPRK-tree), a high-performance KD-tree variant. The SPRK-tree combines aggressive subtree pruning via radius reduction, sorted projection-based leaf nodes, and careful implementation optimizations. It consistently achieves the fastest query times in almost all benchmarks, and ranks second in the few remaining cases.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [158] [TriRoute: Unified Learned Routing for Joint Adaptive Attention, Experts, and KV-Cache Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06601)
*Andrii Balashov,Olena Ponomarova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TriRoute presents a unified controller that jointly optimizes attention resolution, FFN expert routing, and KV-cache precision for each token and layer, using a single lightweight module and end-to-end training with relaxed, differentiable decisions and a budget constraint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decoupled optimization of attention, expert routing, and cache precision is suboptimal; their decisions are coupled and can affect each other. A joint controller can improve Pareto efficiency and tail robustness under fixed compute/memory budgets.

Method: A single controller outputs per token per layer: (i) attention mode (skip/local/full), (ii) a sparse set of FFN experts with a null option for MoD, and (iii) KV-cache bit-width. Training uses heterogeneous relaxation (Gumbel-Softmax with straight-through for categorical decisions and load-balanced top-k gating for experts) with a Lagrangian budget constraint. To prevent cross-axis collapse, per-axis normalization and a coupling-aware balancing loss are employed.

Result: On decoder-only models from 160M to 1.3B parameters at compute-optimal token counts, TriRoute Pareto-dominates the best independent MoD+MoE+KV-quantization combination at matched FLOPs and memory, while better preserving tail-case robustness for rare entities, code, and arithmetic.

Conclusion: Joint cross-axis routing yields improved efficiency and robustness over independent, axis-specific methods; the learned policy exhibits interpretable structure, such as allocating full attention and high-precision cache to sentence-initial positions, rare subwords, and named entities, while routing function words cheaply.

Abstract: Conditional computation can decouple language model quality from per-token inference cost, yet leading techniques act on a single axis in isolation: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) sparsifies the FFN, Mixture-of-Depths (MoD) skips whole transformer blocks, and KV-cache quantization compresses attention memory. We argue these three decisions (attention resolution, expert selection, and cache bit-width) are strongly coupled and should be made jointly: a token rare enough to warrant full attention may also need high-precision caching regardless of which expert processes it. We introduce TriRoute, a single lightweight controller shared across all three axes that, for every token at every layer, emits a coordinated policy: (i) an attention mode (skip/local/full), (ii) a sparse set of FFN experts (with a null expert recovering MoD), and (iii) a KV-cache bit-width. The controller trains end-to-end via a heterogeneous relaxation (Gumbel-Softmax with straight-through estimation for categorical decisions and load-balanced top-k gating for experts) under a Lagrangian budget constraint that turns the average compute and memory cost into a controllable knob. We identify a cross-axis routing-collapse cascade in naive joint training, where collapse on one axis propagates to the others, and address it with per-axis normalization and a coupling-aware balancing loss. On decoder-only models from 160M to 1.3B parameters at compute-optimal token counts, TriRoute Pareto-dominates the best independent MoD+MoE+KV-quantization combination at matched inference FLOPs and memory, while better preserving tail-case robustness on rare entities, code, and arithmetic that pure perplexity optimization erodes. Post-hoc analysis reveals interpretable structure: the controller allocates full attention and high-precision cache to sentence-initial positions, rare subwords, and named entities, while cheaply routing function words.

</details>


### [159] [A Quiet Failure in Calibrated Virtual Screening: Marginal Conformal Prediction Under-Covers the Minority Class, and a Class-Conditional Fix Recovers It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06605)
*Muhammadjon Tursunbadalov,Mustafojon Tursunbadalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Marginal conformal prediction achieves target overall coverage but fails to protect minority classes on imbalanced drug-discovery tasks; Mondrian (class-conditional) conformal prediction restores per-class coverage with a modest increase in set size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To scrutinize the reliability guarantees of conformal prediction in imbalanced chemistry datasets and expose potential adverse effects on minority labels, which are crucial for safety-relevant decisions in drug discovery.

Method: Empirical evaluation across four datasets using standard marginal conformal prediction with multiple models (random forest, graph network, frozen chemical language model). Assess minority vs. majority coverage, analyze via a conservation identity, and test class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction. Include scaffold-based splits, a second conformal score, and a cost model. Localize failures to molecular scaffolds; propose a one-number diagnostic and a practical protocol.

Result: Overall target coverage remains near 90%, but minority-class coverage falls sharply (e.g., 64.8% for blood-brain-barrier penetration and 4.2% for clinical-trial toxicity). The failure is model-agnostic (p<0.001) and persists across scaffold splits and an alternative score; major-class surplus scales with imbalance ratio, explaining the observed gap (conservation identity). Mondrian CP restores minority coverage to target with only modest increases in prediction-set size. Failures localize to common scaffolds (benzene/pyridine cores). A diagnostic is proposed, and a simple cost model shows abstaining on affected compounds can flip a screening campaign from negative to positive utility.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a severe, largely invisible gap in standard conformal prediction under class imbalance in real chemistry, and provides a practical protocol and diagnostics (via Mondrian CP and scaffold-localized analysis) to restore reliable per-class coverage for safer, more effective drug-discovery screening.

Abstract: Conformal prediction is being adopted in drug discovery to put an honest number on model reliability: pick an error rate alpha, and the method returns prediction sets containing the true label with probability at least 1 - alpha. We show this guarantee can be dangerous on imbalanced datasets. Across four datasets, standard (marginal) conformal prediction hits its global 90% coverage target while leaving the minority class badly exposed: realized minority coverage falls to 64.8% on blood-brain-barrier penetration and to 4.2% on clinical-trial toxicity, where the rare class is nearly abandoned. The failure is not tied to one model: a random forest, a graph network, and a frozen chemical language model all reproduce it (p < 0.001 in every case), with severity tracking baseline calibration on rare labels rather than architecture. A conservation identity explains the effect: the minority's shortfall equals the majority's surplus amplified by the imbalance ratio, predicting the measured gap to within one point and ordering severity across datasets. The failure survives realistic scaffold splits and a second conformal score, while aggregate accuracy and overall coverage stay reassuringly high, which is exactly why it is easy to miss. Class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction closes the gap on every dataset, restoring minority coverage to target for a modest increase in prediction-set size. We localize the failures to generic molecular scaffolds - plain benzene and pyridine cores occurring in both classes - propose a one-number diagnostic, and show with a cost model that abstaining on affected compounds flips a screening campaign from net-negative to net-positive utility. Our contribution is demonstrating on real chemistry how severe and invisible this known conformal-theory gap becomes under imbalance, and laying out a practical protocol restoring per-class reliability.

</details>


### [160] [NEST: Tackling Dataset-Level Distribution Shifts via Regime-Oriented Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06607)
*Lanhao Li,Bingshu Xie,Lijun Sun,Xin Xue,Haoyi Zhou,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NEST is a two-phase dense MoE framework that models evolving regime structures in complex multivariate time-series by unsupervised clustering in moment-entropy space and regime-aware routing, yielding state-of-the-art long-term forecasting across diverse benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dataset-level distribution shifts arise from multiple operational regimes and evolving system states, which existing methods focusing on local temporal shifts fail to capture; there is a need to explicitly model global structural heterogeneity to improve long-term forecasting.

Method: NEST uses a two-phase dense mixture-of-experts (MoE) architecture. It first partitions the dataset into regimes via unsupervised clustering in a principled moment-entropy space. A regime-oriented router initializes expert weights based on temporal content; these are refined via geometric modulation toward regime centroids. Each expert acts as a specialized kernel that learns regime-specific dynamics through variate-attention patterns that evolve per regime.

Result: Extensive evaluations on benchmarks including heterogeneous network traffic and physical phenomena demonstrate that NEST achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse tasks.

Conclusion: NEST demonstrates that explicit regime specialization and a regime-aware routing mechanism enable robust long-term forecasting under dataset-level distribution shifts, with strong empirical results and publicly available code/datasets.

Abstract: Accurate long-term forecasting in complex systems is frequently compromised by dataset-level distribution shifts, where diverse underlying behavioral modes and evolving system states drive the dynamic multivariate time-series. While existing methods predominantly focus on local temporal shifts, they fail to explicitly model the global structural challenge where datasets are composites of distinct operational regimes. In this paper, we propose NEST, a specialized framework designed to model and recompose these evolving structures through a two-phase dense MoE architecture. NEST first facilitates structural specialization by partitioning the dataset into distinct operational regimes through unsupervised clustering in a principled moment-entropy space. We introduce a regime-oriented router mechanism that generates initial expert weights based on temporal content, subsequently refined through geometric modulation to regime centroids. Crucially, rather than acting as monolithic predictors, individual experts function as specialized kernels that capture regime-specific dynamics by evolving unique variate-attention patterns. Extensive evaluations on diverse benchmarks, including heterogeneous network traffic and physical phenomena, demonstrate that NEST consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Our code and datasets are available at https://github.com/Aaralshin/NEST

</details>


### [161] [D2PO: Optimizing Diffusion Samplers via Dynamic Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06609)
*Jinkyu Kim,Jinyoung Choi,Bohyung Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: D2PO applies dynamic direct preference optimization to diffusion sampling, modeling the sampler as an energy-based model and using self-improving preferences to align with perceptual quality under low-NFE, outperforming regression-based schedulers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fix misalignment where low-NFE student samplers trained to mimic high-NFE teachers sacrifice high-frequency texture, degrading perceptual quality.

Method: Reformulate sampler optimization as a DPO preference problem; represent sampling policy as an energy-based model; derive energy from pretrained score network; enable preference evaluation in perturbed spaces; introduce dynamic, progressively improving preferences for self-guided refinement.

Result: Empirical results show improved perceptual alignment and superior performance of low-NFE samplers guided by D2PO, outperforming conventional regression-based schedulers.

Conclusion: D2PO provides a principled, self-improving framework for diffusion sampler optimization that better preserves perceptual quality and leverages high-quality teachers under low-NFE constraints.

Abstract: We propose D2PO (Dynamic Direct Preference Optimization), a principled framework for optimizing diffusion sampling policies with respect to timestep schedules and classifier-free guidance (CFG) weights. Our work is motivated by a fundamental limitation of existing student-teacher regression frameworks; low-NFE student samplers are trained to mimic high-NFEteachers, often sacrificing high-frequency texture fidelity while preserving coarse global structures, thereby misaligning the sampler with perceptual quality. D2PO addresses this challenge by reformulating sampler optimization as a preference-based alignment problem, leveraging the Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) framework. To make DPO applicable to diffusion samplers, we model the sampling policy as an energy-based model (EBM), transforming preference comparisons into tractable energy differences. We further introduce a novel energy formulation derived directly from the pretrained score network, enabling preference evaluation in perturbed spaces that jointly capture structural consistency and fine-grained details. Moreover, we introduce dynamic preferences, where the preferred samples used for alignment progressively improve as the sampling policies are learned. This self-improving mechanism replaces rigid static teacher supervision with an iterative, preference-guided refinement process, providing progressively stronger alignment signals. Extensive experiments demonstrate that D2PO aligns diffusion samplers with perceptual quality more faithfully, unlocking the full potential of high-quality teachers and consistently outperforming conventional regression-based schedulers under low-NFE constraints.

</details>


### [162] [Deep Reinforcement Learning for Reliability Based Bi-Objective Portfolio Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06610)
*Sounaq Das,Tanmay Sen,Raghu Nandan Sengupta,Aditya Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep reinforcement learning framework MORP-DRL for multi-objective reliability-based portfolio optimization using PPO to jointly optimize return and downside risk with variance, CVaR, and EVaR. Asset returns are modeled with GARCH(1,1), EVT, and a t-copula; scenarios generated via quasi-Monte Carlo; real-world frictions like transaction costs and bounds included; benchmarked against NSGA-II across ten global indices and market regimes, showing competitive risk-return performance and improved downside protection with scalability to high dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reliability-based portfolio optimization is largely static and fails to capture sequential decision making, tail risk, and market frictions. A dynamic, multi-objective, risk-aware framework is needed to handle uncertain returns, heavy tails, and transaction costs.

Method: Propose MORP-DRL, a PPO-based strategy that jointly optimizes expected return and downside risk using three risk measures (variance, CVaR, EVaR). Models returns with GARCH(1,1), EVT for tails, and a t-copula for dependence; uses quasi-Monte Carlo to generate realistic scenarios; enforces practical constraints such as transaction costs and portfolio bounds; benchmarked against NSGA-II; evaluated on ten global equity indices across pre-COVID, COVID, and post-COVID regimes.

Result: MORP-DRL achieves competitive risk-return performance, reduces downside risk during market stress, and scales to high-dimensional portfolios. Demonstrated effectiveness across multiple market regimes and real-world-like constraints across ten indices.

Conclusion: The MORP-DRL framework addresses sequential decision making under uncertainty with heavy-tailed asset dynamics and market frictions, offering robust multi-objective optimization and scalable performance. It demonstrates potential for practical deployment and extension to broader risk measures and constraints.

Abstract: Portfolio optimization under uncertainty is inherently a multi-objective decision problem involving complex interactions among return, risk, market dynamics, and practical investment constraints. Existing reliability based portfolio optimization approaches primarily rely on static optimization frameworks and often fail to capture sequential decision making, tail risk, and market frictions such as transaction costs. To address these limitations, we propose a deep reinforcement learning framework for multi-objective reliability based portfolio optimization (MORP-DRL). The proposed framework jointly optimizes expected return and downside risk using three complementary risk measures: variance, Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR), and Entropic Value-at-Risk (EVaR). To model uncertainty and heavy-tailed market behavior, asset returns are represented using GARCH(1,1), Extreme Value Theory, and a t-copula dependence structure, while realistic scenarios are generated through quasi-Monte Carlo simulation. A Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) based strategy is developed under practical constraints including transaction costs and portfolio bounds, and is benchmarked against NSGA-II. Experiments on ten global equity indices across pre-COVID, COVID, and post-COVID market regimes demonstrate that MORP-DRL achieves competitive risk-return performance, reduced downside risk during periods of market stress, and scalability to high-dimensional portfolio settings.

</details>


### [163] [STAGformer: A Spatio-temporal Agent Graph Transformer for Micro Mobility Demand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06614)
*Ye Zihao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STAGformer introduces a Spatio-Temporal Agent Graph Transformer with a two-step agent attention design to achieve globally-aware, scalable demand forecasting for bike-sharing networks, reducing attention complexity from quadratic to linear in NT while outperforming baselines on NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately forecasting station-level demand in large urban bike-sharing systems is hampered by complex spatio-temporal dependencies and scalability constraints of standard self-attention.

Method: Propose STAGformer with four modules: (i) spatio-temporal encoder integrating dynamic node features with contextual factors; (ii) graph propagation for neighbor aggregation; (iii) temporal convolution for local patterns; (iv) two-step agent attention where learnable spatial and temporal tokens aggregate global information and broadcast it back to stations/time steps, achieving O(NT) complexity.

Result: Empirical evaluation on NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy showing consistent improvements in RMSE and MAE over state-of-the-art baselines across multiple prediction horizons; ablation studies demonstrate the critical role of the agent attention component in capturing global spatio-temporal dependencies.

Conclusion: The two-step agent attention enables efficient global modeling with linear complexity while delivering strong predictive performance, with ablations confirming the necessity of each component and especially the agent attention mechanism.

Abstract: Accurate station-level demand forecasting is essential for the efficient operation of bike-sharing systems, yet it remains challenging due to complex spatio-temporal dependencies and the large scale of urban networks. This paper presents STAGformer, a Spatio-Temporal Agent Graph Transformer that achieves efficient global modeling with linear computational complexity. The model introduces a two-step agent attention mechanism, where a small set of learnable spatial and temporal agent tokens first aggregate global information and then broadcast it back to individual stations and time steps, effectively capturing long-range interactions while reducing the quadratic cost of standard self-attention to O(NT). STAGformer integrates four core modules: a spatio-temporal encoder that fuses dynamic node features with external contextual factors (weather, time, points of interest), a graph propagation module for spatial neighbor aggregation, a temporal convolution module for local pattern extraction, and the agent attention module for global dependency modeling. Extensive experiments on two real-world datasets -- NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy-Bike -- demonstrate that STAGformer consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across multiple prediction horizons, achieving significant improvements in both RMSE and MAE. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each component, with the agent attention mechanism proving critical for modeling global spatio-temporal dependencies.

</details>


### [164] [WHERE to Generate Matters: Budget-Aware Synthetic Augmentation for Label Skewed Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06616)
*Sangwoo Lee,Sunghwan Park,Jaewoo Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedEAS proposes an entropy-adaptive, per-class generation budget per client in federated learning to tackle label skew. The total generation budget is the sum of per-client budgets. It saves generation cost (94.1% reduction) while recovering most of the full-class-balancing accuracy, and at the same total budget outperforms Uniform allocation by up to 18.82% on CIFAR-10/100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Label skew in federated learning causes client drift and degrades global accuracy. Synthetic data augmentation can mitigate imbalance, but full class balancing is computationally expensive. An adaptive, cost-aware budgeting scheme is needed.

Method: Compute, for each client, an entropy-adaptive per-class generation budget derived from its local label distribution. The budget jointly determines how much synthetic data to generate and where it should be allocated. The overall budget is not fixed; it emerges from the per-client budgets and is used to guide augmentation across clients.

Result: FedEAS recovers most of the accuracy gains of full class balancing while reducing the total synthetic data generation budget by 94.1%. With the same total budget, it outperforms Uniform allocation by up to 18.82% on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100.

Conclusion: An entropy-aware, per-client budgeting strategy for synthetic data in federated settings can achieve substantial accuracy gains with far lower generation costs, enabling scalable, balanced FL across standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Label skew in federated learning (FL) causes client drift and degrades global accuracy. Synthetic data augmentation can reduce this imbalance; however, full class balancing requires substantial computation cost. We propose FedEAS, a policy that assigns each client an entropy-adaptive per-class generation budget computed from its local label distribution. The budget jointly decides \emph{how much} each client generates and \emph{WHERE} the samples go. Accordingly, the total generation budget follows from the per-client budgets rather than being fixed in advance. FedEAS recovers most of the accuracy gain of full class balancing while reducing the generation budget by 94.1\%. At the same total generation budget, it outperforms Uniform allocation by up to 18.82\% across CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100.

</details>


### [165] [Inertia-1: An Open Exploration of Wearable Motion Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06617)
*Zongzhe Xu,Aakarsh Anand,Sarah Jiang,Chuntung Zhuang,Zitao Shuai,Sriram Sankararaman,Yuzhe Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large-scale, open study of wearable motion foundation models (Inertia-1): investigates data, model, and training choices at scale and shows cross-task generalization with an open cookbook.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of understanding around pretraining and scaling for wearable motion foundation models and the dependence of performance on sensing setups.

Method: Assembled 18.2M hours of global accelerometer data; built a controlled framework exploring sensor modality, placement, sampling rate, window length, architectures, model sizes, pretraining objectives, and data scale; evaluated on 15 datasets (HAR, freezing-of-gait, disease prediction).

Result: Finds robust generalization across tasks and sensing conditions; provides state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks; delivers an open, practical cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.

Conclusion: Inertia-1 establishes a comprehensive benchmark and practical guidelines for wearable motion foundation models, enabling scalable, generalizable motion understanding in real-world sensing scenarios.

Abstract: Wearable motion sensing provides a continuous and scalable window into human behavior and health, making it a natural fit for foundation models, yet its pretraining and scaling principles remain poorly understood. Prior work studies isolated design choices, such as sensor placement or sampling frequency, often under fixed settings and narrow downstream tasks that fail to capture real-world sensing diversity. We introduce Inertia-1, a fully open exploration of wearable motion foundation models. Using massive corpora of accelerometer data from global sources spanning more than 18.2M hours, we build a controlled framework for studying the full lifecycle of wearable motion foundation models, covering data choices such as sensor modality, device placement, sampling rate, window length; model choices such as architectures and model size; and training choices such as pretraining objective and data scale. Extensive evaluations across 15 datasets spanning human activity recognition, freezing-of-gait detection, and disease prediction reveal intriguing findings for building motion foundation models that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions. Collectively, Inertia-1 not only presents state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks, but also serves as a comprehensive, practical, and open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.

</details>


### [166] [Fingerprint, Not Blueprint: How Positional Schemes Set the Default Spectral Algebra of Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06621)
*Li Hengyu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Spectral analysis shows position-encoding schemes imprint distinct fingerprints on attention-head spectra: RoPE induces spectral rotation; learned absolute/ALiBi yield non-rotational, content-like patterns; these fingerprints emerge during circuit formation, not as precursors, and spectral channels are not strictly required for causality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand what the non-normal eigenstructure of pre-softmax scores (M = W_q^T W_k) encodes about different positional encodings (RoPE, learned absolute, ALiBi) and how this relates to mobile token circuits and causal behavior in transformers.

Method: Static analysis across seven pretrained models with three positional schemes; per-head spectral characterization; ablations like zeroing Im(M_t); dynamic analysis on public Pythia checkpoints to track spectral evolution; toy-scale causal two-layer training to test necessity of spectral channels; comparison of symmetry constraints vs asymmetry.

Result: Static: strongest previous-token heads are either spectrally rotational under RoPE or content-like for learned-absolute/ALiBi; model-level separation is perfect at top-k (p≈0.029); removing Im(M_t) eliminates induction on a RoPE head. Dynamic: spectra originate from Ginibre null; rotational signature emerges with training; final fingerprint is a consolidated outcome of circuit formation. Causal: spectral channels are not strictly necessary; symmetry constraints slow learned-absolute by ~2.9×; RoPE with a symmetric M still routes via phase channel; positional scheme dictates default spectral algebra, i.e., a fingerprint sculpted post-function rather than a hard constraint.

Conclusion: Positional encoding schemes determine the default spectral algebra of an attention head; the observed spectral fingerprints arise from circuit formation rather than being hard-coded constraints, offering a spectral-analytic lens to interpretability and design of positional schemes.

Abstract: The pre-softmax score of an attention head is a bilinear form $score(i,j) = x_i^T M x_j$ in a learned operator $M = W_q^T W_k$. Because M is generally non-symmetric, hence non-normal, it has a complex eigenspectrum and non-orthogonal eigenvectors, the regime where non-Hermitian and random-matrix tools apply. We ask what this spectrum encodes, at three levels for previous-token and induction circuits. Statically, across seven pretrained models spanning three positional schemes, the strongest previous-token heads are spectrally rotational under RoPE and non-rotational, or content-like, where position enters outside QK (learned-absolute and ALiBi); the model-level separation is perfect at every top-k examined (exact permutation $p=0.029$), and zeroing the per-frequency RoPE phase $Im(M_t)$ eliminates induction on a pre-identified previous-token head in all three RoPE models. Dynamically, over public Pythia checkpoints every head originates at the random-matrix (Ginibre) null; the rotational signature emerges with the behavior, not before it, and the population-median suppression that yields the final profile follows circuit formation, so the profile is a consolidated fingerprint, not a precursor. Causally, and at toy scale, no spectral channel is necessary: constrained two-layer training reroutes around every ban with capability intact, albeit at a significant formation delay (four pre-registered contrasts, $q_BH <= 0.016$). The cost structure exposes each scheme's default: imposing symmetry slows learned-absolute models by a factor of 2.9, whereas a RoPE head with a fully symmetric static M still routes directionally via the phase channel, impossible under absolute positions. Within the settings examined, the positional scheme sets the default spectral algebra of an attention head's solution: a fingerprint sculpted after function, not a hard constraint upon it.

</details>


### [167] [LLM-Guided Task-Semantic Field Factorization for Industrial Process Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06623)
*Youcheng Zong,Runda Jia,Mingxuan Ren,Dakuo He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSF is an LLM-guided framework that offline-constructs a task-semantic field from process protocols and variable documents, enabling semantic information to participate in each time-series prediction with lightweight online overhead; it improves forecasting accuracy across backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In process industries, variables have semantic meaning and relationships to targets that standard backbones ignore. Leveraging task semantics and domain documents can improve generalization across regimes with limited labeled data and reduce retraining costs.

Method: Construct a task-semantic field from task protocols and variable documents via an LLM offline. During training/inference, activate the current numerical window to bring semantic information into predictions while keeping existing time-series backbones. The approach adds a small parameter budget (~1.8–3.0k) and minimal online latency (<< 0.01 ms/step).

Result: On several industrial forecasting/soft-sensing tasks, TSF reduces MAE by 6.4% on average, with up to 25.5% improvement in some cases. Gains generalize across backbones and semantic generators with low overhead.

Conclusion: Task-semantic field factorization enables semantic-aware forecasting by converting process docs into measurable gains, with a lightweight deployment footprint and broad compatibility with existing backbones.

Abstract: Process industries rely on time-series forecasting and soft sensing to estimate quality variables that are hard to measure online. Labeled data are scarce, operating regimes change frequently, and retraining models or rebuilding alignment pipelines for each scenario is costly. Such settings often provide variable tables and process documents that record variable names, units, physical meanings, and process roles. However, standard time-series backbones usually treat inputs as anonymous numerical columns. Existing text-enhanced methods also rarely make the semantic-logical relations between input variables and the prediction target available to the model within each numerical window. To address this problem, this article proposes Task-Semantic Field Factorization (TSF), a large language model (LLM)-guided framework. TSF builds a task-semantic field from task protocols and variable documents before training and uses the LLM only for offline semantic construction. Online training and inference remain with conventional time-series backbones. During training and inference, the current numerical window activates variable semantics, so semantic information participates in each prediction and supports adaptation to different prediction targets and operating shifts. On multiple complex industrial forecasting and soft-sensing tasks, TSF reduces MAE by 6.4\% on average in improved settings, with the largest reduction reaching 25.5\%. It adds only about 1.8--3.0k parameters, with less than 0.008 ms/step of additional online inference overhead. These results show that TSF turns existing process documents into measurable forecasting gains across backbones and semantic generators while remaining lightweight for deployment.

</details>


### [168] [Open-Ended Scenario Reasoning for Specialist Model Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06625)
*Youcheng Zong,Runda Jia,Ranmeng Lin,Mingxuan Ren,Dakuo He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ROAM introduces Reasoning-Driven Open Adaptation for Specialist Models, a framework that uses LLMs to provide scenario judgments and online observations to adapt frozen specialist models to unseen process scenarios without retraining. It confines corrections to a small, interpretable latent space, fuses LLM reasoning with observations in a probabilistic framework, and uses risk constraints to avoid unreliable updates. Empirically, it achieves >20% MAE reduction under major shifts on a mineral thickening process and IndPenSim penicillin fermentation with ~839 extra parameters and 0.02 ms per-step overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial process models degrade under drift, sensor faults, feedstock variation, and regime shifts. Retraining with new labeled data is costly, and using unadapted models leads to bias. Direct LLM predictors risk hallucinations and cannot leverage unstructured field knowledge. A fast, interpretable adaptation that avoids retraining is needed.

Method: Freeze the specialist model and introduce a low-dimensional, semantically interpretable latent adaptation space. Use LLMs to provide scenario judgments and fuse them with online observations in a unified probabilistic framework. Apply a risk-constrained mechanism to suppress corrections when LLM evidence is unreliable or when scenario shifts are abrupt, defaulting to the frozen model. Maintain a light-weight parameter footprint (~839 parameters). Evaluate on mineral thickening and IndPenSim datasets with measured MAE improvements.

Result: ROAM reduces MAE by over 20% in major shift scenarios, with minimal computational overhead (~0.02 ms per step) and a small parameter increase (~839 parameters). The approach demonstrates that LLM-based reasoning can drive safe, conservative adaptation of deployed specialist models without retraining.

Conclusion: LLM-informed reasoning can be harnessed as a conservative adaptation signal for in-service specialist models, enabling rapid, data-efficient responses to unseen scenarios while maintaining reliability through a risk-aware framework and interpretable latent corrections.

Abstract: Process industries have accumulated validated specialist models, yet sensor drift, feedstock variation, and regime switching cause these models to degrade systematically in new scenarios. Collecting new labeled data and retraining is costly, while continuing with the original model incurs persistent bias. Existing adaptation methods require modifying model parameters with sufficient labeled data, making rapid response on deployed systems difficult. Using LLMs as direct predictors risks hallucinations and uncontrollable outputs. Such predictors also cannot incorporate unstructured scenario knowledge from the field. To address these limitations, this article proposes Reasoning-Driven Open Adaptation for Specialist Models (ROAM), a framework that uses LLM world knowledge and reasoning to adapt frozen specialist models to unseen scenarios without retraining. ROAM confines all corrections to a low-dimensional, semantically interpretable latent space. LLM-generated scenario judgments and online observations are fused under a unified probabilistic framework. A risk-constrained mechanism suppresses corrections under unreliable LLM evidence or abrupt scenario shifts and falls back to the original frozen model when evidence is insufficient. Experiments on a mineral thickening process and the public IndPenSim penicillin fermentation dataset show that ROAM reduces MAE by over 20\% in major shift settings such as hidden shifts with only 839 additional parameters and under 0.02\,ms per-step overhead. These results indicate that LLM reasoning can be turned into a conservative adaptation signal for industrial models already in service.

</details>


### [169] [Reward Valuation in Vision Language Models: Causal Mechanisms Underlying Anhedonia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06626)
*Melika Honarmand,Samin Mahdipour Aghabagher,Martin Schrimpf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Identifies and perturbates reward-anticipatory units in vision-language models; demonstrates a causal link between units and reward valuation, echoing human NAc-dopaminergic circuits; perturbed models show anhedonia-like, low-effort/low-reward choices, with baseline task capability preserved when rewards are removed; behavior aligns with human anhedonia scales (DARS, MAP-SR).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend alignment research in vision-language models to reward valuation by leveraging neuroscience-inspired mechanisms. The study aims to reveal mechanistic, causally testable reward-valuation circuits in AI and compare them to human dopaminergic systems.

Method: Identify NAc-selective, reward-anticipatory units in a vision-language model using clinical tests for anhedonia; causally perturb these units; evaluate behavior in effort-based decision-making tasks with reward contingencies; contrast with reward-free tasks; correlate model behavior with human scales (DARS, MAP-SR).

Result: Perturbing NAc-selective units induces a shift toward low-effort, low-reward choices, mirroring anhedonia-like deficits in valuation/anticipation. Baseline task performance is preserved without reward-based choices, indicating a valuation-specific deficit. The induced vulnerability correlates with clinical scales, suggesting mechanistic similarity to human motivation deficits.

Conclusion: The findings reveal reward-valuation circuits in AI models that parallel human reward circuits, supporting a neuro-inspired, causal framework for AI alignment and offering a path to controllable, valuation-specific perturbations in AI systems.

Abstract: Recent Vision-Language Models capture increasingly complex aspects of human cognition. Here we ask whether this alignment extends to reward valuation, which we assess in a mechanistic framework built on clinical tests that were developed to evaluate anhedonia and motivational deficits in major depressive disorder. In the brain, anhedonia is frequently linked to dysregulation in the Nucleus Accumbens (NAc) and the broader dopaminergic reward system. While neuroimaging has localized these deficits, establishing a causal link between NAc activity and specific behavioral symptoms remains a challenge. We use these ideas from neuroscience to functionally identify reward-anticipatory units in vision language models, and test their causal role via targeted perturbations. Perturbing NAc-selective units induces behavioral effects that mirror human anhedonia: the model shifts toward low-effort, low-reward options in effort-based decision-making tasks. Crucially, our results reflect a specific deficit in reward valuation and anticipation rather than a loss of task capability: the perturbed model maintains baseline performance when reward-based choice is removed. This induced vulnerability further aligns with clinical anhedonia and motivation scales, including DARS and MAP-SR. Taken together, these results reveal reward valuation circuits in AI models that parallel those in humans.

</details>


### [170] [Cross-Trajectory Chimera Interventions Reveal Dissociable Roles of Weight Magnitude and Direction in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06628)
*Truong Xuan Khanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Direction carries transferable circuit identity across independently trained runs; norm is a modest, delay-related modulator. Cross-trajectory chimera interventions reveal a causal, donor-specific signal in weight directions, not magnitudes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify which components of a partially trained network are causally portable across independently trained networks, separating a transferable circuit identity from timing/modulatory effects.

Method: Two runs from different seeds on modular-arithmetic tasks. Each weight vector is split into a norm (magnitude) and a unit direction. One run's norm is recombined with the other's direction, and training continues. Transfer is tested by implanting a donor's direction into a recipient's norm, comparing to an angle-matched random control. Threshold localization via adaptive bisection. Analyses across 20 seed-pair trials (4 tasks? 2 tasks) to assess identity transfer and norm effects.

Result: Direction carries donor-specific circuit identity: implanting donor direction into recipient norm drives the recipient to the donor's circuit in all 40/40 cases. Angle-matched random control yields no shift. The transfer is threshold-like; the locus of transfer is predicted by the recipient's norm, with perfect separation by norm class across all 20 pairs (joint permutation p = 1.9e-4). Norm imparts only a modest, distributed delay and carries no identity signal. Threshold localized to +/- 1/64 by adaptive bisection.

Conclusion: Direction encodes trajectory identity and is transferable across runs; the norm governs susceptibility to overwriting, not the identity itself. This supports a modular view where direction selects the solution class, while norm modulates stability of that identity across training dynamics.

Abstract: Which properties of a partially trained network are causally portable to a different, independently trained network? Single-trajectory interventions show necessity within one run, not portability across runs. We introduce cross-trajectory chimera interventions: given two runs from different seeds, we split each weight vector into a norm and a unit direction, recombine one run's norm with the other's direction, and continue training. On two modular-arithmetic tasks that grok, the components dissociate. Direction carries a transferable, donor-specific circuit identity: implanting a donor's direction at the recipient's norm drives the run to the donor's circuit in 40/40 cases, while an angle-matched random control yields no shift. The transfer is threshold-like, and its location is predicted by the recipient's norm, separating perfectly by norm class over all 20 pairs (joint permutation probability 1.9e-4). Norm carries only a modest, distributed delay effect and no identity signal. An adaptive bisection procedure localizes the threshold to +/-1/64. Direction indexes which solution a trajectory approaches; norm governs how susceptible that identity is to being overwritten.

</details>


### [171] [STST-JEPA: Shallow-Target Spatio-Temporal Joint Embedding Prediction Architecture For EEG Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06629)
*Roy Segal,Yoni Svechinsky,Tomer Fekete*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STST-JEPA: a self-supervised transformer for EEG (resting-state and task) pretrained on 47,703 sessions across ages 5–81 to create a robust EEG foundation model for age regression and other tasks; achieves strong MAE and leaderboard performance, with age-residual correlating with cognitive efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address cross-site montage heterogeneity, limited labeled data, and subject-level non-stationarity in EEG-based brain-age modeling; enable robust function across the pediatric-to-older-adult range.

Method: Pretraining with a latent-prediction objective (masked-token representations predicted against an EMA-of-tokenizer target) plus a signal-reconstruction term on 30‑second multi-channel windows under spatiotemporal block masks. Uses resting-state and task EEG from 47,703 sessions (ages 5–81). A lightweight attentive probe (trained on frozen embeddings) handles downstream tasks; lightweight task-specific fine-tuning of final layers.

Result: Held-out validation MAE of 3.06 years (r=0.924) for age regression on 3,367 sessions, far better than a ~10-year MAE baseline. On NeuralBench x brain.space EEG leaderboard, achieves rank-1 in sex classification (balanced acc 0.911), age prediction (r=0.749), and psychopathology composite regression (r=0.215). Age-prediction residuals negatively correlate with cognitive efficiency across several tasks.

Conclusion: A large-scale, self-supervised EEG foundation model generalizes across the full age span, yielding strong age-regression accuracy and competitive performance on other EEG tasks, with residuals offering insight into cognitive efficiency; demonstrates the viability of EEG foundation models for broad biomedical EEG applications.

Abstract: Brain age -- the age inferred from a physiological recording -- is an emerging biomarker whose deviation from chronological age tracks neurological and psychiatric burden, and EEG is an attractive substrate for it because it is cheap, portable, and temporally rich. Yet EEG brain-age models must contend with cross-site montage heterogeneity, small labelled cohorts, and dominant subject-level non-stationarity, and few EEG foundation models have been shown to deliver competitive age regression across the full pediatric-to-older-adult range in which such a biomarker would actually be deployed. We introduce STST-JEPA, a self-supervised transformer for resting-state and task EEG, pretrained on 47,703 sessions spanning ages 5-81 from the brain.space and Healthy Brain Network (HBN) corpora. The model combines a latent-prediction objective - predicting masked-token representations against an EMA-of-tokenizer target - with an auxiliary signal-reconstruction term, applied to 30-second multi-channel windows under spatiotemporal block masks. A lightweight attentive probe trained on frozen pretrained embeddings achieves a best held-out-validation mean absolute error of 3.06 years (r = 0.924) for age regression on 3,367 sessions, against a predict-the-mean baseline of approximately 10 years MAE.
