With light task-specific fine-tuning of the model's final layers, the same pretrained encoder achieves rank-1 placements - with the model's native 30-second windows - on the public NeuralBench x brain.space EEG leaderboard for sex classification (balanced accuracy 0.911), age prediction (r = 0.749), and psychopathology composite regression (r = 0.215). We further show that the model's age-prediction residual is negatively correlated with cognitive efficiency over several tasks we examined.

</details>


### [172] [When Certificates Fail: A Unified Safety Framework for Embedded Neural Interface Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06630)
*Jasmeet Singh Bindra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Formal robustness certificates for embedded neural-interface models can pass while task accuracy collapses under attack; a broader verification gap exists between certification and real-world safety, necessitating an empirical safety audit framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between mathematical certificates and user welfare in neural-interface systems; demonstrate that verification alone is insufficient to guarantee safe operation across EEG decoders.

Method: Apply a unified empirical audit framework to BCI datasets (BCI Competition IV 2a, SEED-IV) using multiple EEG decoders (EEGNet, CSP+LDA, FBCSP+LDA) with official session validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests; evaluate verification insufficiency, proxy-fidelity divergence, and latent information exfiltration.

Result: Observed persistent verification gap: certificates remain valid while performance degrades under perturbations (e.g., 25.7% drop at e=0.25 under projected-gradient attack). Proxy-fidelity divergence evidenced by time-domain objective improvements coinciding with worse spectral MSE; latent information exfiltration shown by embeddings re-identifiable (subject identity recoverable at 48.1% vs 6.7% chance). Architecture-independent gap across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, FBCSP+LDA.

Conclusion: Operational safety auditing is necessary in addition to certificate verification for responsible neural-interface deployment; the framework generalizes beyond a single architecture and datasets.

Abstract: Formal robustness certificates for embedded neural-interface models can pass while task accuracy collapses: at perturbation budget e=0.25, EEGNet classification accuracy drops by 25.7% under projected-gradient attack while the Lipschitz-style certificate remains valid for all 9 tested subjects. We argue that this gap between mathematical certification and operational safety is one instance of a broader alignment failure in neural interfaces, where training objectives diverge from user welfare. We propose a unified empirical audit framework organized around three such failures: verification insufficiency, in which certificates pass while task behavior degrades; proxy-fidelity divergence, in which task-optimized representations damage neural signal structure (a time-domain auxiliary objective reduces reconstruction MSE by 0.1132 while worsening spectral log-MSE); and latent information exfiltration, in which public-task embeddings retain private attributes (subject identity recoverable at 48.1% versus 6.7% chance). We instantiate the framework on BCI Competition IV 2a and SEED-IV using multiple deep and classical EEG decoders, official session-level validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests. The verification gap persists across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA, and is therefore architecture-independent. Our results establish that operational safety auditing, not certificate verification alone, is necessary for responsible neural-interface deployment.

</details>


### [173] [Does Demand Response Increase Vulnerability to Cyber Attacks by Adversarial Data Modifications?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06632)
*Clemens Kortmann,Eike Cramer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adversarial manipulation of price forecasts can degrade industrial demand response profits; effectiveness hinges on perturbation orientation and detectability. With constrained perturbations, demand response retains ~90% of its financial advantage; attack impact is driven by perturbation direction, not only magnitude. Attack design should incorporate scheduling model sensitivities for rigorous risk assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in adversarial risk assessment for demand-side energy management by analyzing how distorted price forecasts affect optimization-based production scheduling and economics, complementing grid-focused false data injection studies.

Method: Use a generalized production scheduling model and design adversarial price perturbations to distort forecast inputs. Solve scheduling optimization under distorted prices across varying process flexibility, evaluating profits and sensitivity to perturbation orientation vs magnitude.

Result: Adversarial attacks can reduce profits from demand response; with limited, hard-to-detect perturbations, about 90% of the financial advantage is preserved. The impact depends more on perturbation orientation than magnitude, highlighting the need to consider model sensitivities in attack design.

Conclusion: Attack analyses should explicitly incorporate the sensitivities of scheduling optimization models; develop robust or adversarially aware demand response strategies and detection mechanisms that account for perturbation structure and direction rather than solely magnitude.

Abstract: Adversarial attacks are crafted data manipulations that aim to deteriorate the outcomes of prediction or decision-making algorithms. In the energy systems literature, adversarial attacks have been studied with a focus on problems regarding the electricity grid. Such problems include forecasting and grid state estimation, where adversarial attacks are also known as false data injection attacks. Only few studies have analyzed the potential impact that adversarial attacks have on the demand side. We analyze how manipulated price forecasts impact the decision-making in industrial demand response. To this end, we design adversarial attacks that aim to deteriorate the output of electricity price forecasting models and solve scheduling optimization problems of energy-intensive production processes using the distorted price forecasts. We make use of a generalized process model to investigate the vulnerability to adversarial attacks for a range of production scheduling problems with different levels of process flexibility. We find that adversarial attacks can erode the profits gained from demand response. However, when perturbations are limited in extent (so that they are hard to detect by the human user), demand response preserves about 90\% of its financial advantage compared to steady-state process operation. Further, we find that the impact of adversarial attacks on demand response does not only depend on the magnitude of the perturbations but rather on the orientation of the adversarial perturbations. Therefore, we argue that attack analyses should explicitly incorporate the sensitivities of scheduling optimization models into the attack design to enable more rigorous assessments of decision-making under adversarial attacks.

</details>


### [174] [When Do Geometric Algebra Layers Beat Scalarization? A Controlled Study on SO(3)-Equivariant Vector Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06634)
*Fabien Polly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Geometric algebra Cl(3,0) networks are not a universal shortcut for low-data 3D learning; they offer advantages only for deeply compositional tasks that nest group operations, while in single-stage tasks or rotation-free cases simple scalar-based equivariant baselines perform as well or better.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether the geometric algebra structure itself provides benefits beyond exact equivariance by comparing Cl(3,0) networks to a minimal invariant-dot-product baseline, across both single-stage and compositional 3D tasks, and by exploring data efficiency and ablations.

Method: Construct compact Cl(3,0) networks and a scalarization baseline using invariant dot products with coefficients on the equivariant basis {v_i, v_i x v_j}. Evaluate on: (1) single-stage laws (axis-angle rotation, cross product, central force) and (2) compositional targets involving nested rotations and force-torque propagation. Perform ablations varying network depth, augment baselines with triple-product invariant, more parameters, Vector Neurons/e3nn, and multiplicative coefficient networks. Measure data efficiency (sample counts to reach performance) and extrapolation to invariant magnitudes.

Result: On single-stage tasks, the invariant-scalar baseline matches or outperforms Cl(3,0) with lower training cost. On compositional targets with nested group operations, Cl(3,0) networks excel, achieving similar performance with far fewer samples (100 vs 3000) and maintaining the gap despite stronger baselines. Ablations show depth tracks rotation chain length; scalarization underperforms constant predictors for chains of four rotations. For rotation-free nested cross products, scalarization can outperform Cl(3,0) by ~24x. Across models, extrapolating invariant magnitudes (radius/separation) fails; all models perform worse than a constant predictor when errors are normalized.

Conclusion: Geometric algebra layers are not a universal shortcut for low-data 3D learning. Their advantage appears specifically when the target composition involves deep chaining of group operations; otherwise, simpler equivariant or invariant baselines suffice.

Abstract: Compact networks built from Clifford algebra Cl(3,0) primitives are exactly SO(3)-equivariant and learn synthetic 3D vector laws from few samples. We ask whether the geometric algebra structure itself contributes anything beyond exact equivariance. We compare against a minimal scalarization baseline: invariant dot products fed to a small MLP that outputs coefficients on the equivariant basis {v_i, v_i x v_j}, which is also exactly equivariant. On single-stage laws (rotation by axis-angle, cross product, central force), scalarization matches or beats the Cl(3,0) network at a fraction of the training cost, so the geometric algebra adds nothing there. On compositional targets whose computation graph nests group operations (apply R2 R1 to a point; map a local force through an orientation, then take a torque), the Cl(3,0) network beats scalarization by an order of magnitude in the low-data regime, reaching with 100 samples what the baseline needs 3000 for, and the gap survives strengthening the baseline with the triple-product invariant and 17x more parameters, external Vector Neurons and e3nn baselines, and a multiplicative coefficient network. Ablations show the required network depth tracks the rotation chain length, and scalarization falls below the constant predictor on chains of four rotations. The advantage is not composition per se: on a rotation-free nested cross product, which flattens into polynomial invariant coefficients, scalarization wins by 24x. No tested model, equivariant or not, extrapolates invariant magnitudes: on radius and separation shifts every model is worse than a constant predictor once errors are normalized. We conclude that geometric algebra layers are not a general shortcut for low-data 3D learning, but become useful precisely when the target composes group elements in depth.

</details>


### [175] [Optimized Instance Alteration for Explaining and Assessing Robustness of Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06637)
*Evgenii Kuriabov,David Miller,Jia Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unified optimization framework for diagnosing misclassification and assessing black-box classifier robustness via explainability-aware sparse perturbations and the TOR-Confusion Matrix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need to diagnose misclassifications in black-box models and quantify robustness to tolerance-bounded perturbations with interpretable perturbations.

Method: Optimization over input perturbations using an XA-L0 penalty that yields sparse, interpretable changes together with a classifier loss to steer toward a target label; introduce TOR-Confusion Matrix to model class-to-class transitions under tolerance constraints; validate on image and tabular data.

Result: Demonstrated interpretability of the perturbations and quantified robustness by mapping misclassification pathways and transition probabilities across classes using TOR-Confusion Matrix.

Conclusion: Offers a unified framework for interpretable misclassification analysis and robustness evaluation in black-box classifiers; TOR-Confusion Matrix provides a probabilistic tool to characterize sensitivity to tolerance-bounded perturbations.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a unified approach for diagnosing misclassification and assessing the robustness of black-box classifiers. Central to our method is an optimization framework that modifies an instance so that the classifier predicts a specified target label, while ensuring that the modification remains easily explainable. The objective function contains two components: an explainability-aware $L_0$ (XA-$L_0$) penalty that promotes sparse and interpretable modifications, and a classifier loss objective that steers the perturbed instance toward the desired output. This integrated optimization formulation is used both to identify the underlying causes of misclassification and to evaluate robustness by determining how an instance can change within a tolerance region before being reassigned to another class. To quantify robustness, we introduce the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-Confusion Matrix), which measures a classifier's susceptibility by modeling the class-to-class transition probabilities induced by tolerance-bounded perturbations. We validate the proposed method on both image and tabular datasets, demonstrating its ability to jointly deliver interpretability and robustness assessment.

</details>


### [176] [UASPL: Uncertainty-Aware Self-Paced Learning with Evidential Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06638)
*Yifan Zhang,Yuxin Hu,Zhuobin Hao,Xiaozhuan Gao,Lipeng Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes UASPL: an uncertainty-aware self-paced learning method using evidential neural networks and Subjective Logic to embed predictive uncertainty into sample selection, yielding better performance and interpretability across SPL variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-paced learning relies on loss-based sample ordering, but low-loss samples are not always reliable. Incorporating predictive uncertainty can improve sample selection and interpretability.

Method: Introduce a general loss function within the Subjective Logic framework that (i) leverages evidential neural networks to estimate uncertainty, (ii) couples sample selection preference for interpretability, and (iii) is adaptable to different SPL variants. The approach includes an uncertainty-aware weighting mechanism integrated into SPL.

Result: Experimental results on multiple datasets show that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in classification performance, interpretability, and generality; code is provided.

Conclusion: UASPL effectively integrates predictive uncertainty into SPL via evidential networks and Subjective Logic, offering improved performance and interpretability with broad applicability across SPL variants.

Abstract: Self-paced learning (SPL) is an effective learning paradigm that simulates the human learning process by progressing from easy to difficult samples based on the value of the loss function during the learning process. It has shown great potential in improving model performance and training efficiency. However, the prediction results of samples with smaller loss values are not necessarily reliable, indicating that such samples are not always simple samples for the model. Hence, this article proposes an uncertainty-aware self-paced learning based on evidential neural networks, termed UASPL, which integrates predictive reliability into sample selection through a general loss function within the Subjective Logic framework. This loss function incorporates uncertainty estimation and can be extended to different variants of SPL. Moreover, this loss function couples a sample selection preference, thereby ensuring the interpretability of the sample selection process. Finally, the experimental results on multiple datasets show that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in terms of classification performance, interpretability, and generality. The source code is available at: https://github.com/treelife979/UASPL.

</details>


### [177] [At-Grok Is Not Converged:A Measurement-Validity Audit for Grokking Representation Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06639)
*Truong Xuan Khanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Grokking-related embedding compression persists after generalization; effective rank overstates convergence, especially in MLPs; compression lags the grokking transition by a large margin. LayerNorm shifts the compression dynamics; an audit toolkit separates onset from compression, flags censoring, and excludes boundary cells; results are not universally consistent across MLP and transformer. Code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify the relationship between grokking (generalization) and embedding compression via effective rank, and assess the reliability and generality of such metrics across architectures (MLP vs transformer).

Method: Quantify effective rank at grokking transition for MLP and transformer; perform a one-variable ablation by adding LayerNorm; use a pre-registered control to rule out scale invariance; develop an audit framework to separate onset from compression, flag censoring, exclude boundary/generalizing-incompletely cells, and ensure the reference floor plateau; include an adversarial suite to test robustness; test a secondary MLP-specific depth law with norm budget across architectures; release code and toolkit.

Result: Effective rank at grokking transition overstates converged value by 3–5x for MLPs and by 1.3–1.5x for transformers; in MLP compression erases which cells compress; compression lags the accuracy transition by about the time-to-grok (at least 10,000 steps); LayerNorm moves the fraction of compression driven by the grok step from 0.87 to 0.25; the control rules out scale invariance as the mechanism; the MLP-specific depth-law linking norm budget to converged floor fails to generalize to transformers and flips sign under weight decay; the audit framework separates onset from compression, flags censoring, excludes boundary cells, and validates the plateau; an adversarial suite caught a false-confidence bug; code and toolkit released.

Conclusion: Metrics based on effective rank during grokking must be treated with caution: onset (grokking) and compression are distinct, and normalization choices (e.g., LayerNorm) can dramatically alter compression dynamics. Claims of convergence derived from the grokking-axis compression should be validated with robust audits and cross-architecture checks; reproducibility is supported by the released code and toolkit.

Abstract: On modular arithmetic, a network's embedding keeps compressing for tens of thousands of steps after it has already generalized. Reading effective rank at the grokking transition overstates the converged value by 3-5x on an MLP, and by 1.3-1.5x on a transformer trained to convergence; on the MLP it also erases which cells compress at all. Compression lags the accuracy transition by an amount on the order of the time-to-grok, at least 10,000 steps, rather than coinciding with it. A one-variable ablation shows what sets the lag size: adding LayerNorm to an otherwise identical transformer moves the fraction of compression done by the grok step from 0.87 to 0.25, and a pre-registered control rules out scale invariance as the mechanism. We package this as an audit that separates onset from compression, flags censoring, excludes boundary cells that never fully generalize, and checks that the reference floor has plateaued, with an adversarial suite that caught a false-confidence bug in our own branch. A secondary, MLP-specific depth law linking norm budget to converged floor fails a generality test on a transformer and flips sign under free weight decay. Code and the toolkit are released.

</details>


### [178] [The Rank-One Corner: How Much Value Equivalence Does a Task Need from a World Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06640)
*Donna Vakalis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The dimensionality of the training objective determines how much predictive structure (closure) a latent world model encodes; scalar rewards yield a rank-one projection, while higher-dimensional objectives reveal multiple predictive directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluations (reconstruction or reward prediction) do not reveal which predictive coordinates the model relies on. This work investigates how the objective's dimensionality shapes the latent closure that a DreamerV3 stack can represent.

Method: Controlled experiments in a DreamerV3 stack with known ground-truth closure; vary the objective dimensionality from 1 to 4; use linear probes and auxiliary heads to measure recovered structure; compare scalar vs full objective; perform capacity-matched tests and in-situ pressure checks; also test on a companion closed-loop task with frame-by-frame structure.

Result: Replacing the scalar objective with the full objective increases recoverable structure from R^2 ≈ 0.10 to ≈0.76; the model installs exactly as many predictive directions as the objective dimensionality (1–4) in both an auxiliary head and the model’s value head; the effect is dimensional, not an artifact of head form; in a companion closed-loop task, a scalar objective suffices for reconstruction, but the objective overall governs latent representations when predictive signals are richer.

Conclusion: Value equivalence is fundamentally dimensional: the single-reward objective resides at the rank-one corner, and a model encodes as much of a task’s predictive structure as the dimensionality of the objective it is trained to predict.

Abstract: A learned world model is usually judged by how faithfully it reconstructs its observations or predicts reward, as though quality were something the model simply has or lacks. But what a task actually needs from a model is narrower: the few predictive coordinates its queries depend on, which we call the closure. We show that how much of that closure a latent comes to represent is set not by the model's capacity or its observations but by the dimensionality of the objective it is trained against, and we measure this directly on a DreamerV3 stack in a controlled environment with known ground-truth closure. An aligned scalar value signal -- the objective at the heart of value equivalence -- installs only a one-dimensional projection of a closure that needs several dimensions: read through a single linear probe, the recoverable structure rises from R^2=0.10 to 0.76 as the scalar is replaced by the full objective. Sweeping the objective's dimensionality from one to four installs exactly that many predictive directions through an auxiliary head, and the same staircase appears -- at attenuated magnitude but the same rank -- through the model's own value head, so the dissociation is dimensional rather than an artifact of head form. Capacity-matched comparisons and in-situ pressure checks rule out the obvious alternatives. The law governs a regime, and we measure its boundary: on a companion closed-loop task whose structure is observable frame by frame, reconstruction installs that structure and the scalar objective suffices -- the objective decides what a latent represents exactly where cheaper training signals cannot already recover it. Value equivalence is thus not all-or-nothing but dimensional: the familiar single-reward objective is its rank-one corner, and a model installs as much of a task's structure as the objective it is asked to predict.

</details>


### [179] [The Approximation Ratio for the Risk of Myopic Bayesian Active Learning for Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06642)
*Stephen Mussmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theoretical study establishing a tight approximation ratio for greedy active learning in linear regression, scaling linearly with MILS, and supported by simple simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how well greedy (one-step myopic) data selection performs in Bayesian active learning for linear regression and to identify factors determining its performance.

Method: Provide a first-of-its-kind approximation ratio bound for the greedy algorithm's risk; introduce MILS (Maximum Initial Leverage Score) as a key quantity; prove the bound is tight up to an absolute constant; validate with numerical simulations.

Result: The greedy algorithm's risk is bounded by a constant times MILS; the approximation ratio grows linearly with MILS; MILS is shown to be fundamental and the bound is tight up to a fixed constant; numerical experiments corroborate.

Conclusion: This work characterizes the performance of greedy experimental design in linear regression, highlighting MILS as a core determinant and establishing near-optimality of the greedy approach up to a constant.

Abstract: Active learning studies the fundamental question: what data should we choose to observe? The greedy algorithm in optimal experiment design is a common heuristic and also equivalent to myopic Bayesian active learning for linear regression, the common framework where long-term planning is replaced with the one-step optimal choice. In this work, we prove a first-of-its-kind approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm's risk that is tight up to an absolute constant. The approximation ratio is linear in the maximum initial leverage score (MILS), a newly identified quantity fundamental to the greedy algorithm's performance. Finally, we illustrate the results with simple numerical simulations.

</details>


### [180] [Diffusion enabled Optimal Transport distances for graph matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06646)
*Iman Seyedi,Francesco Archetti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DsrFGW extends semi-relaxed Gromov-Wasserstein by incorporating diffusion-based information propagation, unifying node features and structure for robust graph matching; shows improved accuracy and clustering metrics on synthetic tasks under noise and partial observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate sensitivity to sparsity, noise, and partial observability in graph matching by leveraging diffusion patterns of information flow, following the Graph Diffusion Distance concept.

Method: Introduce Diffusion Semi-Relaxed Fused GW (DsrFGW) that fuses node features and structure via an optimal transport framework with diffusion processes; evaluates on 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy/medium/hard) with adaptive diffusion scales.

Result: DsrFGW consistently outperforms srFGW, achieving 0–20 percentage point accuracy gains and substantial ARI improvements; in medium difficulty, srGW may yield negative ARI while DsrFGW improves both internal (Adjusted Rank Index) and external (Accuracy) clustering metrics; robust to severe noise with diffusion scale adapting to task difficulty (92% of tasks).

Conclusion: DsrFGW provides a robust, diffusion-informed framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty, effectively capturing local and global patterns while reducing sensitivity to noisy or missing edges.

Abstract: This paper introduces Diffusion Semi-Relaxed Fused Gromov-Wasserstein (DsrFGW), a novel method for graph comparison that unifies node features and structural connectivity through optimal transport. While traditional Gromov-Wasserstein and semi-relaxed variants (srGW, srFGW) capture graph structure, they often struggle with sparse, noisy, or partially observed graphs. Inspired by Graph Diffusion Distance, which posits graphs are similar if they enable similar information transmission patterns, DsrFGW incorporates diffusion processes allowing information propagation across nodes, capturing local and global structural patterns while reducing sensitivity to noise or missing edges. An extensive evaluation on 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy, medium, hard) demonstrates consistent superiority over srFGW, achieving accuracy improvements of 0-20 percentage points and dramatic Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) gains: in medium-difficulty scenarios, srFGW often achieves negative ARI (worse than random) while DsrFGW offers better performance in terms of both internal and external clustering quality measures (i.e., Adjusted Rank Index and Accuracy with respect to the true underlying clusters, respectively). Even under severe noise, DsrFGW improves clustering quality in 92% of the synthetic tasks with optimal diffusion scales adapting to problem difficulty, establishing DsrFGW as a robust framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty.

</details>


### [181] [Final Checkpoints Are Not Enough: Analyzing Latent Reasoning Faithfulness Along Training Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06648)
*Hengyu Jin,Shu Yang,Di Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent reasoning methods show faithfulness dynamics across training: at convergence, output-level faithfulness under counterfactual edits can appear similar across paradigms yet follow different trajectories; at the activation level, the causal contribution of latent steps to the final answer tends to decay during training for both paradigms, with the same instances flipping outputs; and the activation trajectory depends on answer format (decays for binary choices, rises for open-ended decoding).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how faithfulness of latent reasoning evolves during training, not just at convergence, by tracking multiple latent reasoning paradigms with verifiable input edits and activation perturbations.

Method: Monitor faithfulness across saved training checkpoints for different latent reasoning paradigms; apply a verifiable counterfactual input edit and a noise-ablation activation patch to the latent steps; assess at both output-level and activation-level across training and for different answer formats.

Result: Output-level: latent reasoning methods can look similarly unfaithful at convergence under counterfactual edits but exhibit qualitatively different trajectories across training. Activation-level: the causal contribution of latent reasoning steps to the final answer decays over training for both paradigms, and the same examples that flip outputs under edits are the ones showing decay. Activation trajectories diverge by answer format, decaying for binary choices and increasing for open-ended decoding.

Conclusion: Faithfulness of latent reasoning is contingent on both training stage and answer format; convergence-based analyses may obscure the dynamics of faithfulness during training.

Abstract: Latent reasoning methods perform multi-step inference entirely in the model's continuous hidden states, promising more compact and efficient reasoning. However, these opaque hidden states raise a question of faithfulness: whether these latent reasoning steps causally drive the final answer. Prior work investigates this question at converged checkpoints and reports several unfaithful behaviors, such as latent reasoning steps that can be replaced without changing the answer, but leaves how these behaviors form during training unexamined. We instead track how faithfulness evolves across saved checkpoints for different latent reasoning paradigms, applying a verifiable counterfactual edit on the input and a noise-ablation activation patch on the latent reasoning steps. We find that (i) at the output level, latent reasoning methods can look similarly unfaithful at convergence under counterfactual edits while following qualitatively divergent trajectories; (ii) at the activation level, the causal contribution of latent reasoning steps to the final answer decays across training for both paradigms, with the examples that flip on the output side in (i) also being the examples on which this contribution decays; and (iii) the activation-level trajectory diverges by answer format, decaying on binary choice and rising on open-ended decoding. These findings highlight that latent reasoning faithfulness depends on training stage and answer format.

</details>


### [182] [Entropy-Guided Tensor Compression for Multimodal Federated Learning on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06651)
*Quoc Bao Phan,Tuy Tan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Entropy-guided, modality-aware MPS update compression for heterogeneous FL; provable rank allocation and convergence with substantial communication reduction and small accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning on resource-constrained, multimodal clients suffers from uniform compression policies that ignore layer/modality spectral variability; need adaptive, theoretically-grounded compression.

Method: Compute layer-wise spectral entropy via truncated SVD; adaptively allocate MPS reconstruction ranks across layers, modalities, and devices under client payload budgets; use majorization order to relate entropy to rank; prove convex surrogate for rank allocation, monotonicity under exact payload; derive convergence with compression-error term.

Result: Empirical results on 15-node Raspberry Pi cluster: up to 56.8x update compression; final accuracy improvement up to 2.01% over uncompressed FedAvg; data transmitted to convergence reduced up to 66x.

Conclusion: Entropy-guided MPS-based update compression enables modality-heterogeneous FL with strong communication efficiency, convergence guarantees, and modest accuracy benefits.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) over mobile and edge devices increasingly involves multimodal models in which clients differ in both sensing capability and computational capacity. Existing update compression schemes typically apply uniform policies across layers and devices, without accounting for modality-specific differences in spectral structure and compressibility. We propose MESH-FL, an entropy-guided matrix product state (MPS) update-compression framework for modality-heterogeneous FL on resource-constrained devices. MESH-FL estimates the spectral entropy of each layer-wise update via truncated singular value decomposition and allocates MPS compression ranks adaptively across layers, modalities, and devices under per-client payload budgets. We show that higher spectral entropy necessitates a higher reconstruction rank under the majorization order on singular-value energy distributions. Building on this result, we prove that the proposed entropy-guided allocation solves a convex surrogate rank-allocation problem, preserves monotonicity under the exact payload model, and achieves convergence with an explicit compression-dependent error term. Experiments on a 15-node heterogeneous Raspberry Pi~4/5 cluster with modality-heterogeneous clients show that MESH-FL achieves up to $56.8\times$ compression while surpassing the uncompressed FedAvg baseline in final accuracy by up to 2.01%, and reduces total transmitted data to reach convergence by up to $66\times$.

</details>


### [183] [From Jumps to Signatures: a Generative Method for Temporal Point Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06652)
*Niels Cariou-Kotlarek,Vasileios Lampos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a pathwise framework to extend rough path signatures to discrete-event sequences via an interarrival embedding, enabling a signature-based generative model for TPPs (sigTPP) trained on full trajectories, plus three distributional discrepancies for evaluation; shows strong empirical performance across synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between continuous-path signature theory and cadlag event sequences, and the lack of global sequence-level loss/evaluation in neural TPPs.

Method: Introduce the interarrival embedding, a stable, injective lift from jump paths to continuous paths of bounded variation; apply the signature framework to discrete-event sequences; develop sigTPP, a signature-based generative model trained with path-level loss on complete trajectories; analyze the space of counting paths and derive three distributional discrepancies for evaluation.

Result: Empirically, sigTPP achieves best average rank across eight metrics, outperforms or matches the strongest baseline in 64% of dataset-metric pairs, and shows at least 19% average improvement on a relative score across baselines on synthetic and real-world data.

Conclusion: The interarrival embedding enables rigorous extension of signature methods to TPPs, yielding a principled, path-level generative model and robust evaluation tools for TPPs with strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Rough path signatures are a universal feature map for continuous paths and, via the expected signature, characterise path distributions. These guarantees do not directly extend to cadlag paths of Temporal Point Processes (TPPs), limiting the use of signature methods for event sequences. Furthermore, neural TPP models, including recent generative approaches, optimise per-event objectives with no global sequence-level loss, while evaluation of variable-length event sequences lacks distributional discrepancy measures. This paper proposes a common pathwise framework for addressing these limitations. We introduce the interarrival embedding, a stable, injective lift from jump paths to continuous paths of bounded variation, extending signature methods to discrete event sequences. Our theoretical contributions give rise to sigTPP, the first signature-based generative model for TPPs, trained using a path-level loss on complete trajectories. We further analyse the space of counting paths and derive three distributional discrepancies, providing mathematically justified tools for evaluating generative TPP models. Across synthetic and real-world datasets, sigTPP achieves the best average rank based on eight complementary metrics, outperforms or is within a standard error of the strongest baseline in 64% of the dataset-metric pairs, and according to a relative score, improves against every baseline by at least 19% on average.

</details>


### [184] [Dual Attention Heads for Personalized Federated Learning in ECG Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06653)
*Kien Le,Joseph Lindley,Quoc Bao Phan,Tuy Tan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedDualAtt personalizes federated ECG classification by splitting transformer attention heads into a global, aggregated part and a local, client-specific part, improving cross-site robustness on FedCVD; global-local head sharing reveals varying degrees of needed personalization across clients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address non-iid, institution-specific variations in ECG data that hinder robust federated learning for cardiovascular disease detection.

Method: Partition transformer attention heads into global (fedAvg-aggregated) and local (client-specific) branches. Global heads capture shared cross-site patterns; local heads adapt to institution-level recording characteristics. Train with FedAvg for global heads while keeping local heads on each client.

Result: FedDualAtt outperforms existing FL and personalized FL methods on ECG classification in the FedCVD benchmark. Analysis shows that different clients benefit from different global-local head ratios, indicating diverse personalization needs across institutions.

Conclusion: Architectural personalization through head-level splitting improves ECG classification performance in FL and highlights the importance of adapting the level of personalization to site-specific recording characteristics.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across institutions without sharing sensitive patient data. However, the inherent heterogeneity of electrocardiogram (ECG) data across healthcare providers presents significant technical challenges for robust classification. We propose FedDualAtt, a personalized federated learning approach that splits transformer attention heads into global and local branches. Global heads are aggregated via FedAvg to capture shared cross-site patterns, while local heads remain client-specific to adapt to institution-level recording characteristics. Experiments on FedCVD, an FL benchmark for cardiovascular disease detection, demonstrate that FedDualAtt outperforms existing FL and personalized FL methods in ECG classification tasks. Analysis of global-local head ratios reveals that different clients benefit from varying levels of architectural personalization.

</details>


### [185] [Robust Human-AI Complementarity under Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06656)
*Yewon Byun,Bryan Wilder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Negative error correlation between human and AI predictions enables robust complementary strategies, guaranteeing improvements in expected utility, as shown by real-world forecasting benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate why humans often fail to extract complementary value from AI predictions and how information asymmetry about model vs. human information quality interacts with error correlation to affect complementarity.

Method: Theoretical analysis of how error-correlation structure under asymmetric information influences extractable value from AI assistance, deriving conditions for robust complementarity; empirical evaluation on real-world forecasting benchmarks to test prevalence of these conditions.

Result: Identifies negative correlation between human and AI errors as a key condition enabling robust complementary strategies that improve expected utility; empirical benchmarks used to assess the practicality of these conditions.

Conclusion: Error-correlation structure is central to realizing human-AI complementarity under information asymmetry; when AI errors are negatively correlated with human errors, decision-makers can guarantee improvements in utility, and this complementarity is empirically testable on forecasting benchmarks.

Abstract: Machine learning models are often intended to augment rather than replace human decision makers, by providing information that is complementary to human judgement.
  Yet, in practice, human decision makers routinely fail to realize such complementary gains, even when models provide useful signal.
  In this work, we study how asymmetric information about the quality of information available to a human decision maker vs. an AI impacts the ability of a decision maker to extract complementary value from AI predictions.
  We show that a key factor is the error correlation structure between human and AI predictions. In particular, when the AI's prediction errors are \textit{negatively correlated} with those of the human, the decision maker can construct robust strategies which guarantee improvements in expected utility. We empirically investigate whether these conditions for complementarity arise in practice, using real-world forecasting benchmarks.

</details>


### [186] [Trees from Marginals: Autoregressive drafting with factorized priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06763)
*Yuma Oda,Ryan Mathieu,Roman Knyazhitskiy,Artur Chakhvadze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weaver is a lightweight autoregressive adapter for speculative decoding that constructs proposal trees from the top-K marginals of a factorized drafter, restoring dependencies and enabling fast verification; yields substantial speedups (4.37x over autoregressive, 24.7% over DFlash) with optimized CUDA kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address degradation of acceptance rates in factorized draft models as speculative budget increases; enable fast verification for Gated Delta Net layers.

Method: Builds proposal trees from top-K marginals of the factorized drafter; avoids full-vocabulary projection; introduces rollback-free tree verification; implements CUDA kernels in SGLang.

Result: Achieves 4.37x speedup vs autoregressive decoding; outperforms DFlash baseline by 24.7%.

Conclusion: Combining model-level proposal-tree construction with systems-level verification and acceleration yields practical, high-speed speculative decoding, reclaiming efficiency and enabling scalable autoregressive decoding workflows.

Abstract: Speculative decoding greatly increases the interactivity of autoregressive language models by trading off computation for extra tokens generated in a single forward pass. Factorized draft models are especially efficient because they predict future-token marginals in parallel, but their independence assumption causes acceptance rates to degrade sharply as the speculative budget grows. We analyze this limitation and introduce Weaver, a lightweight autoregressive adapter that constructs proposal trees from the top-K marginals of a factorized drafter. Weaver restores conditional dependencies between proposed tokens while avoiding a full-vocabulary projection. To support fast verification for models with Gated Delta Net layers, we derive a rollback-free tree-verification algorithm and implement optimized CUDA kernels in SGLang. By combining these model and systems contributions we achieve a 4.37-fold speedup over autoregressive decoding, and outperform a highly optimized DFlash baseline by 24.7%.

</details>


### [187] [Efficient Long-Horizon Learning for Learned Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06772)
*Xiaolong Huang,Benjamin Thérien,James Harrison,Eugene Belilovsky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient long-horizon learned optimizers (ELO) enable scalable meta-training and better performance/generalization, with low compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome two bottlenecks in learned optimizers: (1) scaling meta-training to long-horizon inner problems, and (2) surpassing hand-designed optimizers.

Method: Introduce Efficient Long-hOrizon learning (ELO) with two components: (1) reallocating meta-training compute to longer failure regimes to enable long-horizon learning; (2) decoupled progressive expert supervision to provide stable meta-learning signals and improve generalization; evaluate on element-wise and matrix-based learned optimizers (LOs).

Result: ELO improves long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization of base LOs across tasks (GPT-2 variants on FineWeb; ViT-B/16 and ResNet-50 on ImageNet-1K). ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms well-tuned AdamW and is competitive with Muon on language modeling. Notably, all ELO baselines require <7 H100 GPU-hours for meta-training.

Conclusion: ELO provides an effective, efficient framework for meta-training learned optimizers, enabling better long-horizon optimization and generalization with modest compute.

Abstract: Learned optimization aims to improve upon hand-designed optimizers (e.g., Adam and Muon) by meta-learning small neural network optimizers over a distribution of tasks. While recent work has greatly advanced the architectural design and inductive biases of learned optimizers (LOs), current meta-training approaches still suffer from two main difficulties: (1) they cannot efficiently scale meta-training to long-horizon inner problems and (2) they often fail to surpass comparable hand-designed optimizers. To address these limitations, we propose Efficient Long-hOrizon (ELO) learning, an efficient meta-training algorithm that (1) reallocates redundant meta-training compute to longer failure regimes, achieving efficient long-horizon learning, and (2) enforces decoupled progressive expert supervision, providing stable meta-learning signals that additionally improve the generalization of LOs. Our empirical study evaluates ELO for meta-training both element-wise and matrix-based LOs. Across downstream language modeling (GPT-2-124M/350M on FineWeb) and image classification (ViT-B/16, ResNet-50 on ImageNet-1K) tasks, ELO substantially improves the long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization of the base LOs. In particular, ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms well-tuned AdamW across all evaluated tasks, while remaining competitive with Muon on language modeling. \textit{Notably, all ELO baselines require less than 7 H100 GPU-hours for meta-training.}

</details>


### [188] [Efficient Bayesian Deep Ensembles via Analytic Predictive Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06776)
*Sina Aghaee Dabaghan Fard,Marie Maros,Jaesung Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Bayesian deep ensemble method for predictive regression that achieves calibrated uncertainty and competitive performance by combining a small ensemble of independently trained neural predictors via closed-form Bayesian linear aggregation, enabling scalable and interpretable inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve interpretability and calibrated uncertainty in deep ensembles while preserving scalability and efficiency; provide interpretable posterior weights and a modular component suitable for broader learning systems.

Method: Three components: (i) low-dimensional ensemble representation—predictions are a weighted combination of a small number of neural predictors; (ii) closed-form Bayesian aggregation—weights are obtained via Bayesian linear regression, yielding interpretable posterior weights and calibrated uncertainty without approximate inference; (iii) independent ensemble training—networks are trained separately to promote diversity and robust uncertainty.

Result: Empirical results on standard regression benchmarks show competitive predictive performance and reliable uncertainty estimates across settings.

Conclusion: The approach delivers interpretable, calibrated uncertainty with scalable inference, suitable as a standalone predictor or as a component in larger learning systems.

Abstract: We introduce an efficient Bayesian deep ensemble method for predictive regression designed to enhance interpretability while maintaining competitive predictive performance and computational efficiency. Our method combines the statistical rigor of Bayesian inference with the scalability of deep ensembles, providing calibrated uncertainty estimates that enable its use not only for standalone prediction but also as a component within broader learning systems. To achieve these goals, our work relies on three key design components: (i) low-dimensional ensemble representation: predictions are expressed as a combination of a small number of trained neural predictors, enabling scalable inference whose cost depends on ensemble size rather than dataset size; (ii) closed-form Bayesian aggregation: ensemble predictions are combined using Bayesian linear regression, yielding interpretable posterior weights and calibrated uncertainty without approximate inference; and (iii) Independent ensemble training: multiple neural networks are trained separately, producing diverse predictive representations that improve robustness and uncertainty calibration. Empirical results on standard regression benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves competitive predictive performance while maintaining reliable uncertainty estimates across settings.

</details>


### [189] [On Explicit Super-Expressive Approximation for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06781)
*Feng-Lei Fan,Ze-Yu Li,Chen-Yu Wang,Jian-Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fixed-architecture neural networks with explicit, non-asymptotic parameter bounds achieve approximation of Lipschitz and Hölder functions via a constructive CRT-based encoding; width/depth are fixed while parameter magnitude grows with the target error, yielding a dual result to existing parameter-bounded, architecture-unbounded work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap of quantitative, non-asymptotic characterizations of required parameter magnitude for fixed-architecture networks. Provide explicit trade-offs between error, depth, width, and parameter size.

Method: Use the Chinese Remainder Theorem as a constructive encoding mechanism to represent function-approximating parameters within a fixed network architecture. Design networks with specified fixed widths and depths for Lipschitz and Hölder classes, and derive explicit bounds on the parameter magnitude P as a function of the approximation error ε and function smoothness.

Result: For Lipschitz on [0,1]^D: width max{D,4}, depth 5. For Hölder C^{r,γ}_A([0,1]^D): width max{2D, D+5N+1}, depth r+9. Parameter magnitude bound: log2 P = O(ε^{-2D/(r+γ)} log(1/ε)). This provides a dual perspective to prior parameter-bounded/architecture-unbounded results by fixing architecture and letting parameters grow.

Conclusion: The CRT-based encoding enables non-asymptotic, parameter-controlled approximation using fixed architectures, offering a complementary perspective to the traditional trade-off where either architecture grows to achieve accuracy or parameter bounds are imposed with potentially large networks.

Abstract: In this work, we investigate the fixed-architecture neural network approximation with explicit parameter bounds and elementary activations. While prior work demonstrated super-expressive approximation using fixed-size networks, they lack quantitative and non-asymptotic characterizations of parameter magnitude with respect to the approximation error. We resolve this issue by introducing the Chinese Remainder Theorem as a constructive encoding mechanism. For Lipschitz continuous functions on $[0,1]^D$, we construct a width-$\max\{D,4\}$, depth-$5$ network with explicit parameter-error trade-offs. For Hölder-smooth functions in $C^{r,γ}_A\left([0,1]^D\right)$, our fixed network of width $\max\{2D,\ D+5N+1\}$ and depth $r + 9$ achieves the parameter magnitude $\mathcal{P}$ bounded by $\log_2 \mathcal{P}=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2D/(r+γ)}\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)$. This is the dual result compared to those in the parameter-bounded and architecture-unbounded paradigm.

</details>


### [190] [Enhancing deep learning models for time series classification via knowledge distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06796)
*Javidan Abdullayev,Maxime Devanne,Jonathan Weber,Germain Forestier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Knowledge distillation improves efficiency in time series classification across FCN, Inception, and ConvTran, with intermediate-complexity student models offering the best trade-off; notable parameter reductions while preserving or closely matching teacher performance; experiments on the UCR Archive with open-source code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High computational and memory demands of state-of-the-art time series models hinder deployment in resource-limited settings; KD can transfer knowledge from large teachers to smaller students while maintaining performance.

Method: Systematically apply KD to three architectures (FCN, Inception-based, and transformer-based ConvTran) for time series classification. Modify architectural components (convolutional filters, Inception modules, and attention heads) and evaluate on the UCR Archive datasets, comparing parameter counts and accuracy against teachers. Provide open-source implementation.

Result: Distilled student models of intermediate complexity yield the most consistent gains across all architectures. Distilled FCN reduces parameters by 38x; distilled Inception achieves near-teacher accuracy with 42% fewer parameters; distilled ConvTran with 2 attention heads shows the largest improvement. The authors provide code for reproducibility.

Conclusion: Knowledge distillation is effective for time series classification across diverse architectures, with intermediate-sized students offering the best efficiency-accuracy balance. The work supports broader adoption of KD in TSC and provides a reproducible implementation.

Abstract: Deep learning has achieved remarkable success in various domains including time series analysis, computer vision and natural language processing. However, high computational and memory demands of state-of-the-art architectures pose challenges for deployment in resource-limited environments. Knowledge Distillation (KD) addresses this by transferring knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller, more efficient student model while maintaining competitive performance. In this work, we investigate the effectiveness of KD for Time Series Classification (TSC) across three architectures: the classical Fully Convolutional Network (FCN), the convolutional Inception model and the transformer-based ConvTran model. We evaluate our approach on UCR Archive, the largest benchmark repository of time series datasets, by modifying architectural components such as convolutional filters, Inception modules and attention heads across the three architectures. Our results consistently show that KD most effectively benefits student models of intermediate complexity across all three architectures, with the distilled FCN student reducing parameters by a factor of 38, the distilled Inception student achieving nearly the same performance as the teacher with 42% fewer parameters and the distilled ConvTran student with 2 attention heads showing the most significant improvement through distillation. To encourage further research and reproducibility, we provide our implementation at https://github.com/MSD-IRIMAS/KD-4-TSC.

</details>


### [191] [What Predicts Correctness in Text-to-SQL? A Selective-Prediction Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06799)
*Robert Richardson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Verification-based signals and cross-provider ensembles significantly improve uncertainty estimation for AI-generated SQL; best AUROC ~0.82; in-domain verifiers helpful, but cross-schema generalization remains challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty estimation for AI-generated text-to-SQL is needed to decide when a query is likely correct. Prior signals have limited discrimination; this work evaluates multiple signals across benchmarks, explores verifiers, and analyzes generalization across schemas.

Method: Evaluate a range of signals (black-box: string, structural, execution self-consistency, schema-relevance, executability; white-box: log-probability) on BIRD and Spider. Use LLM judges from GPT-4o-mini and Claude to assess correctness, and form two-provider ensembles with calibration and abstention. Also train verifiers (encoder and generative), test fine-tuning and scaling to 7B, incorporating schema diversity and rationale distillation, and assess cross-benchmark transfer.

Result: Black-box signals yield 0.61–0.68 AUROC (string self-consistency ~0.675; log-prob ~0.67). LLM judges achieve 0.72–0.78 AUROC. A two-provider ensemble reaches 0.82 AUROC with expected calibration error ~0.03 and useful abstention frontiers (e.g., 27% questions at 24% selective risk). Results hold across two benchmarks, two generators, two judge providers. Verifiers (fine-tuned, encoder/generative) reach 0.77–0.79 AUROC in-domain but drop to ~0.66 on unseen schemas. Scaling to 7B, adding schema diversity, distilling rationales, and cross-benchmark training do not close the gap. Cross-schema transfer tracks model scale and reasoning rather than fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Correctness uncertainty in text-to-SQL is primarily governed by reasoning-based signals. A fine-tuned verifier is effective in-domain, but generalization across schemas currently requires a large frozen reasoning model; cross-schema transfer hinges on scale/reasoning capability rather than fine-tuning.

Abstract: Evaluating uncertainty in AI-generated SQL queries requires estimating whether a query is correct, where correct means it executes to the same result as a human-written reference. We study which signals predict correctness on hard multi-table text-to-SQL, using AUROC to measure how well each ranks correct queries above incorrect ones. On BIRD and Spider, black-box signals such as string, structural, and execution self-consistency, a schema-relevance score, and query executability all fall between about 0.61 and 0.68 AUROC, with string self-consistency strongest at 0.675; white-box log-probability is similar (0.67). The signals that move past this ceiling are verification-based: an LLM judge scores from 0.72 (GPT-4o-mini) to 0.78 (Claude). Judges from different providers make different errors, so a two-provider ensemble reaches 0.82 AUROC with a well-calibrated probability (expected calibration error 0.03) and supports useful abstention frontiers (for example, answering 27% of questions at 24% selective risk) where self-consistency offers no valid low-risk subset. The pattern holds across two benchmarks, two generators, and two judge providers. We also ask whether a verifier can be trained. Fine-tuned verifiers, both encoder and generative, reach about 0.77 to 0.79 AUROC in-distribution but fall to about 0.66 on unseen schemas; scaling to 7B, adding schema diversity, distilling a strong judge's rationales, and cross-benchmark training all fail to close that gap. Cross-schema transfer appears to track model scale and reasoning rather than fine-tuning. In practice, correctness uncertainty for text-to-SQL lives in reasoning-based signals: a fine-tuned verifier is a good in-domain tool, but a verifier that generalizes across schemas currently means a large frozen reasoning model.

</details>


### [192] [Generative Diffusion Models of Stochastic Graph Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06833)
*Yiğit Berkay Uslu,Samar Hadou,Sergio Rozada,Shirin Saeedi Bidokhti,Alejandro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a diffusion-based framework for conditional graph signal generation using a U-GNN, enabling sampling from conditional distributions on graphs, demonstrated on stock forecasting and wireless resource allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevailing methods regress to conditional means and lack principled samplers for conditional graph signals; a unified, generative approach conditioned on topology and node features is needed.

Method: Train a reverse diffusion process parameterized by graph neural networks; employ a U-Graph Neural Network (U-GNN) with multi-resolution encoder–decoder architecture; pooling/unpooling implemented via learned nested selection matrices and zero-padded lifting; graph convolutions are performed on the original graph with a controllable hop reach, avoiding explicit coarsening at each resolution.

Result: Empirical validation on stock price forecasting and optimal wireless resource allocation with extensive numerical results.

Conclusion: Proposes a unified conditional graph signal generative model using diffusion, realized by U-GNN, enabling sampling from conditional distributions on graphs with scalable, graph-native architecture and broad applicability.

Abstract: Sampling stochastic signals supported on a graph underlies many graph machine learning tasks, including recommender systems, forecasting in financial markets, and wireless network optimization. In these settings, the target signals are realizations of unknown conditional distributions. However, prevailing approaches rely mostly on intricate, application-tailored designs that often regress to a conditional mean instead of sampling from the conditional law. This paper unifies such problems as conditional graph signal generative modeling and tackles them with a single denoising diffusion framework. We learn a reverse diffusion process, parametrized by graph neural networks (GNNs), that draws graph signals conditioned directly on the graph topology and on node-feature side information. The reverse process is realized by a novel architecture, the U-Graph Neural Network (U-GNN), which generalizes the image-convolutional U-Net to graph-structured signals. The U-GNN performs multi-resolution encoder--decoder processing in which pooling and unpooling reduce to a learned node selection, expressed by nested selection matrices, and a zero-padded lifting of coarse signals back to the full node set. The graph convolutions are carried out on the original graph, with a stride that sets their hop reach, so the U-GNN bypasses explicit graph coarsening at every resolution. We demonstrate our method on two generative tasks: stock price forecasting and optimal wireless resource allocation, with extensive numerical results in both domains.

</details>


### [193] [LEMUR 2: Unlocking Neural Network Diversity for AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06839)
*Tolgay Atinc Uzun,Waleed Khalid,Saif U Din,Sai Revanth Mulukuledu,Akashdeep Singh,Chandini Vysyaraju,Raghuvir Duvvuri,Avi Goyal,Yashkumar Rajeshbhai Lukhi,Muhammad A. Hussain,Krunal Jesani,Usha Shrestha,Yash Mittal,Roman Kochnev,Pritam Kadam,Mohsin Ikram,Harsh R. Moradiya,Alice Arslanian,Dmitry Ignatov,Radu Timofte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEMUR 2 presents a large-scale, cross-domain NAS benchmark and deployment framework that unifies generative design, evaluation, and deployment. It covers 14k+ architectures and 750k+ training records, produced via AST mutation, genetic/RL evolution, fractal synthesis, and LLM-guided methods, with NN-RAG motifs. It includes automated deployment/latency benchmarking on mobile and Unity VR, across multimodal tasks, enabling architectural transferability studies and data-driven AutoML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NAS benchmarks are narrow and task-specific, lacking cross-domain and deployment-aware evaluation. There is a need for a scalable, diverse, reproducible dataset and framework to study architectural generalization and to support LLM-driven AutoML across modalities and hardware.

Method: Architectures generated via AST-based code mutation, genetic algorithms, reinforcement-learning evolution, fractal architectures, and LLM-guided synthesis. Deep models derived with NN-RAG using motifs from ~900 PyTorch modules. Deployment/latency benchmarking via NN-VR and NN-Lite on mobile and Unity VR platforms. Multimodal tasks include image captioning, text-to-image, and language modeling. Dataset: >14,000 architectures and >750,000 training records documenting performance, hyperparameters, and task outcomes. Cross-domain analysis of architectural transferability enabled by linking architectures, tasks, and deployment data.

Result: Provides a data foundation for LLM fine-tuning and coupling diverse architectural origins with large-scale, cross-platform empirical validation. Enables reproducible, data-driven AI design and advances LLM-driven AutoML and architectural generalization across modalities and hardware.

Conclusion: LEMUR 2 defines a new basis for reproducible, data-driven AI design, expanding architectural generalization across modalities and hardware and advancing LLM-driven AutoML.

Abstract: Existing NAS benchmarks (e.g., NAS-Bench, NATS-Bench) cover only narrow, task-specific regions of the architectural design space and lack cross-domain or deployment-aware evaluation. LEMUR 2 introduces a large-scale, extensible framework unifying generative, evaluative, and deployment pipelines to unlock neural-network diversity. It comprises over 14,000 distinct architectures and more than 750,000 structured training records documenting model performance, hyperparameters, and task outcomes. These models were produced through AST-based code mutation, genetic and reinforcement-learning evolution, generation of fractal architectures, and synthesis guided by a Large Language Model (LLM). This includes deep models generated with the retrieval-augmented system NN-RAG, which derived and used architectural motifs from over 900 PyTorch modules extracted from public repositories. LEMUR 2 further employs NN-VR and NN-Lite pipelines for automated deployment and latency benchmarking on heterogeneous mobile and Unity-based VR platforms, providing real-device performance metadata. It spans multimodal tasks, image captioning, text-to-image synthesis, and language modeling, supporting cross-domain analysis of architectural transferability. By linking diverse architectures, tasks, and deployment data, LEMUR 2 provides the data foundation for LLM fine-tuning and coupling diverse architectural origins with large-scale, cross-platform empirical validation. This dataset defines a new basis for reproducible and data-driven AI design, advancing the emerging paradigm of LLM-driven AutoML and architectural generalization across modalities and hardware.

</details>


### [194] [A Gold-Standard Study of What Makes a Lightweight Game-Playing Agent Strong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06854)
*Nima Kelidari,Mohammadsaeed Haghi,Mahdi Salmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, self-play framework for imperfect-information games using a fixed rule-based expert as a yardstick yields competitive agents across Gin Rummy and Leduc Hold’em; key training design choices improve efficiency, while many complex ideas underperform; extra model capacity offers little gain; the approach generalizes and is released as a reusable package.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imperfect-information games pose challenges for training and benchmarking reinforcement learning agents, as performance is highly opponent-dependent and difficult to assess. There is a need for a simple, scalable, and game-agnostic training recipe that yields competitive agents without relying on expert data.

Method: Train lightweight agents via self-play using a fixed rule-based Gin Rummy expert as a yardstick (not for training). Employ a set of design elements: trust-region updates, a well-chosen reward signal, a curriculum of increasingly tougher opponents, warm starting, and keeping the best checkpoint; combine multiple policies (stacking) to form a champion. Evaluate against baselines (neural fictitious self-play, information-set Monte Carlo) and test generalization by applying the approach to Leduc Hold’em. Compare different encoders (MLP, CNN, set-based, attention, RNN) to assess the impact of capacity.

Result: The rule-based expert beats all trained agents in 70–99% of games. Across many runs, the lightweight self-play recipe reveals which components improve performance: trust-region updates, reward shaping, curriculum, warm start, and checkpointing; stacking boosts performance from ~30% to ~36% win rate against the expert. Several ideas (short- and long-term reward shaping, learned state embeddings, imitation/DAgger, live LLM opponent) are ineffective or impractical at scale. Encoder capacity beyond a small model provides limited gains. The approach generalizes to Leduc Hold’em, where optimal strategies are computable, and yields a robust, reusable package for training competitive agents without expert data.

Conclusion: A general, game-agnostic, lightweight recipe for training competitive imperfect-information agents without expert data. Key design choices (trust regions, shaped rewards, curriculum, warm starts, and checkpointing with ensembling) yield robust performance across Gin Rummy and Leduc Hold’em. Increasing network size offers diminishing returns, suggesting information content limits performance. The method scales to additional small games and is released as a reusable package.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents for imperfect-information card games are only as strong as the opponents they train against, and they are hard to grade, since they beat a random opponent over 99 percent of the time and only tie copies of themselves. So we build a strong, fixed, rule-based expert for Gin Rummy and use it only as a yardstick, never for training. It beats every agent we trained 70 to 99 percent of the time. Across more than a hundred runs, we isolate what makes a lightweight agent stronger. Trust region updates, a well-aimed reward, a curriculum of tougher opponents, warm starting, and keeping the best checkpoint all help, and stacking them lifts a self-play champion from about 30 to 36 percent against the expert. Several ideas did not pay off. Short-term and longer-term reward shaping, learned state embeddings, imitation and DAgger, and a live large language model opponent were each unhelpful, too slow, or too heavy to train at scale. Comparing MLP, convolutional, set-based, attention, and recurrent encoders shows that extra capacity does little to break the ceiling, suggesting the limit is information rather than network size. We add standard baselines (neural fictitious self-play and information set Monte Carlo search) and confirm the approach carries over to Leduc Hold'em, where the optimum is computable. The result is a lightweight, game-agnostic recipe that trains competitive agents without training on the expert, for any game a small model can handle, reported with robust statistics and released as a reusable package.

</details>


### [195] [Geometric Self-Distillation for Reasoning Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06855)
*Josip Jukić,Ivan Titov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoSD introduces a geometry-aware self-distillation objective to counter drift during on-policy distillation by combining a Hellinger-based teacher pull (scaled by overlap) with a Fisher-Rao proximal term, optimized via natural gradients on next-token distributions. It preserves in-distribution gains while improving OOD accuracy across 1.7B–32B models and three families, by keeping high-entropy alternatives in reach rather than draining mass toward confident but wrong answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation provides dense supervision but risks drift and overconfident agreement on incorrect answers, especially when the teacher has privileged information. The challenge is to maintain ID gains while improving OOD robustness in self-distillation.

Method: Introduce GeoSD: a geometric self-distillation objective operating in the geometry of next-token distributions. Use a Hellinger loss that scales teacher preference by the overlap between student and teacher distributions to attenuate pulls on tokens the student cannot yet support. Add a proximal term based on Fisher-Rao distance to penalize drift from a recent checkpoint. Update parameters with natural-gradient steps in the distribution geometry rather than parameter space.

Result: GeoSD preserves the in-distribution gains of self-distillation and improves average OOD accuracy by 5.7–8.6 points across three model families and scales from 1.7B to 32B. Analysis indicates standard matching drains probability mass from high-entropy states, yielding confident yet incorrect answers, whereas GeoSD maintains alternatives in reach, mitigating this drift.

Conclusion: Geometric self-distillation mitigates drift from privileged teacher supervision, maintaining ID gains while boosting OOD robustness. Its gradient updates stay faithful to the distribution geometry, and its combination of overlap-scaled pulls and Fisher-Rao proximal regularization yields consistent improvements across model sizes and families.

Abstract: On-policy distillation is a practical post-training recipe for large language models, supplying dense teacher supervision on the student's own trajectories. In privileged-context self-distillation, teacher and student are the same model conditioned on the same prefix, but the teacher also sees a hint or the full solution trace. This makes supervision abundant but harder to trust: the teacher can be confident about continuations its privileged view makes obvious but the student cannot yet justify. The distillation pull is strongest where teacher and student disagree most, and over many updates it accumulates into drift that degrades out-of-distribution (OOD) reasoning. We introduce GeoSD, a geometric self-distillation objective that treats this drift as movement in the student's predictive behavior and counters it in two complementary ways. A Hellinger loss scales each teacher preference by the overlap the student already shares with it, attenuating the pull on tokens the student cannot yet support. Since these pulls still compound over training, a proximal term penalizes how far the student's predictions drift from a recent checkpoint, measured as a Fisher-Rao distance. Both are distances in the same geometry of next-token distributions, and a natural-gradient update takes its steps in that geometry rather than in parameter space. Across mathematical reasoning benchmarks and three model families, GeoSD preserves the in-distribution gains of self-distillation while improving average OOD accuracy by 5.7-8.6 points over the base model, with gains holding across model scales from 1.7B to 32B. Analyzing why standard matching fails out of distribution, we find it wins agreement with the teacher by draining mass from alternatives at high-entropy states, resulting in confident agreement on wrong answers, whereas GeoSD keeps those alternatives in reach.

</details>


### [196] [Best-Arm Identification with Generative Proxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06879)
*Tianyi Ma,Hanzhang Qin,Ruihao Zhu,Jierui Zuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PROBE is a phase-elimination algorithm for fixed-confidence best-arm identification that leverages cheap, correlated proxy scores. It treats the proxy mean as known and online-learns the correlation with rewards via OLS, maintaining an exact chi-square-based residual-variance certificate to ensure correctness. It achieves δ-PAC and near-oracle sample complexity scaled by (1−ρ^2).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce sampling cost in best-arm identification by using cheap proxies (e.g., ML predictions or language-model scores). The key challenge is unknown correlation with rewards, learned online with costly samples. The goal is to preserve statistical validity while achieving sample savings proportional to the proxy's informativeness.

Method: Convert to a heteroscedastic identification problem via a control-variate adjustment. PROBE maintains an upper certificate on the residual variance using an ordinary least squares fit, exploiting the exact chi-square distribution to remain valid even with unknown correlation. It uses a phase-elimination scheme to compare arms and prune suboptimal ones while updating the variance certificate online.

Result: PROBE is δ-PAC and attains the known-correlation oracle sample complexity up to a constant factor and an additive calibration cost. The approach extends to (ε, δ)-PAC with minor modifications. Numerical experiments on synthetic data and an auto-loan pricing replay with LLM/tabular proxies show sample savings scale with the strength of reward-proxy correlation, aligning with theory.

Conclusion: Using a robust online variance certificate grounded in OLS, PROBE effectively leverages proxies to reduce sampling in fixed-confidence BAI without sacrificing correctness, even when the reward-proxy correlation must be learned online. This establishes a practical path for cost-efficient identification in settings with cheap predictions.

Abstract: Best-arm identification is a canonical model for data-driven decision-making, but in many applications each reward observation is costly.
