Motivated by the growing availability of cheap predictions from machine learning and large language models, we study fixed-confidence best-arm identification in which each costly reward pull is paired with a cheap but correlated proxy score. The marginal mean of the proxy can be estimated offline and is treated as known, whereas its correlation $ρ$ with the reward, which governs how much the proxy helps, is unknown and must be learned online in pair with real rewards. We show that a control-variate adjustment turns this model into a heteroscedastic identification problem whose oracle sample complexity improves by residual variance $1-ρ^2$. The central difficulty is that the correlation must be learned from the same costly samples that identification consumes online, and that a plug-in estimate of the residual variance is anti-conservative and can compromise correctness. We propose PROBE (PRoxy OLS for Best-arm Exploration), a phase-elimination algorithm that directly maintains an upper certificate on the residual variance with an ordinary least squares fit, whose exact chi-square law keeps the certificate valid regardless of the unknown correlation. We prove that PROBE is $δ$-PAC and attains the known-correlation oracle sample complexity up to a constant multiplicative factor and a constant additive calibration cost. The guarantee extends to the $(ε,δ)$-PAC setting under minimal changes to the algorithm. Numerical experiments on synthetic instances and on an auto-loan pricing replay with large language model and tabular proxies confirm that the sample savings of PROBE scale with the strength of the reward-proxy correlation, exactly as the theory predicts.

</details>


### [197] [Converge to Surprise: Evolutionary Self-supervised Image Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06887)
*Canlin Zhang,Xiuwen Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a target-free self-supervised image clustering framework. Uses a maximum-entropy null hypothesis (H0) and a surprise score to quantify non-randomness, optimized via an evolution-strategy (ES) outer loop (no gradient) with a periodic gradient-descent inner loop that leverages ES-discovered clusters as targets; achieves state-of-the-art results in non-parametric clustering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most SSL clustering methods rely on explicit per-step targets or pseudo-labels; the paper seeks a target-free objective to avoid handcrafted targets and supervisions, leveraging information-theoretic surprise under a maximum-entropy null to drive learning.

Method: Define H0 as i.i.d. pixels per Maximum Entropy. Construct a surprise score measuring how unlikely the model's representation is under H0. Use an ES outer loop to maximize surprise without gradients, coupled with a periodic inner loop of gradient descent that uses the discovered clusters as surrogate targets.

Result: On standard image benchmarks, the framework achieves new state-of-the-art results in non-parametric self-supervised image clustering (the strictest deep-clustering setting where the number of classes is unknown).

Conclusion: A target-free, surprise-driven optimization paradigm for image clustering is feasible and competitive, demonstrating that ES can effectively discover meaningful clusters that gradient-based methods can refine further when guided by surrogate targets.

Abstract: Most self-supervised image clustering models, actually almost all deep learning approaches, are based on gradient descent: In order to calculate the loss, every optimization step requires a clearly defined target, whether a contrastive split, a masked patch or entity, an EMA-teacher output, a pseudo-label, or a differentiable information-theoretic functional. We propose a self-supervised framework that drops this requirement for image clustering. Without any prior knowledge, we have to assume that each pixel is i.i.d. according to the Principle of Maximum Entropy. Taking this as our null hypothesis H0, we define a "surprise score" that measures how unlikely the model's output representation would be under H0. Maximizing the surprise score forces the deep learning model to reject H0 - equivalently, to discover non-random feature from data. Also, here is our fundamental assumption: a surprise score cannot, in general, be reduced to a per-step loss. Hence, we propose the "converge-to-surprise" scheme to optimize our model: an evolution-strategy (ES) outer loop, which directly maximizes the surprise score without needing its gradient, paired with a periodic gradient-descent inner loop, which uses the surprising clusters already discovered by ES as surrogate targets. On standard image benchmarks, our framework achieves new state-of-the-art results in non-parametric self-supervised image clustering - the strictest deep-clustering setting, in which the number of ground-truth classes is not given to the model.

</details>


### [198] [Latency-Constrained DNN Architecture Learning for Edge Systems using Zerorized Batch Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06922)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A latency-aware DL training method with a universal hardware-aware predictor enables one-shot optimization to meet strict edge-device latency constraints, achieving minimal accuracy loss and notable speedups (GoogLeNet, VGG-19) with quantization improvements; open-sourced framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge devices face strict real-time latency limits; conventional neural-network optimization does not directly target inference latency, hindering deployment under latency constraints while preserving accuracy.

Method: Introduce a latency-oriented learning objective coupled with a hardware-customized latency predictor. Utilize a one-shot training process to search/adjust network size (neuron counts) to satisfy latency constraints. Optionally apply quantization to further improve latency and accuracy. Provide hardware-aware optimization for platforms like NVIDIA Jetson Nano/TX2.

Result: Under a 34 ms latency constraint on ImageNet-100, GoogLeNet latency reduced from 40.32 ms to 34 ms on Jetson Nano with 0.14% accuracy loss; with quantization, 0.04% loss. On Jetson TX2, VGG-19 latency reduced from 119.98 ms to 34 ms with a 0.5% accuracy gain; GoogLeNet scaled to 34 ms with a 0.78% accuracy gain. The framework is open-sourced.

Conclusion: The approach effectively prioritizes latency in model design, enabling hardware-aware, latency-constrained high-accuracy models on edge devices, with demonstrable gains and a readily reusable framework.

Abstract: Deep learning applications have been widely adopted on edge devices, to mitigate the privacy and latency issues of accessing cloud servers. Deciding the number of neurons during the design of a deep neural network to maximize performance is not intuitive. Particularly, many application scenarios are real-time and have a strict latency constraint, while conventional neural network optimization methods do not directly change the temporal cost of model inference for latency-critical edge systems. In this work, we propose a latency-oriented neural network learning method to optimize models for high accuracy while fulfilling the latency constraint. For efficiency, we also introduce a universal hardware-customized latency predictor to optimize this procedure to learn a model that satisfies the latency constraint by only a one-shot training process. The experiment results reveal that, compared to state-of-the-art methods, our approach can well-fit the 'hard' latency constraint and achieve high accuracy. Under the same training settings as the original model and satisfying a 34 ms latency constraint on the ImageNet-100 dataset, we reduce GoogLeNet's latency from 40.32 ms to 34 ms with a 0.14% accuracy reduction on the NVIDIA Jetson Nano. When coupled with quantization, our method can be further improved to only 0.04% drop for GoogLeNet. On the NVIDIA Jetson TX2, we compress VGG-19 from 119.98 ms to 34 ms and even improve its accuracy by 0.5%, and we scale GoogLeNet up from 20.27 ms to 34 ms and achieve higher accuracy by 0.78%. We also open source this framework at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/ZeroBN

</details>


### [199] [Intrinsic-Noise Consolidation: A Doob-Barrier-Conditioned Diffusion Turns Analog Device Noise into a Continual-Learning Resource](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06924)
*Gunner Levi Howe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Barrier-conditioned Doob h-transform applied to per-synapse dynamics; intrinsic analog noise becomes a consolidation signal, yielding nonmonotonic (inverted-U) memory retention with real hardware evidence on Split-MNIST and BrainScaleS-2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question whether intrinsic device noise on analog neuromorphic hardware can enhance memory retention, and propose a principled stochastic conditioning rule (Doob barrier-conditioning) for synapses to convert noise into consolidation.

Method: Apply Doob h-transform conditioning to the stochastic diffusion of each synaptic weight, enforcing a memory barrier and incorporating an anchored drift term (sigma^2 d/dw log h). Compare to OU/EWC/MESU baselines; perform ablations; test on single-head Split-MNIST (8 seeds) with interior optimum barrier, and on BrainScaleS-2 silicon with noise in training loop; preregister go/no-go gate.

Result: Conditioned diffusion yields an inverted-U with respect to intrinsic noise; on Split-MNIST, retention improves by 10.9 points at an interior optimum (p=0.004 vs matched anchors). Ablation removes the effect; results persist across second task stream and when noise is present in the forward pass. On BrainScaleS-2, barrier-conditioning yields 15.6-point retention advantage over matched controls at matched accuracy; energy modelled; effect reflects stability-plasticity shift rather than pure accuracy gain.

Conclusion: Intrinsic analog noise can be harnessed as a consolidation dividend via barrier-conditioned synaptic dynamics; the rule makes falsifiable predictions (nonmonotonic retention with noise), passes preregistered testing, and demonstrates hardware-supported memory stabilization, suggesting a new direction for neuromorphic learning where noise is beneficial rather than solely detrimental.

Abstract: On analog neuromorphic hardware, intrinsic device noise is normally an accuracy tax. We ask whether it can instead consolidate memories. We cast per-synapse consolidation as a Doob h-transform: condition each weight's stochastic dynamics on never crossing a memory-critical barrier around its consolidated value. The conditioned diffusion gains an extra drift sigma^2 d/dw log h, a restoring force amplified by the noise variance itself that diverges at the barrier. We are explicit about novelty: the anchored drift -s(w-mu) our rule also contains is not ours (the limit of OUA, MESU, and EWC), and we surrender it. We claim only the conjunction of (a) the Doob barrier-conditioning as a synaptic rule, to our knowledge unclaimed (every h-transform use we found is generative modeling, none synaptic), and (b) a falsifiable prediction: increasing intrinsic noise non-monotonically improves sequential-task retention, an inverted-U that anchored-drift methods cannot produce. We pre-registered this as a go/no-go gate; it passes. On single-head Split-MNIST (8 seeds) the rule lifts retention 10.9 points at an interior optimum (paired Wilcoxon p=0.004), while matched OU/EWC/MESU anchors are monotone. Ablating the conditioning removes the effect; the optimum tracks the barrier; the inverted-U survives a second task stream and the realization where noise enters the forward pass. We then measure the intrinsic noise on real BrainScaleS-2 silicon (additive, trial-to-trial independent, tunable via on-chip averaging) and run the rule on the chip with its noise in the training loop: barrier-conditioning retains a prior task 15.6 points better than the matched control at matched average accuracy, a stability-plasticity shift, not a net-accuracy win (single seed; retention measured, energy modelled). Intrinsic analog noise thus becomes a consolidation dividend a digital accelerator must spend energy to generate.

</details>


### [200] [Imputation Meets Clustering: Exploiting Latent Subgroup Structure for Missing Data Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06930)
*Chuyao Zhang,E Li,Taochen Chen,Yiqun Zhang,Yuzhu Ji,Shuping Zhao,Peng Liu,Yiu-ming Cheung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cluster-aware generative imputation method (CAGI) jointly learns subpopulation structure and data imputation by alternating clustering and GAN-based imputation. It uses a Partition-Guide-Restore loop where cluster assignments condition the generator, refined iteratively with distribution-level regularization, achieving superior results on 14 datasets against 15 baselines. Code is provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Missing-data imputation in real-world data is challenged by latent heterogeneity across subgroups. Traditional imputation can blur subgroup boundaries and lose instance-level fidelity. While leveraging subgroup information could help, identifying subgroups requires complete data, creating a circular dependency. A method is needed to jointly infer structure and fill in missing values.

Method: CAGI formulates clustering and imputation as a mutually reinforcing co-optimization. It employs a Partition-Guide-Restore strategy: dynamic cluster assignments serve as local priors to condition a Generative Adversarial Network. An iterative feedback loop updates both cluster structures and imputations. A multi-level objective combines instance-level reconstruction with distribution-level regularization to stabilize results.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 14 benchmark datasets against 15 representative baselines shows the proposed method (CAGI) outperforms existing approaches in imputation with consideration of population structure. The authors provide source code at the stated GitHub link.

Conclusion: CAGI effectively resolves the clustering-imputation coupling, achieving faithful subgroup-aware imputations, improved instance-level fidelity, and better distributional alignment. The approach is validated empirically and is reproducible via open-source code.

Abstract: Missing data is prevalent in practical applications, making effective imputation an essential preprocessing step for downstream analysis. Real-world datasets often exhibit complex latent structures composed of multiple subgroups with distinct distributions. However, existing methods often overlook such population heterogeneity. Without explicit structural guidance, these methods tend to produce generic estimates that blur subgroup boundaries and lack instance-level fidelity. While incorporating subgroup information offers a remedy, it faces a circular dependency: reliable subgroup identification requires complete data, while data completion is the imputation objective itself. To resolve this, we propose CAGI (Cluster-Aware Generative Imputation), a framework that reformulates clustering and imputation as a mutually reinforcing co-optimization process. CAGI employs a ``Partition-Guide-Restore'' strategy where dynamic cluster assignments act as local priors to condition a Generative Adversarial Network. An iterative feedback loop is established to progressively refine both cluster structures and imputed values toward faithful subgroup distributions. To ensure distributional stability, CAGI further employs a multi-level optimization objective combining instance-level reconstruction with distribution-level regularization. Extensive experiments on 14 benchmark datasets with 15 representative baselines demonstrate the superiority of CAGI. The source code is available at: https://github.com/supercocachii/CAGI

</details>


### [201] [Physical activities enable scalable foundation modelling for broad-spectrum health prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06954)
*Zhenghuang Wu,Yuyao Zhu,Songlin Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A step-counter–based foundation model (StepFM) is proposed for broad-spectrum health risk prediction, offering privacy-preserving, computation-efficient transfer across 20+ health tasks using low-dimensional step data, with interpretable activity–health relationships and robust cross-device/region performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limited transferability of disease-specific wearable models and the privacy/computation burdens of high-frequency raw sensor data, by leveraging ubiquitous, low-dimensional step data as a generalizable signal for health risk inference.

Method: Pre-train a scalable model on large-scale step-sequence data to capture temporal dynamics and behavioral patterns, then transfer to diverse health risk tasks across devices, regions, and disease types (20+ tasks). The approach emphasizes privacy and efficiency due to the simplicity of step data.

Result: StepFM achieves strong performance compared with existing methods and shows robustness across heterogeneous settings; analyses reveal interpretable, generalizable links between physical activity patterns and various health risks.

Conclusion: Step-based sensing can serve as a viable foundation for scalable, real-world health monitoring, offering privacy-preserving, computation-efficient health prediction across a broad spectrum of risks.

Abstract: Wearable and mobile sensing technologies have demonstrated strong potential for health inference; however, most sensor models are designed for specific disease types, limiting their transferability across different health risks. Wearable foundation models offer a more generalizable approach in diverse health risk types. Nevertheless, most existing methods rely on high-frequency raw sensor data, raising concerns about privacy, computational overhead, and scalability across devices and populations. In this paper, we propose StepFM, a foundation model built solely on step counter data for broad-spectrum health prediction. Leveraging the ubiquity and low-dimensional nature of step data, StepFM provides a practical, privacy-preserving, and computation-efficient alternative to traditional sensor-based models. We design a scalable pre-training framework that captures temporal dynamics and behavioral patterns from large-scale step sequences, enabling transfer across more than 20 health risk prediction tasks spanning diverse devices, new regions, and novel disease types. Extensive experiments demonstrate that StepFM achieves strong performance compared to existing methods while maintaining robustness across heterogeneous settings. Furthermore, our analysis reveals interpretable and generalizable relationships between physical activity patterns and various health risks, offering new insights into activity-based health modeling. Our work establishes step-based sensing as a viable foundation for scalable and real-world health monitoring.

</details>


### [202] [Rethinking Multimodal Time-Series Forecasting Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06973)
*Haoxin Liu,Yichen Zhou,Rajat Sen,B. Aditya Prakash,Abhimanyu Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new multimodal time-series forecast benchmark, TimesX, offers scalable, real-world data with rich textual contexts to improve generalization and reduce data leakage; zero-shot evaluation reveals that simple ensembles leveraging textual context outperform strong baselines on TimesX, challenging existing benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal forecasting benchmarks suffer from limited scale, synthetic data, narrow textual contexts, and vulnerability to data leakage. TimesX aims to provide diverse, real-world time series with accompanying textual contexts via an automated data-generation pipeline to enable robust evaluation and better generalization.

Method: Construct TimesX using an automated data-generation pipeline that pairs high-quality real-world time series with diverse textual contexts. Evaluate a suite of zero-shot multimodal forecasting approaches on TimesX, and compare against strong baselines including simple ensemble methods that exploit rich textual context.

Result: Many approaches that perform well on existing benchmarks underperform on TimesX. In contrast, simple ensemble methods that leverage rich textual context accompanying time series outperform strong baselines on TimesX.

Conclusion: TimesX presents a more challenging and realistic benchmark for multimodal forecasting. It highlights the importance of diverse, real-world data and rich textual contexts, and suggests a shift toward ensemble strategies that utilize textual information to achieve better generalization while mitigating data leakage.

Abstract: We introduce a new context-enriched, multimodal time series forecasting benchmark, TimesX. TimesX contains a wide selection of high-quality real-world time series with diverse domains and textual contexts obtained from an automated data generation pipeline, which helps address three main issues of existing multimodal forecasting benchmarks: (1) poor generalization due to the small scale and synthetic nature of benchmark data, (2) very limited types of textual contexts in the benchmarks, and (3) an inability to mitigate data leakage in evaluation. We conduct a thorough empirical study of zero-shot multimodal forecasting approaches on TimesX. Our results suggest that many approaches that perform well on existing benchmarks may fail on TimesX. In contrast, simple ensemble methods that leverage rich textual context accompanying time-series can outperform strong baselines on TimesX.

</details>


### [203] [Hybrid Least Squares/Gradient Descent Methods for MIONets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06976)
*Jun Choi,Chang-Ock Lee,Minam Moon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient hybrid LSGD for MIONets via alternating least squares with tensor-algebra-based factorization to reduce large LS system cost; extends LSGD from DeepONets; supports general L2 losses with regularization on last-layer branch parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accelerate training of MIONets by exploiting multilinearity in the last-layer parameters and decoupling optimization across branch networks, reducing computational burden and memory footprint.

Method: Treat MIONet training as alternating least squares across branch last-layer parameters. Solve least-squares for one branch at a time. Use Kronecker and Khatri-Rao product structures and tensor permutation matrices to factor a large LS system into smaller ones. Compatible with general L2 losses with regularization on last-layer parameters; allows linear operators on outputs within the loss.

Result: Claimed to accelerate training through the ALS-based factorization and structured LS solving; no empirical results are described in the abstract.

Conclusion: Proposes a scalable, structure-exploiting optimization framework for MIONets, extendable to DeepONet-like architectures, with broad loss formulations including regularization and linear operator terms.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose an efficient hybrid least squares/gradient descent (LSGD) method for MIONets to accelerate training. This method generalizes the LSGD method for DeepONets. Since MIONet is the sum of the entrywise product of multiple branch networks and a trunk network, it can be viewed as a multilinear function with respect to the last layer parameters of each branch network. These sets of parameters can be optimized using the alternating least squares method, where we solve the LS system for a single branch network in turn. To handle the large-sized system matrix, we introduce Kronecker and Khatri-Rao products and tensor permutation matrices to factor the large matrix into small ones. Our method is compatible with a general type of $L^2$ loss with regularization terms for the last layer parameters of each branch, where linear operators can be applied to the MIONet output in each loss term.

</details>


### [204] [Safe Reinforcement Learning using Ideas from Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07252)
*Georg Schäfer,Jakob Rehrl,Stefan Huber,Simon Hirlaender*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework that merges offline MPC-based safety constraints with deep RL by projecting RL actions onto a verified safe set, enabling safe exploration and stable policy learning on a hardware 1-DoF system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hard safety guarantees are essential for RL in CPS/robotics to prevent damage during exploration; purely data-driven RL can violate hardware limits.

Method: Compute a feasible state-action set using offline MPC with a mathematical model; during training and deployment, constrain the agent by projecting actions onto this set via a safety filter, ensuring constraint satisfaction.

Result: On a non-linear 1-DoF lab setup, the approach enabled safe exploration and convergent policy learning on real hardware.

Conclusion: Integrating DRL with MPC-based safety guarantees provides a general framework for safe learning in CPS; practical utility depends on model fidelity and computational tractability, with potential conservatism and scalability considerations.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables the synthesis of control policies directly from data, making it highly appealing for complex cyber-physical systems (CPSs) and robotics. A persistent challenge, however, is ensuring strict, hard safety constraints during the active learning phase. In real-world physical systems, violating mechanical limits can cause irreversible damage, necessitating that exploration remains strictly within safe operational regions. We propose a generalized framework that combines the adaptive, high-performance nature of deep reinforcement learning (DRL) with the formal safety guarantees of model predictive control (MPC). Using a mathematical model of the system dynamics, offline MPC computations define a feasible state-action space, representing all safe combinations of system states and control inputs that guarantee constraint satisfaction. During training and deployment, the RL agent's instantaneous actions are projected onto this globally verified feasible set via a safety filter. We systematically evaluate our generalized approach on a non-linear 1-DoF laboratory testbed, demonstrating successful exploration and stable policy convergence on physical hardware.

</details>


### [205] [Robust Federated Learning Under Real-World Client Churn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06979)
*Dhruv Garg,Neha Lakhani,Debopam Sanyal,Myungjin Lee,Alexey Tumanov,Ada Gavrilovska*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FeLiX is a federated learning (FL) orchestration framework that minimizes wall-clock time-to-target accuracy on live interaction streams by addressing transient client availability, dynamic data heterogeneity, and delays between predictions and outcomes through three primitives: streaming-aware availability tiers, fresh-utility selection, and informativeness-aware delay-robust aggregation. It achieves up to 2.37x faster time-to-target accuracy and 1.30x bandwidth reduction on CIFAR-10, Google Speech, and low-availability traces compared to state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FL deployments suffer from slow refresh cycles due to coordinating massive client populations. For applications like feed ranking, ad targeting, and personalized recommendations, rapid model freshness is critical to adapt to volatile user data. Key challenges overlooked by existing FL systems are transient client availability, dynamic data heterogeneity, and delays between model predictions and observable outcomes, which can cause stale models and missed opportunities.

Method: FeLiX introduces three primitives: (i) streaming-aware availability tiers that use lightweight telemetry to identify ready clients at scale; (ii) fresh-utility selection, a dual-tier mechanism that prioritizes statistically valuable updates from devices able to meet tight refresh deadlines; and (iii) informativeness-aware, delay-robust aggregation that can incorporate late updates with ground-truth outcomes without biasing the global model toward stale distributions.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows FeLiX reduces wall-clock time-to-target accuracy by up to 2.37x and lowers communication bandwidth by 1.30x relative to state-of-the-art synchronous and asynchronous FL baselines, with experiments on CIFAR-10, Google Speech, and realistic low-availability traces.

Conclusion: FeLiX achieves near-oracular performance in real-world settings by explicitly tackling transient availability, data heterogeneity, and outcome delays, enabling faster, more bandwidth-efficient FL refreshes for time-sensitive applications.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables training shared models on private, on-device data, but production deployments remain constrained to slow, multi-day refresh cycles due to the complexity of coordinating massive client populations. For applications such as feed ranking, ad targeting, and personalized recommendation, model freshness: the ability to rapidly adapt to new user-local data is critical for maximizing objectives like click-through rate. This lag leaves models stale and unresponsive to volatile data distributions driven by viral trends and shifting user intent. Bridging this gap requires addressing three challenges overlooked by existing FL systems: transient client availability, dynamic data heterogeneity, and delays between model predictions and observable outcomes. We present FeLiX, an FL orchestration framework that minimizes wall-clock time-to-target accuracy on live interaction streams. FeLiX introduces three primitives: (i) streaming-aware availability tiers that leverage lightweight telemetry to identify ready clients at scale; (ii) fresh-utility selection, a dual-tier mechanism that prioritizes statistically valuable updates from devices able to meet tight refresh deadlines; and (iii) informativeness-aware, delay-robust aggregation that incorporates late, high-value updates containing ground-truth outcomes without biasing the global model toward stale distributions. Unlike prior systems that rely on unrealistic oracular knowledge of client availability, FeLiX achieves near-oracular performance in real-world settings. Across CIFAR-10, Google Speech, and realistic low-availability traces, FeLiX reduces wall-clock time-to-target accuracy by up to 2.37X while reducing communication bandwidth by 1.30X compared to state-of-the-art synchronous and asynchronous FL baselines.

</details>


### [206] [UP: Unbounded Positive Asymmetric Optimization for Breaking the Exploration-Stability Dilemma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06987)
*Chongyu Fan,Pengfei Liu,Jingjia Huang,Sijia Liu,Yi Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unbounded Positive Asymmetric Optimization (UP) alleviates the exploration–stability tradeoff in RL for LLM training by uncliping positive updates via stop-gradient anchoring to the current policy, while applying standard clipping to negative updates. It is a universal plug‑and‑play objective applicable across token-level (GRPO, DAPO) and sequence-level (GSPO) frameworks, and across architectures and modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Importance sampling–based RL training of LLMs is prone to training instability. Conservative clipping restricts exploration, and the paper formalizes Probability Capacity (Cap) to explain why correct but low-confidence paths are truncated. A method that preserves useful positive updates while keeping stability is needed.

Method: Introduce Unbounded Positive Asymmetric Optimization (UP) with a stop-gradient anchor to the current policy, creating asymmetric gradients: unclipped positive advantages to boost exploration, and clipped negative advantages to prevent instability. The approach is Zh flexible across optimization granularities and extends to token-level (GRPO, DAPO) and sequence-level (GSPO) settings, functioning as a universal plug‑and‑play enhancement.

Result: Empirical evaluations show that UP enhances exploration and improves reasoning accuracy across diverse RL algorithms (DAPO, GSPO, GRPO), model architectures (Dense, MoE, vision-language), and training modalities (language and multimodal).

Conclusion: UP provides a universal plug‑and‑play objective that reconciles exploration with stability in RL-based training of LLMs, with broad applicability across granularities, architectures, and modalities.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become the standard paradigm for enhancing the complex reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). To achieve sample efficiency, modern RL frameworks rely on importance sampling (IS). However, these algorithms suffer from an exploration-stability dilemma. Pure IS often leads to catastrophic training instability, while standard clipping mechanisms used to mitigate this instability strictly constrain the policy update budget. By formalizing the concept of Probability Capacity (Cap), we reveal that conservative clipping structurally stifles exploration by prematurely truncating the update budget for correct but low-confidence reasoning paths. To break free from these constraints, we propose Unbounded Positive Asymmetric Optimization (UP), a universal and plug-and-play objective. UP theoretically restructures the optimization process by anchoring the policy to its current state via the stop-gradient operator. This asymmetric design unleashes unclipped, stable gradients for positive advantages to maximize exploration, while maintaining standard clipping safeguards for negative advantages to prevent training instability. Furthermore, our formulation readily extends across different optimization granularities, including token-level (GRPO, DAPO) and sequence-level (GSPO) frameworks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that UP enhances exploration capacity and achieves superior reasoning accuracy across diverse RL algorithms (DAPO, GSPO, and GRPO), model architectures (Dense, MoE, and vision-language), and training modalities (language and multimodal), validating UP as a truly universal plug-and-play enhancement for RL-based training.

</details>


### [207] [Physics-guided spatiotemporal neural models for fuel density prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06999)
*Tolga Caglar,Jaynil Jaiswal,Saqib Azim,Yudhir Gala,Mai H. Nguyen,Ilkay Altintas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-guided ML framework (PGML) for fuel density forecasting that embeds physics constraints into deep learning models (ConvLSTM, AFNONet, ViViT) to improve accuracy and stability. It uses differentiable physics terms (mass-conserving fuel transport and rate-of-spread) in the loss. PGML outperforms purely data-driven baselines and enables efficient, physically plausible fire forecasting for adaptive prescribed burn management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine data-driven spatiotemporal modeling with physical conservation laws to enhance predictive reliability, physical plausibility, and stability in fire density forecasts.

Method: Evaluate three architectures—ConvLSTM, Adaptive Fourier Neural Operator (AFNONet), and Video Vision Transformer (ViViT)—to model spatiotemporal fuel density. Incorporate differentiable physics-informed loss terms: mass-conserving fuel transport and rate-of-spread estimation. Train across multiple independent trials and compare to baseline data-driven models.

Result: The PGML framework achieves higher accuracy and stability than purely data-driven baselines across trials. It yields computationally efficient, physically plausible fuel-density forecasts suitable for fire forecasting and adaptive prescribed burn management.

Conclusion: Physics-guided ML enhances fuel-density forecasting, offering modularity and improved performance while preserving physical plausibility, enabling practical burn management decisions.

Abstract: This paper presents a physics-guided machine learning (PGML) framework for fuel density prediction, integrating physics constraints and domain knowledge into deep learning models to enhance model accuracy and stability. We explore three deep learning architectures -- ConvLSTM, Adaptive Fourier Neural Operator (AFNONet), and Video Vision Transformer (ViViT) -- to model the spatiotemporal evolution of fuel density. Our approach incorporates differentiable physics-informed terms in the loss function, including a mass-conserving fuel transport term and a rate-of-spread estimation. Experimental results, averaged across multiple independent trials, demonstrate that the proposed PGML framework outperforms purely data-driven baselines without physics constraints in both accuracy and stability. This framework enables computationally efficient, physically plausible fire forecasting to support adaptive prescribed burn management.

</details>


### [208] [Dissociating the Internal Representations of Sycophancy in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07003)
*Anthony Baez,Sheer Karny,Pat Pataranutaporn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dissociates sycophancy into factual vs opinion subtypes, probes their representations, and tests transfer between subtypes across LLMs, revealing model-dependent sharing and organization of these subtypes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the multi-faceted behavior of sycophancy in LLMs has distinct internal representations and whether these representations are shared across subtypes or segregated, varying by model.

Method: Train linear probes and steering vectors on activations corresponding to one subtype (factual or opinion) and evaluate transfer to the other subtype to assess representational sharing; compare across LLMs.

Result: Different LLMs show different patterns: some unify subtypes into shared representations; others exhibit distinct, causally interfering representations; transfer performance varies by model and subtype alignment.

Conclusion: The dissociation framework is a promising approach to study the representational structure of complex model behaviors and can illuminate how multifaceted phenomena are encoded internally.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) frequently exhibit sycophancy, where they agree with a user's statement even when incorrect. While sycophancy is often treated as a single defined behavior, it can manifest in substantially distinct ways and circumstances, raising the question of whether this multi-faceted nature is reflected in its internal mechanisms. To address this gap, we dissociate the representations of sycophancy into factual and opinion subtypes -- motivated by the distinction between verifiable claims and subjective beliefs. We train linear probes and construct steering vectors on activations of one subtype and evaluate their transfer to the other subtype to measure to what extent they share representations. We find evidence that different LLMs represent these subtypes differently, with either more unified or more distinct and causally interfering representations. This method of dissociation offers a promising framework for studying the representational structure of complex model behaviors.

</details>


### [209] [Multimodal Spatiotemporal-Frequency Fusion with Peak Enhancement for Cellular Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07016)
*Qingzhong Li,Yue Hu,Hui Ma,Yajun Zhang,Xinjun Pei,Ming Yan,Fei Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal cellular traffic forecasting framework (MSPF-Net) that combines spatiotemporal-frequency encoding, burst-aware peak enhancement, and exogenous News signal embeddings with a dynamic fusion predictor to improve accuracy across Milano, Trento, and LTE datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world cellular traffic is bursty and influenced by external urban events. Existing methods largely model intrinsic patterns and single modalities, failing to capture bursts and exogenous context jointly. A multimodal approach incorporating burst dynamics and contextual signals can enhance predictive performance.

Method: MSPF-Net comprises (1) a Spatiotemporal-Frequency Traffic Encoder for temporal, spatial, and spectral patterns; (2) a Peak Enhancement Module to extract burst-aware representations; (3) a News Context Representation Module to encode urban news streams into embeddings; (4) a Dynamic Fusion Prediction Module to adaptively fuse these heterogeneous signals for forecasting.

Result: Experiments on Milano, Trento, and LTE datasets show that jointly modeling traffic dynamics, burst patterns, and news contextual signals yields improved forecasting performance compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The MSPF-Net framework effectively leverages multimodal signals, including burst and exogenous context, to enhance cellular traffic forecasting and offers a viable approach for more accurate, context-aware network planning.

Abstract: Accurate forecasting of cellular network traffic is essential for network planning, resource allocation, and quality-of-service assurance in modern mobile communication systems. Real-world traffic often exhibits bursty endogenous dynamics and disturbances triggered by external urban events, which makes reliable prediction highly challenging. Most existing spatiotemporal traffic forecasting methods primarily focus on intrinsic traffic patterns or structural relationships within a single modality, and rarely model burst behavior together with exogenous contextual signals. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{MSPF-Net}, a multimodal cellular traffic forecasting framework that integrates external contextual information. Specifically, MSPF-Net consists of a Spatiotemporal-Frequency Traffic Encoder for capturing temporal, spatial, and spectral traffic patterns, a Peak Enhancement Module for extracting burst-aware representations of sudden spikes, a News Context Representation Module for encoding urban news streams into exogenous contextual embeddings, and a Dynamic Fusion Prediction Module for adaptively integrating these heterogeneous signals to generate forecasts. Experiments on the Milano, Trento, and LTE traffic datasets demonstrate that jointly modeling traffic dynamics, burst patterns, and news contextual signals can effectively improve forecasting performance.

</details>


### [210] [Online Data Selection Is Implicit Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07023)
*Aoxiong Zeng,Yuxin Yang,Xiangquan Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Online data selection during supervised fine-tuning acts as a hidden alignment mechanism by shaping behavioral preferences, even without explicit preference optimization. The work introduces auditing and diagnostic selectors to measure and constrain drift in behavior caused by data selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Separating SFT and alignment is incomplete: online scoring and data selection implicitly bias model behavior. Understanding and controlling this drift is critical for reliable alignment and safety.

Method: Compare random, loss-based, quality-based, and diversity-based online selectors under the same base model, optimizer, and token-budget. Formalize online selection as a reweighted SFT objective. Propose Alignment Drift Auditing (ADA) for quantifying drift and Alignment-Aware Selection (AAS) to constrain drift while preserving data efficiency; evaluate across metrics like helpfulness, refusal rate, verbosity, truthfulness, sycophancy, calibration, jailbreak robustness, and mode over-representation.

Result: Empirically, selectors with similar task accuracy diverge in behavioral traits (e.g., refusal rate, verbosity, sycophancy). The drift direction is predictable from the data attribute mix. ADA enables controlled auditing of selection-induced movement, and AAS offers data-efficient selection with drift constraints along safety and style axes.

Conclusion: Online data selection is a significant driver of alignment; using ADA and AAS provides practical tools to audit and constrain drift, enabling more predictable and controllable alignment outcomes during SFT without additional preference optimization.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is often treated as a capability-adaptation step, while alignment is attributed to later preference optimization or reinforcement learning. This separation is incomplete: when examples are scored and kept online during fine-tuning, the choice of which data to train on already changes the model's behavioral preferences. We study online data selection as an implicit alignment mechanism. Given the same base model, optimizer, and selected-token budget, we compare random, loss-based, quality-based, and diversity-based online selectors and measure the behavioral drift they induce without any preference optimization. The proposed evaluation tracks helpfulness, refusal rate, verbosity, truthfulness, sycophancy, calibration, and jailbreak robustness, together with diagnostics for which behavioral modes are over-represented in the selected data. We formalize online selection as a reweighted SFT objective whose weights define an implicit preference over response styles and safety postures, so that an online scorer plays the role usually assigned to a reward model. This view predicts that high-scoring data can systematically favor longer, more assertive, more compliant, or more refusal-prone behaviors depending on how the online score is defined. Empirically, selectors that are statistically indistinguishable in task accuracy diverge sharply in refusal rate, verbosity, and sycophancy, and we show that the direction of the shift is predictable from the attribute mixture of the selected data. We introduce Alignment Drift Auditing (ADA), a controlled protocol for quantifying selection-induced behavioral movement, and Alignment-Aware Selection (AAS), a diagnostic online selector that retains data efficiency while constraining drift along safety and style axes.

</details>


### [211] [Constrained Decoding for Diffusion Language Models via Efficient Inference over Finite Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07026)
*Meihua Dang,Stefano Ermon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exact, tractable constrained sampling for diffusion LMs under finite-automaton constraints; enables parallel, depth-reduced decoding with constraint guarantees and strong empirical gains across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained decoding is crucial for structure-adhering outputs; diffusion LMs sample multiple positions simultaneously, invalidating autoregressive masking assumptions. There is a need for exact, tractable constrained inference compatible with diffusion models and parallel decoding.

Method: Model finite automata as graphical models to derive tractable representations of the constrained mean-field posterior. Develop an exact algorithm that guarantees constraint satisfaction for both greedy and sampling-based decoding, compatible with parallel/block decoding and arbitrary remasking. Use depth-reduction techniques from arithmetic circuit theory to reduce sampling depth from linear to logarithmic in sequence length.

Result: Empirical gains on Dream-7B and LLaDA-8B across tasks such as function calling, planning, text-to-SQL, and math reasoning. Notable: BFCL-Live results show Dream-7B greedy accuracy from 63.9% to 71.5% and sampling accuracy from 22.3% to 69.0%, with under 5% wall-clock overhead compared to unconstrained decoding.

Conclusion: Exact constrained sampling for diffusion LMs under finite-automata constraints is practical and effective, enabling guaranteed-valid outputs with low overhead and broad applicability across decoding regimes and task domains.

Abstract: Constrained decoding is essential for serving LLMs, ensuring that generated outputs follow specific structures such as JSON schema-formatted function calls. Existing systems are designed for autoregressive models and assume left-to-right generation, masking out invalid next tokens at each step. Diffusion language models, however, break this assumption: they sample multiple positions simultaneously from a fully-factorized mean-field distribution at each denoising step. In this paper, we present an exact and tractable algorithm for sampling from the constrained mean-field posterior under any constraint expressible as a finite automaton. Viewing finite automata as graphical models, we obtain tractable representations of the constrained distribution that enable efficient inference. The approach guarantees constraint satisfaction by construction, supports both greedy and sampling-based decoding, and is compatible with parallel and block-wise decoding under arbitrary remasking schedules. Applying depth-reduction techniques from arithmetic circuit theory, we further reduce sampling depth from linear to logarithmic in the sequence length. Empirical evaluations on Dream-7B and LLaDA-8B show substantial accuracy gains across various tasks including function calling (xLAM, BFCL), planning (Sudoku, Countdown), text-to-SQL (Spider), and math reasoning (GSM-Symbolic), with little inference overhead relative to unconstrained decoding. For example, on BFCL-Live, our approach improves Dream-7B's greedy decoding accuracy from 63.9% to 71.5%, and stochastic sampling accuracy from 22.3% to 69.0%, where the unconstrained baseline collapses, with under 5% wall-clock overhead.

</details>


### [212] [Latent graph encoding of multimodal neuroimaging features with generative AI architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07027)
*Ishaan Batta,Meenu Ajith,Vince Calhoun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal graph VAE (gMMVAE) integrates structural MRI gray matter volume and static functional connectivity into a latent, modality-aware graph representation, outperforming other generative architectures in generation, reconstruction, efficiency, and interpretability for multimodal neuroimaging analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance multimodal neuroimaging analysis by identifying encoding strategies and latent fusion methods that best capture the structural–functional brain properties, while leveraging graph-based representations for connectivity.

Method: Systematic evaluation of encoders (vectorized, graph-based, and space-based) across VAEs, transformers, GANs, and diffusion models for GMV and sFNC features. Introduces a modality-aware graph encoding to map functional connectivity into a lower-dimensional latent space, culminating in a multimodal graph VAE (gMMVAE) that fuses structural and functional features.

Result: Graph-based, modality-aware encodings outperform vectorized or direct data-space encoders. The gMMVAE variant achieves superior performance across generation fidelity, reconstruction quality, computational efficiency, and latent space discriminability compared with other generative variants.

Conclusion: A multimodal graph-based VAE framework (gMMVAE) offers robust, efficient, and discriminative modeling for joint structural–functional MRI analysis, with broad potential for enhanced neuroimaging studies requiring integrated multimodal representations.

Abstract: While generative models enable encoding of complex neuroimaging data for feature generation and reconstruction, developing optimal architectural frameworks with appropriate encoding and latent space processes is crucial for studying structural and functional properties of the brain. We design a multimodal generative framework for structural and functional magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) features through systematic evaluation of encoding strategies, latent multimodal fusion, and generative model selection. Using structural gray matter volume (GMV) and static functional network connectivity (sFNC) features from a large neuroimaging dataset, we analyze generative frameworks involving variational autoencoders (VAEs), transformers, generative adversarial networks (GANs), and diffusion models. Architectures that employ modality-aware graph encoding of functional connectivity into a lower-dimensional latent space outperform vectorized encoders or direct data space approaches. The proposed multimodal graph VAE (gMMVAE) surpasses alternative generative variants across multiple metrics for generation fidelity, reconstruction quality, efficiency, and latent space discriminability, highlighting its potential for robust multimodal neuroimaging analysis.

</details>


### [213] [Gimitest: A Comprehensive Tool for Testing Reinforcement Learning Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07029)
*Dennis Gross,Quentin Mazouni,Helge Spieker,Arnaud Gotlieb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Gimitest, an open-source framework for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies under varied conditions, compatible with multiple gym ecosystems, with a detailed description of its functionality and architecture, and demonstration on Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL policies can be unsafe and vulnerable to attacks; current automated testing targets a narrow set of environments and algorithms. A comprehensive, flexible testing framework is needed to improve reliability across diverse settings.

Method: Development of Gimitest, an extensible testing framework that supports various gym frameworks and allows modification of integrated components; the article details the framework's functionality and software architecture and demonstrates its use.

Result: Empirical demonstration of the framework's effectiveness in testing multiple RL policies across environments such as Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo; Gimitest is released as open-source software.

Conclusion: The work provides a comprehensive, extensible testing solution for RL policies, enabling broader testing across single- and multi-agent settings and diverse gym-based environments.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) policies can be unsafe and vulnerable to attacks. Ensuring their reliability is often a pain point as existing automated testing methods target only selected environments, testing scenarios, and RL algorithms. To address this, we propose a comprehensive framework for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies under varying conditions. Our implementation of this framework, Gimitest, is an open-source tool that supports various gym frameworks and allows for modifications of their integrated components. This article describes the framework and details Gimitest's functionality and architecture. It showcases its effectiveness in testing multiple RL policies in environments such as the official Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo.

</details>


### [214] [Gauge-Invariant Learnable Spectral Positional Encodings for Directed Graphs via Hermitian Block Krylov Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07032)
*Jiaqing Xie,Yuxin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learnable, gauge-invariant spectral positional encodings for directed graphs via a magnetic Krylov approach that avoids costly eigendecompositions and attains favorable generalization with low-depth, random-probe matrix functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directed graphs with spectral PEs face two main issues: (i) magnetic Laplacians require expensive Hermitian eigendecompositions per potential, and (ii) eigenvectors are defined up to unitary gauge, complicating basis-sensitive methods. The goal is a scalable, gauge-invariant PE that works directly from sparse matrix operations.

Method: Define A_q as a normalized magnetic operator for directionality. Construct a learnable spectral filter h_θ and a random-probe block R, forming the PE as h_θ(A_q) R. Compute this as a matrix function within a Hermitian block Krylov subspace using only matrix–vector products. Prove that the required block dimension k grows only as O(log(1/ε)) for any heat–resolvent family, and provide a covering-number argument to explain why low-dimensional structured families generalize better than per-eigenvalue weights. Show that on a directed SBM with uninformative symmetrization, direction-blind PEs perform at chance while magnetic Krylov PEs converge to the exact-eigendecomposition oracle with depth. Also obtain gauge-invariant pairwise features with Monte-Carlo error ~1/√s; in undirected q=0, improve heterophilous benchmarks relative to no-PE and polynomial baselines.

Result: The approach yields gauge-invariant matrix-function PEs computed via Krylov subspaces, with k = O(log(1/ε)) steps guaranteeing spectral-approximation accuracy across heat–resolvent families. Empirically, on a directed SBM with uninformative symmetrization, the proposed magnetic Krylov PEs outperform direction-blind PEs and converge to the eigenbasis as depth increases. Random-probe-derived pairwise features achieve ~1/√s Monte-Carlo error. In the undirected q=0 case, the method improves heterophily benchmarks over baselines.

Conclusion: Learnable spectral PEs based on gauge-invariant matrix functions in a Krylov subspace provide scalable, robust representations for directed graphs, overcoming gauge ambiguity and expensive eigendecomposition, with strong theoretical guarantees and empirical gains on directed and undirected benchmarks.

Abstract: Spectral positional encodings (PEs) for \emph{directed} graphs face two obstacles: magnetic Laplacians require an $O(n^3)$ Hermitian eigendecomposition per potential, and their complex eigenvectors are defined only up to unitary gauge, which prior work handles with basis-invariant architectures. We propose learnable spectral PEs of the form $h_θ(A_q)\,R$, where $A_q$ is a normalized magnetic operator, $h_θ$ a learnable scalar spectral response, and $R$ a block of random probes. Because the PE is a \emph{matrix function} of the operator, it is gauge-invariant by construction. We compute it in a Hermitian block Krylov subspace from sparse matrix--vector products only, prove that $k = O(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ block steps suffice uniformly over heat--resolvent response families, and give a covering-number argument for why low-dimensional structured families generalize where free per-eigenvalue weights overfit. On a directed SBM whose symmetrization is uninformative by construction, direction-blind PEs stay at chance while magnetic Krylov PEs converge to the exact-eigendecomposition oracle as the depth grows. The same probes yield gauge-invariant pairwise features with $1/\sqrt{s}$ Monte-Carlo error, and the undirected $q{=}0$ case improves heterophilous benchmarks over no-PE and polynomial baselines.

</details>


### [215] [Intrinsic Green's Learning: Supervised Learning on Manifolds via Inverse PDE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07034)
*Alexandre Quemy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IGL models a target function on a manifold as the solution to a learned- source linear PDE, using a Green's kernel and a learned low-dimensional chart to achieve linear-time, intrinsic-dimension-aware inference; validated on synthetic manifolds and MNIST with strong classification and intrinsic dimension recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce high-dimensionality and computational cost in manifold learning by recasting function approximation as learning a source term for a linear PDE, enabling low-rank tensor decompositions and intrinsic-dimension discovery.

Method: Formulate the target as a PDE solution with a learnable source term. Use a Green's kernel to integrate the source. An encoder discovers a low-dimensional chart on which the source and kernel decompose as low-rank tensors, turning a high-dimensional integral into independent 1D integrals. A two-stage pipeline separates coordinate discovery from source fitting, with a near-convex linear solve. Learnable gates on coordinates infer intrinsic dimension.

Result: Demonstrates on synthetic manifolds and MNIST that IGL achieves near-optimal classification while automatically recovering the intrinsic dimension of the data manifold.

Conclusion: IGL provides an efficient, interpretable approach to function learning on manifolds by combining PDE-based representation with data-driven source learning, explicit intrinsic-dimension discovery, and a two-stage optimization that preserves tractability.

Abstract: We introduce Intrinsic Green's Learning (IGL), a framework that models a target function on a manifold as the solution to a linear PDE whose source term is learned from data. Rather than approximating the target directly, IGL learns a source and integrates it against a Green's kernel. An encoder discovers a low-dimensional coordinate chart on the manifold where both the source and the kernel decompose as low-rank tensors, collapsing a high-dimensional integral into independent one-dimensional integrals with cost linear in the intrinsic dimension. A two-stage algorithm separates coordinate discovery from source fitting, a near-convex linear solve, preventing the dimensional collapse of joint training. Learnable gates on each coordinate automatically discover the intrinsic dimension of the manifold. We validate IGL on synthetic manifolds and on MNIST, where it simultaneously achieves near-optimal classification and automatic recovery of the intrinsic dimension.

</details>


### [216] [On the Principles of Deep Feedforward ReLU Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07035)
*Changcun Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Path-based, piecewise linear analysis of deep ReLU networks showing deep units create input partitions with piecewise linear manifolds, extending two-layer insights to deeper networks and explaining backpropagation solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the mechanism of deep feedforward ReLU networks and demystify the black box by extending two-layer results to deeper architectures using path relationships.

Method: Theoretical analysis starting from the two-layer ReLU network, introducing the notion of 'paths' and their relations; show that deep units form piecewise linear manifolds; generalize two-layer principles to deeper architectures, including multiple strict partial orders and continuity constraints.

Result: A framework that explains training solutions found by backpropagation, showing how hidden units create linear pieces and partitions and how basic principles extend to deeper networks.

Conclusion: Path-based decomposition reveals the internal mechanics of deep ReLU networks and demonstrates how simple principles combine to yield complex training solutions, thereby demystifying the black box.

Abstract: The architecture of deep feedforward neural networks is ubiquitous in deep learning, either as a whole system or as a subnetwork of other architectures, and thus its mechanism is a key ingredient of the black box of neural networks. On the basis of the simplest two-layer ReLU network, this paper systematically studies the mechanism of deep feedforward ReLU networks with multiple hidden layers and successfully explains the training solution obtained by the back-propagation algorithm. The concept of a path, especially in terms of the relationships between paths, plays a central role in uncovering the mystery of the black box. It is shown that a unit of a deep ReLU network can form a piecewise linear manifold to divide the input space, instead of a hyperplane of the two-layer case. How to efficiently use the hidden-layer units to produce both linear functions and partitions of the input space is also a central problem. The principles of a two-layer ReLU network can be generalized to the deeper case to a large extent, such as multiple strict partial orders and continuity restriction. The combination of the basic and simple principles proposed can yield complicated instantiations including the training solutions, and in this sense the black box of deep feedforward ReLU networks is revealed.

</details>


### [217] [Complexity-Budgeted, Interaction-Aware Interpretable Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07060)
*Srikumar Krishnamoorthy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IAIML is an interaction-aware interpretable ML framework for tabular data that overcomes marginal-screening limitations by adaptive discretization, pairwise interaction scoring, and budgeted explanations, achieving competitive AUC with far fewer components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marginal screening in interpretable models can discard interactions that only emerge in joint configurations; there is a need for interpretable models that capture pairwise interactions without inflating explanation size.

Method: Three coordinated mechanisms: adaptive per-feature discretization; finite-grid pairwise interaction scoring; partitioned explanation budget. Detected interactions are routed via two strategies: (1) relax the screening filter so interaction-supported variables enter the pattern search, or (2) construct explicit pair terms for a sparse downstream classifier.

Result: On a 40-dataset panel (24 real-world benchmarks + 16 synthetic stress tests) evaluated with nested cross-validation, IAIML achieves mean AUC within 1.4 points of tuned gradient-boosted ensembles while requiring roughly 14–28x fewer fitted explanation components. On datasets with strong pairwise interaction structure and low marginal signal, IAIML outperforms baselines. Among compact interpretable methods, IAIML is comparable to RuleFit in AUC and component count and is cheaper to tune. EBM has a small but significant AUC advantage across the panel but a much larger lookup footprint. Performance degrades when higher-order interactions are needed. Component-isolated ablations show adaptive discretization and interaction-aware admission each contribute incrementally.

Conclusion: IAIML is a compact, interaction-aware framework suitable for settings where bounded explanation size and controlled handling of feature interactions are design requirements; effective when pairwise interactions dominate, though higher-order interactions beyond pairwise reduce its advantage.

Abstract: Inherently interpretable classifiers for tabular data typically rely on sparse features, rules, or patterns that users can inspect directly. The marginal feature-screening step common to these methods can discard variables whose predictive value emerges only through joint configurations with other variables. We present Interaction Aware Interpretable Machine Learning (IAIML), a framework that addresses this limitation through three coordinated mechanisms: adaptive per-feature discretization, finite-grid pairwise interaction scoring, and a partitioned explanation budget. Detected interactions are routed through one of two strategies: relaxing the screening filter so that interaction-supported variables enter the pattern search, or constructing explicit pair terms for a sparse downstream classifier. On a 40-dataset panel comprising 24 real-world tabular benchmarks and 16 synthetic interaction stress tests, evaluated under nested cross-validation, IAIML achieves mean AUC within 1.4 points of tuned gradient-boosted ensembles while requiring roughly 14--28 times fewer fitted explanation components. On datasets with strong pairwise interaction structure and low marginal signal, IAIML outperforms all baselines. Among compact interpretable methods, IAIML is comparable to RuleFit in AUC and component count and is less expensive to tune. EBM obtains a small but significant AUC advantage across the full panel, with a substantially larger lookup-table footprint. Performance degrades on datasets requiring higher-order interactions beyond the pairwise scope. Component-isolated ablations confirm that adaptive discretization and interaction-aware admission each contribute incrementally. These results support IAIML as a compact, interaction-aware framework appropriate for settings where bounded explanation size and controlled treatment of feature interactions are design requirements.

</details>


### [218] [Multiplication Beyond Groups: Stratified Fourier Mechanisms in Transformer Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07066)
*Zitong Andrew Chen,Junaid Hasan,Akhil Srinivasan,Hemkesh Bandi,Jarod Alper*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a monoid extension of Group Composition via Representation (GCR) to explain how small transformers learn modular integer multiplication over composite moduli, a non-invertible operation. Shows local, region-based representations and Fourier mechanisms; embeddings, attention, and logits are organized in a class-sensitive, localized manner, extending representation-theoretic insights beyond groups to monoids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand mechanistic reasoning in transformers for non-invertible, algebraic tasks (modular multiplication with zero-divisors) and to generalize GCR from groups to monoids by exploring localized representations.

Method: Train small transformers on square-free modular multiplication; analyze embeddings, attention patterns, and write directions; formulate monoid extension of GCR; examine locality and Fourier-like mechanisms in learned representations.

Result: Evidence of local algebraic regions where group-like structure persists; embeddings cluster around these regions; attention shows class-sensitive routing and low-rank write directions; local character features explain a large portion of output logits; representation-theoretic concepts extend to non-group structures.

Conclusion: Representation-theoretic mechanisms previously observed for group operations can extend to monoids; a localized, region-based decomposition (monoid extension) provides a framework to understand non-invertible algorithmic reasoning in transformers.

Abstract: Transformers have demonstrated a remarkable ability to learn algorithmic reasoning, yet mechanistic analyses have mostly focused on globally invertible operations such as cyclic addition and group composition. In this work, we investigate how small transformers learn modular integer multiplication over composite moduli, a fundamentally non-invertible operation due to the presence of zero-divisors. We propose the monoid extension: a localized generalization of Group Composition via Representation (GCR) that suggests the learned computation does not rely on a single global representation space. Instead, the model partitions the input space into local hierarchical algebraic regions, where group-like structure survives and Fourier mechanisms can be applied. In transformers trained on square-free modular multiplication, we find that embeddings organize around these regions, attention exhibits class-sensitive routing and low-rank write directions, and local character features explain a large fraction of the model's output logits. Our results suggest that representation-theoretic mechanisms previously identified for group operations can extend beyond groups to more general structures.

</details>


### [219] [An Hybrid Quantum-Classical Diffusion Model for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07072)
*Qipeng Qian,Keli Deng,Yuntao Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable hybrid quantum-classical generative pipeline using a classical autoencoder for dimensionality reduction and a mixed-state quantum denoising diffusion model (MSQuDDPM) in latent space; reverse dynamics simplified via analytic backward propagation by predicting the clean state ρ0 at t; demonstrated on MNIST under realistic qubit budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum diffusion models face scalability hurdles when applied to classical high-dimensional data due to qubit requirements for state encoding and the computational burden of simulating large density operators. A scalable hybrid approach is needed to leverage quantum diffusion while remaining feasible on near-term hardware.

Method: 1) Train a classical autoencoder to compress data into compact latent codes suitable for small-qubit embedding. 2) Embed latent codes into a small-qubit Hilbert space and model a distribution over latent density operators with a mixed-state quantum diffusion model (MSQuDDPM). 3) Simplify reverse dynamics by predicting the clean state ρ0 at timestep t and applying an analytic backward propagation rule to perform the one-step reverse update, avoiding an explicit predictor for ρ_{t-1}. 4) Decode samples back to the original data space. 5) Validate concept on MNIST image generation.

Result: Conceptual demonstration on MNIST; shows the approach can operate within realistic qubit budgets and positions mixed-state quantum diffusion as a practical backbone for hybrid quantum-classical generative modeling.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline offers a scalable pathway for quantum diffusion-based generative modeling by leveraging classical compression to reduce quantum resource needs while preserving a quantum diffusion backbone; it enables practical exploration of quantum generative models for high-dimensional data.

Abstract: Quantum diffusion models provide a physics-consistent route to generative learning by formulating noising and denoising directly on quantum states. However, applying such models to classical high-dimensional data is constrained by the qubit cost of state encoding and the computational burden of simulating large density operators. We propose a scalable hybrid generative pipeline that combines a classical autoencoder for dimensionality reduction with a mixed-state quantum denoising diffusion probabilistic model (MSQuDDPM) operating in the learned latent space. The autoencoder compresses data into compact latent codes that can be embedded into a small-qubit Hilbert space, after which the quantum diffusion model learns a generative distribution over latent density operators and decodes samples back to the original domain. Algorithmically, we simplify the reverse dynamics by predicting an estimate of the clean state $ρ_0$ at timestep $t$ and computing the one-step reverse update via an analytic backward propagation rule, rather than learning an explicit predictor for $ρ_{t-1}$. We demonstrate the proposed approach on MNIST image generation and discuss how mixed-state quantum diffusion can serve as a practical backbone for hybrid quantum--classical generative modeling under realistic qubit budgets.

</details>


### [220] [Structural Adversarial Attacks on Relational Deep Learning under Integrity Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07089)
*Alan Gany,Bogdan Cautis,Silviu Maniu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Relational Deep Learning pipelines are vulnerable to adversarial perturbations of the upstream database under strict schema constraints; gradient-guided attacks outperform random baselines, especially for regression tasks, while classification is more robust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess robustness of relational deep learning to upstream foreign-key rewiring attacks that respect schema integrity, intractable to exhaustively search due to combinatorial edit space and graph non-additivity.

Method: Model an attacker with white-box knowledge of graph construction and model training. Restrict perturbations to upstream DB edits (rewiring foreign keys) within a global budget while preserving foreign-key validity, degree-one FK, and functional dependencies. Evaluate seven attack heuristics (2 random, 5 gradient-guided via differentiable edge masks) on RelBench rel-f1.

Result: Gradient-based attacks consistently outperform random baselines for regression tasks; gains for classification are smaller, attributed to low label-flip rates and higher local stability of classification outputs.

Conclusion: Adversarial robustness of RDL pipelines is limited under realistic schema-constrained perturbations; gradient-guided edge-masking attacks are particularly effective for regression, highlighting the need for defenses and robust evaluation under constrained, upstream perturbations.

Abstract: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) has become a standard methodology for machine learning on relational databases: the database is encoded as a heterogeneous temporal graph in which tuples become nodes and primary-key to foreign-key (PK-FK) dependencies become typed edges, over which a graph neural network is trained for downstream prediction. We study the adversarial robustness of this pipeline. We consider a white-box attacker who knows how the graph is built and the model is trained, reasons about perturbations on the graph, but can only act on the upstream database, by rewiring foreign-key references while preserving the integrity constraints of the schema (foreign-key validity, the degree-one FK constraint, and functional dependencies). This restricts the attacker to a constrained, combinatorial set of admissible edits under a global perturbation budget, which is intractable to explore exhaustively and made non-additive by GNN message passing. We investigate seven attack heuristics - two random sampling baselines and five gradient-guided variants that exploit differentiable edge masks - and evaluate them on the RelBench rel-f1 benchmark. Gradient-based attacks consistently outperform random baselines on regression tasks, whereas gains on classification are smaller, which we attribute to low label-flip rates and greater local stability of classification outputs.

</details>


### [221] [A knowledge-augmented dataset of high-risk driving scenarios with LLM annotations for autonomous driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07103)
*Heye Huang,Jingguang Li,Zhiyuan Zhou,Paul Liang,Mingyu Wu,Kitae Jang,Jianqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: K-Risk is a knowledge-augmented driving dataset that unifies 20 European, Chinese, and U.S. trajectory datasets to produce 31,398 high-risk events and 1,036 near-collision extreme cases, each with synchronized trajectories, metadata, and language triplets (some with causal risk analyses and action recommendations) vetted in simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the scarcity and limited labeling of high-risk, long-tail driving events in existing data; enable interpretable language supervision and verifiable decision-making for risk-aware autonomous driving.

Method: A unified risk-centric extraction pipeline aggregates diverse trajectory datasets, generates large-language-model–based semantic annotations for safety-critical scenarios, and produces synchronized trajectory–metadata–language triplets. It includes an extreme-subset curation, abnormal-behavior signals, and a subset with causal risk analyses and action recommendations, validated through a closed-loop simulator with iterative reflection.

Result: Release of a standardized, multi-source dataset comprising 31,398 high-risk events and 1,036 extreme near-collision events, each with trajectories, metadata, and language annotations; a subset includes causal analyses and action recommendations validated in simulation; provides a foundation for risk-aware autonomous driving development and evaluation.

Conclusion: K-Risk links structured driving data with semantic reasoning and decision supervision, enabling standardized evaluation and development of risk-aware autonomous driving agents across diverse driving contexts.

Abstract: Safe autonomous driving requires both rapid responses to common high-risk events and deeper reasoning over rare, extreme long-tail scenarios in traffic safety. These scenarios are severely under-represented in naturalistic driving data, and existing trajectory and language-augmented datasets seldom provide high-risk event labels, semantic annotations, and verifiable safety signals.
