Here we present K-Risk, a knowledge-augmented dataset that combines structured driving trajectories with large language model generated semantic annotations for safety-critical driving scenarios. K-Risk integrates 20 human-driven and autonomous-vehicle trajectory datasets from Europe, China, and the United States, covering highways, urban freeways, intersections, and roundabouts. Using a unified risk-centric extraction pipeline, K-Risk curates 31,398 high-risk events, together with a 1,036-event extreme subset of near-collision cases. Each event is released as a synchronized trajectory, metadata, and language triplet containing structured scenario descriptions, abnormal-behavior notifications, and, for a representative subset, causal risk analyses and action recommendations validated through a closed-loop simulator with iterative reflection. By combining multi-dimensional risk annotations, interpretable language supervision, and verifiable decisions, K-Risk bridges structured traffic trajectories, semantic reasoning, and decision supervision, providing a standardized foundation for developing and evaluating next-generation risk-aware autonomous driving agents.

</details>


### [222] [Distributed Sparse Interventions in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07128)
*Maximilian S. Ernst,Lorenz Linhardt,Aaron Peikert,Oliver Eberle*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI), a neuron-level method for causally steering instruction-tuned language models by identifying sparse, nonlinear neuron sets that elicit task behavior, outperforming global activation-space approaches and enabling fine-grained control and interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand causal contributions of individual neurons and their nonlinear interactions to task behavior, addressing the limitations of steering via global, linear activation-space directions.

Method: DSI identifies sparse, distributed neuron sets across layers that interact nonlinearly to elicit task-relevant computations. It accounts for neuron interactions beyond linear additivity and uses a set-based perspective to analyze neuron roles, demonstrating that as few as 0.01% of neurons can activate task behavior across multiple tasks.

Result: DSI can activate task behavior by intervening on tiny, distributed neuron sets, localizes task-relevant neuron groups, reveals nonlinear neuron effects not captured by prior methods, and provides interpretability through set-based analysis of neuron roles across tasks.

Conclusion: Sparse, distributed, neuron-level interventions enable fine-grained control over model behavior and offer deeper insights into task composition, suggesting a powerful approach for causal analysis and steering of instruction-tuned language models.

Abstract: Language models perform a wide range of tasks at varying levels of abstraction with the capacity to flexibly infer tasks from context, execute multiple tasks simultaneously, and select among competing tasks. To study the role of model components in task behaviour, their causal influence can be investigated through interventions. Prior work on model steering has largely focused on interventions along global directions in activation space, modeling task representations as approximately linear and additive. By studying interventions at the neuron level, we find substantial, neuron-specific nonlinear effects on model outputs that are not captured by current steering approaches. We introduce Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI), an intervention approach that considers nonlinearities and interactions between neurons across layers to identify sparse sets of neurons that elicit task-relevant computations. Across a range of tasks, we demonstrate that DSI can activate task behaviour in instruction-tuned language models by localising and intervening on as few as 0.01% of neurons, highlighting the effectiveness of sparse, distributed interventions in the neuron basis. Additionally, adopting a set-based perspective enables computations over the identified neuron sets, offering insights into the roles of individual neurons by analysing their effects across tasks. Through sparse interventions, DSI enables fine-grained control over model behaviour, localisation of task-relevant neuron sets, and furthers our understanding of task composition.

</details>


### [223] [Fractal KV-Cache Archives: Lossless Symbolic Storage with In-Place Retrieval for Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07144)
*Vladimir Gusev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A contractive iterated-map code-based archive stores quantized KV caches as low-dimensional vectors, enabling exact O(1) access, linear-time processing, and in-vector substring search, achieving 36–54x storage reduction with modest perplexity cost, and revealing a key/value quantization asymmetry to guide hybrid bit allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the memory footprint of long-context KV caches in autoregressive inference while preserving access patterns and enabling efficient search on stored representations.

Method: Apply contractive iterated-map codes to serialize quantized KV symbol streams into low-dimensional vectors; use per-head residual vector quantization; evaluate on GPT-2 with 1024-token contexts, keeping a small exact window and archiving the rest; analyze key vs value quantization and implement a hybrid scheme; demonstrate queryable vector archive with direct decoding of matched context.

Result: The archive is lossless, linear-time, with O(1) random access and amortized append. It achieves 36–54x archival reduction versus an fp16 cache at 11–15% perplexity cost. Key quantization is ~4× more damaging than value quantization. The archive also serves as a search index enabling approximate substring queries directly on stored vectors and decoding matched context without raw surrounding text. All code is released and reproducible on a laptop CPU.

Conclusion: The proposed vector-archive approach provides a practical, lossless, and search-enabled storage format for quantized KV caches, with empirical guidance on allocating bits between keys and values and strong reproducibility on commodity hardware.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache dominates the memory cost of long-context autoregressive inference, and a growing body of work compresses it through quantization, eviction, or offloading. We study a complementary question: once a position's KV state has been quantized to codebook indices, how should the resulting symbol stream be stored, and can the storage layer do more than store? A family of contractive iterated-map codes that serialize a symbol sequence into a sequence of low-dimensional real vectors is revisited, and it is shown that they form a natural archive format for a quantized KV cache with the following features. The method provides exactly the access pattern a growing cache requires. It is lossless, it runs in linear time, and supports O(1) random access and O(1) amortized append. A controlled study of the quantizer feeding this archive is conducted on GPT-2 with 1024-token contexts. Keeping a small exact window (4 attention sinks + 32 recent tokens) and archiving the rest, per-head residual vector quantization reduces the archived cache by 36-54x relative to an fp16 cache at a perplexity cost of 11-15%, and we quantify a sharp key/value asymmetry -- quantizing keys is roughly 4x more damaging than quantizing values, consistent with prior low-bit KV work -- and use it to allocate bits in a hybrid scheme. Finally, we show the archive is simultaneously a search index: approximate substring queries execute directly on the stored vectors, and matched context is decoded from the matched vector without ever materializing the surrounding text. We release all code; every number reproduces from a single command on a laptop CPU.

</details>


### [224] [Prior-matched evaluation of operational Earth-observation classifiers: a three-number reporting method demonstrated on Sentinel-1 internal-wave detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07146)
*Joao Pinelo,Joao Goncalves,Arun Shukla,Adriana Santos-Ferreira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A rare-event detector for internal solitary waves shows that training with a fixed, balanced prior inflates reported precision; when evaluated with the true operational prior, precision collapses. The paper proposes a prior-matched reporting framework using balanced-test, operational-prior, and real post-deployment metrics, achieving high precision (0.927) at the true prior while maintaining a recall floor (0.80) and leak-free calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To correct evaluation bias in rare-event detection for Earth observation, where training/test priors differ from real-world priors, leading to overestimated performance and misallocation of adjudication resources.

Method: Introduce a prior-matched reporting protocol with three metrics (balanced-test, operational-prior, real post-deployment). Maintain a minimum recall floor, enforce leakage-controlled development, and monitor performance as the stream reveals the prior. Use feature aggregation and a fixed operating point calibrated to the prior rather than the training prior.

Result: Balanced-test precision was 0.794 but real-world precision at the operational prior was 0.192. Under the proposed prior-matched reporting, maintaining 0.80 recall yielded 0.927 precision at the operational prior; an out-of-time check showed discrimination transferred to unseen periods, whereas a fixed operating point did not.

Conclusion: Prior-matched reporting enables honest, deployment-relevant evaluation and improves rare-event detectors in Earth observation by aligning training/test reporting with the data prior that will be encountered, enabling reliable transfer and efficient use of adjudication resources.

Abstract: The Internal Waves Service screens the Sentinel-1 Wave-mode archive for internal solitary waves, routing detections to experts whose adjudication time is the resource the effort exists to conserve. Because attention is the cost of error, precision leads. Its classifier was trained and reported at a one-to-one class balance, fixed before the operational rate could be known. That rate has since emerged at roughly one scene in twenty, and a balanced-test score badly overstates the precision a validator meets. A model that scores 0.794 balanced-test precision scores 0.192 in real operation: the gap is a systematic artefact of reporting at the wrong prior, invisible to the metric most work quotes. We show the mismatch to be an evaluation problem in the costume of a training one at a fixed recall, prior correction and calibration cannot move precision, and answer it with a prior-matched reporting method based on three figures: balanced-test, operational-prior, and real post-deployment, whose contrast is the honest measure. A precision-first, leakage-controlled development cycle then improves the classifier lever by lever, each promoted only against a pre-registered margin; added capacity not clearing it, calibration inert, feature aggregation the one real lift, so the honest negatives are as much a result as the gain. Holding recall at a floor of 0.80 and certifying against a sealed, single-read lockbox, the promoted model reports 0.927 precision at the operational prior; an out-of-time check confirms discrimination transfers to unseen periods while a fixed operating point does not. Prior-matched reporting, begin balanced, then move to the prior as the stream reveals it, transfers to any operational Earth-observation service bootstrapping a rare-event detector under a prior it has yet to discover.

</details>


### [225] [Information Allocation Dynamics in Neural Network Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07156)
*Zhang Gongyue,Liu Donghan,Ren Weihong,Sheng Yixuan,Wang Zhiyong,Liu Honghai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reframes optimizer implicit bias as dynamic allocation of training signals between weight- and bias-like parameter pathways, controlled by a continuous preconditioning exponent p; analyzes a minimal linear model to show how preconditioning modulates updates and impacts training dynamics and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work attributes bias to final solution geometry; this work seeks an internal mechanism during training by viewing bias as part of update dynamics rather than only as a property of the end state.

Method: Introduce information allocation dynamics; analyze the update contributions of weight and bias to the same residual in a minimal linear model; derive preconditioned updates with exponent p; show how different preconditioning factors adjust the relative strength of weight vs bias corrections, i.e., two projection pathways of the residual.

Result: Demonstrates that optimizer implicit bias is reflected in the training dynamics, not just the final solution; the weight correction preserves input-dependent residuals while the bias correction preserves the residual mean direction; preconditioning controls their relative strength, affecting parameter trajectories and generalization.

Conclusion: Shifts analysis of optimizer implicit bias from solution-space geometry to update dynamics during training; the relative write-in ratio of training signals across weight- and bias-like pathways is a key dynamical mechanism influencing trajectories and generalization.

Abstract: Different optimizers have different update biases, but these biases are usually implicit. Existing studies mainly analyze or control such biases from the geometry of the final solution. However, how optimizer bias forms during training still lacks a clear internal mechanism.
  This paper proposes an information allocation dynamics perspective. It interprets optimizer implicit bias as the relative allocation of training signals between weight-like and bias-like parameter pathways. This allocation can be described and adjusted by a continuous preconditioning exponent \(p\).
  To characterize this mechanism, we first analyze the update contributions of weight and bias to the same residual signal in a minimal linear model. The weight correction term preserves input-dependent residual signals, while the bias correction term preserves the residual mean direction. They therefore correspond to different projection pathways of the residual signal. After substituting the preconditioned update into the residual update equation, the optimizer can change the relative strength of the weight correction term and the bias correction term through different preconditioning factors. Therefore, optimizer implicit bias is not only reflected in the final solution or the global training trajectory. It is also reflected in the relative write-in ratio of training signals across different parameter pathways.
  Overall, this paper moves the analysis of optimizer implicit bias from solution-space geometry to update dynamics during training. It reveals that the relative update allocation between weight and bias-like parameters is an important dynamical mechanism that affects parameter trajectories and generalization behavior.

</details>


### [226] [Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization for Multi-Task Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07178)
*Zetian Hu,Shunyu Liu,Junjie Zhang,Yongcheng Jing,Ting-En Lin,Yongbin Li,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EPPO introduces entropy-aware per-task adaptive clipping to coordinate exploration across tasks in multi-task agentic LLMs, addressing inter-task entropy crossovers and outperforming GRPO-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-task agentic RL, tasks influence each other through entropy dynamics: easy tasks can prematurely converge to low-entropy policies that hinder harder tasks, while harder tasks can keep easy tasks in a high-entropy exploration regime. This inter-task interaction causes instability and crossovers in entropy, motivating a mechanism to align exploration-exploitation pace across tasks.

Method: Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization (EPPO) uses a task-wise dynamic clipping mechanism that replaces the fixed GRPO clipping threshold with a per-task, entropy-aware adaptive bound. The bound tightens updates for over-confident tasks and relaxes them for under-explored tasks, coordinating entropy across tasks during learning.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multi-task agentic benchmarks show EPPO achieves superior performance compared with baselines (including GRPO-based methods), indicating more stable optimization and better overall multi-task learning performance.

Conclusion: Entropy-aware, per-task adaptive clipping is an effective mechanism to mitigate inter-task entropy crossovers in multi-task agentic RL, enabling more stable and effective simultaneous learning across diverse tasks; the approach may generalize to other multi-task RL settings.

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs of Reinforcement Learning (RL) have highlighted its potential for complex agentic Large Language Model (LLM) tasks. However, existing efforts largely focus on single-task settings, whereas real-world deployment necessitates a generalist agent capable of solving multiple tasks simultaneously. In this work, we identify a critical yet underexplored phenomenon in multi-task agentic RL: different tasks can exhibit exploration-exploitation pace mismatch. Specifically, easier tasks may converge early to low-entropy policies that hinder learning on harder tasks, while harder tasks can, in turn, push easier tasks back toward high-entropy exploration. This back-and-forth interaction creates inter-task entropy crossovers and frequent entropy spikes. Inspired by this observation, we introduce Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization (EPPO) for multi-task agentic LLMs, which coordinates entropy across tasks to stabilize multi-task optimization. At the core of EPPO is a task-wise dynamic clipping mechanism that replaces the fixed clipping threshold in Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a task entropy-aware adaptive bound, tightening updates for over-confident tasks while relaxing them for under-explored ones. Experiments on the multi-task agentic benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed EPPO yields results superior to its counterparts.

</details>


### [227] [Predicting LLM Safety Before Release by Simulating Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07184)
*Marcus Williams,Hannah Sheahan,Cameron Raymond,Tomek Korbak,Deng Pan,Peilin Yang,Leon Maksin,Ningyi Xie,Phillip Guo,Ian Kivlichan,Micah Carroll*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deployment simulation regenerates model responses from fixed prefixes using prior de-identified conversations to estimate post-deployment misbehavior; it yields more accurate, evaluation-aware prevalence estimates than traditional pre-deployment tests and can be seeded from public data for external validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-deployment safety tests typically suffer from limited coverage, unrepresentativeness, and recognizable test formats, making it hard to predict real-world misbehavior. A deployment-grounded evaluation aims to bridge the gap between test data and production risk.

Method: Take de-identified conversations from a prior deployment, fix the initial conversation prefix, and regenerate the next response using a candidate model. Audit generated responses for novel misalignments and estimatemisbehavior prevalence. Evaluate across four GPT-5-series deployments with outcome-blinded predictions for GPT-5.4 and retrospective analyses of earlier releases. Investigate tool-use resampling realism and explore seeding from public chat datasets to enable external replication.

Result: Deployment simulation produced informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior and outperformed baselines based on adversarial production data. Evaluation-aware estimates were closer to production traffic than traditional evaluations. Tool resampling realism emerged as a central challenge but is solvable; public data seeding can still yield informative results for external researchers.

Conclusion: Deployment simulation is a practical approach to forecast real-world model behavior and quantify deployment risk, offering improved evidence over conventional evaluations and enabling external, data-agnostic replication with public datasets.

Abstract: Pre-deployment safety evaluations aim to inform the downstream risks of releasing a new AI model. Yet most evaluations provide limited evidence about how often undesired model behavior will occur in deployment: they generally have insufficient coverage, are unrepresentative, and are generally recognizable as tests. To address these concerns, we study a simple way to simulate a model deployment: starting from de-identified conversations from a previous model deployment, we hold fixed the initial conversation prefix and regenerate the next response using a candidate model. The resulting responses can then both be audited for novel misalignments and used to estimate the prevalence of model misbehavior before deployment. We evaluate deployment simulation across four GPT-5-series deployments, using registered, outcome-blinded predictions for GPT-5.4 and retrospective analyses of three earlier releases. We find that deployment simulation produces informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior rates and outperforms baselines based on adversarially selected production data; its evaluation-awareness point estimates were also much closer to production traffic than those from traditional evaluations. We also identify the realism of tool resampling as a central challenge for further improving predictions and share results suggesting that this challenge is surmountable even in complex tool-use settings. Finally, we show that deployment simulation can be seeded from public chat datasets and remain informative about production misbehavior rates, suggesting a path for external researchers to run deployment-grounded evaluations without access to private production logs. Overall, deployment simulation helps evaluators forecast how language models will behave in the real world and supports more quantitative assessment of deployment risk.

</details>


### [228] [Geometric--Nongeometric Optimizer Calculus: A Modular Language for Reachable Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07206)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a modular theory-language for adaptive optimizers (geometric and nongeometric modules) with a direction-expressivity result, restricted direction residuals, and Pareto design over module budgets; includes diagnostic prototypes but no universal SOTA claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To audit and compare adaptive optimizers via explicit oracle/budget/state/rule constraints, separating geometry from nongeometric mechanisms to understand which directions are available and how they are used.

Method: Introduce geometric--nongeometric optimizer calculus with modules: geometric cometric (positive definite mapping covectors to directions) and nongeometric modules (information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, discretization). Prove a direction-expressivity theorem (away from critical points: full metric expresses exactly the strict descent directions). Define restricted direction residuals for admissible metric families; derive exact expressivity for diagonal and block geometries; lift residuals to trajectory-level residual complexity; propose a Pareto optimization over module budgets as the design problem. Include diagnostic prototypes (full-metric probe on deterministic quadratic benchmarks; Muon-style PyTorch candidate) as evidence for the language. 

Result: Establishes a formal calculus to audit and compare optimizers via a modular language. Proves that, under certain conditions, metric geometry can exactly capture descent directions; characterizes residuals for specific metric structures; introduces trajectory-level complexity; frames optimizer design as Pareto optimization over module budgets; provides preliminary diagnostics rather than claiming state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: The work is a theory-and-benchmark language manuscript that provides a modular, auditable framework for optimizer design and analysis, emphasizing trade-offs over a single universal optimizer and offering initial diagnostic prototypes rather than large-scale empirical claims.

Abstract: Adaptive optimizers mix several mechanisms: a metric or preconditioner maps gradients to descent directions, while estimation, memory, step-size control, constraints, stochasticity, target modification, and discretization determine which directions are available and how they are used. We introduce geometric--nongeometric optimizer calculus, a modular language for auditing reachable gradient methods under explicit oracle, budget, state, and rule constraints. The geometric module is a positive cometric family that maps covectors to parameter-space directions; the nongeometric modules are information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, and discretization mechanisms. The main formal result is a direction-expressivity theorem: away from critical points, full positive-definite geometry expresses exactly the strict descent directions. We then define restricted direction residuals for admissible metric families, prove exact expressivity conditions for diagonal and block geometries, and separate this direction-level diagnostic from condition-number geometric complexity. The resulting design problem is a Pareto optimization over module budgets, not a single universal optimizer ordering. We also lift pointwise residuals to a trajectory-level residual complexity that couples direction mismatch with the variation of the explaining geometry. We include diagnostic prototypes only as evidence for the language: a high-information full-metric probe solves deterministic quadratic benchmarks to numerical precision, while a practical Muon-style PyTorch candidate gives small-scale evidence that matrix-operator updates can be audited by the calculus. The paper is a theory and benchmark-language manuscript; it does not claim large-scale optimizer state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [229] [HPG-Diff: Hierarchical physics-guided diffusion with differentiable connectivity constraints for topology optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07233)
*Jinbo Yang,Mingyue Yuan,Boyuan Zhang,Yoshifumi Kitamura,Shikai Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based topology optimization method with physics-guided hierarchical features and a floating-material suppression loss that improves generalization and connectivity; demonstrated by low compliance errors and reduced floating artifacts, with preliminary adaptation to rectangular non-square domains via LoRA fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative diffusion models offer rapid design exploration for topology optimization but lack intrinsic physics guidance, leading to poor generalization to unseen boundary conditions and floating material artifacts.

Method: Two mechanisms: (1) hierarchical physics-guided strategy that aligns precomputed physics features with the denoising process to steer material distribution toward optimal load paths; (2) floating material suppression loss, a differentiable connectivity constraint inspired by thermal conduction, implemented as a virtual heat propagation from load positions to penalize floating material during training. Additional note: lightweight LoRA fine-tuning enables adaptation to rectangular non-square domains; validated on 3:1 rectangular domain with cantilever and bridge benchmarks.

Result: Average compliance error: 0.87% in-distribution; 5.29% out-of-distribution. Floating material ratios: 2.90% in-distribution; 2.44% out-of-distribution. Case studies on a 3:1 rectangular domain (cantilever and bridge) show preliminary evidence that LoRA fine-tuning with a small dataset can adapt HPG-Diff to rectangular domains.

Conclusion: HPG-Diff improves physics-consistency and connectivity in diffusion-based topology optimization, enhancing generalization to unseen boundary conditions; preliminary evidence that lightweight adaptation (LoRA) enables extension to rectangular non-square domains; promising for rapid, robust design exploration under varied loading conditions.

Abstract: Deep generative models offer a promising paradigm for topology optimization, enabling rapid design exploration. However, these approaches lack intrinsic physics guidance, often leading to poor generalizability across unseen boundary conditions and the formation of floating material artifacts. To address these limitations, we propose Hierarchical Physics-Guided Diffusion (HPG-Diff), a novel diffusion framework that enforces physics consistency through two synergistic mechanisms. First, we introduce a hierarchical physics-guided strategy that aligns different precomputed physics features with the denoising process, guiding material distribution toward optimal load paths to enhance generalizability. Second, we propose a floating material suppression loss as a differentiable connectivity constraint inspired by thermal conduction to improve topological connectivity. By simulating a virtual heat propagation process from load positions, this mechanism explicitly penalizes floating material during training. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate that HPG-Diff achieves average compliance errors of 0.87% (in-distribution) and 5.29% (out-of-distribution), while reducing floating material ratios to 2.90% and 2.44%, respectively. Furthermore, case studies on a 3:1 rectangular domain, including cantilever and bridge benchmarks, provide preliminary evidence that lightweight LoRA fine-tuning with a small dataset can support the adaptation of HPG-Diff to rectangular non-square domains.

</details>


### [230] [ORCAID: Oblique Rule-Based Continuous-Action Interpretation for Deep RL Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07235)
*Ignacio D. Lopez-Miguel,Ezio Bartocci,Thomas Eiter,Martin Tappler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An oblique decision-tree approach (ORCAID) to distill interpretable rule-based policies from deep RL with continuous action spaces, achieving competitive performance with compact representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explainability in RL, especially for continuous action spaces; difficult to extract interpretable policies from deep agents in complex environments.

Method: Proposes ORCAID: an efficient oblique decision-tree training algorithm that partitions the state space with hyperplanes and fits local linear policies. Three-stage split search: random initialization, local refinement, backward elimination. Adjacent leaves are merged to produce concise interpretable rules describing the deep RL policy.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple RL environments shows that the extracted rule-based policies retain strong performance with a small number of parameters and can even improve the original deep RL policy.

Conclusion: ORCAID provides an effective and compact method for explaining and potentially enhancing deep RL policies in mixed continuous-discrete, continuous-action settings; the approach balances interpretability and performance and may enable broader deployment of RL agents.

Abstract: Explainability remains a key issue in reinforcement learning (RL). Distilling an interpretable policy from an agent trained in a complex environment is particularly challenging when the action space is continuous. We introduce ORCAID, a novel method for extracting interpretable rule-based policies from RL agents operating in mixed continuous-discrete environments with continuous action spaces. Our main contribution is an efficient oblique decision tree training algorithm that partitions the state space by hyperplanes and fits local linear models. The key idea lies in a three-stage split search: efficient random initialization, local refinement, and backward elimination. Finally, adjacent leaves are merged to yield a concise set of interpretable rules describing a given deep RL policy. We evaluate ORCAID across multiple RL environments, demonstrating that the extracted rule-based policies maintain strong performance with a low number of parameters and can even be used to improve the performance of the original deep RL policy.

</details>


### [231] [FMMVCC: Fuzzy Mamba-based Multi-View Contrastive Clustering for Univariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07258)
*Donato Cerciello,Leonardo Schiavo,Angel Panizo-LLedot,Javier Huertas Tato,David Camacho*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes FMMVCC, a scalable, Mamba-based deep clustering framework for time series that uses state-space sequence modeling and multi-view self-supervised learning to achieve strong clustering performance with linear complexity across 15 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlabeled time-series data and the need to capture long-range dependencies efficiently motivate a scalable unsupervised clustering approach.

Method: FMMVCC combines a Mamba-based framework with state-space sequence modeling to obtain linear-time representations and employs multi-view self-supervised learning with temporal masking and data augmentations.

Result: On 15 benchmarks, it outperforms baselines, attaining best metrics in 29/60 and the highest average rank across scenarios.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effective, scalable deep clustering for time series with strong empirical gains and efficient representations.

Abstract: In many realistic scenarios, large volumes of time series data are generated with limited or expensive annotations. This limitation makes supervised learning methods difficult to apply and leads to the use of unsupervised approaches capable of discovering meaningful structures directly from raw data. Clustering therefore plays a crucial role in organizing time series into groups that share similar temporal patterns, enabling exploratory analysis and downstream tasks without requiring manual labeling. However, existing deep clustering methods often struggle to capture long-range temporal dependencies or rely on architectures with high computational cost. This paper introduces FMMVCC, a Mamba-based deep clustering framework for time series that leverages state space sequence modeling to efficiently learn temporal representations with linear complexity. Additionally, it utilizes multi-view self-supervised learning with temporal masking and augmentations. Experimental evaluation in 15 benchmark datasets proves that FMMVCC consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving the best overall performance in 29 of 60 total metric evaluations and the highest average rank in all tested scenarios.

</details>


### [232] [BubbleSH: A Dataset of Rising Bubbles with Deformable Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07275)
*Rachna Ramesh,Kiet Bennema ten Brinke,Douwe Orij,Ivo Roghair,Vlado Menkovski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BubbleSH: a lightweight yet physically expressive 3D bubbly-flow dataset from DNS for bubble swarm dynamics; includes time-resolved trajectories, velocities, and spherical-harmonics shape representations; offers metrics and a benchmark for equivariant probabilistic emulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for high-fidelity, compact datasets capturing deformable bubble interactions to enable data-driven modeling of chaotic multiphase flows.

Method: Direct numerical simulations of rising bubbles in a periodic domain; extract bubble trajectories, velocities, and shape coefficients (spherical harmonics); analyze kinematics, morphology, and interactions; propose trajectory/shape prediction metrics; evaluate a permutation- and translation-equivariant probabilistic emulator.

Result: A new dataset and benchmark: BubbleSH with features and evaluation metrics; demonstrated usefulness for learning distributions over future trajectories; provides a compact, high-fidelity resource for deformable multiphase systems.

Conclusion: BubbleSH enables robust data-driven modeling of deformable multiphase flows and serves as a benchmark for equivariant generative models; fosters development of bubble-flow simulators.

Abstract: Bubbly flows exhibit complex multiscale dynamics, with deformable bubbles interacting through the surrounding liquid and giving rise to strongly coupled kinematic and morphological behavior. We present BubbleSH, a bubbly flows dataset consisting of transient, three-dimensional bubble-swarm dynamics obtained from high-fidelity direct numerical simulations of bubbles rising in a periodic domain. The dataset provides time-resolved bubble trajectories, velocities, and shape evolution, with bubble morphology compactly represented using spherical harmonics. Designed to be lightweight yet physically expressive, the dataset enables data-driven modeling of bubbly flow simulators where shape deformation and bubble-bubble interactions play a central role. We characterize the dataset with bubble kinematics, morphology, and interaction patterns, and introduce evaluation metrics for both trajectory and shape prediction. The sensitivity of bubble-swarm dynamics to local perturbations makes BubbleSH particularly well suited to generative models that learn distributions over possible future trajectories. We evaluate a permutationally and translationally equivariant probabilistic emulator on BubbleSH given the proposed metrics. Therefore, we establish a compact, high-fidelity dataset and a benchmark for developing and evaluating data-driven models of deformable, chaotic multiphase systems.

</details>


### [233] [Nonlinear Bandit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07304)
*Tianshuo Zheng,Ting Wu,Zhi-Hua Zhou,Keqin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: We propose an adaptive Huber-based, one-pass online algorithm (EHM) for generalized linear bandits under heavy-tailed noise, achieving near-optimal regret with O(1) per-round computation. Extensions include PGLB-EHM for piecewise-constant contexts, NB-EHM for nonlinear bandits with bisection, and an affine-lifting approach enabling sublinear regret for general NB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-tailed noise in real-world bandit problems (e.g., recommendations, finance, medicine) motivates robust, efficient algorithms with strong regret guarantees. The work additionally addresses two extensions: piecewise-constant contexts and nonlinear bandits, seeking to preserve regret order and computational efficiency.

Method: Builds on online mirror descent (OMD) with an adaptive Huber loss (EHM). Features one-pass update with O(1) time per round and horizon. Utilizes a special link-function property to remove the need for a certain parameter. Extends to piecewise-constant contexts to yield PGLB-EHM. For nonlinear bandits, introduces NB-EHM with a bisection procedure and restrictions. Applies affine lifting to extend NB-EHM to general NB.

Result: GLB: regret bounded by Õ(T^{1/(1+ε)}), i.e., almost optimal. PGLB-EHM preserves the same regret order under piecewise-constant contexts. NB-EHM achieves sublinear regret with its construction; affine lifting allows applying NB-EHM to general NB with sublinear regret.

Conclusion: The EHM framework provides a robust, computationally efficient approach for GLB under heavy-tailed noise, with strong regret guarantees and versatile extensions to contextual piecewise settings and nonlinear bandits via NB-EHM and affine lifting.

Abstract: In this paper we first study the problem of generalized linear bandit (GLB) under heavy-tailed noise. The characteristics of heavy-tailed distributions are widely observed in real-world applications such as personalized recommendation, financial markets, and medical treatments. Based on the online mirror descent (OMD) method, we propose an algorithm EHM that extends the adaptive Huber loss method (Wang et al., 2025) with one-pass update ($\mathcal{O}(1)$ computational complexity with respect to current round $t$ and the time horizon $T$), which simultaneously achieves an almost optimal regret of $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T^{\frac{1}{1+ε}})$ where $T$ is the time horizon. In addition, by utilizing a special property of some link function (Sawarni et al., 2025), our algorithm eliminates the need to know a commonly used parameter. Next, we study the GLB problem under the case when contextual characteristic becomes piecewise constant, and we slightly revised former algorithm to obtain the PGLB-EHM algorithm. After theoretical analysis, we prove that the regret upper bound order stays the same. Furthermore, we look deeper into a special case of nonlinear bandit (NB) and present the NB-EHM algorithm with bisection method and special restriction. Eventually we utilize the affine lifting approach and show that the general NB problem can be applied with NB-EHM to achieve a sublinear regret bound.

</details>


### [234] [FedCVESA: Taking Away Training Data in Federated Learning via Correlation Value Encoding and Segmented Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07314)
*Chongkai Li,Bang Zhang,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCVESA demonstrates a white-box TATD attack in federated learning where a malicious server encodes private training data into model parameters (carrier parameters) via a Pearson-correlation regularizer, using segmented aggregation to preserve those carriers, enabling retrieval of private data while maintaining acceptable main-task performance under Dirichlet-non-IID splits on MNIST/Fashion-MNIST/CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess privacy risks of memorization-based data exfiltration in federated learning when a central server can influence global model updates, and determine whether training data can be covertly encoded into parameters despite multi-client averaging.

Method: Propose FedCVESA, a federated variant of CVEA. The server selects n target clients and incentivizes encoding of private data into certain carrier parameters by adding a Pearson-correlation regularizer to the loss of target clients. Introduce segmented aggregation to protect carrier parameters from being overwritten by standard averaging. Evaluate on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10 with Dirichlet non-IID partitions, comparing data leakage against main-task accuracy.

Result: Experiments show semantically meaningful private training images can be reconstructed from the trained model, indicating a parameter-level memorization channel. The attack maintains acceptable main-task utility in a controlled setting, demonstrating FL can be exploited for active TATD in a white-box malicious-server scenario.

Conclusion: FL poses privacy risks beyond gradient leakage; under a white-box malicious server, training data can be covertly memorized in model parameters. This calls for defense strategies (secure aggregation, differential privacy, robust aggregation) and more systematic analysis of memory-based threats in FL.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) avoids explicit data exposure by keeping raw data on local clients, yet privacy risks remain in the training process and the learned model itself. Recently, centralized Taking Away Training Data (TATD) attacks have shown that malicious training could abuse the memorization capacity of deep models to store and later recover training data. However, this memorization-based threat has not been systematically studied under FL environments, where multi-client averaging could overwrite encoded training data. In this paper, we study a white-box TATD attack in which a malicious server selects n target clients from K participating clients and actively writes private training data into the global model during federated training. We propose FedCVESA, a federated variant of Correlation Value Encoding Attack (CVEA), by adding a Pearson-correlation regularizer to the loss function of target clients, so that private training data are gradually encoded into selected model parameters, referred to as carrier parameters. To reduce the overwriting of carrier parameters during server aggregation, we further propose segmented aggregation over dispersed carrier parameters, preserving selected carrier parameters while keeping standard averaging on the remaining parameters. Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 under Dirichlet non-IID partitions show that the proposed method can steal semantically meaningful private training images from the trained model while maintaining acceptable main-task utility in a controlled proof-of-concept setting. These results demonstrate that FL can become a parameter-level memorization channel for active TATD attack under the studied white-box malicious-server setting.

</details>


### [235] [Mechanistic Interpretability for Neural Networks: Circuits, Sparse Features and Symbolic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07316)
*Pranav Sawant,Jakub Krejčí*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of mechanistic interpretability in neural networks, focusing on Transformer circuits, disentangling polysemantic representations, and connecting neural activations to explicit logical rules via neurosymbolic AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the opacity of modern AI (black-box issue) to enable safety, auditability, and controllability in high-stakes applications.

Method: Synthesizes Transformer circuit analysis (residual stream, attention, induction heads), techniques for disentangling activations (SAEs, transcoders), methods to steer and intervene (steering vectors, causal interventions), and links to neurosymbolic frameworks.

Result: Conceptual framework for mechanistic interpretability; demonstrated decomposition of complex activations into interpretable features; proposed practical intervention mechanisms; integrated with neurosymbolic rule extraction.

Conclusion: Mechanistic interpretability is a viable path toward transparent and controllable AI, enabling translation of neural representations into executable rules; supports safer AI deployment.

Abstract: This article offers a comprehensive overview of mechanistic interpretability, an emerging field that seeks to reverse-engineer the internal algorithms of modern neural networks. While traditional explainable AI methods often stop at surface-level input-output correlations, this approach directly addresses the opaque "black box" nature of machine learning models, which is essential for ensuring safety and auditability in high-stakes deployments. The paper provides a detailed examination of Transformer circuit analysis, exploring how internal components like the residual stream, attention mechanisms, and induction heads drive complex tasks and in-context learning. It subsequently tackles the core challenge of superposition and polysemanticity, demonstrating how tools like Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) and transcoders can decompose tangled network activations into distinct, human-interpretable features. Furthermore, the paper explores methods for actively controlling and modifying model behavior through steering vectors and causal interventions. Finally, it connects these mechanistic insights with neurosymbolic AI frameworks designed to translate neural representations into explicit, executable logical rules.

</details>


### [236] [Hypergraph Neural Stochastic Diffusion: An SDE Framework for Uncertainty Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07330)
*Zhiheng Zhou,Mengyao Zhou,Dengyi Zhao,Xingqin Qi,Guiying Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces HyperNSD, a hypergraph SDE framework to model uncertainty evolution during representation learning; uses neural drift and diffusion to capture deterministic higher-order diffusion and structural noise; provides intrinsic, trajectory-based uncertainty and theoretical guarantees; empirically shows improved OOD/misclassification uncertainty with competitive accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty in hypergraphs arises from incidence-structure variations and higher-order dependencies; limitations of post-hoc or ensemble methods; need uncertainty propagation during representation learning.

Method: Hypergraph Neural Stochastic Diffusion (HyperNSD) uses a stochastic differential equation with learnable drift and diffusion networks; representations evolve over node–hyperedge incidence structures; predictive uncertainty is derived from trajectory variability; joint learning of drift/diffusion; theoretical analyses on well-posedness, perturbation stability, permutation equivariance, and numerical convergence.

Result: Empirical benchmarks show reliable uncertainty estimation for out-of-distribution and misclassification detection while maintaining competitive prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: Provides a principled stochastic-dynamical framework for trustworthy higher-order representation learning, enabling intrinsic uncertainty propagation and improved OOD/misclassification detection.

Abstract: Hypergraph neural networks have shown powerful capability in modeling higher-order relations, yet their predictive uncertainty remains underexplored. Unlike pairwise graphs, uncertainty in hypergraphs arises not only from noisy attributes and ambiguous labels, but also from variations in node-hyperedge incidence structures and complex higher-order dependencies. Existing approaches mainly estimate uncertainty from final predictions or rely on computationally expensive ensembles and Bayesian inference, limiting their ability to capture uncertainty evolution during representation learning. In this paper, we propose Hypergraph Neural Stochastic Diffusion(HyperNSD), a stochastic differential equation framework for uncertainty estimation on hypergraphs. HyperNSD models hypergraph representations as stochastic processes evolving over node-hyperedge incidence structures. A learnable drift function captures deterministic higher-order diffusion dynamics, while a learnable stochastic forcing function characterizes structural ambiguity and representation noise. Predictive uncertainty is directly quantified through the variability of stochastic representation trajectories, providing an intrinsic uncertainty measure beyond post-hoc confidence scores. We formulate HyperNSD with neural drift and diffusion networks, enabling joint learning of prediction and uncertainty propagation. Theoretical analyses establish well posedness, perturbation stability,permutation equivariance, and numerical convergence of the proposed stochastic dynamics. Experiments on multiple hypergraph benchmarks demonstrate that HyperNSD achieves reliable uncertainty estimation for out-of-distribution and misclassification detection while preserving competitive prediction accuracy. These results provide a principled stochastic-dynamical framework for trustworthy higher-order representation learning.

</details>


### [237] [Latency-Aware Bid Acceptance under Operational Feasibility: A Public Benchmark with Hindsight Ceilings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07343)
*Aswin Chandrasekaran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Public-calibrated, closed-loop FreightBidBench provides a reproducible benchmark for online truckload bid acceptance with explicit feasibility and economics, delivering tight hindsight ceilings and effective surrogate-rollout cascades that closely match rollout performance while reducing latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public, dynamic benchmarks for online bid acceptance are scarce; existing routing benchmarks are static and private-data-driven. This work creates a reproducible, dependency-free benchmark using public FAF and USDA data to enable rigorous evaluation of real-time decision policies.

Method: Develop FreightBidBench with explicit feasibility (pickup reach, windows, HOS, yard delays) and economics (penalty, terminal value, price window). Establish two hindsight ceilings: LP relaxation and a tighter Lagrangian-per-truck information relaxation preserving per-truck HOS and sequencing. Propose a parametric surrogate-rollout cascade with boundary-band and scarcity-pressure escalation triggers. Evaluate across ten seeds for tight and scarce scenarios; compare policies.

Result: Simple policy achieves 91.0%/86.5% of rollout profit; surrogate achieves 94.2%/89.3%. Cascade recovers ~98% with 40-56% of rollout decision latency. In the tight scenario, cascade is statistically indistinguishable from rollout teacher (95% CI on profit delta spans zero).

Conclusion: FreightBidBench delivers reproducible, public benchmarks for dynamic truckload bidding; the proposed relaxations yield tighter bounds; surrogate-rollout cascades offer near-optimal performance with reduced computation, demonstrating practical viability for real-time decision support.

Abstract: Online truckload bid acceptance is a closed-loop stochastic decision problem in which a carrier or broker must, in real time, accept or reject a tendered load subject to operational feasibility, fleet repositioning costs, and opportunity cost against future demand. Public, reproducible benchmarks for this problem are scarce: existing routing benchmarks are static, while dynamic-fleet studies typically rely on private operator data. We introduce FreightBidBench, a public-calibrated, dependency-free, closed-loop benchmark in which feasibility (pickup reach, appointment windows, simplified hours-of-service, stochastic yard delays) and economics (service-failure penalty, terminal fleet value, daily price-premium window) are explicit, versioned, and reproducible from public Freight Analysis Framework and U.S. Department of Agriculture truck rate data. We develop two full-horizon hindsight ceilings: a simple LP style relaxation and a tighter Lagrangian-per-truck information relaxation that retains per-truck hours-of-service and sequencing structure and is 20.7% tighter than the LP relaxation on a tight-capacity scenario and 39.3% tighter on a scarce-capacity scenario. We introduce a parametric surrogate-rollout cascade with boundary-band and scarcity-pressure escalation triggers. On ten-seed tight and scarce scenarios, the best simple policy retains 91.0% and 86.5% of rollout profit and the standard-library surrogate 94.2% and 89.3%; a cascade at a single escalation band recovers roughly 98% on both at 40-56% of rollout's mean decision latency, and on the tight scenario is statistically indistinguishable from the rollout teacher (paired-bootstrap 95% CI on the profit delta spans zero).

</details>


### [238] [Multi-Agent AI Control: Distributed Attacks Hamper Per-Instance Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07368)
*Oliver Makins,Orazio Angelini,Zohreh Shams,Mary Phuong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces multi-agent AI control and FakeLab to empirically study distributed attacks; finds a fragmentation effect where coordinated attackers elude per-agent monitoring; planner assistance worsens detection; stronger monitors help but remain imperfect; provides FakeLab to the safety community.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-agent AI control studies miss risks that arise when multiple agents act in concert on shared infrastructure. Real deployments can enable model-weight exfiltration and training-run poisoning that require coordination; hence an empirical, distributed framework is needed.

Method: Develops FakeLab: a synthetic AI lab with 9 services, 86 benign tasks, and 4 attack objectives. Conducts experiments comparing single-agent monitoring against distributed attacks, varying agent count, coordination, model capabilities, and monitoring configurations.

Result: Fragmentation effect: as agents coordinate, per-agent monitoring becomes less effective at catching attackers. The effect is not solely due to benign-to-malicious code ratio and likely depends on model capability. An explicit planner amplifies fragmentation, increasing attack completion rate up to sevenfold and weakening theory-of-mind in executors. A weak trusted monitor misses most attacks; a stronger monitor greatly reduces undetected success but does not eliminate it.

Conclusion: The study highlights the need to consider multi-agent coordination in AI control, demonstrates measurable risks with distributed attacks, and offers FakeLab for safety research to advance understanding and mitigation of such risks.

Abstract: AI control is a family of techniques to prevent an AI with malicious goals from subverting its operator's intent. AI Control usually studies a single agent in one trajectory, but real deployments run many agents over shared infrastructure, and the most severe risks (model-weight exfiltration, training-run poisoning) plausibly need several agents acting in concert. We initiate the empirical study of multi-agent AI control, formalising distributed attacks in which several agents jointly aim for a malicious goal. We develop FakeLab: a synthetic AI-lab codebase (9 services, 86 benign tasks, 4 attack objectives). We evaluate single agent monitoring against distributed attacks, varying the number of agents, their coordination, model capabilities and precise monitoring configuration.
  Our central finding is the fragmentation effect: as more agents coordinate to attack, per-agent monitoring becomes less likely to catch any of the attackers. Secondly, this is not driven by the ratio of benign to malicious code -- we argue this is likely dependent on model capability. Third, an explicit planner amplifies the fragmentation effect, raises the rate of completing the attack up to sevenfold, and mildly sharpens executors' poor monitor-theory-of-mind. Finally: a weak "trusted" monitor misses most attacks, while a stronger one cuts undetected success by more than an order of magnitude but remains imperfect. We are happy to share FakeLab, our control setting, with safety researchers on request.

</details>


### [239] [On Adversarial Vulnerability of Vision-Language Models through the Lens of Intermediate Spectral Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07375)
*Chethan Krishnamurthy Ramanaik,Tobias Callies,Michael Hecht,Eirini Ntoutsi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A white-box spectral-subspace-guided attack (SSGRA) on transformer-based vision-language models exploits the spectral structure of intermediate linear layers by aligning representations with the subspace spanned by the bottom-right singular vectors, yielding stronger adversarial attacks and new spectral insights for robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial vulnerability in DNNs has many explanations; this work targets the spectral structure of intermediate linear transformations as an underexplored mechanism, particularly in vision-language models (VLMs) with interpretable spectral decompositions, to understand and potentially improve robustness.

Method: Introduce SSGRA, a white-box attack that guides perturbations to align intermediate representations with the subspace spanned by the bottom-right singular vectors of intermediate linear transforms in transformer-based VLMs.

Result: Experiments show that SSGRA achieves improved attack effectiveness over existing baselines; provides a spectral interpretation of adversarial vulnerability in VLMs and offers insights for robustness improvement.

Conclusion: Spectral structure of DNN linear layers is a meaningful factor in adversarial vulnerability for VLMs; SSGRA provides both stronger attacks and a spectral lens for developing defenses and understanding robustness.

Abstract: Adversarial vulnerability in deep neural networks (DNNs) has been studied from the perspectives of decision-boundary geometry, feature robustness, input-output Jacobians, and the instability of inverse problems. Here, we focus on the spectral structure of intermediate linear transformations that propagate information through modern DNNs, an unexplored mechanism of adversarial vulnerability. Specifically, we investigate transformer-based vision-language models, whose linear layers admit interpretable spectral decompositions and whose widespread adoption makes understanding their robustness increasingly important. We propose a white-box spectral-subspace-guided attack (SSGRA) that aligns intermediate representations with the subspace spanned by the bottom right singular vectors. Our experiments show improved attack effectiveness over existing baselines. In addition, SSGRA offers a spectral interpretation of adversarial vulnerability in VLMs, providing insights for improving their robustness.

</details>


### [240] [Generalist Vision-Language Models for Fast Radio Burst detection: a zero-shot benchmark against a specialized detector](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07382)
*Raiff H. Santos,Amilcar R. Queiroz,Tharcisyo S. S. Duarte,K. E. L. de Farias,Rafael A. Batista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zero-shot, prompt-driven VLMs (Gemma) can rival a specialized FRB detector (SwinYNet) on simulated FRB/dynamics spectra, with strong false-positive control on structured RFI and no false positives on pure noise; three-class framing yields 86% accuracy without false FRBs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate if small open-weight generalist Vision-Language Models can detect Fast Radio Bursts in dynamic spectra without fine-tuning or labeled data, enabling flexible, zero-shot detection with natural-language justifications.

Method: Use two VLMs (Gemma 4 2B and 4B) in zero-shot, prompt-only mode on 3000 simulated L-band dynamic spectra containing FRBs, structured RFI, and noise; create a balanced binary benchmark of 2000 samples; compare against SwinYNet; measure accuracy, false-positive rates, and ROC-AUC; then rewrite prompts for a three-class FRB/RFI/noise classification on the full 3000 spectra and report accuracy and false FRB rate.

Result: Gemma 4 2B achieves 93.65% accuracy (not significantly different from SwinYNet 92.90%); Gemma has a lower structured RFI false-positive rate (6.4% vs. 25.0%) and no false positives on pure noise; SwinYNet has perfect ranking (ROC-AUC 1.0000 vs. VLM 0.9482); on three-class framing, VLMs reach up to 86% accuracy with no false FRBs.

Conclusion: Zero-shot, prompt-based generalist VLMs can approach or match a specialized FRB detector on simulated data, with competitive metrics and improved false-positive control. Prompt changes enable a three-class FRB/RFI/noise task, though performance lags the top detector in ranking and may rely on dataset realism and prompt reliability.

Abstract: Fast Radio Bursts (FRBs) are millisecond-duration radio transients whose automated detection increasingly relies on highly specialized deep learning models. These detectors achieve exceptional performance, but they require large task-specific training datasets and cannot be redefined without retraining. In this work, we evaluate whether small, open-weight, locally run generalist Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can detect FRBs in dynamic spectra under a zero-shot, prompt-only regime, with no fine-tuning and no labeled examples, returning structured decisions with a natural-language justification. From a controlled set of 3000 simulated L-band dynamic spectra containing FRBs, structured Radio Frequency Interference (RFI), and noise, we draw a balanced binary benchmark of 2000 samples and compare two such VLMs (Gemma 4 2B and 4B), sample by sample, against the state-of-the-art specialized detector SwinYNet. At the default threshold, Gemma 4 2B reaches an accuracy of 93.65%, with no statistically significant difference from SwinYNet (92.90%), while showing a significantly lower false-positive rate on structured RFI (6.4% vs. 25.0%) and no false positives on pure noise. SwinYNet retains a perfect probabilistic ranking on this benchmark (ROC-AUC of 1.0000 vs. 0.9482), a ceiling that the zero-shot VLM approaches from general-purpose pretraining alone. Rewriting the prompt alone reconfigures the same models for three-class FRB/RFI/noise classification on the full set of 3000 spectra, where they reach up to 86% accuracy without a single false FRB.

</details>


### [241] [Sparse Delta Memory: Scaling the State of Linear RNNs through Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07386)
*Loïc Cabannes,Pierre-Emmanuel Mazaré,Gergely Szilvasy,Matthijs Douze,Maria Lomeli,Ilze Amanda Auzina,Justin Carpentier,Gabriel Synnaeve,Hervé Jégou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SDM scales the hidden state of gated linear RNNs using a sparse explicit memory, enabling much larger memory capacity with the same FLOPs and parameter count, leading to improved in-context learning and long-context retrieval; learning the initial state yields further gains as a parametric memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention methods offer fixed-state, fixed-compute efficiency but suffer degraded long-context recall due to limited state size. Increasing state size raises FLOPs. A memory architecture that decouples capacity from compute could close this gap.

Method: Introduce Sparse Delta Memory (SDM), extending Gated DeltaNet by replacing dense key-value outer products with sparse reads/writes to a large explicit memory. A sparse addressing scheme enables scaling memory capacity under isoFLOP constraints with the same number of parameters. The initial state of memory can be learned, turning memory into a parametric component.

Result: With isoFLOP constraint and identical parameter count, SDM’s larger memory capacity improves performance on in-context learning and long-context retrieval tasks. Additionally, learning the initial memory state yields further improvements on common-knowledge and reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: SDM demonstrates that high-capacity memory with sparse addressing can overcome long-context limitations of linear attention models without increasing FLOPs or parameters, and that a learned initial memory state provides additional performance gains, broadening applicability to knowledge-intensive tasks.

Abstract: Linear attention models allow a fixed state size and a fixed amount of compute per token. However, due to their limited state size, linear attention models fall behind in long-context recall compared to softmax-attention-based transformer architectures. Increasing the state size of linear attention improves recall performance but at the cost of higher FLOPs. In this work, we introduce Sparse Delta Memory (SDM), an architecture that scales the hidden state of gated linear RNNs to orders of magnitude higher capacity using a sparse addressing scheme. SDM extends the Gated DeltaNet architecture by replacing the dense key-value outer product with sparse reads and writes to a large explicit memory. We show that, under an isoFLOP constraint and with an identical number of parameters, a higher state memory capacity significantly improves performance on in-context learning and long-context retrieval tasks. Moreover, by learning the initial state of the SDM memory and therefore using it as a parametric memory, we show that the model further improves on a wide range of common-knowledge and reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [242] [The Optimal Sample Complexity of Learning Autoregressive Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07423)
*Zhiyuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exact-trace learning for full autoregressive Chain-of-Thought traces in the realizable PAC setting has sample complexity bounded by the standard multiclass rate governed by the Daniely–Shalev-Shwartz (DS) dimension, and this bound is rollout-stable via a new parity-dimension. The bound is independent of rollout length and is worst-case optimal; DS dimension can increase under rollout, necessitating the parity refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the sample complexity of exact-trace learning under autoregressive rollout remains controlled and to develop a dimension that stays stable under rollout.

Method: Introduce parity dimension, based on even pseudo-cubes, as a rollout-stable refinement of DS dimension. Prove a low-coordinate spanning theorem on finite restrictions to bound one-inclusion density, showing parity-dimension bounds the learning problem. Demonstrate that DS dimension can increase under rollout, while parity-dimension does not, and relate the results to stopping rules and ordinary multiclass learning.

Result: PAC sample complexity for Roll_halt(H) is O((DSdim(H) + log(1/δ))/ε) with no dependence on rollout length. This bound is worst-case optimal since one-step stopping recovers ordinary multiclass learning. A parity dimension is introduced, which is rollout-stable and controls one-inclusion density; unlike DS-dimension, parity-dimension does not increase under autoregressive rollout.

Conclusion: A rollout-stable, near-optimal dimension (parity-dimension) bounds the sample complexity of exact-trace learning for full autoregressive chains. The DS-dimension alone can inflate under rollout, so the parity refinement is necessary to obtain rollout-independent PAC guarantees; the method confirms that one-step stopping equates to standard multiclass learning and demonstrates the necessity of the new dimension.

Abstract: We prove that, in the realizable PAC setting, the sample complexity of exact-trace learning for full autoregressive Chain-of-Thought traces is upper bounded by the standard multiclass rate of the local next-token class, where this rate is governed by the Daniely--Shalev-Shwartz dimension. Under exact-trace loss, one wrong action makes the whole trace incorrect; nevertheless, for every stopping rule $\mathtt{halt}$ and every pointwise $\mathtt{halt}$-halting local class $\mathrm{H}$, $n_{\mathrm{PAC}}^{\varepsilon,δ}(\operatorname{Roll}_{\mathtt{halt}}(\mathrm{H}))=O((\operatorname{DSdim}(\mathrm{H})+\log(1/δ))/\varepsilon)$, with no dependence on rollout length. The dependence on $\operatorname{DSdim}(\mathrm{H})$ is worst-case optimal, since one-step stopping recovers ordinary multiclass learning of $\mathrm{H}$. The proof introduces parity dimension, a rollout-stable refinement of DS dimension based on even pseudo-cubes. It controls one-inclusion density via a low-coordinate spanning theorem on finite restrictions and, unlike DS dimension itself, does not increase under autoregressive rollout. We also show why this detour is necessary: DS dimension can increase under rollout.

</details>


### [243] [RLVP: Penalize the Path, Reward the Outcome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07435)
*Bojie Li,Noah Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Penalize the action path while rewarding the final outcome to satisfy verifiable, outcome-neutral constraints and learn efficiently from few samples; four design rules are provided to implement penalties and avoid the inaction trap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world agents interact in costly, often irreversible ways where outcome-only reinforcement learning ignores essential constraints and is sample-inefficient. There is a need for learning that respects path-level constraints and remains data-efficient.

Method: Propose a dual signal: verifiable penalties on the action path combined with outcome-based rewards. Argue that penalties yield variance where outcome signals lack, provide four design rules for effective penalties, and address the inaction trap.

Result: The approach achieves high task success with near-zero constraint violations, outperforming outcome-only training which violates constraints in most episodes. It clarifies when dense progress signals help and when they do not, and offers practical rules for penalty design.

Conclusion: A practical recipe—penalize the path, reward the outcome—for real-agent learning that respects constraints and remains sample-efficient, accompanied by concrete design rules to prevent the inaction trap.

Abstract: Agents acting on our behalf in the real world (e.g. placing phone calls) must learn online from costly, often irreversible interactions rather than cheap simulator steps. Two things follow. First, deployability depends on the path, not only the outcome. An agent must respect outcome-neutral constraints such as not repeatedly calling an unresponsive user, respecting business hours, or completing required authentication constraints that outcome-based rewards cannot express, since violating them frequently improves apparent success. Second, because each interaction is expensive, the agent must learn efficiently from very few examples.
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is blind to both challenges: it optimizes solely on the outcome and wastes expensive rollouts on all-fail groups where group-relative advantage collapses to zero. Attempts to densify supervision by rewarding progress target the hard-to-verify direction. In contrast, real agentic environments can cheaply detect bad moves. Since group-relative advantage is equivalent to within-group variance, a dense signal helps only when it supplies variance the outcome lacks. A verifiable penalty on the path meets this condition reliably, while a progress potential helps only where partial progress is reachable. The resulting recipe "penalize the path, reward the outcome" achieves high task success with near-zero violations, where outcome-only training violates constraints on nearly every episode. We provide four design rules for effective penalties, including avoidance of the inaction trap that arises when a penalty is used in isolation.

</details>


### [244] [Where to Intervene? Benchmarking Fairness-Aware Learning on Differentially Private Synthetic Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07471)
*Vinícius Gabriel Angelozzi,Héber H. Arcolezi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of fairness interventions on differentially private synthetic tabular data using AIM; post-processing fairness methods offer the most stable fairness-utility trade-offs under DP; DP can harm fairness; DP+Fair yields strong fairness improvements with competitive utility; code and data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy-preserving ML in high-stakes domains via differential privacy can unintentionally worsen fairness. There is limited understanding of whether traditional fairness interventions remain effective when data is DP-synthesized; need systematic evaluation across datasets, fairness metrics, and intervention types.

Method: Benchmark study using the Adaptive Iterative Mechanism (AIM) as the state-of-the-art DP synthesizer for tabular data. Evaluate four pipeline configurations (Baseline, DP-only, Fair-only, DP+Fair) across four datasets, multiple group fairness metrics, and three fairness mitigation categories (pre-processing, in-processing, post-processing) under various privacy budgets. Compare performance across DP-synthesized data and original data.

Result: DP alone degrades both utility and fairness. Applying fairness interventions can partially restore equitable outcomes. Post-processing methods provide the most stable fairness-utility trade-offs across privacy budgets and synthesizers, achieving notable fairness gains with competitive utility. DP+Fair configuration often yields strong fairness improvements while maintaining utility.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a meaningful privacy-fairness-utility trade-off and highlights post-processing as a robust mitigation strategy under DP constraints. All code, data, and experimental artifacts are released to support reproducibility and future work in this space.

Abstract: Machine learning models are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains, raising concerns about both privacy and fairness. Differential Privacy (DP) has become a gold standard for privacy-preserving data analysis, while fairness-aware mechanisms aim to mitigate discrimination against underrepresented groups. However, these objectives can conflict: DP often amplifies disparities across demographic groups, and little is known about whether established fairness interventions remain effective under DP constraints. In this work, we present, to our knowledge, the first systematic evaluation of fairness interventions on differentially private synthetic tabular data. Our benchmark centers on the Adaptive Iterative Mechanism (AIM), identified as the state-of-the-art marginal-based DP synthesizer (Cormode et al. 2025). We thus evaluate fairness interventions across four datasets, multiple group fairness metrics, and three categories of mitigation strategies (pre-processing, in-processing, and post-processing) under a wide range of privacy budgets. We compare four pipeline configurations: (Baseline) training on original data; (DP-only) training on DP synthetic data; (Fair-only) applying fairness mechanisms on original data; and (DP+Fair) combining fairness mechanisms with DP synthetic data. Our results demonstrate that while DP alone can degrade both utility and fairness, applying fairness interventions can partially restore equitable outcomes. Among them, post-processing methods tend to provide more stable fairness-utility trade-offs across privacy budgets and synthesizers, achieving strong fairness improvements while preserving competitive utility relative to other intervention stages. We release all code, data, and experimental artifacts in an open-source repository to ensure full reproducibility and to support future research on the privacy-fairness-utility trade-off.

</details>


### [245] [FourierQK: Spectral Preprocessing of Query-Key Projections Improves Transformer Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07478)
*Athanasios Zeris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FFT-based spectral preprocessing of Q/K projections markedly improves bilateral spectral attention for character-level language modeling, with multi-scale learned frequencies delivering large gains over standard dot-product attention; effects rely on global frequency-domain mixing, not metric distortion. Causal spectral filters do not help; method preserves full attention structure and is architecturally distinct from FNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limitations of dot-product attention at character-level tokenization by enabling global, multi-scale interactions via frequency-domain mixing of Q/K projections; explore architectural boundary between bilateral spectral attention and causal spectral attention.

Method: Apply FFT-based spectral preprocessing to Q/K projections in transformer attention. Evaluate a fixed random spectral filter and multiple learned frequencies spanning paragraph to word scales. Compare against random (orthogonal/non-orthogonal) projections to test metric distortion. Assess causal filters (Gaussian, Mexican Hat, Morlet). Validate results with a shuffled-validation diagnostic for positional leakage. Relate findings to FNet and MorletQK companion work; preserve full attention score structure by applying spectral preprocessing only to Q/K.

Result: TinyShakespeare results: fixed random spectral filter val=1.031 (Delta=+0.443); single learned frequency at paragraph scale val=0.608 (Delta=+0.867); four learned frequencies spanning paragraph-to-word scales val=0.309 (Delta=+1.166) — a 79% reduction against standard dot-product attention. The single-frequency result is reproducible across three seeds (mean val=0.236, std=0.019). The four frequencies converge to a near-geometric multi-scale ordering (49, 27, 10, 6 tokens/cycle) corresponding to paragraph, sub-paragraph, phrase, and word scales.
