Random projections do not improve; causal filters do not improve; results verified via shuffled-validation diagnostic for positional leakage. The approach is architecturally distinct from FNet (which replaces attention with Fourier mixing) and preserves the full attention score structure, applying spectral preprocessing only to Q/K.

Conclusion: Spectral preprocessing of Q/K projections yields substantial gains in character-level attention by leveraging global frequency-domain mixing. This establishes bilateral spectral attention as a distinct architectural approach with a boundary from truly causal spectral attention and differentiates from prior Fourier-based methods like FNet.

Abstract: FFT-based spectral preprocessing of learned query-key (Q/K) projections substantially improves transformer attention on character-level language modelling. On TinyShakespeare: a fixed random spectral filter achieves val=1.031 (Delta=+0.443); a single learned frequency at paragraph scale achieves val=0.608 (Delta=+0.867); and four learned frequencies spanning paragraph to word scale achieve val=0.309 (Delta=+1.166), a 79% reduction over standard dot-product attention. The single-frequency result is confirmed across three random seeds (mean val=0.236, std=0.019). The four frequencies converge to a near-geometric multi-scale ordering (49, 27, 10, 6 tokens/cycle) corresponding to paragraph, sub-paragraph, phrase, and word scales. The gain is specific to spectral preprocessing: random orthogonal and non-orthogonal projections of Q/K produce no measurable improvement, suggesting the benefit comes from global frequency-domain mixing rather than metric distortion. All results are verified by a shuffled-validation diagnostic against positional leakage. Causal filters (Gaussian, Mexican Hat, Morlet) do not improve over standard attention at character-level tokenisation: the bilateral FFT kernel is structurally non-causal, coupling every position to future tokens. This defines an architectural boundary between bilateral spectral attention (this paper) and genuinely causal spectral attention at word-scale tokenisation (companion paper MorletQK). This work is architecturally distinct from FNet (Lee-Thorp et al., 2021), which replaces attention with Fourier mixing of token embeddings. Here, spectral preprocessing applies only to Q/K projections while the full attention score structure is preserved.

</details>


### [246] [Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery: A Case Study on Automated Game Testing for NHL26](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07498)
*Florian Fuchs,Jessy Gosselin-Grant,Boris Skuin,Michele Petteni,Alessandro Sestini,Joakim Bergdahl,Amir Baghi,Linus Gisslén*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAID uses a population-based RL to automatically discover diverse goal-scoring exploits in goalie AI; case study in NHL 26 finds six strategies similar to manual test findings, reducing retesting effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Testing AI behavior in games is labor-intensive; RL tends to overfit to a single solution; need methods to find multiple high-quality exploits to cover broader behavior.

Method: Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery (RAID): iteratively trains a population of goal-scoring agents using RL, with an extension to promote diversity so multiple high-quality, distinct exploits emerge.

Result: In a single deployment, RAID uncovered six distinct scoring exploit strategies that qualitatively matched human tester findings obtained from hours of manual testing.

Conclusion: RAID shows promise for automated discovery of diverse behavioral exploits, potentially reducing testing effort; future work includes broader validation, tuning diversity mechanisms, and applicability to other domains.

Abstract: Testing is a major effort for the gaming industry, requiring a significant part of development budget and people power. We present a case study on a development version of the ice hockey game EA SPORTS NHL 26, for which human playtesters test the goalie AI for behavioral exploits. To reduce the effort of re-testing the goalie AI after every game or behavior modification in the development phase, we propose Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery (RAID), a novel approach to automatically find exploits using an iterative Reinforcement Learning (RL) approach that trains a population of goal scoring agents. While previous approaches can already successfully find exploits, RL algorithms tend to overfit to a single solution. We introduce a simple extension on top of existing RL algorithms, such that they find multiple diverse high-quality solutions. For our first deployment of this approach, within a single experiment we were able to find six hockey scoring exploit strategies that were qualitatively similar to those that playtesters had found in hours-long manual testing sessions.

</details>


### [247] [TimEE: End-to-end Time Series Classification via In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07500)
*Jaris Küken,Shi Bin Hoo,Martin Mráz,Frank Hutter,Lennart Purucker*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TimEE is a 4.5M-parameter foundation model for end-to-end time series classification via in-context learning, trained purely on synthetic tasks and achieving state-of-the-art ROC AUC on UCR and strong accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional TSC follows a two-stage pipeline (feature encoding + classifier) which decouples representation learning from the classification objective and cannot leverage label information during inference. TimEE aims to perform end-to-end TSC with in-context learning using synthetic priors, reducing per-dataset training and allowing label information to influence predictions.

Method: TimEE adopts the PFN framework and is meta-trained exclusively on synthetic TSC tasks. Each task has time series with distinct class identities arising from structured distributional shifts in the generative process. At inference, given a labeled support set and a query time series, TimEE outputs a class distribution in a single forward pass without per-dataset training. The model has 4.5M parameters.

Result: On the UCR benchmark, TimEE ranks first in ROC AUC among all compared methods and third in accuracy, outperforming both foundation models and supervised baselines. It is claimed to be the first purely synthetic-pretrained model to reach state-of-the-art on UCR.

Conclusion: End-to-end in-context learning with synthetic priors is a promising direction for time series classification. Scaling, prior design, and richer synthetic generation are natural avenues for improvement and could further enhance performance.

Abstract: Time series classification (TSC) is dominated by a two-stage paradigm: train a feature encoder -- either from scratch on the target dataset or via pretraining on large corpora -- and then fit a task-specific classifier on top. While effective, this decoupling optimizes representation learning independently of the classification objective, requires per-dataset training, and prevents the model from exploiting label information during inference. We introduce TimEE, a 4.5M-parameter foundation model for end-to-end TSC via in-context learning. Given a labeled support set and a query time series, TimEE directly outputs a predicted class distribution in a single forward pass with no per-dataset training required. Following the prior-data fitted network (PFN) framework, TimEE is meta-trained exclusively on synthetic TSC tasks, where each task contains time series with distinct class identities arising from structured distributional shifts in the generative process. Despite seeing no real time series during pre-training, TimEE ranks first in ROC AUC (and third on accuracy) on the UCR benchmark among all compared methods, which include both foundation models and supervised deep learning baselines. To our knowledge, TimEE is the first purely synthetic-pretrained model to reach state-of-the-art performance on the UCR benchmark. These results establish end-to-end ICL with synthetic priors as a compelling, largely unexplored direction for TSC, with scaling, prior design, and richer generation mechanisms as natural avenues for improvement. Code is publicly available at http://github.com/automl/timee.

</details>


### [248] [Single-Rollout Asynchronous Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07508)
*Zhenyu Hou,Yujiang Li,Jie Tang,Yuxiao Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAO introduces single-rollout asynchronous optimization for RL in LLMs, replacing group-wise sampling to enhance stability and generalization; achieves strong results on agentic benchmarks and is deployed for GLM-5.2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asynchronous RL often prioritizes throughput at the expense of stability and task effectiveness; group-wise sampling in GRPO is ill-suited for long-horizon agentic training leading to off-policy instability.

Method: Use single-rollout sampling (one rollout per prompt) to mitigate off-policy effects; incorporate value-model training enhancements and a strict double-side token-level clipping strategy to stabilize optimization.

Result: SAO trains stably for 1000 steps and consistently outperforms GRPO and variants on SWE-Bench Verified, BeyondAIME, and IMOAnswerBench; effective in simulated online learning; deployed in open GLM-5.2 (750B-A40B).

Conclusion: Single-rollout asynchronous optimization effectively addresses stability/off-policy challenges in asynchronous RL for agentic LLM tasks, offering better generalization and online adaptability; scalable to large models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is becoming increasingly important for post-training large language models (LLMs). Previous RL pipelines for LLMs were mostly synchronous and batch-interleaved, which is inefficient for long-horizon agentic tasks. Recently, asynchronous RL has emerged as a more efficient alternative by updating the model as rollouts arrive. However, existing asynchronous RL systems often emphasize throughput, while leaving training stability and task effectiveness largely underexplored. For example, a key challenge is that group-wise sampling in the widely-used GRPO framework does not naturally fit asynchronous agentic training. In this paper, we present Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization (SAO) to address the stability and off-policy challenges in asynchronous RL. To reduce off-policy effects and improve generalization, we replace group-wise sampling with single-rollout sampling, that is, using one rollout per prompt. We further improve this single-rollout strategy with practical value-model training designs. To improve optimization stability, we introduce a strict double-side token-level clipping strategy. SAO is able to train stably for one thousand steps and consistently outperform GRPO and its variants on agentic coding and reasoning benchmarks, such as SWE-Bench Verified, BeyondAIME, and IMOAnswerBench. We also demonstrate that single-rollout RL is particularly effective in a simulated online learning setting, where the model must adapt to changing evolving environments. To this end, SAO is successfully deployed in the agentic RL pipeline for training the open GLM-5.2 model (750B-A40B).

</details>


### [249] [Fast Rates for Semi-Supervised Learning via Data-Augmentation Graph Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07513)
*Adam M. Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Self-supervised learning improves label efficiency by viewing augmentations as a similarity graph and proving a fast transductive rate O(1/n_L) for label-based error, with augmentation quality controlling the bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain theoretically why SSL can match supervised accuracy with far fewer labels and link augmentation quality to sample efficiency.

Method: Model the unlabeled data via a graph induced by augmentations and apply graph-Laplacian-regularized learning; extend Johnson–Zhang leave-one-out stability to the augmentation graph; use a streamlined loss that omits certain components; analyze the augmentation-kernel eigenspace.

Result: Derived a bound on error: E ≤ C/n_L + R_DA(y), with R_DA(y) the graph-cut mass of augmentations crossing a label boundary, and an O(1/n_L) transductive rate; in the infinite-data limit, top-K features are recovered; explains the full accuracy-vs-labels curve.

Conclusion: Theoretical explanation for SSL label efficiency via augmentation-induced graphs, clarifying the role of augmentation quality and providing an explicit error decomposition and more realistic assumptions than prior limit-based analyses.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning matches supervised accuracy from a fraction of the labels, but the labeled-sample efficiency behind this has lacked a theoretical explanation. We provide one. Data augmentation induces a similarity graph on the unlabeled data, so downstream learning on that graph is graph-Laplacian-regularized learning. We prove a fast transductive rate, $O(1/n_L)$ in the number of labels, in place of the supervised $O(1/\sqrt{n_L})$, by carrying the leave-one-out stability apparatus of Johnson and Zhang (JMLR 2007) over to the augmentation graph, and without the unrealistic assumptions of limit-based analyses (exact kernel, generalizing features). The bound makes augmentation quality explicit: the expected error is at most $C/n_L + R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$, where the data-augmentation alignment error $R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$ is the graph-cut mass of augmentations that cross a label boundary, so good augmentations let few labels suffice. The analysis uses a streamlined loss that drops the projector, negative-sample, and orthogonality overhead of standard objectives yet still recovers the top-$K$ ideal features in the infinite-data limit, the augmentation-kernel eigenspace studied by Zhai et al. The result explains the observed accuracy-versus-label-count curve rather than only bounding a generalization gap.

</details>


### [250] [Selective Timestep Weighting and Advantage-Based Replay for Sample-Efficient Diffusion RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07693)
*Eric Zhu,Abhinav Shrivastava,Soumik Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces per-timestep weighting and a trajectory replay mechanism to boost sample efficiency in diffusion RLHF, achieving up to 6x improvement over baselines while preserving generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion RLHF suffers from high feedback requirements; need methods to extract more learning signal from each reward and reuse past rewards.

Method: Two components: (1) a per-timestep denoising weight that reweights steps during PPO-based policy optimization, with a theoretical link to optimal PPO convergence and an empirical approximation of the weighting trend; (2) a replay mechanism that prioritizes informative trajectories to reuse rewards instead of querying new rewards.

Result: Significant improvement in feedback efficiency; up to 6x higher sample efficiency under identical hyperparameters compared to widely used diffusion RLHF baselines; retains generalization to unseen prompts.

Conclusion: Focusing on informative timesteps and trajectories yields substantial gains in diffusion RLHF efficiency; the proposed weighting and replay strategies offer a practical path to more data-efficient alignment of diffusion models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for aligning generative models with human preferences. However, applying RLHF to diffusion models remains highly feedback inefficient, as existing approaches typically require large amounts of human or reward model evaluations. This limitation reduces the practicality of diffusion RLHF in realworld settings where feedback is the primary bottleneck. In this paper, we propose two complementary strategies that substantially improve the feedback efficiency of diffusion RLHF while preserving generalization to unseen prompts. Our key observation is that reward information in diffusion trajectories is unevenly distributed: not all denoising timesteps or trajectories contribute equally to learning from a reward signal. By emphasizing informative timesteps and trajectories during optimization, we obtain more effective gradient updates. First, we introduce a per-timestep weighting scheme that reweights denoising steps during policy optimization. We theoretically connect this weighting to the optimal convergence properties of proximal policy optimization (PPO) and approximate the resulting weighting trend empirically. Second, we introduce a replay mechanism that prioritizes informative trajectories, enabling the model to reuse past samples instead of repeatedly querying new rewards. Together, these strategies significantly improve the feedback efficiency of diffusion RLHF. Under identical hyperparameter settings, our approach achieves up to a 6$\times$ improvement in sample efficiency compared to widely used diffusion RLHF baselines.

</details>


### [251] [Gradient-free Riemannian Langevin Sampler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07519)
*Ricardo Baptista,Olivier Zahm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gradient-free Riemannian Langevin sampler (GRiLS) samples multimodal distributions by reshaping local geometry with a Riemannian metric without gradients; mean/covariance are estimated from an ensemble of particles; empirical results show improved mixing over gradient-based and gradient-free baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MCMC struggles with multimodal targets and derivative-free settings; gradient evaluations are expensive or unavailable; need efficient exploration that uses only distribution moments.

Method: GRiLS defines a gradient-free Langevin proposal using a Riemannian metric constructed from estimated mean and covariance; these are obtained from an interacting-particle ensemble; the metric guides transitions across modes to improve mixing; no gradient of target required.

Result: Benchmark experiments indicate GRiLS achieves superior mixing relative to both gradient-based and gradient-free competitors on multimodal distributions.

Conclusion: GRiLS offers a practical gradient-free, geometry-aware sampler for complex targets; relies on reliable moment estimates; future work could address theoretical convergence, adaptivity of moment estimates, and scalability to high dimensions.

Abstract: We address the problem of efficiently sampling multimodal probability distributions, where standard Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods often suffer from poor mixing and mode trapping. To mitigate these issues, we propose Gradient-free Riemannian Langevin Sampler (GRiLS), a novel proposal that improves exploration without requiring gradient evaluations of the target density. Our approach introduces a Riemannian metric which reshapes the local geometry in order to facilitate transitions across modes. The resulting gradient-free MCMC algorithm is particularly suitable for complex, computationally expensive targets where derivatives are unavailable or impractical. The GRiLS proposal requires knowing the mean and covariance of the target density, which we estimate using an ensemble of interacting particles. Empirical results on multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that GRiLS achieves improved mixing compared to existing gradient-based and gradient-free MCMC approaches.

</details>


### [252] [Avoiding unsafe sets when training with Langevin Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07538)
*Adam M. Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Three probabilistic bounds for the safety of SGD-like training under noisy gradients, using overdamped Langevin dynamics on a smooth strongly convex loss: (i) end-state equilibrium mass in a failure region is exponentially small in dimension; (ii) a time-dependent, shape-free bound ν_t(A_H) ≤ π(A_H) [1 + sqrt(χ_0^2/π(A_H)) e^{-m t}] shows relaxation to twice the static value after burn-in ~ d; (iii) a local relaxation rate based on the spectral measure of the failure indicator tightens bounds for geometrically isolated failure regions, enabling a uniform-in-time cap when combined with a maximum principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify safety risk during training by bounding the probability that the trajectory enters a designated failure region, connecting to the energy landscape, spectral gaps, and local relaxation properties of the optimization dynamics.

Method: Model training with noisy gradients as overdamped Langevin dynamics on a smooth, strongly convex loss in d dimensions. Derive three bounds on the failure-region probability: (i) an equilibrium bound showing π(A_H) is exponentially small in d; (ii) a time-dependent, shape-free bound ν_t(A_H) ≤ π(A_H) (1 + (√(χ_0^2/π(A_H))) e^{-m t}) with burn-in on the order of d and using only the global spectral gap m; (iii) an enhanced local relaxation bound via a spectral measure of the centered indicator of the failure region, which can exceed the global rate for geometrically isolated sets; and a maximum-principle cap to bound the trajectory probability uniformly in time. An Ornstein-Uhlenbeck example is analyzed to illustrate the necessity of burn-in and to motivate the local-rate refinement.

Result: The equilibrium mass in the failure region decays exponentially with dimension; finite-time probability contracts toward at most twice the static mass after a burn-in of order d; a local relaxation rate based on the region’s spectral measure can tighten burn-in bounds for geometrically isolated failure regions; coupling with a maximum-principle cap yields a uniform-in-time bound on the trajectory probability.

Conclusion: Strong convexity governs global convergence speed, while the geometry of the unsafe set governs transient leakage. The interplay between global relaxation and local region structure determines practical safety guarantees for training with noise.

Abstract: Training a model with noisy gradient descent can be idealized as overdamped Langevin dynamics on the loss landscape, and a natural safety question is to bound the probability $ν_t(\mathcal{A}_H) = \mathbb{P}(Q_t \in \mathcal{A}_H)$ that the trajectory lies in a designated failure region $\mathcal{A}_H$. We study this for a smooth, strongly convex loss in $d$ dimensions and a failure region separated from the minimizer by an energy gap. Three bounds emerge. At the end of training, the equilibrium mass $π(\mathcal{A}_H)$ is exponentially small in $d$, with a complementary energy-barrier rate when the noise is small. Along the trajectory, a shape-free bound $ν_t(\mathcal{A}_H) \le π(\mathcal{A}_H)(1 + \sqrt{χ_0^2/π(\mathcal{A}_H)}\,e^{-mt})$ shows that the in-set probability relaxes to (twice) the static value after a burn-in time of order $d$, using only the global spectral gap $m$ of the loss. A worked Ornstein-Uhlenbeck example shows this burn-in is necessary: an angular slice of the equilibrium shell can transiently swell by a factor exponential in $d$, even though its equilibrium mass is tiny. To rule such swelling out we introduce a local relaxation rate attached to the failure region, defined through the spectral measure of its centered indicator rather than a Dirichlet-form Rayleigh quotient. For geometrically isolated regions this rate exceeds the global one, shrinking the burn-in proportionally, and combined with a maximum-principle ceiling it caps the trajectory probability uniformly in time. The picture is that strong convexity sets how fast training relaxes, but the shape of the unsafe set decides whether the trajectory bulges through it on the way home.

</details>


### [253] [Collaborative Synthetic Data Generation for Knowledge Transfer in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07565)
*Maximilian Andreas Hoefler,Karsten Mueller,Wojciech Samek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedKT-CSD enables private, one-shot federated learning by aggregating class-conditional latent statistics from clients using a public autoencoder latent space, applying secure aggregation and differential privacy to reconstruct a synthetic dataset for joint training. It delivers formal DP guarantees, low client overhead, and competitive performance under data heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the trade-off among low communication in one-shot federated learning, heterogeneity robustness, and formal privacy guarantees; prior methods lacked rigorous DP guarantees while achieving privacy through synthetic data or distillation.

Method: Clients encode private data once using a publicly pretrained autoencoder to obtain class-conditional latent statistics. These are sent to the server, which aggregates them with secure aggregation, adds calibrated differential privacy noise, and decodes a synthetic dataset from the shared latent space to train a global model and downstream tasks.

Result: The framework provides formal (ε,δ)-differential privacy by construction, keeps client-side computation/communication light, and is competitive with or outperforms non-private baselines across diverse datasets and heterogeneity settings, scaling to many clients.

Conclusion: FedKT-CSD presents a practical OSFL framework that achieves privacy guarantees and strong performance with efficient client workload, validated by experiments and code release.

Abstract: One-shot federated learning (OSFL) addresses the communication overhead of federated learning by limiting training to a single round, but doing so without sacrificing model quality is non-trivial, particularly when client data distributions diverge. Recent work has addressed this challenge by aggregating client knowledge on the server through the construction of transferable synthetic datasets or distillates. However, most of these methods lack formal privacy guarantees, leaving a gap in jointly achieving low communication, robustness to heterogeneity, and rigorous privacy. We propose FedKT-CSD (Federated Knowledge Transfer via Collaborative Synthetic Data), a framework inspired by neural image compression that closes this gap by leveraging publicly pretrained autoencoders as a shared latent space. Each client encodes its private data in a single forward pass, computes class-conditional latent statistics, and transmits these to the server. The server aggregates these statistics via secure aggregation, adds calibrated differential privacy noise, and decodes a synthetic dataset for training a global model and further downstream tasks. This design provides formal $(\varepsilon,δ)$-differential privacy by construction, while keeping client-side computation and communication lightweight. Despite operating under privacy constraints, FedKT-CSD is competitive with and even outperforms non-private baselines across diverse datasets and heterogeneity settings, and scales to a large number of clients. Our code is available at: https://github.com/an7123/FedKT-CSD

</details>


### [254] [Multi-Class vs. Multi-Label BERT for CVE-to-CWE Mapping: How Taxonomy Structure Shapes the Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07573)
*Ana Schwengber Kelm,Christian Bockermann,Jörg Frochte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-class text classification outperforms multi-label for assigning CWE categories to CVE records, with performance gains that shrink as the label space decreases; thresholding helps the multi-label setup, and error patterns align with the CWE taxonomy. CySecBERT leads, especially in multi-label, and hierarchy-aware evaluation reveals higher underlying classifier quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating CWE-to-CVE assignment is a key step in vulnerability analysis, but it remains largely manual. The study compares single-label vs multi-label formulations and assesses how model choice and taxonomy structure affect performance and error patterns.

Method: Evaluate three transformer encoders (BERT Base, SecureBERT, CySecBERT) on CVE text using three nested label spaces (83, 47, 25 CWE classes). Compare multi-class (single CWE per CVE) vs multi-label (multiple CWE per CVE) formulations. Apply post-hoc threshold optimization for the multi-label case. Conduct confusion analysis to inspect error patterns, and perform a hierarchy-relaxed evaluation that forgiving within-family confusions.

Result: Macro-F1 is higher for the multi-class setup across all label-space settings; the gap to multi-label narrows from 21 percentage points to 2 as the number of classes decreases. Post-hoc threshold tuning on the multi-label side closes the gap on the 25-class setting. Confusion patterns largely follow the CWE hierarchy and are similar across all three encoders (Pearson r > 0.92), suggesting taxonomy design drives errors more than encoder choice. Hierarchy-relaxed evaluation raises macro-F1 from ~81% to ~90%, indicating strict metrics understate branch-level classifier quality. CySecBERT achieves the strongest results overall, with statistically significant gains concentrated in the multi-label setting.

Conclusion: The predominant source of misclassification lies in the CWE taxonomy design rather than encoder differences. Hierarchy-aware evaluation reveals higher underlying classifier quality. CySecBERT is the strongest encoder, particularly in multi-label settings, but the best formulation (multi-class vs multi-label) depends on label-space size and tolerance to hierarchy-level confusions; thresholding can mitigate some multi-label weaknesses for smaller label spaces.

Abstract: Assigning Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) categories to Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) records remains an important but largely manual step in vulnerability analysis. We study this task as a text classification problem and compare two modelling choices: a \emph{multi-class} formulation that predicts a single CWE per CVE and a \emph{multi-label} formulation that allows multiple assignments. Three transformer encoders (BERT Base, SecureBERT, and CySecBERT) are evaluated on three nested label spaces (83, 47, and 25 classes). Multi-class training achieves higher macro-F1 across all settings, although the gap to multi-label narrows from 21 to 2 percentage points as the label space shrinks. Post-hoc threshold optimisation on the multi-label side closes this gap on the 25-class setting. Confusion analysis shows that the dominant misclassification patterns follow the CWE hierarchy and are shared across all three encoders (Pearson $r > 0.92$), which suggests that the error structure is driven more by taxonomy design than by encoder choice. A hierarchy-relaxed evaluation that forgives within-family confusions raises macro-F1 from ${\sim}$81\% to ${\sim}$90\%, indicating that strict metrics understate branch-level classifier quality. CySecBERT achieves the strongest results overall, with statistically significant gains concentrated in the multi-label setting.

</details>


### [255] [Asymmetric Focal Loss Improves Graph Neural Network Prediction of Drug-Drug Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07611)
*Faranak Hatami,Mousa Moradi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Asymmetric (ClinicalFocal) loss integrated into a relation-aware GCN significantly improves multi-relational DDI prediction on TWOSIDES, achieving large gains across accuracy, F1, AUROC, AUCPR, and recall without changing the network architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard binary cross-entropy treats easy and hard examples equally, potentially underemphasizing clinically important but difficult positive drug-drug interactions in polypharmacy side-effect prediction; an asymmetric focal approach aims to focus learning on difficult positives and class-imbalanced data.

Method: Incorporate ClinicalFocal loss into a relation-aware graph convolutional network using molecular fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors, and learned embeddings; evaluate on TWOSIDES with five-fold cross-validation under identical experimental conditions (architecture, features, data partitions, hyperparameters, seeds) for ClinicalFocal loss vs binary cross-entropy baseline.

Result: ClinicalFocal loss yielded substantial performance gains: accuracy 0.699→0.892; F1 0.700→0.894; AUROC 0.766→0.914; AUCPR 0.714→0.860; false-negative rate 29.8%→9.1%; specificity 69.6%→87.5%; overall error 30.1%→10.8% (64.1% relative reduction); improvements consistent across all five folds.

Conclusion: Asymmetric focal optimization improves both classification and ranking in graph-based DDI prediction without altering architecture, demonstrating that loss-function design is a tunable, effective lever for enhancing multi-relational DDI prediction.

Abstract: Background: Graph neural networks improve computational prediction of polypharmacy side effects, but standard binary cross-entropy training allocates equal capacity to well-classified and difficult examples, potentially missing clinically significant interactions. We evaluated whether an asymmetric focal objective could improve multi-relational drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction by emphasizing difficult positive interactions. Methods: ClinicalFocal loss was integrated into a relation-aware graph convolutional network using molecular fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors, and learned embeddings. The model was evaluated on TWOSIDES using five-fold cross-validation with identical experimental conditions (architecture, features, data partitions, hyperparameters, and random seeds) for ClinicalFocal loss and binary cross-entropy baseline. Results: ClinicalFocal loss increased accuracy from 0.699 to 0.892 (+19.3 percentage points) and F1 score from 0.700 to 0.894 (+19.4 percentage points). AUROC increased from 0.766 to 0.914, and AUCPR increased from 0.714 to 0.860. The false-negative rate decreased from 29.8% to 9.1%, while specificity increased from 69.6% to 87.5%. Overall classification error decreased from 30.1% to 10.8%, corresponding to a 64.1% relative reduction. Improvements were consistent across all five folds. Conclusions: Asymmetric focal optimization improved classification and ranking performance while achieving 90.9% recall for observed interaction triples, without modifying the underlying architecture. Loss-function design is a direct, tunable lever for improving graph-based DDI prediction.

</details>


### [256] [Higher-Order Geometric Updates for Levenberg-Marquardt Method via Riemann Normal Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07623)
*Jianing Liu,Dong H. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Riemann-normal-coordinate Levenberg-Marquardt (RNC-LM) improves finite-step consistency by extending geodesic acceleration to arbitrary order, reducing parameter-effects curvature in nonlinear least-squares; enhances convergence, robustness, and speed across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter-effects curvature can dominate nonlinearity in nonlinear least-squares; LM’s tangent-space update in parameter coordinates is inconsistent for finite steps. A finite-step geodesic correction is needed to better align updates with the underlying manifold.

Method: Reformulate the geodesic equation in Riemann normal coordinates, extend geodesic acceleration to higher-order corrections, and construct finite-step updates along an RNC curve with a line search to control step length while maintaining LM-like cost behavior; iteratively remove tangential residual acceleration in a moving tangent frame to improve reparameterization consistency.

Result: Improved convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems; on a reaction-diffusion PINN benchmark the relative L2 error reached ~1e-3; on a large-scale PES fitting task achieved ~34× speedup over standard LM.

Conclusion: RNC-LM provides higher-order finite-step corrections that remove parameter-effects curvature, yielding more consistent objective reduction and better performance of LM in challenging nonlinear least-squares problems across domains.

Abstract: Nonlinear least-squares optimization is central to regression, physics-informed neural networks, and other machine-learning tasks. Such problems have a natural geometric interpretation, model predictions form a manifold in data space, while the chosen parameterization can introduce parameter-effects curvature that becomes a dominant source of nonlinearity. This exposes a limitation of the Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) method, its tangent-space step is applied as a straight update in parameter coordinates. Geodesic acceleration gives a second-order correction, but its removal of parameter-effect curvature is exact only in the infinitesimal-step limit. We propose a Riemann-normal-coordinate Levenberg-Marquardt method (RNC-LM) to improve this consistency for finite optimization steps. By reformulating the geodesic equation, RNC-LM extends geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections and constructs finite-step updates with progressively higher reparameterization consistency. A line search along the resulting RNC curve controls the traveled distance while keeping the cost close to standard LM. The method eliminates the tangential component of residual acceleration order by order in a moving tangent frame, making the actual objective reduction more consistent with the linear model prediction of LM. On classical nonlinear least-squares benchmarks, RNC-LM improves convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems. On a reaction-diffusion PINN failure-mode benchmark, it reduces the relative L2 error to the order of 1e-3 and recovers a physically meaningful solution. On a large-scale machine-learning potential-energy-surface fitting task, it achieves a 34-fold speedup over standard LM.

</details>


### [257] [An optimal control approach for neural network architecture adaptation with a posteriori error estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07637)
*C G Krishnanunni,Thomas Scott,Tan Bui-Thanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive depth neural networks guided by a posteriori error estimates within a continuous-time optimal control framework, inserting layers at maximum estimated error using a dual weighted residual bound; shows improved generalization on Navier–Stokes mappings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiently allocate network capacity across depth by quantifying layer-wise approximation error from a principled error estimator, enabling principled architecture refinement for complex, nonlinear problems.

Method: Formulate NN training as a continuous-time optimal control problem; derive layer-wise error estimates and an error decomposition; model weights/biases as piecewise-linear across layers; apply dual weighted residual (DWR) methodology to obtain computable upper bounds; derive interval-wise error contributions to guide layer insertion; insert new layers at locations with maximal estimated error.

Result: The approach yields a theoretically grounded adaptive-depth architecture; numerical experiments on scientific problems (e.g., Navier–Stokes observable-to-parameter map) show improved generalization compared to existing architecture-adaptation methods.

Conclusion: The work provides a rigorous, computable framework for depth-adaptive neural networks with explicit error bounds, enabling targeted refinement and potential applicability to broader PDE-informed learning tasks.

Abstract: This work presents a novel approach for adapting neural network architecture along the depth based on a posteriori error estimation. By formulating neural network training as a continuous-time optimal control problem, we derive rigorous error estimates that quantify how approximation error distributes across network layers. This error decomposition enables a principled depth adaptation strategy: new layers are inserted at locations of maximum estimated error, allowing the network to efficiently capture complex, nonlinear variations in the underlying problem. Our framework introduces a novel network architecture that treats weights and biases as piecewise linear functions varying across layers, with the error estimator bounding the discrepancy between this discrete representation and the true continuous optimal control solution. The approach leverages dual weighted residual methodology from finite element analysis to derive computable upper bounds on the functional error. A key theoretical contribution is the derivation of explicit error bounds that decompose the total approximation error into interval-wise contributions, providing a rigorous basis for targeted architecture refinement. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on scientific datasets, including learning the observable-to-parameter map for the Navier-Stokes equation. Numerical results reveal that our approach consistently outperforms existing architecture adaptation methods in terms of generalization performance.

</details>


### [258] [ALER-TI: Aligned Latent Embedding Retrieval for Time Series Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07640)
*Xuan-Thong Truong,Trung-Kien Le,Tung Kieu,Thi-Thu Nguyen,Nhat-Hai Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALER-TI is a retrieval-augmented time series imputation framework that uses Latent Embedding Alignment (LEA) to align historical candidates with a corrupted query's missingness pattern, enabling pre-computed histories and a model-agnostic adapter for improved imputation across datasets and missing rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard imputers rely on localized temporal context, which fails under non-stationarity, weak correlations, or rare patterns. Leveraging long-term historical patterns can improve missing-value reconstruction.

Method: LEA aligns latent embeddings between corrupted queries and complete historical candidates via post-hoc latent masking. Historical embeddings are pre-computed and cached for efficient retrieval. ALER-TI serves as a lightweight, model-agnostic adapter that can be integrated with various imputation backbones.

Result: Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets across different missing rates show that ALER-TI consistently improves strong baseline models and enhances robustness in diverse imputation scenarios.

Conclusion: Retrieval-augmented imputation with LEA mitigates representation mismatch between queries and historical candidates, enabling efficient, model-agnostic enhancement of time series imputation by leveraging historical patterns.

Abstract: Deep learning has significantly advanced time series imputation, yet most existing architectures primarily rely on localized temporal context within the corrupted input sequence. This reliance can be limiting in real-world scenarios, where time series often exhibit non-stationary dynamics, weak temporal correlations, and infrequent patterns that are difficult to reconstruct from nearby observations alone. In this paper, we propose ALER-TI, Aligned Latent Embedding Retrieval for Time Series Imputation, a retrieval-augmented framework that explicitly leverages historical patterns to supplement degraded local context for more reliable missing-value reconstruction. The core of ALER-TI is Latent Embedding Alignment (LEA), which mitigates the representation mismatch between corrupted queries and complete historical candidates. By applying post-hoc masking in the latent space, LEA aligns candidates with the query's missingness pattern while allowing historical embeddings to be pre-computed and cached for efficient retrieval. ALER-TI is model-agnostic and can be integrated with various imputation backbones through a lightweight adaptation module. Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets under different missing rates demonstrate that ALER-TI consistently improves strong baseline models and enhances robustness across diverse imputation settings.

</details>


### [259] [Guidance Breaks the Fitted Operator: A Terminal-Fitted Repair for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07665)
*Shiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A simple, one-coefficient repair to classifier-free guidance reduces oversaturation and sigma_min blow-up in high-guidance sampling, improving stability and point-FID on tested models, but is not a universal win.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG strengthens class-conditioning but causes oversaturation and instability at high guidance. The paper frames this with an asymptotic-preserving perspective to understand the discriminative subspace dynamics and proposes a minimal fix.

Method: An asymptotic-preserving numerical-analysis treatment of CFG and DDIM is used. The authors show that guidance re-stiffens the discriminative subspace to an anomalous exponent 1+w, causing residual divergence as sigma_min → 0. They prove a guided clock barrier with three step-size thresholds and derive a one-coefficient replacement for the guidance term: replace w(r-1) with r^(1+w)-r in the guidance direction. The repair is evaluated on calibration models, CIFAR-10 checkpoints, and Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM as a cross-domain test, assessing residual amplification, saturation, point-FID, and classifier-proxy accuracy.

Result: The replacement stabilizes high-guidance sampling: it reduces residual amplification and oversaturation, yielding 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG on the tested grid and preserving target accuracy in hard blocks, without extra computational cost. It acts as a high-guidance stabilizer rather than a universal quality knob.

Conclusion: The proposed one-coefficient repair is a practical, first-order accurate fix for CFG under high guidance, improving stability and some quality metrics on specific models and domains. It is not a universal image-quality win and may not uniformly outperform standard CFG across all settings.

Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is the standard way to strengthen class-conditioning in diffusion and flow-matching samplers, yet at large guidance it oversaturates and destabilizes, symptoms practitioners suppress with more steps or limited-interval schedules. We analyze CFG through an asymptotic-preserving, numerical-analysis lens. Building on a recent result that the deterministic DDIM step is the unique fitted operator for the unguided terminal layer, exact on the final small-sigma stretch of sampling, we show that guidance re-stiffens exactly the discriminative subspace to an anomalous exponent 1+w. DDIM is therefore no longer fitted there, and on coarse meshes its guided residual diverges as sigma_min goes to zero. We prove a guided clock barrier with three ordered step-size thresholds, and read one-step oversaturation as its endpoint: a solver artifact on the calibration model rather than the continuous guided law. The same analysis yields a one-coefficient, zero-extra-NFE repair: replace CFG's w(r-1) by r^(1+w)-r on the guidance direction. On the calibration model's discriminative crossover, this removes CFG's sigma_min-divergent blow-up and is first-order accurate against the exact guided flow as sigma_min goes to zero. On learned CIFAR-10 checkpoints, and as a cross-domain smoke test on Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM, it acts as a high-guidance stabilizer at no extra cost rather than a universal quality knob: it cuts residual amplification and saturation, gives 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG on the tested grid, and preserves classifier-proxy target accuracy in the hard-cell blocks. We report the limits alongside: it is not a universal image-quality win, and against a dense vanilla-CFG reference it is not a uniformly better integrator of that field.

</details>


### [260] [PeTeR: Post-Training Robustification of Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07671)
*Adrian Ciotinga,Yeming Dai,YooJung Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A data-free post-training framework (PeTeR) robustifies pre-trained probabilistic circuits against distribution shifts within a Wasserstein DRO setting, without retraining from scratch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard likelihood-based learning for probabilistic circuits is prone to overfitting and fragile generalization under data noise, small sample sizes, or distribution shifts; a data-free robustness approach is desirable to improve reliability without costly retraining.

Method: PeTeR introduces a data-free post-training procedure that enforces distributional robustness by considering worst-case perturbations within a Wasserstein ball around the empirical distribution, applied to pre-trained probabilistic circuits without retraining from scratch.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multiple density-estimation benchmarks show that PeTeR robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, achieving competitive or superior performance to data-dependent robust learning baselines.

Conclusion: PeTeR demonstrates that robustness to distribution shifts can be achieved post-training on pre-trained probabilistic circuits, avoiding full retraining while delivering competitive robustness to both random and adversarial perturbations.

Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) can model complex joint distributions while supporting exact and efficient computation of many inference queries. However, standard likelihood-based PC learning is vulnerable to overfitting and fragile generalization when confronted with data noise, small sample sizes, or distribution shifts. This can be mitigated using distributionally-robust optimization which consider worst-case distributions within a Wasserstein ball of the empirical distribution, but current methods are limited to training a model from scratch in this framework. Instead, we propose PeTeR: a novel, data-free post-training framework designed to robustify pre-trained PCs against distribution shifts without retraining from scratch. Empirical evaluations across multiple density estimation benchmarks demonstrate that PeTeR effectively robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, achieving competitive or superior performance to data-dependent robust learning baselines.

</details>


### [261] [Max Out GRPO Signal: Adaptive Trace Prefix Control for Hard Reasoning Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07674)
*Vladislav Beliaev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaPrefix-GRPO introduces an adaptive prefixing mechanism to maintain a ~50% success rate during training, turning the difficulty knob into a feedback controller. It significantly improves GRPO performance on hard problems, especially for smaller models, while halving trace length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO stalls when no rollout in a group succeeds, yielding vanishing gradients on hard problems. The work seeks to recover gradient signal by adaptively varying problem difficulty via reference-prefix prefixes, turning static difficulty into a controllable, responsive signal.

Method: Prepend a reference-prefix of varying length to problems, controlled by a feedback loop to keep per-problem success around 50%. Loss includes a mask on prefix tokens; data preparation is the main modification, training remains otherwise standard.

Result: On hard math tasks at matched FLOPs: for a 0.6B model, accuracy more than doubles GRPO (about 2.1x); 1.6x for Qwen3-1.7B; 1.7x on AIME; trace length is roughly halved. Gains are larger for smaller models.

Conclusion: AdaPrefix-GRPO provides a simple, effective augmentation to GRPO by turning the prefix length into a dynamic controller, yielding substantial gains on hard problems with modest training overhead and especially benefiting smaller models.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) stalls on a model's hardest problems: when no rollout in a group succeeds, the group-relative advantages vanish and the problem contributes no gradient, wasting the frontier examples we most want to learn from. Prepending a correct prefix of a reference solution raises the success rate, making prefix length a continuous knob on difficulty. Concurrent methods set the knob once; AdaPrefix-GRPO turns it into a feedback controller: throughout training it adjusts how much of the solution each problem gets, holding its success rate near 50%, where GRPO's gradient signal is largest, then withdraws the assistance entirely, so the deployed model solves problems unaided. On hard math, at matched training FLOPs, it more than doubles GRPO's accuracy on held-out problems from the training distribution for a 0.6B model (2.1x), with 1.6x on Qwen3-1.7B and 1.7x on AIME, while roughly halving trace length. The method is implemented in data preparation plus a loss mask on prefix tokens; the trainer is otherwise stock. The smaller the model, the larger the gain.

</details>


### [262] [Agon: Competitive Cross-Model RL with Implicit Rival Grading of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07690)
*Vladislav Beliaev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Agon trains two models as mutual graders in a competitive setup; they draft and solve, with rewards based on out-performing the rival; enables implicit evaluation of reasoning during training, yielding strong gains on hard problems and generalization across domains; inference uses a two-stage draft-then-answer cascade.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fix the limitation of reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards that only grades final answers (e.g., GRPO). The reasoning process is not rewarded, leading to underdevelopment of thinking on hard problems. Agon uses opponent-driven evaluation to promote better reasoning traces without an explicit reward model.

Method: Two models alternate roles as solver and grader. One drafts a solution; the other reads and solves, and both are rewarded for out-solving the rival. This creates progressively stronger adversaries, solving via competitive reasoning. At inference, the pair forms a two-stage cascade: one drafts, the other answers after reading. Training is self-contained—no external reward model or process labels.

Result: On the DeepMath-hard split with Qwen3, Agon doubles GRPO’s pass@1 and yields roughly an eightfold gain over an untrained Mixture-of-Agents baseline. The ordering generalizes to competitive-programming code and across model families (Qwen3.5, Gemma 4).

Conclusion: The paper suggests extending to latent-space cooperation where the two agents reason together, and deploying the pair as it trains. This approach relies on comparably strong, behaviorally distinct models and reduces dependence on a separate reward model.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (e.g. GRPO) is the engine behind today's reasoning models, yet it grades only the final answer. On hard problems this trains models to write more rather than to think better, since the trace itself is never graded and no label for good thinking exists. We introduce Agon, which makes two competing models each other's graders. Both attempt the same problem; in alternating roles, one drafts a solution and the other reads it while solving, and each is rewarded for out-solving the other. To win, a model must out-reason a rival that has seen its work, so reasoning is judged implicitly during training, with no process labels and no reward model. Because both models are optimized, each faces a progressively stronger rival, which single-model RL cannot provide. The two need only be comparably strong and behaviorally different. At inference the pair deploys as it trains, a two-stage cascade in which one model drafts and the other answers after reading the draft. On the hard split of DeepMath with Qwen3, this doubles GRPO's pass@1, roughly eight times the gain of an untrained Mixture-of-Agents pass over the same base. The ordering replicates on competitive-programming code and across model families (Qwen3.5, Gemma 4). For now the models talk in text; the next step is to let them reason together in latent space.

</details>


### [263] [How Data Shapes RoPE Frequency Usage: From Positional Scale Matching to Length Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07678)
*Xinyi Wu,Siyuan Liu,Ali Jadbabaie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoPE frequency usage in transformers appears data-driven: optimal RoPE frequencies align with the data’s dependency width W, scaling as 1/W. This explains why mid-to-low frequency bands emerge in language models and informs long-context generalization via scale-matching between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how those dependencies extend to longer contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why trained RoPE frequencies are non-uniform and how they relate to the structure of training data; provide a theoretical mechanism for frequency selection and implications for long-context generalization.

Method: Develop a data-centered theoretical framework: treat each RoPE frequency as a positional lens and formalize a field-resolution tradeoff. Derive that the optimal frequency scales as 1/W for a data-induced dependency width W. Validate with observations on synthetic and text data, and connect to length generalization via frequency scaling and self-similarity across positional scales.

Result: A frequency-matching principle emerges: RoPE frequencies are selected to match the training data’s relative-distance structure. Mid-low frequencies reflect the multi-scale dependencies of natural language. Scaling frequencies down expands the effective field and can aid longer-context generalization when dependencies dilate with context, but may fail if dependencies do not scale with context length. Empirical evidence shows approximate self-similarity across positional scales in natural language, supporting test-time frequency scaling as a tool for long-context generalization.

Conclusion: Long-context generalization with RoPE relies on two scale-matching forms: (i) aligning learned frequencies with training-time dependency widths, and (ii) aligning frequency scaling with how those dependencies extend to longer contexts. This data-driven mechanism explains emergent RoPE usage and informs adaptive or data-aware frequency design.

Abstract: Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) provide transformers with a fixed grid of positional frequencies, yet trained models use these frequencies highly non-uniformly. We study what determines this frequency usage and propose a data-centered explanation: RoPE frequencies are selected to match the relative-distance structure of the training data. Viewing each frequency as a positional lens, we formalize a field-resolution tradeoff and show that, for a data-induced dependency profile of width $W$, the optimal frequency scales as $1/W$. This frequency-matching principle explains controlled observations on synthetic and text-based data, and suggests that the mid-low frequency bands observed in language models arise from the multi-scale dependency structure of natural language. We further connect frequency selection to position-interpolation-based length generalization: scaling frequencies down expands the effective field while reducing resolution. This helps when longer-context dependencies are approximate dilations of those seen during training, but can fail when relevant dependencies do not scale with context length. Empirically, we show that natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales, explaining why test-time frequency scaling can support long-context generalization. Overall, our results identify a data-driven mechanism behind emergent RoPE frequency usage and show that long-context generalization depends on two forms of scale matching: between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how those dependencies extend to longer contexts.

</details>


### [264] [Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy for PDE-Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07682)
*Xiangming Huang,Guannan Zhang,Lu Lu,Raphaël Pestourie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neural-operator-enabled topology-informed evolutionary strategy (NOTES) integrates DeepONet with CMA-ES for efficient, transferable inverse design in high-dimensional PDE-constrained problems; demonstrated on nanophotonic beam deflectors and structural optimization, achieving strong dimensionality reduction and performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle the computational burden, non-convexity, and limited transferability of inverse design in PDE-governed systems. Combine a neural operator to learn topology priors with robust global optimization to enable efficient design search across unseen operating conditions.

Method: Couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with CMA-ES, performing global optimization in a compact latent space that encodes topology-aware priors. Dimensionality reduction from 256 to 25 enables efficient search, with topology-learning decoupled from the governing physics in a PDE solver.

Result: Significant dimensionality reduction (256 → 25) and consistently high efficiency (>95%). Outperforms CMA-ES, topology optimization, and baselines. Validated on nanophotonic beam-deflector inverse design (Maxwell's equations) and structural optimization achieving low compliance.

Conclusion: NOTES provides a flexible, transferable framework for PDE-governed inverse design by decoupling topology learning from the solver and leveraging a neural operator-guided latent space for robust, high-performance design under unseen conditions.

Abstract: The inverse design of physical systems governed by partial differential equations is computationally demanding due to the high dimensionality and non-convexity of design spaces. Generative models for inverse design often lack robustness and transferability, whereas evolutionary strategies are robust but struggle in high-dimensional spaces. This paper introduces a Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy (NOTES) that integrates dimensionality reduction, representation learning, and evolutionary optimization for efficient and transferable inverse design. NOTES couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with the Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES) to perform global optimization in a compact latent space that encodes topology-aware priors while discovering high-performance designs for unseen operating conditions. Applied to nanophotonic beam-deflector inverse design governed by Maxwell's equations, NOTES reduces the design dimensionality from 256 to 25 and consistently achieves over 95 percent efficiency, outperforming CMA-ES, topology optimization, and other baselines. Applied to structural optimization, NOTES discovers designs that achieve compliance down to 246. By decoupling topology learning of a DeepONet from the governing physics in a PDE solver, NOTES provides a flexible and transferable framework for the inverse design of physical systems.

</details>


### [265] [ECGLight: Compute-Light Framework For Paper ECG Digitization and Myocardial Infarction Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07683)
*Shreyasvi Natraj,Cyrus Achtari,Felice Gragnano,Andrea Milzi,Marco Valgimigli,Diego Paez-Granados*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: End-to-end on-device pipeline digitizes paper ECGs from smartphone images into calibrated 12-lead signals and detects MI/OMI with SHAP explanations; CPU-only and under 30 seconds per ECG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many remote clinics rely on paper ECGs with limited connectivity and compute; on-device digitization is needed to democratize access to AI-based decision support and reduce treatment delays for MI.

Method: An end-to-end lightweight pipeline that converts smartphone photos/scans of paper ECGs into calibrated 12-lead signals, followed by on-device MI/OMI classification with SHAP-based interpretability. Trained on PTB-XL (21,799 ECGs) and validated on ECG-Matrix; runs on CPU-only hardware (<30 s per ECG).

Result: MI detection on PTB-XL: 95.51% accuracy (F1 = 0.9519). OMI detection on ECG-Matrix: 88.89% accuracy (F1 = 0.8862).

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of digitizing legacy paper records on-device, enabling scalable decision support in low-connectivity settings and broad democratization of ECG analytics.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG) is one of the most widely used tests for diagnosing cardiovascular disease. Yet several remote clinics still utilize paper ECG printouts for their analysis due to limited connectivity and computational capacity. As a result, vast numbers of physical ECGs obtained in remote areas still remain incapable of being accessed by contemporary artificial-intelligence (AI)-based decision support as they require high computational resources or strong high-speed internet connectivity. This causes several cases where conditions like acute coronary occlusion (ACS) is overlooked and reperfusion therapy delayed. Although prior work has tackled digitization and diagnosis separately, and utilized advanced AI models for them, there still remains a lack of a compute-light, on-device framework that reconstructs paper ECGs at high fidelity, while accurately supporting multiple clinically relevant endpoints. We address this need with an end-to-end lightweight on-device digitization-to-diagnosis pipeline that converts a smartphone photo or scan of a paper ECG into a calibrated 12-lead signal and screens for Myocardial Infarction (MI) pathologies, with SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to support interpretability. Trained and evaluated on 21,799 ECGs from the PTB-XL dataset and further validated on hospital-acquired ECG-Matrix dataset, the complete system runs in <30 s per ECG on CPU-only resources, achieving 95.51% accuracy (F1 = 0.9519) for MI detection on PTB-XL and 88.89% accuracy (F1 = 0.8862) for OMI detection on ECG-Matrix. This work showcases that legacy paper records can be reliably democratized in any part of the world, providing a scalable decision support when digital ECG export, connectivity, or high-end compute are unavailable

</details>


### [266] [The Key to Going Linear: Analysis-Driven Transformer Linearization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07706)
*Anna Kuzina,Paul N. Whatmough,Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A structured linearization of causal self-attention reduces its quadratic cost while preserving accuracy, via state-update design insights and targeted architectural interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic complexity of standard causal self-attention bottlenecks long-context transformers; in a frozen-backbone regime, identify which components most affect model quality during linearization.

Method: Analyze state-update behavior under linearized attention; show softmax relies on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections; identify potential false-positives/approximation errors; introduce structural interventions—sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing—and validate across models.

Result: Scale the linearization across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters; outperform prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and match long-context retrieval of complex adaptive-caching frameworks; reduce the remaining performance gap.

Conclusion: Structural interventions can tightly control approximation errors in linearized attention without retraining, providing competitive long-context capabilities and scalable inference for large LLMs.

Abstract: The quadratic cost of causal self-attention severely bottlenecks long-context transformer inference. While numerous post hoc linearization pipelines exist, it is difficult to identify which components preserve model quality. This work isolates the effect of state update design in a strict frozen-backbone regime. We show that softmax relies on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, elucidating why delta-style networks outperform purely gated accumulation. We identify a potential source of approximation errors and introduce structural interventions, specifically sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing, which reduces the remaining gap. We scale this linearization approach across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters, outperforming prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matching the long-context retrieval of complex adaptive-caching frameworks.

</details>
